# Paper 3 – Subspecialities of Urology: All Answers by Section

**Compiled from:** 2015–2025 Paper 3 (12 papers) and Practice Sets 1–8 Paper 3, plus the earlier version of Practice Set 6 Paper 3 Q3c — 161 answers.
**Repeats merged:** questions asked more than once are printed once, with every wording and source listed under "Asked as"; their answers are combined so that no fact from any version is lost. 36 merged topics replace 85 original answers; 112 topics in all.
**Layout:** the 17 revision-book sections; in each, long answer questions (LAQ) first, then short answer questions (SAQ), then a Rapid Revision Sheet of that section's key points.

# Section 1 · Basic Principles — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Therapeutic energy sources in urology (including laparoscopic energy devices)
**Asked as:**
- enumerate various therapeutic energy applications in urology and briefly give their present status in various urological diseases. — *Practice Set 8 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)* (energy physics also answered in 2025-Winter Paper 4, Q2)
- Energy sources in lap uro. — *Practice Set 7 Paper III Q3a (SAQ)* (also answered in 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q3d)

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

### Introduction and principles
- Therapeutic energy is absorbed by tissue or stone and released as **heat, mechanical stress, cavitation or a chemical reaction**. Wavelength or frequency sets penetration; absorption sets the effect.
- All (haemostatic) devices achieve haemostasis by **denaturing protein**, by heat (current, light) or mechanical vibration.
- Heat effects: about **60 °C** coagulates protein; **100 °C** vaporises cell water; higher temperatures carbonise.

### Classification
| Group | Modalities |
|---|---|
| Electrical | Monopolar and bipolar electrosurgery, vessel sealers, RFA (radiofrequency ablation), IRE (irreversible electroporation), EMDA (electromotive drug administration) |
| Electromagnetic | Lasers, MWA (microwave ablation), chemohyperthermia, PDT (photodynamic therapy), ionising radiation |
| Acoustic | SWL (shock wave lithotripsy), intracorporeal ultrasonic and pneumatic lithotripsy, HIFU (high-intensity focused ultrasound), histotripsy, burst wave lithotripsy, Li-ESWT (low-intensity shock wave therapy) |
| Direct contact | Cryoablation, water vapour (Rezum), waterjet (Aquablation) |

### 1. Electrosurgery
- **Principle:** **Electrosurgery** uses high-frequency alternating current at about **500 kHz** that heats tissue (the resistance) without nerve stimulation (does not stimulate nerve or muscle). **Current density** decides heating (the effect): the small tip burns, the large pad does not. Cut = continuous low voltage; coagulation = interrupted high voltage.

| Mode | Waveform | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| **Cut** (50–80 W) | Continuous, low voltage | Cell water flash-boils; clean cut |
| **Coagulation** (30–50 W) | Interrupted (about 6% duty cycle), high voltage | Desiccation, deeper spread |
| Fulguration (spray) | Higher voltage, sparks | Surface charring of oozing beds |
| Blend | Cut with interruptions | Cut plus coagulation |

- **Status:**
  - **TURP (transurethral resection of prostate):** still the reference BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) operation. **Bipolar** in saline matches monopolar efficacy and avoids TUR syndrome (glycine hyponatraemia).
  - **TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour):** standard; obturator reflex risk on lateral wall. En bloc resection is increasing.
  - Vessel sealers (up to 7 mm vessels) are routine in laparoscopic and robotic surgery.
- **EMDA–MMC (mitomycin C):** iontophoresis raises drug uptake in NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer); evidence low quality, not standard.

#### Laparoscopic energy devices
| Device | Principle | Lateral spread | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Monopolar** (hook, scissors) | Tip → patient → return pad | **5–7 mm** | Versatile; stray-current risk |
| **Bipolar** | Current only between jaws | 1–6 mm | No pad; no capacitive coupling |
| **Vessel sealer** (LigaSure) | Bipolar + compression + impedance feedback; built-in blade | 1–3 mm | Seals vessels **≤7 mm**; one application |
| **Ultrasonic** (Harmonic) | Blade vibrating at **25–55 kHz** | **≈1 mm** | No current: safe with pacemakers; jaws **>200 °C**, slow to cool, so do not touch bowel |
| **Argon beam** | Current through argon, non-contact | Superficial | Liver, spleen, raw surfaces; gas embolism |

- **Also know:** lasers (KTP [potassium titanyl phosphate], holmium) rarely used; laparoscopic RFA (radiofrequency ablation, 60 °C) and cryoablation (below −40 °C, two freeze–thaw cycles); robotic HotShears, bipolar Maryland, Vessel Sealer.

#### Electrosurgical injuries
| Mechanism | Prevention |
|---|---|
| Direct activation out of view | Activate only with tip in view |
| **Direct coupling** to another metal instrument | Whole metal tip visible |
| **Capacitive coupling** (metal cannula in plastic retainer) | Never mix metal and plastic ports |
| **Insulation failure** | Inspect; **AEM (active electrode monitoring)** |
| Pad burns; surgical fire | Pad on muscle, away from implants; REM (return electrode monitoring) |
- Bowel injuries present late (days) as peritonitis.

### 2. Lasers
| Laser | nm | Penetration | Present status |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Ho:YAG** (holmium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet) | 2120 | 0.31 mm | Gold standard lithotripsy; **HoLEP** (holmium laser enucleation of prostate) size-independent; strictures, UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma) ablation |
| **TFL** (thulium fibre laser) | 1940 | 0.08 mm | Stones: beats low-power holmium, equals high-power; less retropulsion; ThuLEP (thulium laser enucleation of prostate) |
| **KTP/LBO** (potassium-titanyl-phosphate/lithium triborate; GreenLight) | 532 | 0.8 mm | Photovaporisation; can continue anticoagulation |
| Tm:YAG (thulium:YAG) | 2000 | 0.2 mm | Vaporesection, enucleation |
| Nd:YAG (neodymium:YAG) | 1064 | 10 mm | Deep coagulation (penile CIS [carcinoma in situ], warts); largely obsolete for BPH |

- **Mechanism:** pulse duration decides: long pulses photothermal; short pulses photomechanical.

### 3. Acoustic energy
- **SWL:** shock waves break brittle stones by spallation, shear and cavitation. **Status:** first line for renal stones ≤2 cm and favourable upper ureteric stones; slower rate (about 1 Hz) works better.
- **Intracorporeal:** pneumatic, ultrasonic and combined probes in PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy); electrohydraulic now rare (ureteric injury).
- **HIFU:** focused beam heats a rice-grain focus to necrosis. **Status:** focal or salvage prostate cancer (best for posterior tumours); investigational in guidelines.
- **Histotripsy, burst wave lithotripsy, ultrasonic propulsion:** investigational.
- **Li-ESWT:** for vasculogenic ED (erectile dysfunction) and Peyronie pain; evidence still limited.

### 4. Thermal ablation of tumours
| Modality | Mechanism | Present status |
|---|---|---|
| **Cryoablation** | Argon expansion (Joule–Thomson); **<−40 °C, two freeze–thaw cycles**; visible ice ball | Small renal mass ≤3–4 cm in frail patients; focal or salvage prostate |
| **RFA** | 375–900 kHz; target 60 °C; char at 150 °C | Small renal mass; oncological results similar to cryo; heat sink near vessels |
| **MWA** | 915 MHz or 2.45 GHz; dielectric hysteresis | Renal mass; hotter, less heat sink |
| **IRE** | Microsecond DC (direct current) pulses open permanent membrane pores; non-thermal | Focal prostate; good continence and potency; trials ongoing |

- **Guidelines:** AUA (American Urological Association) 2021: consider thermal ablation for **cT1a ≤3 cm**; biopsy before or at ablation. EAU (European Association of Urology): not routinely >3 cm (cryo >4 cm).

### 5. Light and chemical energy
- **PDT:** photosensitiser + light → singlet oxygen. **VTP (vascular-targeted photodynamic therapy, padeliporfin)** for low-risk prostate cancer: RCT (Azzouzi 2017) halved progression versus AS (active surveillance; 28% vs 58%); not widely adopted. **Photodynamic diagnosis** (blue light) aids TURBT.
- **Chemohyperthermia:** MMC with bladder wall heated to **42 °C** for NMIBC; used after BCG (bacillus Calmette-Guérin) failure or shortage; not standard.

### 6. Prostate (BPH) newer energies
- **Rezum:** steam (103 °C) releases latent heat by convection; no char, zone-confined; preserves ejaculation.
- **Aquablation:** robotic non-thermal waterjet; good for large glands; haemostasis needed.
- **Also know:** TUMT (transurethral microwave thermotherapy) and TUNA (transurethral needle ablation) largely abandoned.

### 7. Ionising radiation
- EBRT (external beam radiotherapy) and **brachytherapy** (LDR [low dose rate] or HDR [high dose rate]): standard for localised prostate cancer; trimodality bladder preservation; penile cancer (brachytherapy for small T1–T2 glans tumours). **Radioligand therapy** (¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA [prostate-specific membrane antigen], VISION trial) and **radium-223** (ALSYMPCA) for mCRPC (metastatic castration-resistant prostate cancer).

**Diagram to draw:** monopolar vs bipolar circuits; laser penetration depths compared, with cryoprobe ice ball.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Spread: monopolar 5–7 > bipolar 1–6 > sealer 1–3 > ultrasonic ≈1 mm.
> - LigaSure seals ≤7 mm; ultrasonic blade hottest after use.
> - Capacitive coupling: avoid hybrid trocars; AEM detects it.
> - Holmium 2120 nm: gold standard for stones and HoLEP.
> - Bipolar TURP in saline avoids TUR syndrome.
> - Thermal ablation for cT1a ≤3 cm; cryo <−40 °C, two cycles.
> - HIFU, IRE, VTP: focal prostate therapy, still evolving.

# Section 1 · Basic Principles — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Asymptomatic haematuria and urinary biomarkers
**Asked as:**
- Asymptomatic Hematuria — *Summer 2016 Paper III Q3d (SAQ)*
- Urinary biomarkers in diagnostic evaluation of hematuria. — *Summer 2021 Paper III Q3f (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Introduction and definitions
- **MH (microhaematuria)**: **3 or more RBCs/HPF (red blood cells per high-power field)** on microscopy of one properly collected sample (AUA/SUFU (American Urological Association/Society of Urodynamics, Female Pelvic Medicine and Urogenital Reconstruction) 2020). A dipstick alone is not enough (myoglobin, semen and peroxidases give false positives).
- **GH (gross haematuria)**: visible; exclude pigmenturia and menstrual blood.
- Prevalence about 6.5%. Malignancy: about 3% of MH; 14–26% of GH (DETECT I, IDENTIFY).
- Haematuria is evaluated by **cystoscopy and upper tract imaging**; urine tests are non-invasive **adjuncts** for bladder cancer.

### Causes
- **Neoplasm**: bladder (over 85% of cancers found), UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma), RCC (renal cell carcinoma) about 1 in 1000 each, prostate.
- **Benign**: BPE (benign prostatic enlargement), stones, UTI (urinary tract infection), stricture, TB (tuberculosis), schistosomiasis.
- **Medical renal** (IgA nephropathy): dysmorphic RBCs, casts, proteinuria.
- **Others**: exercise, endometriosis, sickle cell. Anticoagulants do not explain haematuria; evaluate as usual.

### Initial assessment
- History (smoking, occupation, cyclophosphamide, pelvic radiation, Lynch), examination, blood pressure, creatinine, culture.
- UTI → treat → repeat urinalysis; persistence means full evaluation. Glomerular picture (dysmorphic RBCs (red blood cells), casts or proteinuria suggest a glomerular source) → nephrology **and** (plus) urological evaluation.

### AUA/SUFU risk stratification
| | Low (all) | Intermediate (any) | High (any) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Age | Women under 50, men under 40 | Women 50–59, men 40–59 | 60 or over |
| Smoking (pack-years) | Under 10 | 10–30 | Over 30 |
| RBCs/HPF | 3–10 | 11–25 | Over 25 |
| Gross haematuria history | No | No | **Yes** |
| Cancer risk | 0–0.4% | 1–2.5% | About 6% |
| **Work-up** | Repeat urinalysis in 6 months **or** cystoscopy + RUS (renal ultrasound) | Cystoscopy + RUS | Cystoscopy + **CT urography** |

- CT (computed tomography) contraindicated → MR (magnetic resonance) urography, or retrograde pyelograms.
- Cytology and urine markers are **not** used initially; cytology if CIS (carcinoma in situ) is suspected.
- **Gross haematuria**: cystoscopy, CT urography and cytology at any age.

### Urine cytology
- **Sensitivity 31–62%, specificity 94–100%**: good for **high-grade tumours and CIS (carcinoma in situ)**, poor for low grade.
- **Paris System:** negative for HGUC (high-grade urothelial carcinoma), atypical, suspicious, HGUC (by nuclear-to-cytoplasmic ratio and nuclear features).
- Positive cytology, normal cystoscopy → random and prostatic urethral biopsies, upper tract washings.

### Urinary biomarkers (sensitivity/specificity, Chou 2015 meta-analysis)
| Marker | Principle | Sens / spec | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **NMP22 (nuclear matrix protein 22)** | Nuclear protein; lab or point-of-care | 0.69/0.77 | False positives: inflammation, stones |
| **BTA (bladder tumour antigen)** stat / TRAK | Complement factor H–related protein; dipstick or ELISA (enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay) | 0.64/0.77 | **False positive with blood** (factor H in serum) |
| **UroVysion FISH (fluorescence in situ hybridisation)** | Gain of chromosomes 3, 7, 17; loss of 9p21 | 0.63/0.87 | After atypical cytology |
| **ImmunoCyt** | Antibodies to CEA (carcinoembryonic antigen) + two mucins | 0.78/0.78 | Highest sensitivity; operator dependent |
| **Cxbladder** | RT-PCR (reverse transcription polymerase chain reaction), 5 mRNAs | about 0.82/0.85 | Needs validation |
- **Also know:** Xpert Bladder (mRNA); AssureMDx (FGFR3, TERT, HRAS mutations + methylation).
- **Pattern:** more sensitive, less specific than cytology; miss small low-grade tumours.

### Role of markers in haematuria (guidelines)
- **AUA/SUFU 2020:** **no cytology or markers in the initial evaluation of microhaematuria**; cytology only after a negative work-up if CIS is suspected.
- **Gross haematuria:** cytology with cystoscopy and CT urography. A negative marker still leaves **≥10% cancer probability**, so cystoscopy is still needed.
- **No marker replaces cystoscopy** (AUA, EAU (European Association of Urology)); a negative test may one day help triage low-risk patients.
- **Recent advances:** urinary tumour DNA; methylation assays (Bladder EpiCheck).

### Follow-up
- Negative evaluation → repeat urinalysis within 12 months; release if negative.
- Re-evaluate for GH, rising MH or new symptoms.

**Diagram to draw:** AUA/SUFU flowchart from MH to risk-based work-up (haematuria pathway), with markers as adjuncts.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - MH: 3 or more RBCs/HPF on microscopy, one sample.
> - Low: optional; intermediate: cystoscopy + ultrasound; high: cystoscopy + CT urography.
> - Any gross haematuria = high risk.
> - Cytology: specific for high grade/CIS, insensitive for low grade.
> - BTA is falsely positive with blood.
> - No markers in initial microhaematuria work-up; none replaces cystoscopy.

## Collin's knife.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 3, Q3b

*Taken to mean the **Collings knife**, the knife-shaped cutting electrode of the resectoscope (the reference chapters spell it both "Collins" and "Collings").*

### Description
- A **hot (electrosurgical) knife electrode** fitted to the **resectoscope working element** in place of the loop.
- Thin **straight or angled (L- or hook-shaped) blade**; cuts a precise line with **cutting current** under irrigation and direct vision.
- **Monopolar** versions need **non-conducting irrigant** (glycine, sorbitol or mannitol); bipolar versions allow normal saline.
- Adult and **infant (9.5 Fr) resectoscope** sizes.
- **Principle:** it **incises rather than resects**; a deep, controlled cut releases a fibrous ring with little tissue loss.

### Uses (from the chapters)
| Use | Technique point |
|---|---|
| **TUIP** (transurethral incision of prostate) | Gland under 30 mL, no median lobe; incise at 5 and 7 o'clock from below the ureteric orifice to just proximal to the verumontanum, down to the capsule |
| **Bladder neck contracture** (after TURP (transurethral resection of prostate) or simple prostatectomy) | Incise until the scar springs open, to about 22 Fr; mitomycin C for recurrence |
| **Ureterocele incision** (older children) | Low transverse full-thickness incision near the bladder floor to reduce reflux |
| **PUV (posterior urethral valve) ablation** | Infant resectoscope only if urethral calibre is normal; incise at 5, 7 (± 12) o'clock; avoid hot-loop resection (stricture) |
| **Bladder diverticulum neck** | Incise the narrow neck so the diverticulum drains |
| **PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) tract** | Incise scarred fascia when rigid dilators fail |

### Alternatives
- **Cold knife (Sachse)** urethrotome: preferred for urethral stricture; hot knife unnecessary there.
- **Holmium or thulium laser**, Bugbee electrode, hook electrode, **Mohan's valvotome** (for valves).

### Precautions and complications
- Stay away from the **ureteric orifices** and external sphincter; control depth (perforation, extravasation).
- TUR (transurethral resection) syndrome with monopolar irrigant if veins open.
- **Retrograde ejaculation** after bladder neck incision; stricture if the urethra is too small for the sheath.

**Diagram to draw:** resectoscope tip with L-shaped knife electrode, and the 5 and 7 o'clock TUIP incisions.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Knife-shaped resectoscope electrode for incision, not resection.
> - Uses: TUIP, bladder neck contracture, ureterocele, PUV, diverticulum neck.
> - Monopolar needs glycine or sorbitol; bipolar uses saline.

## Baceriuria and use of antibiotic policy in pregnancy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2019-Summer Paper 3, Q3b

*Read as: bacteriuria in pregnancy and the antibiotic policy for it.*

### Definition
- **ASB (asymptomatic bacteriuria):** ≥10⁵ CFU (colony-forming units)/mL of one organism without urinary symptoms.

### Why pregnancy matters
- Prevalence is **not raised** (about 2–7%), but ASB **persists and ascends**.
- **Changes:** progesterone relaxes ureteric muscle and the uterus compresses the ureters at the pelvic brim (right more), causing stasis and hydroureteronephrosis. GFR (glomerular filtration rate) rises 30–50%, lowering drug levels.
- Untreated ASB led to **pyelonephritis** in 15–45% (historical), mostly in the third trimester, causing **preterm birth and low birth weight**.
- **Cochrane 2019:** treatment reduces pyelonephritis, preterm delivery and low birth weight.

### Screening
- **Urine culture at 12–16 weeks** or first visit (USPSTF [US Preventive Services Task Force]); dipstick misses cases.

### Antibiotic policy
| Safe | Restricted or avoided |
|---|---|
| Penicillins (amoxicillin), cephalosporins: throughout | **Nitrofurantoin:** stop near term (from 35 weeks): neonatal haemolysis (G6PD [glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase] deficiency) |
| Fosfomycin: cystitis only | **TMP-SMX (trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole):** avoid 1st trimester (folate, neural tube); caution near term (kernicterus) |
| Gentamicin: pyelonephritis | **Fluoroquinolones** (cartilage), **tetracyclines** (teeth, bone), chloramphenicol: avoid |

- ACOG (American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists) 2017: nitrofurantoin and sulfonamides first-line in 2nd–3rd trimester; 1st only if no alternative.
- **ASB or cystitis:** culture-directed, **4–7 days** (cures more than single dose); **repeat culture** after treatment.

### Pyelonephritis
- **Admit**; IV (intravenous) ampicillin plus gentamicin, ceftriaxone or aztreonam, then oral cephalexin; 10–14 days. No improvement by 72 h: renal ultrasound.

### Prophylaxis
- After one pyelonephritis or two cystitis episodes: nightly nitrofurantoin or cephalexin until delivery (Cochrane 2015: close surveillance equally effective).
- **Also know:** diversion, augmentation, SCI (spinal cord injury): treat ASB, consider prophylaxis.

**Diagram to draw:** right hydroureter at the pelvic brim.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Screen by culture at 12–16 weeks; treat all ASB.
> - β-lactams safe throughout; no fluoroquinolones or tetracyclines.
> - Nitrofurantoin not near term; TMP not in 1st trimester.

## Hemostasis in laparoscopic surgery.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 3, Q3f
(also answered in Practice Set 2 Paper 4, Q3f; partial nephrectomy haemostasis in 2019-Summer Paper 3, Q1)

### Principles
- Devices seal by **denaturing protein**. Plan from imaging; keep a **haemorrhage tray** (laparoscopic Satinsky, 10-mm suction, vascular suture with Lapra-Ty, haemostatic agent).

### Effect of pneumoperitoneum
- CO₂ at 12–15 mmHg **tamponades venous bleeding**. Before exit, **lower pressure to about 5 mmHg** and inspect the operative site and each port.

### Energy devices
| Device | Lateral thermal spread | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Monopolar | 5–7 mm | Capacitive coupling, insulation failure, direct coupling |
| Bipolar | 1–6 mm | Current only between the jaws |
| Vessel sealer (LigaSure) | 1–3 mm | Seals vessels **≤7 mm**; one application only |
| Ultrasonic (Harmonic) | ≈1 mm | No current; blade >200 °C, cools slowly |
| Argon beam | Superficial | Raw surfaces; gas embolism risk |

### Clips
- **Titanium clips:** no diathermy near them (slip).
- **Hem-o-lok** (locking polymer): circumferential dissection, see the tip beyond the vessel, feel the snap, at least two clips on the patient side with a 2-mm cuff. The manufacturer **contraindicates it on the living-donor renal artery** after stump haemorrhages.
- **Lapra-Ty** absorbable clips act as knots (renorrhaphy).

### Staplers
- Endo GIA via a 12-mm port; **grey (2.0 mm) or white (2.5 mm)** loads for renal vessels; blue or green for thick tissue.
- **Never fire across clips.**

### Sutures and haemostatic agents
- Intracorporeal or barbed sutures; fibrin sealant, gelatin–thrombin matrix, oxidised cellulose.

### Managing bleeding
- **Venous injury:** raise pressure to 25 mmHg → suction to identify → pledget tamponade → sealant → extra 5-mm port for suturing or Satinsky → hand-assist → convert. Keep the patient well filled (gas embolism).
- **Arterial:** minor, tamponade; renal artery, staple; aorta, convert.
- **Port-site bleeding:** cantilever the trocar to find it; diathermy or transfascial suture. Blunt trocars reduce it.

**Diagram to draw:** Hem-o-lok placement with two patient-side clips and a 2-mm cuff.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Pneumoperitoneum hides venous bleeding: inspect at low pressure before exit.
> - LigaSure seals ≤7 mm; spread monopolar 5–7 mm, ultrasonic ≈1 mm.
> - Hem-o-lok contraindicated on the donor renal artery.
> - Never staple across clips; grey or white loads for vessels.

## APOGEE
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 7 Paper 3, Q3c

*Assumed meaning: the Apogee transvaginal mesh kit (American Medical Systems) for posterior vaginal wall and vault prolapse. It is not described in the chapter notes; facts below are general.*

### Definition
- A **prepackaged polypropylene mesh kit** placed transvaginally to support the **posterior compartment and vaginal apex** (DeLancey levels I and II). Anterior companion: **Perigee** (transobturator).
- Mesh: **type I macroporous monofilament polypropylene** (Amid classification), the only type that integrates into tissue.

### Indications (historical)
- Symptomatic posterior and vault prolapse, mostly **recurrent** or with poor native tissue, avoiding abdominal surgery.

### Technique (principle)
1. Midline posterior vaginal incision; dissect the rectovaginal space to the ischial spines.
2. Two small perianal incisions lateral and posterior to the anus.
3. Curved trocars pass through the **ischiorectal fossa**, piercing the iliococcygeus near the ischial spine, and deliver the mesh arms.
4. Mesh body fixed to the apex and laid over the rectovaginal septum **tension-free**; epithelium closed without trimming.

### Complications
- **Mesh exposure** (IUGA/ICS [International Urogynecological Association/International Continence Society] term; "erosion" deprecated), extrusion, **perforation** into rectum or bladder.
- **Dyspareunia**, buttock and pelvic pain, mesh contraction, defecatory dysfunction, ischiorectal haematoma or abscess, rectal injury.
- **Management:** small exposure → vaginal oestrogen, then partial excision; pain or perforation → excision of mesh and arms.

### Regulatory status
- **FDA (US Food and Drug Administration) 2011:** serious complications of transvaginal POP (pelvic organ prolapse) mesh "not rare", with no clear benefit over native repair. Reclassified high-risk in 2016.
- **2019:** FDA ordered remaining manufacturers to **stop selling transvaginal POP mesh**; Apogee-type kits are off the market. Synthetic mesh improves anatomy but complicates more (Cochrane 2016).
- This does **not** apply to mid-urethral slings or **sacrocolpopexy mesh**.

### Current alternatives
- Native posterior colporrhaphy or site-specific repair; uterosacral suspension or **sacrospinous fixation**; **sacrocolpopexy** (abdominal, laparoscopic, robotic) for apical prolapse; colpocleisis in the frail.

**Diagram to draw:** posterior mesh with two arms through the ischiorectal fossae.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Apogee: transvaginal posterior/apical polypropylene mesh kit.
> - Exposure, dyspareunia and pain drove withdrawal.
> - FDA 2019 stopped transvaginal POP mesh sales.

## Describe principles of tissue engineering and what its status in managemet of urological diseases is.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 7 Paper 3, Q3e
(also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 1, Q1 and 2022-Summer Paper 4, Q2)

### Definition
- **Tissue engineering** applies engineering and life sciences to build biological substitutes that restore or improve tissue function (Langer and Vacanti, 1993).
- **Rationale:** bowel in the urinary tract causes mucus, stones, acidosis and cancer; grafts are scarce.

### Principles
```
Biopsy -> cells expanded -> seeded on scaffold (+ signals)
       -> bioreactor conditioning -> implant -> host vascularises
```
| Element | Details |
|---|---|
| **Cells** | Autologous urothelial and smooth muscle cells (no rejection); adult stem cells (bone marrow, adipose, urine-derived; mainly paracrine); embryonic and iPSCs (induced pluripotent stem cells): tumour risk |
| **Scaffolds** | Biocompatible, porous, degrade as tissue forms. Natural (collagen), **acellular matrices** (bladder matrix, SIS [small intestinal submucosa]), synthetic (PGA, PLGA [polyglycolic, poly(lactic-co-glycolic) acid]) |
| **Seeding** | Urothelium inside, muscle outside. **Unseeded** matrix works only for short onlay defects; tubes need seeded scaffolds or they contract |
| **Signals** | Growth factors (e.g. VEGF [vascular endothelial growth factor]), matrix and mechanical cues |
| **Bioreactor** | Perfusion and cyclic stretch to mature the construct |
| **Vascularity** | Oxygen diffuses only **100–200 µm**: main barrier; omental wrap, angiogenic factors |

### Status in urology
| Organ | Status |
|---|---|
| Bladder | **Atala 2006**: seeded constructs in 7 myelomeningocele patients improved compliance; phase II trial showed **no significant gain**. Experimental |
| Conduit | Neo-Urinary Conduit trial: **half developed stenosis** |
| Urethra | Acellular onlay for short strictures; **Raya-Rivera 2011**: seeded tubular urethras in **5 boys**, functional up to 6 years |
| Sphincter | Muscle-derived cell injection for SUI (stress urinary incontinence): investigational |
| Vagina | Engineered vaginas in MRKH (Mayer–Rokitansky–Küster–Hauser) syndrome |
| Penis, kidney, testis | Corporal grafts, kidney organoids, spermatogonial stem cell banking: experimental |

- **Recent advances:** 3D bioprinting, organoids, vascularised composite bladder allograft.
- **Challenges:** vascularity, innervation, fibrosis, cost, regulation.

**Diagram to draw:** biopsy → culture → scaffold → bioreactor → implant.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Cells, scaffold, signals, bioreactor, blood supply.
> - Diffusion limit 100–200 µm.
> - Still experimental except short urethral onlays.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 1 · Basic Principles

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Therapeutic energy sources in urology (including laparoscopic energy devices) (LAQ)

- Spread: monopolar 5–7 > bipolar 1–6 > sealer 1–3 > ultrasonic ≈1 mm.
- LigaSure seals ≤7 mm; ultrasonic blade hottest after use.
- Capacitive coupling: avoid hybrid trocars; AEM detects it.
- Holmium 2120 nm: gold standard for stones and HoLEP.
- Bipolar TURP in saline avoids TUR syndrome.
- Thermal ablation for cT1a ≤3 cm; cryo <−40 °C, two cycles.
- HIFU, IRE, VTP: focal prostate therapy, still evolving.

#### Asymptomatic haematuria and urinary biomarkers (SAQ)

- MH: 3 or more RBCs/HPF on microscopy, one sample.
- Low: optional; intermediate: cystoscopy + ultrasound; high: cystoscopy + CT urography.
- Any gross haematuria = high risk.
- Cytology: specific for high grade/CIS, insensitive for low grade.
- BTA is falsely positive with blood.
- No markers in initial microhaematuria work-up; none replaces cystoscopy.

#### Collin's knife. (SAQ)

- Knife-shaped resectoscope electrode for incision, not resection.
- Uses: TUIP, bladder neck contracture, ureterocele, PUV, diverticulum neck.
- Monopolar needs glycine or sorbitol; bipolar uses saline.

#### Baceriuria and use of antibiotic policy in pregnancy (SAQ)

- Screen by culture at 12–16 weeks; treat all ASB.
- β-lactams safe throughout; no fluoroquinolones or tetracyclines.
- Nitrofurantoin not near term; TMP not in 1st trimester.

#### Hemostasis in laparoscopic surgery. (SAQ)

- Pneumoperitoneum hides venous bleeding: inspect at low pressure before exit.
- LigaSure seals ≤7 mm; spread monopolar 5–7 mm, ultrasonic ≈1 mm.
- Hem-o-lok contraindicated on the donor renal artery.
- Never staple across clips; grey or white loads for vessels.

#### APOGEE (SAQ)

- Apogee: transvaginal posterior/apical polypropylene mesh kit.
- Exposure, dyspareunia and pain drove withdrawal.
- FDA 2019 stopped transvaginal POP mesh sales.

#### Describe principles of tissue engineering and what its status in managemet of urological diseases is. (SAQ)

- Cells, scaffold, signals, bioreactor, blood supply.
- Diffusion limit 100–200 µm.
- Still experimental except short urethral onlays.

# Section 2 · Oncology, DSD, Gender & Ageing — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## DSD: Evaluation and Management of the Newborn with Ambiguous Genitalia
**Asked as:**
- Management of DSD — *Summer 2019 Paper III Q3f (SAQ)*
- Evaluation and treatment of newborn with ambiguous genitalia — *Practice Set 4 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

(DSD management also answered as an SAQ in 2019-Summer Paper 3, Q3f)

*DSD read as: disorders/differences of sex development. Detrusor–sphincter dyssynergia is covered in 2016-Summer Paper 1, Q3a.*

### Introduction and definition
- Atypical ("ambiguous") genitalia point to a **DSD (difference/disorder of sex development)**: congenital conditions with atypical chromosomal, gonadal or anatomical sex (Chicago consensus 2006).
- It is a **medical emergency only for salt-wasting CAH (congenital adrenal hyperplasia)**; sex designation is not a "social emergency". Evaluate within 24–48 hours (neonatal evaluation, first 24–48 h).

### Classification (by karyotype)
| Group | Main causes (examples) |
|---|---|
| **46,XX DSD** | **CAH** (commonest; 21-hydroxylase 95%, 11β-hydroxylase about 5%, 3β-HSD (hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase) <1%); maternal androgens or aromatase deficiency; ovotesticular; testicular (SRY (sex-determining region Y) positive, SRY+) |
| **46,XY DSD** | Partial/complete gonadal dysgenesis; androgen synthesis defects (17β-HSD3, 5α-reductase); androgen insensitivity (CAIS/PAIS, complete/partial; PAIS = partial androgen insensitivity); LH (luteinising hormone) receptor defect |
| **Sex chromosome DSD** | 45,X/46,XY MGD (mixed gonadal dysgenesis); 45,X Turner; 47,XXY Klinefelter; chimerism |

### History
- Consanguinity, **unexplained infant deaths** (CAH), infertility or amenorrhoea in relatives; maternal virilisation, progestogens or androgens; prenatal cfDNA (cell-free DNA) karyotype versus ultrasound discordance.

### Examination (gender-neutral terms)
- Stretched phallic length, glans width, meatus, number of perineal openings, labioscrotal fusion and rugosity, pigmentation, anogenital distance. **Prader grade (stage) I–V** for virilisation.
- Dehydration, hypotension, hyperpigmentation (CAH); dysmorphism.
- **Gonads are the key sign.** Ovaries never descend, so **a palpable gonad means testicular tissue**. Bilateral impalpable gonads with atypical genitalia = CAH until proven otherwise (impalpable gonads suggest CAH). Asymmetry suggests MGD or ovotesticular DSD.
- Impalpable testis with hypospadias, or proximal hypospadias, also needs DSD work-up (Kaefer: DSD in about 30%, 50% if the testis is impalpable).

### Investigations (tests)
- **Karyotype** at once (preliminary 2–3 days); **FISH (fluorescence in situ hybridisation) for X/Y/SRY** within hours.
- **CAH screen:** **17-OHP (17-hydroxyprogesterone) at about 36 hours** (after 36 h; earlier values are falsely high); electrolytes and glucose daily. **Emergency:** exclude **salt-wasting CAH** (low sodium, high potassium, shock); **salt-wasting appears around day 5–7** (usually after day 5–7), so normal early electrolytes do not exclude CAH.
- **At about 1 week:**

| Test | Meaning |
|---|---|
| AMH (anti-Müllerian hormone) | Present = functioning Sertoli cells |
| Testosterone, LH, FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone) | Testicular function (mini-puberty) |
| Androstenedione, DHEA-S (dehydroepiandrosterone sulfate) | Adrenal enzyme block |
| Testosterone:DHT (dihydrotestosterone) >20:1 | 5α-reductase deficiency |
| hCG (human chorionic gonadotropin) stimulation | No rise = no Leydig tissue or LH receptor defect |

- **Pelvic ultrasound:** uterus (well seen neonatally), gonads, kidneys, cerebriform adrenals (CAH); then genitography, endoscopy, laparoscopy ± biopsy, genetic panels.
- **Genitography / endoscopy:** urogenital sinus, confluence level relative to bladder neck, cervix.
- If unclear: MRI (magnetic resonance imaging), **laparoscopy ± deep longitudinal gonadal biopsy**, gene panels (genetic panels), microarray, exome. **No gonadectomy during diagnosis (while diagnosing).**

### Diagnostic algorithm
```
Palpable gonads: 0 -> CAH likely; 1 -> MGD, PAIS, ovotesticular;
                 2 -> 46,XY DSD (PAIS, 5α-reductase, synthesis defect)
46,XX + uterus + high 17-OHP           -> CAH
46,XY, no uterus                       -> androgen synthesis/action defect
46,XY or 45,X/46,XY + uterus           -> gonadal dysgenesis / MGD
```
- A precise diagnosis is reached in nearly all 46,XX but under 50% of 46,XY DSD.

### Treatment
**1. Emergency and medical management (CAH)**
- Salt-wasting crisis: IV (intravenous) saline, dextrose, stress-dose hydrocortisone.
- Maintenance (Endocrine Society 2018): hydrocortisone 10–15 mg/m²/day in divided doses, fludrocortisone, salt supplements in infancy; stress dosing during illness and surgery.
- **Puberty:** oestrogen or testosterone induction if gonads fail.

**2. Team and family**
- **MDT (multidisciplinary team):** urologist, endocrinologist, geneticist, psychologist, neonatologist, ethicist; gender-neutral language. Congratulate parents; say "your baby", never "it"; open disclosure, peer support.

**3. Sex of rearing (gender assignment)**
- Weighs diagnosis, prenatal androgen exposure, likely gender identity, fertility, sexual function, family wishes and culture.
- Usually female: 46,XX CAH, CAIS (complete androgen insensitivity), complete gonadal dysgenesis. Trend male: PAIS, 5α-reductase and 17β-HSD3 deficiency.
- Micropenis (stretched length under 2 cm) alone is no reason for female rearing.

**4. Surgery: timing and consent**
- Irreversible surgery is ideally deferred until the child can participate (consent); parents decide with the team after balanced counselling.
- **Feminising** surgery (clitoroplasty, vaginoplasty, labioplasty) is irreversible, so deferral until the child can consent is favoured unless obstructed; severe CAH is debated.
  - Clitoroplasty (ventral, nerve-sparing, glans preserved; Prader III–V); vaginoplasty by confluence (flap for low, partial/total urogenital mobilisation, pull-through for high); labioplasty. The vagina's position relative to the bladder neck, not common-channel length, decides the vaginoplasty. Stress steroids in CAH.
- **Masculinising:** proximal hypospadias repair, orchidopexy, often done in infancy (complication rates high).

**5. Gonads (gonadal tumour risk)**
| Risk | Condition | Plan / management |
|---|---|---|
| High | Dysgenesis with Y, intra-abdominal (15–35%); PAIS non-scrotal (50%) | Gonadectomy |
| Intermediate | Turner with Y (12%) | Gonadectomy |
| Low | CAIS (2%); ovotesticular (3%) | Keep CAIS gonads until after puberty; ovotesticular: remove discordant tissue |

### Follow-up and recent advances
- Pubertal induction, psychology, fertility preservation (experimental gonadal tissue cryopreservation), transition to adult care.
- Prenatal cfDNA diagnosis; rapid exome/genome sequencing. Prenatal dexamethasone for CAH is experimental.

**Diagram to draw:** Prader stages I–V and the urogenital sinus with confluence level; karyotype-based DSD algorithm.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Palpable gonad = testicular tissue; impalpable gonads = CAH until proven otherwise.
> - 17-OHP after 36 h; salt crisis around day 5–7.
> - Karyotype + uterus + gonads triad guides diagnosis; no gonadectomy while diagnosing.
> - Defer irreversible surgery; CAIS gonads kept until after puberty (till puberty).

# Section 2 · Oncology, DSD, Gender & Ageing — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Prepucioplasty
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2019-Summer Paper 3, Q3a

*Read as: preputioplasty, a foreskin-preserving operation for phimosis.*

### Definition
- An operation that widens the tight preputial ring but keeps the foreskin. It is an alternative to circumcision.

### Background
- **Physiological phimosis** at birth is due to glans–preputial adhesions. Smegma and erections separate them. About 90% retract by 3 years; fewer than 1% are phimotic at 17 years.
- **Secondary phimosis** follows scarring: forced retraction, balanoposthitis or BXO (balanitis xerotica obliterans).

### Indications
- Symptomatic phimosis: recurrent balanoposthitis, UTI (urinary tract infection), paraphimosis, painful erection.
- Failed **topical steroid** (betamethasone 0.05% twice daily for about 4 weeks with gentle retraction). About 1 in 6 fail.
- Wish to keep the foreskin (cultural reasons, cosmesis).

### Contraindications
- **BXO** with scarring: circumcision is preferred. Triradiate incision with triamcinolone has been reported in selected boys.
- Physiological non-retractility needs only reassurance. Treat active infection first.

### Techniques
| Technique | Method |
|---|---|
| Dorsal slit with transverse closure | Longitudinal incision through the ring, closed transversely (Heineke–Mikulicz principle) |
| Triple incision | Three longitudinal cuts over the ring, each closed transversely |
| Y–V plasty | V-flap of outer skin advanced into a Y-cut |

- **Principle:** cutting the ring lengthwise and closing it crosswise widens its circumference.
- General anaesthesia or penile block; incise only the band; confirm retraction; absorbable sutures.

### Postoperative care
- **Daily retraction from the first week**, continued for weeks, prevents re-stenosis. A topical steroid may help.

### Outcomes and complications
- Most achieve a retractile foreskin. It is quicker and less painful than circumcision.
- Complications: re-phimosis (needs circumcision), bleeding, infection, poor cosmesis, paraphimosis.
- **Also know:** preputial reconstruction during hypospadias repair is also called preputioplasty; dehiscence is its risk.

**Diagram to draw:** dorsal incision through the ring, closed transversely.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Steroid first; about 1 in 6 fail.
> - Cut lengthwise, close crosswise; daily retraction after.
> - BXO: circumcision preferred.

## Mixed gonadal dysgenesis.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 3, Q3e
(DSD management in general in 2019-Summer Paper 3, Q3f)

### Definition
- MGD (mixed gonadal dysgenesis): a **dysgenetic testis on one side and a streak gonad on the other**, with persistent Müllerian structures and variable virilisation.
- A sex chromosome DSD (difference of sex development), usually **45,X/46,XY mosaicism** (about 1 in 5,000 births). **Second commonest cause of neonatal atypical genitalia after CAH (congenital adrenal hyperplasia).**

### Pathogenesis
- The Y-bearing line forms a testis; the 45,X line leaves a streak.
- AMH (anti-Müllerian hormone) and testosterone act **locally**, so anatomy is asymmetric: Wolffian duct on the **testis side**; tube and hemiuterus on the **streak side**.
- 45,X/46,XY spectrum: female (Turner + Y), atypical (MGD) or male with normal testes (about 85%).

### Clinical features
- **Asymmetric genitalia:** phallus with chordee, perineal hypospadias, urogenital sinus; one testis in a hemiscrotum, the other side impalpable. Hernia may contain a uterus or tube.
- **Turner features:** short stature, coarctation, horseshoe kidney.

### Evaluation (MDT, multidisciplinary team)
- **Karyotype** (many cells, for mosaicism); **17-OHP (17-hydroxyprogesterone)** to exclude CAH; LH (luteinising hormone), FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone), testosterone, AMH.
- **Pelvic ultrasound** (uterus, asymmetric gonads); genitography, endoscopy; **laparoscopy with gonadal biopsy**. No gonadectomy during diagnosis.
- Echocardiography, renal ultrasound (Turner screening).

| | MGD | Ovotesticular DSD |
|---|---|---|
| Karyotype | 45,X/46,XY | Mostly 46,XX |
| Gonads | Dysgenetic testis + streak | Ovary + testis, or ovotestis |
| Tumour risk | 15–35% | About 3% |

### Management
- **Sex of rearing:** individualised; more androgen exposure predicts male identity; dysphoria is commoner when raised female. Avoid irreversible infant surgery.
- **Gonads:** germ cell neoplasia 15–35% (gonadoblastoma, then dysgerminoma or seminoma) in streak and testis alike. **Remove streaks** (biopsy first if tissue uncertain).
  - Raised male: keep a scrotal testis under surveillance; bring down an intra-abdominal one if possible.
  - Raised female: remove or relocate the testis.
- **Surgery:** male: staged hypospadias repair, orchidopexy; female: feminising surgery deferred.
- **Hormones:** pubertal sex steroids; growth hormone. Infertility usual.

**Diagram to draw:** asymmetric anatomy: testis with vas; streak with tube and hemiuterus.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 45,X/46,XY; testis + streak + Müllerian remnants; asymmetric.
> - Second commonest cause of atypical genitalia after CAH.
> - Tumour risk 15–35%: remove streaks.
> - Screen for Turner features.

## Telomerase
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 6 Paper 3, Q3b

### Telomeres
- **Telomeres** are repetitive sequences (TTAGGG repeats) at chromosome ends. They protect ends from degradation and fusion.
- DNA polymerase cannot copy the very end of the lagging strand (end-replication problem). So about **100 base pairs are lost per division**: the "mitotic clock".
- Critical shortening triggers **replicative senescence** (Hayflick limit).

### Telomerase: structure and function
- A specialised **reverse transcriptase** that adds telomeric repeats.
- Two core parts: **hTERT** (human telomerase reverse transcriptase, catalytic protein) and **hTR** (telomerase RNA, the template).
- **Normal expression:** only rapidly dividing tissues: germ cells (testis), intestinal epithelium, endometrium, activated lymphocytes. Most somatic cells lack it.

### Role in cancer
```
Proliferation -> moderate shortening -> senescence/apoptosis (barrier 1)
-> checkpoint lost (p53) -> severe shortening -> chromosomal instability (barrier 2)
-> telomerase activation -> immortal cancer cell
```
- **85–90% of human tumours** express high telomerase, giving unlimited replication.
- **ALT (alternative lengthening of telomeres):** telomerase-independent recombination; linked to ATRX or DAXX loss.
- **TERT promoter mutations** switch on hTERT.

### Urological relevance

| Organ | Finding |
|---|---|
| **Bladder** | TERT promoter mutation is the commonest mutation (over 60% even in PUNLMP, papillary urothelial neoplasm of low malignant potential). Urine markers: telomerase activity by **TRAP (telomeric repeat amplification protocol)**, a PCR (polymerase chain reaction)-based assay; hTERT messenger RNA; AssureMDx (FGFR3, TERT, HRAS mutations plus methylation). Inflammation gives false positives; not routine |
| **Upper tract** | hTR positive in almost all UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma); rises with stage |
| **Prostate** | Short telomeres in cancer and premalignant lesions; short tumour telomeres predict recurrence; variable stromal telomeres predict cancer death |

- **Also know:** senescent cells secrete SASP (senescence-associated secretory phenotype) cytokines in ageing and BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia).

### Therapy
- **Imetelstat** (telomerase inhibitor) is used in myelodysplastic syndrome.
- hTERT-based vaccines (UV1, GX301, INO-9012) are in early prostate, renal and bladder trials.

**Diagram to draw:** telomere-to-cancer flowchart with two barriers.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Telomerase = hTERT (catalytic) + hTR (RNA template).
> - About 100 base pairs lost per division; 85–90% of tumours express telomerase.
> - TERT promoter mutation is the commonest mutation in bladder cancer.
> - TRAP assay detects urinary telomerase; inflammation gives false positives.

- TRAP assay detects urinary telomerase; inflammation gives false positives.

# Section 3 · Infections & Inflammation — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Renal and genitourinary TB: etiopathogenesis, presentation, (laboratory and newer) diagnosis and management
**Asked as:**
- Describe etiopathogenisis, clinical presentation, diagnosis and management of Renal TB? — *Summer 2022 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)* (also answered in 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q1)
- Laboratory Diagnosis of Genitourinary Tuberculosis. — *Winter 2023 Paper III Q3f (SAQ)* (also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q2, as part of renal TB)
- Describe newer diagnostic modalities for Gu Kochs. Briefly outline role of all present diagnostic tests and give diagnostic algorithm. — *Practice Set 6 Paper III Q3d (SAQ)* (also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 3, Q3f)

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

*Assumed meaning: "Gu Kochs" = genitourinary tuberculosis.*

### Introduction
- Caused by the MTBC (*Mycobacterium tuberculosis* complex); *M. bovis* (and BCG [bacille Calmette–Guérin]) is innately resistant to pyrazinamide.
- UG (urogenital) TB (tuberculosis) is 15–20% of pulmonary TB in developing countries; second extrapulmonary site after lymph nodes. **Kidney is involved in 80%.**

### Etiopathogenesis
- **Primary infection:** inhaled bacilli spread to hilar nodes, then by blood to the well-perfused kidney and epididymis, before immunity develops (up to 12 weeks).
- **Granuloma:** epithelioid cells and Langhans giant cells in a lymphocyte cuff, maintained by IFN-γ (interferon-γ). In 90–95% infection stays latent.
- **Reactivation** (lifetime 5–15%), even decades later: old age, diabetes, renal failure, HIV (human immunodeficiency virus), malnutrition, immunosuppression.
- **Routes:** haematogenous (principal); ascending (intravesical BCG, about 1%); contiguous (psoas, bowel).
- **Renal sequence:** bacilli lodge in **periglomerular capillaries** → cortical granulomas coalesce → **caseation** and cavities open into calyces → papillary necrosis, abscess, calcification → bacilluria seeds ureter and bladder.
- **Autonephrectomy:** caseo-cavernous type, or fibrotic calcified "putty" kidney.
- **Ureter and bladder (descending spread):** mural granulomas give strictures and a beaded or pipestem ureter. Cystitis starts around the ureteric orifices; after about a year of fibrosis the bladder contracts (<100 mL; thimble bladder <20 mL).
- **Key concept:** healing occurs by fibrosis, so infundibular and ureteric strictures (commonest at the **VUJ (vesicoureteric junction)**) and bladder contraction can worsen during treatment.
- **Also know:** granulomatous interstitial nephritis; miliary renal TB; amyloidosis; squamous metaplasia of the pelvis (squamous cell carcinoma risk); ESRD (end-stage renal disease) in about 7%.

### Clinical presentation
- Renal TB is silent until it reaches the bladder; asymptomatic in about 8%.
- **Storage LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms)** and dysuria (commonest), haematuria and flank pain (about one-third), colic <10% (sloughed papillae, clot, debris).
- Constitutional symptoms <20%. Recurrent "UTI (urinary tract infection)" not responding to antibiotics.
- Signs: renal angle tenderness, flank sinus, beaded vas, epididymal nodule; hypertension; renal failure if bilateral.

### Diagnosis

#### Principle
- GUTB (genitourinary tuberculosis, TB) is paucibacillary with intermittent shedding, so **multiple samples** are needed: send 3–5 early-morning urine samples.
- **Hierarchy of proof:** positive culture or DNA → caseating granulomas on histology → clinical picture with response to treatment.

#### Specimen
- **Early-morning first-void urine (EMU), 3–5 samples on consecutive days**, sent (processed) at once (acidic urine kills bacilli). Avoid prior fluoroquinolones.
- Also tissue, pus, FNAC (fine-needle aspiration cytology) or semen.

#### Laboratory tests

| Test | Method | Comment / Role |
|---|---|---|
| **Urine routine** | Microscopy | **Sterile (acid) pyuria** ± (microscopic) haematuria in >90% |
| **ZN (Ziehl–Neelsen) smear** | Carbol fuchsin, acid-alcohol, methylene blue: red bacilli. Auramine fluorescence is more sensitive | Quick; sensitivity <50% (under 50%); saprophytic mycobacteria give false positives |
| **Culture (urine AFB [acid-fast bacilli] culture): gold standard** | Decontaminate and concentrate. **LJ (Löwenstein–Jensen)** solid (medium): 4–6 weeks. **MGIT (mycobacteria growth indicator tube)** liquid: about 10 days (as little as 10 days) | 80–90% sensitivity with ideal technique; diagnosis; gives DST (drug-susceptibility testing / drug susceptibility); **monitors treatment** (the only test that monitors treatment) |
| **NAAT (nucleic acid amplification test): GeneXpert MTB/RIF** | Automated cartridge PCR (polymerase chain reaction); about 2 hours (result in 1–2 days) | Rapid diagnosis; detects MTB and **rifampicin resistance** (MDR [multidrug-resistant] TB surrogate). **Xpert Ultra** is more sensitive. Stays positive after cure, so cannot monitor treatment |
| TrueNat | Indian chip-based NAAT | NTEP (National TB Elimination Programme) |
| **LPA (line probe assay)** | DNA strip hybridisation | Rifampicin, isoniazid or second-line drug resistance |
| **LF-LAM (lateral-flow lipoarabinomannan)** | Urine antigen strip, <30 min (under 30 minutes); point-of-care urine test in people with HIV | HIV (human immunodeficiency virus) with symptoms, CD4 <200 or advanced disease (WHO [World Health Organization] 2019) |
| **Histopathology** | Biopsy (bladder, epididymis) | Caseating granulomas; NAAT on tissue raises yield |
| **TST (tuberculin skin test)** | 5 TU (tuberculin units) PPD intradermal; read induration at 48–72 h | Shows (proves; TST/IGRA prove) infection only, not active disease; BCG false positive |
| **IGRA (interferon-γ release assay)** | ESAT-6/CFP-10 blood test (QuantiFERON, T-SPOT) | Not affected by (unaffected by) BCG (bacille Calmette–Guérin); cannot separate latent and active; shows infection, not active disease |

#### Newer modalities
- **Liquid culture, MGIT (mycobacteria growth indicator tube):** about 10 days versus 4–6 weeks on LJ (Löwenstein–Jensen).
- **NAAT (nucleic acid amplification test): GeneXpert MTB/RIF** (Xpert Ultra more sensitive): automated cartridge PCR, about 2 hours; detects **rifampicin resistance**. Also TrueNat; **LPA (line probe assay)** for isoniazid resistance.
- **Urine LF-LAM:** strip, under 30 minutes; WHO 2019.
- **IGRA:** unaffected by BCG.
- **Imaging:** CT (computed tomography) urography; **MR (magnetic resonance) urography with DWI (diffusion-weighted imaging)** grades fibrosis.

#### Supportive tests
- ESR, blood count, renal function, HIV, blood sugar; sputum and **chest radiograph in all** (mandatory; pulmonary / lung TB coexists in up to one-fifth).

#### Imaging and endoscopy
| Imaging | Findings / Role |
|---|---|
| KUB (kidney–ureter–bladder) film | Calcification (24–44%); lobar "putty" kidney |
| IVU (intravenous urography) / CT (computed tomography) urography | **Moth-eaten calyx**, **phantom calyx** (infundibular stenosis), cavities, hydrocalycosis, **Kerr's kink**, pipestem or beaded corkscrew ureter, small bladder; IVU replaced by CT |
| Ultrasound | Children, pregnancy, scrotum; follows hydronephrosis during treatment |
| MRU (magnetic resonance urography) | Children, pregnancy |
| Cystoscopy (and biopsy) | **"Golf-hole" ureteric orifice**, ulcers; biopsy if culture negative (yield 19–52%) |
| Chest radiograph | Mandatory (lung TB in up to one-fifth) |

#### Diagnostic algorithm
```
Suspicion (sterile pyuria, storage symptoms, epididymal nodule)
-> 3-5 EMU: ZN + culture (LJ + MGIT) + Xpert
-> chest X-ray; CT urography
Positive -> treat; DST guides regimen
Negative -> cystoscopy/biopsy, aspiration + NAAT on tissue
Still negative -> empirical trial if clinically compatible
```

### Management
**Medical (all patients)**
- **Multidrug therapy** because bacilli exist in different metabolic states and single drugs select resistance.
- Baseline liver and renal function, HIV, hepatitis, diabetes; DOT (directly observed therapy).
- **2HRZE/4HR:** isoniazid, rifampicin, pyrazinamide, ethambutol for 2 months, then isoniazid and rifampicin for 4 months; **pyridoxine** against isoniazid neuropathy. India's NTEP (National TB Elimination Programme) uses 2HRZE/4HRE.
- Complicated disease: some treat 12–24 months, or ≥6 months after surgery. The new 4-month rifapentine–moxifloxacin regimen is not used for UG TB.
- Toxicity: hepatitis (all except ethambutol); optic neuritis (ethambutol).
- **Monitor ultrasound** for worsening obstruction; steroids (anecdotal) for strictures.
- MDR-TB: second-line drugs (bedaquiline, pretomanid, linezolid).

**Surgical (about half need it), after 4–6 weeks of ATT (antituberculous therapy)**

| Problem | Procedure |
|---|---|
| Obstruction | Early **DJ (double-J) stent** (succeeds in 41%) or **PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy)** |
| Localised polar lesion not responding | Partial nephrectomy |
| Non-functioning kidney + persistent disease or hypertension | **Nephrectomy** (laparoscopic preferred; ligate artery and vein separately) |
| PUJ (pelviureteric junction) stricture | Pyeloplasty; ureterocalicostomy |
| Lower ureteric stricture | Reimplantation, psoas hitch, Boari flap; ileal ureter if long |
| Contracted bladder | Augmentation; substitution if <20 mL |

- Stricture not improving after 6 weeks of drugs → open repair.
- Contested: nephrectomy of a non-functioning kidney without active disease.

### Follow-up
- Relapse 6.3–22% (mean about 5 years): urine culture and imaging for up to 10 years.

**Diagram to draw:** kidney showing caseous cavity, phantom calyx and VUJ stricture; stepwise diagnostic flowchart (algorithm above) from sterile pyuria to culture and DST.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Kidney 80%; periglomerular seeding; heals by fibrosis, so obstruction worsens on treatment.
> - Sterile pyuria; 3–5 early-morning urine AFB cultures; culture is the gold standard and the only test that monitors treatment.
> - GeneXpert (Xpert) detects rifampicin resistance in 2 hours; cannot monitor treatment.
> - LF-LAM for HIV with CD4 under 200.
> - TST and IGRA show infection, not active disease.
> - 2HRZE/4HR with pyridoxine; surgery after 4–6 weeks of ATT.
> - Relapse up to 22%; follow up 10 years.

## Discuss the Aetiopathogenesis, Clinical feature and management of Chyluria.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 3, Q2
(also answered in 2018-Summer Paper 1, Q1, with lymphatic anatomy)

### Definition
- **Chyluria** is the passage of **chyle** (intestinal lymph rich in chylomicrons and protein) in the urine, making it milky.
- It needs an abnormal **lymphourinary fistula**, usually at a renal calyceal fornix (pyelolymphatic fistula).

### Relevant lymphatic anatomy
- Intestinal lacteals → intestinal trunk → **cisterna chyli** (L1–L2) → **thoracic duct** → left subclavian–jugular junction.
- Lumbar (para-aortic) trunks from the kidneys join the cisterna. Lymph flows cephalad and right to left.
- Valves normally stop backflow from the cisterna into renal hilar lymphatics.

### Aetiology
| Parasitic (commonest; India, China) | Non-parasitic |
|---|---|
| **Wuchereria bancrofti** (lymphatic filariasis; Culex mosquito in urban India) | Congenital: lymphangiectasia, renal lymphangioma |
| Rarely other parasites | Trauma or surgery (partial nephrectomy, RPLND [retroperitoneal lymph node dissection]) |
|  | Retroperitoneal tumour, fibrosis, tuberculosis, pregnancy |
- Genital and urinary filariasis is almost always *W. bancrofti*; Brugia species spare the genitalia.

### Pathogenesis
- **Life cycle:** a mosquito deposits infective larvae, which mature in central lymphatics over 6–9 months. Adults live 5–7 years in retroperitoneal and genital lymphatics. Females release microfilariae with **nocturnal periodicity**.
- **Obstructive theory:** adult worms and the host reaction cause **lymphangitis and fibrosis** of lymphatics between the intestinal trunks and thoracic duct.
- Lymphatics below the block **dilate**, their **valves fail**, and chyle **refluxes** into renal hilar and peripelvic lymphatics.
- Rising pressure ruptures the thin-walled lymphatics into a calyceal fornix, creating a **pyelolymphatic fistula**. Rarely the fistula is in the ureter or bladder.
- Chyluria appears **earlier than elephantiasis**, is usually **intermittent** and may remit spontaneously.

### Clinical features
- **Milky urine**, worse after fatty meals and on standing; may clear after fasting.
- **Chylous clots:** colic, retention.
- **Haematochyluria** (blood-stained chylous urine).
- **Systemic effects** of fat and protein loss: weight loss, **hypoproteinaemia**, oedema, anaemia, fatigue, lowered immunity.
- Endemic residence, filarial fever, hydrocele or lymphoedema.
- **Severity:** mild (intermittent, no systemic effect); moderate (persistent, clots); severe (haematochyluria, clot retention, weight loss, hypoalbuminaemia).

### Evaluation
**1. Confirm chyle**
- **Ether (chloroform) test:** milky urine shaken with ether clears, because the fat dissolves.
- **Urine triglycerides** positive; Sudan III staining shows fat globules; urine protein raised.
- Differentiate from **phosphaturia** (clears with acetic acid) and **pyuria** (leucocytes, no clearing).

**2. Filarial tests**
- Night (midnight) blood smear: often negative by this stage.
- **Circulating filarial antigen** (card test), eosinophilia, PCR (polymerase chain reaction); ultrasound "filarial dance sign" in scrotal lymphatics.

**3. Lateralise and localise the fistula**
- **Cystoscopy after a fatty meal:** milky efflux from one ureteric orifice; bilateral in some.
- **Retrograde pyelography:** pyelolymphatic backflow.
- **Lymphoscintigraphy:** non-invasive; tracer seen in the kidney and bladder. MR lymphangiography or CT; pedal lymphangiography is the historical gold standard.

**4. Baseline:** albumin, haemoglobin, renal function.

### Management
```
Mild             -> diet + antifilarial drugs
Moderate/persist -> add endoscopic sclerotherapy (repeatable)
Severe or failed -> renal hilar lymphatic disconnection
```
**1. Conservative**
- **Low-fat, high-protein diet with MCT (medium-chain triglyceride) oil**, plenty of fluids. MCTs are absorbed directly into portal blood, not lacteals, so less chyle forms.
- **DEC (diethylcarbamazine)** 2 mg/kg three times daily, traditionally for 12 days (a 1-day course is as effective). It kills microfilariae but only modestly kills adults.
- **Doxycycline** (anti-*Wolbachia*, 200 mg daily for 4–8 weeks); albendazole; ivermectin.

**2. Endoscopic sclerotherapy (renal pelvic instillation)**
- Ureteric catheter on the affected side, head-down position. Instil **silver nitrate (0.5–1%)** or **povidone-iodine**, repeated over several sessions.
- **Mechanism:** chemical lymphangitis and fibrosis seal the fistula.
- Good initial response; recurrence common.
- **Complications:** flank pain, haematuria, papillary necrosis, ureteric stricture, anuria; rarely renal failure or death (silver nitrate).

**3. Surgery: renal hilar lymphatic disconnection (chylolymphatic disconnection)**
- **Indications:** severe disease, failed sclerotherapy, haematochyluria with clot retention, marked weight loss.
- **Steps:**
  1. Full renal mobilisation (nephrolysis).
  2. **Skeletonise the renal artery and vein**, ligating, clipping or sealing every hilar lymphatic.
  3. Strip the upper ureter; remove perinephric fat.
  4. Nephropexy; optional **omental wrap** around the hilum.
  5. On-table cystoscopy confirms clear efflux.
- Reflux occurs only through **hilar lymphatics**, so upper-pole dissection can be limited.
- **Approach:** open, laparoscopic (transperitoneal or retroperitoneal) or robotic. **Retroperitoneoscopic** ligation beats open (shorter operation and stay, less blood loss, fewer complications).
- **Also know:** lymphovenous anastomosis; autotransplantation (rare); nephrectomy obsolete.

**4. Prevention**
- Mass drug administration (albendazole with DEC or ivermectin), bednets; clip lymphatics during renal and nodal surgery.

**Diagram to draw:** retroperitoneal lymphatics with obstructed thoracic duct, dilated hilar lymphatics and pyelolymphatic fistula at a calyceal fornix.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Chyluria = pyelolymphatic fistula, usually from *W. bancrofti* obstruction.
> - Ether test and urine triglycerides confirm; cystoscopy after a fatty meal lateralises.
> - Diet with MCT and DEC → silver nitrate sclerotherapy → laparoscopic hilar lymphatic disconnection.
> - Chyluria precedes elephantiasis and is often intermittent.

# Section 3 · Infections & Inflammation — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Prognostic indicators in emphysematous pyelonephritis.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 3, Q3c

### Definition
- EPN (emphysematous pyelonephritis) is an acute **necrotising** infection of the renal parenchyma and perirenal tissue by **gas-forming organisms**. It is a urological emergency.
- Most patients are **diabetic**; obstruction in about a quarter.
- Organisms: *E. coli* (commonest), *Klebsiella*, *Proteus*. Proposed mechanism: high tissue glucose fermented to CO₂ in poorly perfused tissue.

### Imaging-based prognostic classifications
**1. Huang–Tseng CT classification (2000)**
| Class | Gas location |
|---|---|
| 1 | Collecting system only (emphysematous pyelitis) |
| 2 | Renal parenchyma, no extrarenal extension |
| 3A | Extension into **perinephric** space |
| 3B | Extension into **pararenal** space |
| 4 | **Bilateral** EPN or EPN in a **solitary kidney** |

**2. Wan classification (CT pattern)**
| Type | CT | Mortality |
|---|---|---|
| I | Streaky or mottled gas, **no fluid**; rapid parenchymal destruction | 50–60% |
| II | Renal or perirenal **fluid**, bubbly or loculated gas, or gas in collecting system | <20% |
- **Why:** fluid means a walled-off collection that can be drained. Streaky gas without fluid means necrosis faster than the host can contain it.

### Clinical and laboratory prognostic factors
- **Huang–Tseng risk factors:** **thrombocytopenia**, **AKI (acute kidney injury)/raised creatinine**, **altered sensorium**, **shock**.
- **Other adverse factors:** hypoalbuminaemia, bacteraemia, need for haemodialysis, polymicrobial infection.

### How prognosis guides management
```
All: resuscitation, glucose control, IV antibiotics, relieve obstruction (stent/PCN)
Class 1–2               -> antibiotics + PCD
Class 3–4, <2 risk factors -> antibiotics + PCD (usually succeeds)
Class 3–4, >=2 risk factors or PCD failure -> nephrectomy
```
- PCD (percutaneous catheter drainage); PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy).
- Reported mortality 19–43%; routine emergency nephrectomy still carried 40–50%, so drainage-first care is now standard.
- **Also know:** emphysematous pyelitis (gas in the collecting system only) is often non-diabetic and responds to antibiotics.

**Diagram to draw:** CT sketches of Huang–Tseng classes 1, 2, 3A/3B and 4.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Huang–Tseng: 1 pelvis, 2 parenchyma, 3A perinephric, 3B pararenal, 4 bilateral or solitary.
> - Four risk factors: thrombocytopenia, AKI, altered sensorium, shock.
> - Wan type I (no fluid) 50–60% mortality; type II <20%.
> - Drainage first; nephrectomy for extensive disease with ≥2 risk factors.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 3 · Infections & Inflammation

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Renal and genitourinary TB: etiopathogenesis, presentation, (laboratory and newer) diagnosis and management (LAQ)

- Kidney 80%; periglomerular seeding; heals by fibrosis, so obstruction worsens on treatment.
- Sterile pyuria; 3–5 early-morning urine AFB cultures; culture is the gold standard and the only test that monitors treatment.
- GeneXpert (Xpert) detects rifampicin resistance in 2 hours; cannot monitor treatment.
- LF-LAM for HIV with CD4 under 200.
- TST and IGRA show infection, not active disease.
- 2HRZE/4HR with pyridoxine; surgery after 4–6 weeks of ATT.
- Relapse up to 22%; follow up 10 years.

#### Discuss the Aetiopathogenesis, Clinical feature and management of Chyluria. (LAQ)

- Chyluria = pyelolymphatic fistula, usually from *W. bancrofti* obstruction.
- Ether test and urine triglycerides confirm; cystoscopy after a fatty meal lateralises.
- Diet with MCT and DEC → silver nitrate sclerotherapy → laparoscopic hilar lymphatic disconnection.
- Chyluria precedes elephantiasis and is often intermittent.

#### Prognostic indicators in emphysematous pyelonephritis. (SAQ)

- Huang–Tseng: 1 pelvis, 2 parenchyma, 3A perinephric, 3B pararenal, 4 bilateral or solitary.
- Four risk factors: thrombocytopenia, AKI, altered sensorium, shock.
- Wan type I (no fluid) 50–60% mortality; type II <20%.
- Drainage first; nephrectomy for extensive disease with ≥2 risk factors.

# Section 4 · Paediatric Urology 1 — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Management of bilateral VUR: medical treatment, surgical techniques, selection and long-term outcome
**Asked as:**
- Describe medical management of VUR. Describe treatment of an 8-yr male child with b/l grade IV VUR. — *Summer 2015 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)*
- Discuss mgt. of b/l VUR. — *Practice Set 2 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)*
- Discuss treatment of b/l VUR. Describe surgical techniques and criteria for their selection — *Practice Set 2 Paper III Q3d (SAQ)*
- Discuss the management of B/l VUR and discuss its long term outcome — *Practice Set 6 Paper III Q1 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

(also answered in 2015-Summer Paper 3, Q2 and 2020-Summer Paper 1, Q2; long-term outcome added from Practice Set 6 Paper 3, Q1. The SAQ Practice Set 2 Paper III Q3d: **See Practice Set 2 Paper 3, Q2 (full answer).**)

### Introduction: definition and pathophysiology
- **VUR (vesicoureteral reflux)**: retrograde flow of urine from the bladder to the upper tract. **Primary**: deficient UVJ (ureterovesical junction) flap-valve; short intravesical tunnel; tunnel length to ureteric diameter should be about **5:1** (Paquin). Early budding gives a lateral orifice and dysplasia.
- **Secondary**: high bladder pressure from PUV (posterior urethral valves), neurogenic bladder or BBD (bladder and bowel dysfunction).
- In about **30% of children with UTI (urinary tract infection)** and 30% of siblings.
- **Bilateral VUR is a high-risk feature:** it resolves less and scars more.
- **Infection, not reflux (not sterile reflux), injures/damages the kidney.** Infected urine plus intrarenal reflux causes scars, hypertension and CKD (chronic kidney disease); some high-grade "scars" are congenital dysplasia. So management treats/targets infection risk, not the reflux itself.

### International grading (VCUG, voiding cystourethrogram)
| Grade | Findings |
|---|---|
| I | Non-dilated ureter only (ureter only, not dilated) |
| II | Ureter, pelvis and calyces reached; no dilatation, sharp fornices |
| III | Mild–moderate dilatation of ureter and pelvis; minimal forniceal blunting |
| IV | Moderate dilatation and tortuosity; fornices obliterated but **papillary impressions kept** |
| V | Gross dilatation and tortuosity; **papillary impressions lost**; intrarenal reflux |
- **Also know:** grading varies between observers; UDR (ureteral diameter ratio) predicts resolution better.

**Diagram to draw:** grades I–V and a Cohen cross-trigonal reimplant.

### Evaluation
- History of febrile UTIs and BBD; BP (blood pressure), growth, spine, urine protein (proteinuria), creatinine.
- **RBUS (renal-bladder ultrasound)**: a normal scan does not exclude VUR.
- **VCUG**: grade and urethra (excludes PUV). **Nuclear cystogram** (lower dose) for follow-up; **ceVUS (contrast-enhanced voiding urosonography)** avoids radiation.
- **DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid) scan**: scars and split function; a defect persisting 6 months is a scar.
- **UDR (ureteral diameter ratio)** predicts resolution and breakthrough UTI better than grade.

### Natural history
- Resolution falls with grade and age: grade II 63–85% resolve, grade III about 50%, **grades IV–V and bilateral III rarely**. **Bilateral grade IV persists most** (AUA (American Urological Association) 1997: about 10% resolve in 5 years). Infants with high grade still resolve fairly well.

### Risk stratification (Hidas)

| Low risk | High risk |
|---|---|
| Male, >5 years | Female, <1 year (infant) |
| Unilateral, grade I–II | **Bilateral**, grade III–V |
| No BBD, no scars | BBD, scars, multiple febrile UTIs |

- Two-year breakthrough UTI on CAP (continuous antibiotic prophylaxis): 8.6% low, 26% intermediate, 62.5% high risk.

### Medical management
- **Aim**: prevent febrile UTI and scars while VUR resolves.
- **Treat BBD first:** timed voiding, hydration, constipation treatment, anticholinergics if needed. BBD raises breakthrough UTI and lowers resolution and endoscopic success.
- **CAP (continuous antibiotic prophylaxis)**: one low nightly dose at night, a quarter of the treatment dose.

| Drug / Agent | Age | Dose | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| Trimethoprim or amoxicillin | <2 months (young infants) | — | Avoid sulfonamide (jaundice; neonatal jaundice) |
| TMP-SMX (trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole) | >2 months | 2 mg/kg TMP | Avoid under 2 months (kernicterus); used in RIVUR |
| Nitrofurantoin | >2 months | 1–2 mg/kg | Avoid in G6PD (glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase) deficiency; rare lung, liver toxicity |

- **RIVUR (2014)**, 607 children: recurrent UTI **23.6% → 12.9%** on TMP-SMX; **scarring unchanged**; most benefit with BBD; benefit mainly in high risk.
- **Swedish Reflux Trial** (grade III–IV, age 1–2 years): girls had febrile UTI 19% on CAP, 23% after endoscopy, 57% on surveillance; **no difference in boys**.
- **Also know:** IRSC (International Reflux Study in Children) and Birmingham trials: surgery equalled medical care for scarring (new scars); PRIVENT: 6% absolute UTI reduction.
- **Circumcision** or topical steroid for phimosis: about 4 boys with VUR circumcised prevent one UTI (NNT (number needed to treat) about 4).
- After stopping CAP, 8–10% have a febrile UTI.
- **Guidelines**: AUA 2010 recommends CAP for VUR with BBD and treating BBD first; EAU/ESPU (European Association of Urology/European Society for Paediatric Urology) give high-risk children CAP and intervene early after breakthrough infection (correct after breakthrough infection).

### Indications for correction
- Breakthrough febrile UTI despite good bladder and bowel habits.
- New scars or falling function.
- Persistent high-grade (IV–V) bilateral VUR (girls traditionally corrected before puberty; benefit unproven).
- Non-adherence (poor adherence) or intolerance to CAP; parental choice.
- **Controversy:** asymptomatic bilateral high-grade VUR in an older boy with normal kidneys can be observed (controversial).

### Case: 8-year-old boy, bilateral grade IV
- Exclude PUV, neurogenic bladder and BBD; DMSA, creatinine, BP.
- This rarely resolves at 8 years, but older boys have low UTI risk, so treating an **asymptomatic** boy is **controversial**.
- **No UTI, no scars**: observation or CAP with BBD therapy. **Breakthrough UTI, scars or falling function**: correct surgically.
- **Choice here: bilateral Cohen.**

```
Exclude PUV/neurogenic bladder, treat BBD
 -> febrile UTI, scars or renal loss? Yes -> bilateral Cohen (or Dx/HA)
                                     No  -> observe or CAP; correct if UTI/scar
```

### Surgical options and techniques
**Principles:** exclude secondary VUR; gentle handling; tension-free; tunnel 5:1; straight course.

**1. Endoscopic injection (Dx/HA, dextranomer/hyaluronic acid)**
- **STING** (subureteric Teflon injection): 2–3 mm below the orifice. **HIT** (hydrodistension implantation): inside the orifice, better (HIT beats STING). **Double HIT** about 90%.
- Aim for a "volcano" mound; day case. Repeat once or twice, then reimplant.
- Success per injection (Elder 2006): grade I–II about 79%, III 72%, **IV 63%**, V 51%.
- Antibiotics 3 months, ultrasound at 2–6 weeks.
- Recurrence about 20% at 2 years (20% recurred by 2 years); late obstruction reported.

**2. Open ureteral reimplantation** (success **95.9%** overall, Elder 1997)

| Technique | Method / Tunnel | Key point: strength / weakness |
|---|---|---|
| **Cohen** (intravesical) | Cross-trigonal | Commonest, success over 98%; one bladder opening (cystotomy) for both sides; later ureteroscopy difficult/hard |
| Politano–Leadbetter (intravesical) | New superomedial hiatus above the old one | Long tunnel; blind hiatus risks bowel injury |
| Glenn–Anderson (intravesical) | Advanced towards/to bladder neck | Least kinking; short tunnel |
| **Lich–Gregoir** (extravesical) | Detrusorotomy, ureter laid in | No haematuria or spasm; **retention up to 10% after bilateral repair** (pelvic plexus injury) |
| **RALUR / RALUR-EV** (robot-assisted laparoscopic ureteral reimplantation; robot-assisted extravesical) | Extravesical; Y-shaped detrusorotomy | Less pain; results approaching open |

### Criteria for selection (best option for each situation)

| Situation | Preferred option |
|---|---|
| Grades II–IV, parents want least invasive | Dx/HA injection (lower success at higher grade) |
| **Bilateral VUR, small or trabeculated bladder** | **Cohen** (both sides through one cystotomy) |
| Grade V, dilated ureter | Intravesical reimplant ± tapering (Politano–Leadbetter or Cohen) |
| Unilateral, older child | Lich–Gregoir or RALUR-EV |
| Bilateral extravesical | Nerve-sparing dissection; warn about retention |
| Duplex system | Common sheath reimplant |
| Failed injection | Reimplant (not harder after Dx/HA) |
| Neurogenic or PUV bladder (secondary VUR) | Treat the bladder first (anticholinergics, CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation)) |

### Complications and follow-up
- Ultrasound at 6–12 weeks. Complications: transient obstruction, contralateral VUR (about 1 in 10, usually resolves). Persistent reflux usually resolves by 1 year. Obstruction early (oedema): stent or nephrostomy; late (ischaemia, kink): redo.
- Lifelong BP checks; scarred women risk pre-eclampsia.

### Long-term outcome

| Outcome | Facts |
|---|---|
| **Renal scarring** | About 15% get a febrile UTI within 2 years; about 15% of those scar. Risk highest under 1 year. Much "scarring" in boys with high grade is **congenital dysplasia**, not preventable by any treatment |
| **Hypertension** | Reflux nephropathy may be the commonest cause of childhood hypertension after essential hypertension (renin–angiotensin). **34–38%** of scarred children are hypertensive as adults. Correcting VUR does not lower BP; nephrectomy of a small scarred kidney helps selected cases |
| **CKD (chronic kidney disease) and ESKD (end-stage kidney disease)** | Now rare: RRT (renal replacement therapy) from reflux nephropathy fell from 1.9 to 0.6 per million (USRDS (United States Renal Data System), 1996–2014). Bilateral scars cause hyperfiltration, proteinuria, concentrating defect, tubular acidosis |
| **Pregnancy** | VUR in late pregnancy: pyelonephritis about one-third versus under 5%. Scars: about **3× hypertension, 8× pre-eclampsia**; bilateral worse. Impaired function: fetal loss. Reimplanted women still get UTIs |
| Growth; offspring | Catch-up growth after infection control; about 43% of screened offspring have VUR |

- **Concept:** in adult life **the scar, not the reflux, carries the risk**, so correction does not remove it. Scarred patients need lifelong BP, proteinuria and creatinine checks.

```
Treat BBD -> low risk: observe | high risk (bilateral): CAP
Breakthrough UTI / new scar / persistent IV-V -> Dx/HA or Cohen
Scars -> lifelong BP and proteinuria checks
```

> **Key points to remember (including SAQ angle):**
> - Grade V loses papillary impressions; bilateral grade IV rarely resolves (persists most); bilateral VUR is high risk.
> - RIVUR: CAP halves recurrent UTI but not scarring.
> - Treat BBD (bladder and bowel dysfunction) and give CAP (continuous antibiotic prophylaxis) first; correct for breakthrough febrile UTI (urinary tract infection), new scars or persistent grade IV–V.
> - Endoscopic Dx/HA (dextranomer/hyaluronic acid) by HIT (hydrodistension implantation): least invasive; lower success at higher grades.
> - Open reimplantation 95.9%; Cohen cross-trigonal reimplant (over 98%) suits bilateral VUR (vesicoureteral reflux) and does both sides through one cystotomy.
> - Bilateral extravesical (Lich–Gregoir) repair risks retention up to 10%.
> - 34–38% of scarred children become hypertensive; correction does not lower BP.
> - Scars bring 8× pre-eclampsia risk: the scar, not the reflux, matters.

## Embryology of the penis and management of proximal hypospadias (including Koyanagi repair)
**Asked as:**
- Describe embryology of penis. Describe management of proximal hypospadias. — *Winter 2017 Paper III Q1 (LAQ)*
- Koyanagi Repair: Indication, procedure and complications? — *Summer 2021 Paper III Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

### A. Embryology of the penis
**Indifferent stage (up to 8–9 weeks)**
- Cloacal folds form the **genital tubercle**; after the cloaca divides (7 weeks), **urethral folds** flank the urogenital membrane, with **labioscrotal swellings** laterally. Both sexes look identical until about 9 weeks.

**Hormonal control**
- SRY (sex-determining region Y) → testis → **Leydig cells (about 8 weeks)** secrete testosterone.
- **Type 2 5α-reductase** in the tubercle converts it to **DHT (dihydrotestosterone)**, which acts on androgen receptors. **DHT at 8–12 weeks** is the key mediator; the critical window is weeks 8–16. Without androgen action the default is female.
- First sign: longer anogenital distance, then tubercle elongation (sonic hedgehog-driven).

| Primordium (germ layer) | Male | Female |
