|---|---|---|
| Genital tubercle (mesoderm: corpora; ectoderm: skin, prepuce) | Glans, corpora | Clitoris |
| Urethral folds | Fuse → penile urethra, median raphe | Labia minora |
| Labioscrotal swellings | Scrotum | Labia majora |
| Urethral plate (endoderm) | Whole urethra, including glanular | Vestibule |

**Urethral formation: the "double zipper"**
1. **Opening zipper** (both sexes): the urethral plate canalises proximal to distal into a urethral groove.
2. **Closing zipper** (androgen-dependent, male only): the folds fuse in the ventral midline, forming the tubular urethra and raphe.
- The **glanular urethra** forms by direct canalisation of the endodermal plate; Glenister's ectodermal ingrowth theory (1954) is outdated.
- Timetable: coronal sulcus 11–12 weeks; shaft fused by 16 weeks; complete by about 18 weeks; fetal ventral curvature gone by 20 weeks.
- **Prepuce** fuses dorsally first; ventral fusion awaits urethral closure, hence the **dorsal hood** in hypospadias.
- Dorsal nerves run at 11 and 1 o'clock, leaving a **12 o'clock nerve-free zone** (safe plication site).
- **Hypospadias = arrest of the closing zipper**: the meatus stops where closure stopped, and unfused dysplastic ventral tissue tethers the shaft into curvature.

### B. Proximal hypospadias: evaluation
- Meatus proximal shaft, penoscrotal, scrotal or perineal (10–25%). Severity is judged **after degloving**: meatus, plate and curvature (GMS (glans–meatus–shaft) score).
- Examine stretched penile length, glans width, penoscrotal transposition, bifid scrotum and **both testes**.
- **DSD (disorder of sex development) work-up** if hypospadias plus an undescended testis (especially non-palpable), perineal meatus or ambiguity: karyotype, ultrasound for Müllerian remnants, testosterone, LH (luteinising hormone), FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone), AMH (anti-Müllerian hormone). A 46,XY karyotype can still hide 5α-reductase deficiency or androgen insensitivity.
- No routine VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) or renal ultrasound; prostatic utricle commoner.

### C. Preparation
- **Timing:** 6–18 months (AAP (American Academy of Pediatrics): 6–12 months).
- **Preoperative androgen** if glans width <14–15 mm: e.g. testosterone 2 mg/kg IM (intramuscular) at 5 and 2 weeks before surgery (about 4 mm glans gain). Side effects transient; evidence mixed, but two RCTs (randomised controlled trials) showed fewer complications.

### D. Curvature correction (decides one- vs two-stage)
```
Deglove + excise dysplastic dartos + artificial erection
   ├─ straight        → urethroplasty
   ├─ residual <30°   → dorsal midline plication (12 o'clock)
   └─ >30°            → divide urethral plate
                          └─ still >30° → ventral corporotomy ± graft → staged repair
```
| | Dorsal plication (Nesbit, Baskin) | Ventral lengthening |
|---|---|---|
| Method | Shortens the convex dorsal side | 1–3 transverse corporotomies, or one full-thickness 9-to-3 o'clock corporotomy with dermis, tunica vaginalis or SIS (small intestinal submucosa) graft |
| Use | Mild (≤15–30°) | Moderate–severe |
| Recurrence | 28% | 9% |

### E. Urethroplasty
| Approach | Technique | Comment |
|---|---|---|
| One-stage **proximal TIP (tubularised incised plate)** | Plate kept, incised and tubularised | Recurrent curvature, stricture: largely abandoned for severe cases |
| One-stage **onlay island flap** | Inner preputial flap on preserved plate | Diverticulum, fistula |
| One-stage **TPIF (transverse preputial island flap, Duckett tube)** | Tube after plate division | High complications |
| **Two-stage Bracka / STAG (staged tubularised autograft)** | Stage 1: correct curvature, divide plate, quilt inner-prepuce or buccal graft. Stage 2 (≥6 months): tubularise, barrier layer | **Preferred for most proximal cases**; STAG lowest complications (about 23%) |
| **Two-stage Byars** | Dorsal preputial flaps moved ventrally, tubularised later | Complications 43–81% |
- **Why stage?** A urethroplasty laid over a fresh corporal graft fails more often; staging gives a supple, vascular bed.
- Principles: magnification, tension-free closure, offset suture lines, **vascularised barrier layer** (dartos, tunica vaginalis), fine absorbable sutures, no hair-bearing skin. Stent, compressive dressing, oxybutynin; scrotoplasty for transposition.

### E2. Koyanagi repair (one-stage meatus-based parameatal foreskin flap): indication, procedure and complications
(proximal hypospadias also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q1)
#### Definition
- **One-stage repair of proximal hypospadias** with a **meatus-based parameatal foreskin flap** (Koyanagi). Skin strips round the meatus continue into the inner prepuce on both sides; they are swung ventrally and tubularised.
- **Principle:** the neourethra stays attached to the native meatus, so there is **no circular proximal anastomosis**, and the urethral plate can be divided to correct severe chordee.

#### Indications
- **Proximal hypospadias** (penoscrotal, scrotal, perineal) with **severe curvature** needing plate transection, and an **ample, unoperated prepuce**.
- **Contraindications:** prior circumcision or failed repair; small glans (give preoperative testosterone); curvature needing a ventral corporal graft (stage instead).

#### Procedure
```
Stay suture; artificial erection
→ incise round the meatus, along both sides of the plate to the
  corona and round the inner prepuce (meatus-based "wings")
→ deglove; divide the plate; excise dysplastic dartos
→ residual curvature <30° → dorsal midline plication
→ split the dorsal prepuce; bring both wings ventrally on dartos pedicles
→ join inner edges, then outer edges, over a catheter → tube
→ glanuloplasty, meatus at the tip
→ dartos barrier layer; skin cover ± scrotoplasty
→ stent 7–10 days; compressive dressing
```
- Modified and "neo-modified" versions add flap fixation and barrier layers to reduce fistula and diverticulum.

#### Complications
| Complication | Cause |
|---|---|
| **Fistula** (commonest) | Long suture line; repair ≥6 months later |
| Meatal stenosis, stricture | Distal flap ischaemia |
| **Diverticulum** | No spongiosum; flap not fixed to the shaft |
| Glans dehiscence | Tension |
| Residual or recurrent curvature | Plication alone in severe cases |
| Flap necrosis, infection, poor cosmesis | Pedicle injury |
- Proximal repairs overall have **23–68%** complications, so many now prefer **two-stage repair** (STAG (staged tubularised autograft), Bracka).

**Also know:** Duckett TPIF (transverse preputial island flap); onlay island flap; Byars two-stage (see table above).

**Diagram to draw (Koyanagi):** meatus-based foreskin wings split dorsally and joined ventrally into a tube.

### F. Complications and outcomes
- Complications **23–68%**; 25–75% need further surgery after planned staging.
- **Fistula** commonest (repair ≥6 months later; exclude distal obstruction), glans dehiscence, meatal stenosis (<8 Fr before puberty), stricture, diverticulum (no spongiosum), **recurrent curvature >30°**, BXO (balanitis xerotica obliterans).
- A long, rigid neourethra raises resistance (Poiseuille), causing fistula and diverticulum.
- Follow through puberty; patient-reported outcomes (PPS (Penile Perception Score), HOPE (Hypospadias Objective Penile Evaluation) score).

**Diagram to draw:** double-zipper urethral formation; curvature algorithm.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - DHT at 8–12 weeks; opening then closing zipper; 12 o'clock nerve-free zone.
> - Hypospadias plus undescended testis → DSD work-up.
> - <30° plication; >30° divide plate ± ventral graft and stage.
> - STAG/Bracka preferred; fistula commonest complication.
> - Koyanagi: one stage; meatus-based parameatal foreskin flap.
> - Koyanagi: plate divided; no proximal circular anastomosis.
> - Koyanagi: fistula commonest; diverticulum, meatal stenosis.

## Embryology and management of ectopic ureter.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 3, Q1

### Definition
- An **ectopic ureter** is any ureter, single or duplex, that does **not open on the trigone**. True ectopia means an orifice **at or distal to the bladder neck**.
- Commoner in girls. In girls most ectopic ureters drain the **upper pole of a duplex kidney**; in boys most are **single-system**.

### Embryology
- **Week 4–6:** the **ureteric bud** arises from the **mesonephric (wolffian) duct** and induces the metanephric blastema to form the kidney.
- The duct segment distal to the bud is absorbed into the **urogenital sinus**; the ureteric orifice then moves cranially and laterally, the wolffian orifice caudally to the verumontanum.
- **Mackie–Stephens bud theory:**

| Bud position on wolffian duct | Absorption | Orifice | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| Too caudal | Early | High and lateral, short tunnel | VUR (vesicoureteral reflux) |
| Too cranial | Late or never | Low and medial (bladder neck, urethra) or stays on wolffian duct | **Ectopic ureter**, often obstructed |

- A bud from the wrong place meets poor blastema, so **renal dysplasia increases with the degree of ectopia**.
- **Duplex system:** the upper-pole bud is more cranial, absorbed later, and ends **lower and more medial** (**Weigert–Meyer law**): upper pole obstructs; lower pole refluxes.

### Sites of ectopia (the key sex difference)
| | Female | Male |
|---|---|---|
| Sites (commonest first) | Urethra, vestibule, vagina; rarely cervix, uterus, rectum (via **Gartner duct**) | Posterior urethra, seminal vesicle; also vas, ejaculatory duct |
| Relation to sphincter | Can open **below** the external sphincter | Always **above** the external sphincter |
| Continence | **Continuous dribbling with normal voiding** | **Never incontinent** |
| Typical presentation | Constant dampness, perineal pus, UTI (urinary tract infection) | **Epididymo-orchitis**, UTI, flank pain, hydronephrosis |

- **Zinner syndrome:** ectopic ureter into the seminal vesicle with ipsilateral renal agenesis or dysplasia and a seminal vesicle cyst.

### Clinical features
- **Prenatal hydronephrosis**: most are detected this way.
- **Girl:** wet day and night despite normal voids; she is never dry for 30–60 minutes. Often misdiagnosed as voiding dysfunction.
- **Infection:** low-grade fever with spikes; urine culture may be negative because the infected moiety does not drain into the bladder.
- **Boy:** recurrent epididymitis in a young boy demands an upper-tract ultrasound.
- **Also know:** pain then perineal discharge; bilateral single-system ectopia with small bladder and wide bladder neck.

### Evaluation
| Test | What it shows |
|---|---|
| **Ultrasound** (first) | Dilated upper pole and ureter behind the bladder; parenchyma. Bladder view separates it from ureterocele |
| **VCUG** (voiding cystourethrogram) | Lower-pole or contralateral VUR ("drooping lily"), reflux into the ectopic ureter |
| **DMSA** (dimercaptosuccinic acid) | Gold standard for moiety function ("some" versus "none"); MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) if drainage needed |
| **MR (magnetic resonance) urography** | Non-dilated upper pole, small dysplastic kidney, complex anatomy |
| **Examination / cystovaginoscopy** | Perineal orifice between urethra and vagina, cannulated for contrast |

- **Also know:** clear urine leaking despite methylene blue in the bladder suggests an ectopic ureter; CT for a small unseen kidney.

### Management
**Goals:** preserve renal function; remove infection, obstruction and reflux; achieve continence; with least morbidity. Start antibiotic prophylaxis in the neonate with significant dilation.

```
Neonate with sepsis or massive dilation
   → end cutaneous ureterostomy (or nephrostomy); DMSA later
DUPLEX system
   Upper pole non-functioning → upper-pole heminephroureterectomy
   Upper pole worth keeping   → ureteroureterostomy (UU) / pyeloureterostomy
   + lower-pole VUR           → common sheath reimplant
                                (or lower-pole reimplant + distal UU)
SINGLE system
   Functioning     → ureteral reimplant (± tapering)
   Non-functioning → nephroureterectomy (laparoscopic)
   Bilateral       → bladder neck reconstruction ± augmentation
```

- **Upper-pole heminephroureterectomy** (open, laparoscopic or robotic): upper-pole ureter as a handle, passed behind the hilum; ligate polar vessels; excise the pole; close over fat. Dissect on the ureter wall to protect the **lower-pole blood supply**. The stump is rarely a problem.
- **Ureteroureterostomy (UU):** end-to-side upper into lower pole ureter, distal (inguinal incision) or proximal (preferred with big size mismatch). **Stent the recipient lower-pole ureter** cystoscopically for identification. Now used even for non-functioning poles; feared hypertension has not been shown.
- **Common sheath reimplantation:** do not separate the two ureters (they share blood supply).

### Complications
- Loss of lower-pole function (most serious); urinoma (up to 20% after minimally invasive partial nephrectomy, mostly harmless); stump infection; new reflux; persistent incontinence.

### Recent advances
- Robotic UU and heminephrectomy; single-site surgery; non-dismembered side-to-side ureterovesicostomy in infants.

**Diagram to draw:** duplex kidney showing Weigert–Meyer orifices, and female versus male ectopic sites relative to the external sphincter.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ectopia = orifice at or below the bladder neck; bud too cranial on the wolffian duct.
> - Girls: continuous dribbling with normal voiding; boys: never incontinent, epididymitis.
> - DMSA decides keep (UU/reimplant) versus remove (heminephroureterectomy).
> - Protect the lower-pole blood supply; stent the recipient ureter for UU.

## Development and clinical anatomy of Vesico-Ureteric junction and management of grade 3 VUR in adult?
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2020-Summer Paper 3, Q2

### Development (summary; for detail see 2020-Summer Paper 1, Q2)
- **Weeks 4–6:** the ureteric bud arises from the mesonephric (Wolffian) duct. The common excretory duct is absorbed into the UGS (urogenital sinus), so ureter and duct open separately. The ureteric orifice moves cranially and laterally; the ejaculatory duct ends caudally in the prostatic urethra.
- **Bud position (Mackie–Stephens):** a bud arising too low reaches the UGS early → high, lateral orifice → short tunnel → **VUR (vesicoureteral reflux)**, often with a dysplastic kidney. A bud arising too high → caudal, medial, ectopic orifice.
- **Weigert–Meyer** (duplex): lower-pole orifice lateral, refluxes; upper-pole caudal-medial, ectopic.

### Clinical anatomy of the VUJ (vesico-ureteric junction)
- **Course:** juxtavesical ureter in **Waldeyer's sheath** → oblique intramural segment through the detrusor hiatus → submucosal tunnel → orifice (caudal and medial to the entry).
- **Intramural length 1.2–2.5 cm in adults** (0.5–0.8 cm in neonates). **Narrowest part** of the ureter (about 3–4 mm): commonest stone impaction site.
- **Trigone:** superficial trigone (ureteric longitudinal muscle, to the verumontanum); deep trigone (Waldeyer's sheath plus detrusor, to the bladder neck). Base is the interureteric ridge.
- **Antireflux mechanism:**
  - **Passive flap valve:** filling compresses the submucosal ureter against firm detrusor backing.
  - **Paquin:** tunnel length to diameter **5:1** in normal VUJs vs 1.4:1 in refluxing ones.
  - **Active:** ureteric peristalsis opens the tunnel for each bolus, then it closes.
  - **Bladder pressure:** high storage or voiding pressure overwhelms a normal valve.
- **Orifice (Lyon):** position A (medial trigone) to C (lateral); shape cone (normal) → stadium → horseshoe → **golf-hole**. The more lateral and open, the more reflux.
- **Relations:** vas (male) or uterine artery (female) crosses the ureter. **Pelvic plexus nerves lie dorsomedial to the VUJ**; wide extravesical dissection causes voiding dysfunction.
- **Clinical:** Hutch diverticulum at the hiatus; "fish-hook" ureter with BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia).

### Grade 3 VUR (International Reflux Study)
- Mild–moderate dilatation of ureter, pelvis and calyces, minimal forniceal blunting.

### VUR in adults
- **Adults rarely lose function from reflux alone**, unless high bladder pressure, stasis and UTI (urinary tract infection) coexist. The **scar**, not the reflux, carries the risk. Spontaneous resolution is unlikely.
- **Causes:** persistent primary VUR; **secondary**: BOO (bladder outlet obstruction), neurogenic bladder, resection near the orifice, ureterocele incision, paraureteral diverticulum, transplant ureter, TB (tuberculosis).
- **Presentation:** recurrent febrile UTI, pyelonephritis (especially in pregnancy), loin pain on voiding, hypertension, proteinuria, CKD (chronic kidney disease).

### Evaluation
- **VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram):** grade, bladder, diverticulum, outlet.
- US (ultrasound) and **DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid) scan** for scars and split function; MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) renogram if obstruction is suspected.
- **UDS (urodynamics):** compliance, voiding pressure, residual. Cystoscopy; blood pressure, creatinine, urine protein.

### Management
**1. Treat secondary causes first** (relieve BOO; CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) and antimuscarinic for neurogenic bladder), then reassess.

**2. Primary grade 3 VUR:**
| Situation | Management |
|---|---|
| Incidental; no UTI, no scars | Observe: timed voiding, bowel care, prompt UTI treatment |
| Recurrent febrile UTI, new scars, falling function | **Correct the reflux** |
| Woman planning pregnancy with scars or prior pyelonephritis | Consider correction before pregnancy |
| Poorly functioning scarred kidney with infection or hypertension | Nephrectomy |
- Low-dose prophylaxis selectively; adult evidence is limited.

**3. Correction:**
- **Endoscopic injection** (Dx/HA (dextranomer/hyaluronic acid)): day case, repeatable, but less effective in adults (see 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3c).
- **Ureteroneocystostomy** (open, laparoscopic or robotic): gentle handling, tension-free, straight course, tunnel **5:1**.

| Technique | Type | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Lich–Gregoir / **RALUR-EV** (robot-assisted extravesical) | Extravesical detrusorotomy | Suits adults; no cystotomy; stay close to ureter (nerves) |
| Politano–Leadbetter | Intravesical, new hiatus | Long tunnel |
| Cohen cross-trigonal | Intravesical | Over 98% success; later retrograde access hard |
- **Psoas hitch** if length is needed; tapering seldom needed in grade 3.

### Complications and follow-up
- Persistent or contralateral reflux, obstruction, leak, retention after bilateral extravesical repair.
- US at 6–12 weeks; lifelong blood pressure and renal checks if scarred.

**Diagram to draw:** oblique intramural ureter in Waldeyer's sheath with submucosal tunnel and detrusor backing.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bud too low → lateral orifice → short tunnel → VUR.
> - Adult intramural ureter 1.2–2.5 cm; Paquin 5:1.
> - In adults the scar, not the reflux, carries the risk; exclude secondary causes first.
> - Correct for recurrent pyelonephritis, new scars or pregnancy plans.

# Section 4 · Paediatric Urology 1 — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Bilateral Wilms tumour: treatment strategies
**Asked as:**
- Treatment strategies for bilateral Wilm's tumour — *Summer 2016 Paper III Q3c (SAQ)*
- Management of bilateral Wilms Tumor? — *Summer 2020 Paper III Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Introduction
- **Synchronous bilateral WT (Wilms tumour)** is **5–7%**: **COG (Children's Oncology Group) stage V**. Each kidney is staged separately.
- Linked to (syndromes) **WAGR (Wilms, aniridia, genitourinary anomalies, retardation)**, Denys–Drash (about 50% risk) and BWS (Beckwith–Wiedemann syndrome); **nephrogenic rests in over 90%**.
- Aim (goal): **cure while preserving renal tissue** (nephrons). Renal failure is about 15% at 15 years; ESRD (end-stage renal disease) mainly follows (from) bilateral nephrectomy.

### Evaluation
- Ultrasound (US) → contrast CT (computed tomography) or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) abdomen, chest CT; IVC (inferior vena cava) and coagulation checks; syndrome review.
- **No upfront biopsy and no upfront nephrectomy.**

### COG approach (AREN0534)
```
VCR + AMD + DOX x 6 weeks (no upfront biopsy)
        |
   imaging at week 6
   /              \
NSS feasible     not feasible
 -> surgery       -> 6 more weeks chemotherapy
                   -> surgery by week 12
                   -> if poor response: open BILATERAL biopsy
```
```
VCR + AMD + DOX × 6 weeks (no biopsy)
→ imaging at week 6
   ├─ resectable → bilateral NSS
   └─ not → 6 more weeks → surgery by week 12
        poor response → bilateral OPEN biopsy
→ further therapy by each kidney's stage and histology ± flank RT
```
- VCR = vincristine, AMD = actinomycin D (dactinomycin), DOX = doxorubicin; NSS = nephron-sparing surgery, RT = radiotherapy.
- **Why stop at 12 weeks**: in NWTS-4 (National Wilms Tumor Study 4), prolonged chemotherapy in poor responders delayed surgery and led to anaplasia (some tumours became anaplastic). Non-shrinkage (non-shrinking tumour) usually means differentiation (is often differentiated), cured by excision.
- **Why open, bilateral biopsy**: anaplasia is discordant (differs) between kidneys in about 4 of 5.

### Surgery
- **Bilateral NSS** (partial nephrectomy or enucleation); frozen section; lymph node sampling; avoid spill.
- Compressed normal parenchyma is often underestimated, so try NSS in every case. Expert centres achieve bilateral NSS in over 90%. Enucleation: positive margins 23–36%, good outcome with (flank) radiotherapy.
- Unresectable both sides: bilateral nephrectomy and dialysis only for refractory tumour; transplant after **1–2 years** tumour-free.

### Adjuvant
- Guided by worst histology and margins: anaplasia or positive margin → intensified chemotherapy and flank radiotherapy.

### Outcomes
- AREN0534: surgery by week 12 in 84%; bilateral NSS 39%; **4-year EFS (event-free survival) 82.1%, OS (overall survival) 94.9%**, against (vs) 56% and 80.8% in (on) NWTS-5.

### SIOP (International Society of Paediatric Oncology)
- Same principle (no biopsy, NSS by week 12) but **two drugs, VCR + AMD**, if non-metastatic.

**Follow-up:** long term (lifelong): blood pressure, proteinuria, creatinine (renal function), imaging (late relapse occurs / possible).

**Diagram to draw:** bilateral tumours with planned partial nephrectomies; AREN0534 timeline (weeks 0, 6, 12).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Stage V, 5–7%; no upfront nephrectomy or biopsy.
> - COG: three drugs for 6 weeks (reassess at 6 weeks), surgery by week 12; SIOP: two drugs.
> - AREN0534: 4-year OS about 95%.
> - Bilateral NSS to avoid ESRD.

## Congenital ureteral anomalies
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Summer Paper 3, Q3f

### Embryology basis
- The **ureteric bud** arises from the **mesonephric (wolffian) duct** at about week 5 and induces the metanephric blastema.
- Anomalies arise from **abnormal budding**, **failed distal canalisation**, **abnormal muscle maturation** or **persistent embryonic vessels**.

### Classification
| Category | Anomaly | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| **Number** | **Duplication**: incomplete (bifid, Y-ureter) or complete | About 1 in 125; unilateral 6× bilateral. Lower pole: VUR (vesicoureteric reflux), PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction. Upper pole: ectopia, ureterocele |
| **Termination (orifice)** | **Ectopic ureter** | Opens at or below the bladder neck. **Females**: bladder neck to vagina, uterus; **continuous dribbling with normal voiding**. **Males**: always above external sphincter (posterior urethra, seminal vesicle, vas); **never incontinent**; epididymitis. Zinner syndrome |
| **Structure** | **Ureterocele** | Cystic dilation of distal submucosal ureter (Chwalle membrane). Intravesical vs ectopic (Glassberg); cecoureterocele (Stephens) |
| | **PUJ obstruction** | Intrinsic aperistaltic segment (infants) or crossing lower-pole vessel (older children) |
| | **Megaureter** (≥7 mm) | Obstructed, refluxing, non-refluxing non-obstructed, refluxing-obstructed; primary obstructive = adynamic distal segment |
| | **Primary VUR** | Short submucosal tunnel, lateral orifice |
| **Position** | **Preureteral (retrocaval) vena cava** | Persistent subcardinal vein traps the right ureter; fishhook (S) shape at L3–L4; type I obstructs, type II does not |
| | Retroiliac ureter; herniation | Rare |

### Weigert–Meyer law
- In complete duplication the **upper-pole ureter opens lower and more medial** (ectopic, obstructs, ureterocele); the **lower-pole ureter opens higher and lateral** (refluxes).
- Reason: the upper-pole bud arises more cephalad on the wolffian duct, is incorporated later and ends more caudally.
- **Also know:** blind-ending ureter, inverted-Y ureter, triplication, congenital stricture, ureteral valves, fibroepithelial polyp.

### Evaluation
- Ultrasound, VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram), **DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid)** scan, MR (magnetic resonance) urography.

### Management principles
- **Ureterocele:** endoscopic incision, upper-pole heminephrectomy or ureteroureterostomy.
- **Ectopic ureter:** heminephroureterectomy if non-functioning; otherwise ureteroureterostomy or reimplantation.
- **PUJ obstruction:** pyeloplasty. **Megaureter:** observe most; taper and reimplant (5:1) if function falls.
- **Retrocaval ureter:** divide, bring anterior to the cava, excise the aperistaltic segment, reanastomose.

**Diagram to draw:** complete duplication showing Weigert–Meyer orifice positions.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Upper pole obstructs (ectopia, ureterocele); lower pole refluxes.
> - Female ectopic ureter: continuous dribbling with normal voiding; male never incontinent.
> - Retrocaval ureter is a venous anomaly; always right-sided.

## Retrocaval ureter
**Asked as:**
- Retrocaval ureter. — *Winter 2017 Paper III Q3d (SAQ)*
- Retrocecal ureter — *Practice Set 1 Paper III Q3c (SAQ)* (also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q3d)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*Assumed meaning: retrocaval (circumcaval) ureter; "retrocecal" is taken as a typo.*

### Definition
- The **right ureter** passes **behind the IVC (inferior vena cava)**, then crosses in front of it from medial to lateral to resume a normal course.
- Better termed (called) "**preureteral vena cava**": the anomaly is venous; the ureter is trapped.

### Embryology
- Paired fetal veins (posterior cardinal, supracardinal, subcardinal) form a venous collar around each ureter and ascending kidney: supracardinal veins dorsally, subcardinal veins ventrally.
- Normally the right **supracardinal** vein, **dorsal** to the ureter, forms the infrarenal IVC, so the ureter lies in front.
- If a vein **ventral** to the ureter persists as the IVC (the lumbar part of the ventral **subcardinal** vein in Campbell; "persistent posterior cardinal vein" in many texts), the ureter is trapped behind it.
- Variant: double right IVC (ureter between limbs).

### Incidence and presentation
- About **1 in 1500** autopsies; **male 3:1**.
- Presents in the **third–fourth decade** with right flank pain, UTI (urinary tract infection), stones or incidental hydronephrosis. Associations: horseshoe kidney, left renal anomalies.

### Classification (Bateson & Atkinson)
| | **Type I (low loop), commoner** | **Type II (high loop)** |
|---|---|---|
| Crossing | Lower, at L3–L4 | Higher, at (near the) renal pelvis level |
| Shape | **"Fishhook" / reverse-J**, S-curve | Smooth horizontal **sickle** curve |
| Obstruction | Moderate–severe, at the iliopsoas edge | Mild or none (pelvis also crosses) |

### Investigations
- **CT urography (computed tomography urography): investigation of choice**, showing ureter and cava together; MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) alternative.
- IVU (intravenous urography): fishhook, ureter lost (not seen) beyond the J hook. Retrograde pyelography: **S-curve** at L3–L4.
- **Diuretic renogram:** obstruction and split function.
- Pitfall: mistaken for **PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction**; pyeloplasty fails.

### Management
- **Non-obstructed (often type II): observe.**
- **Obstructed:** **dismembered ureteroureterostomy** (or ureteropyelostomy): divide the ureter, transpose it anterior to (in front of) the IVC, spatulated tension-free anastomosis over a **double-J stent**. Excise or bypass the **aperistaltic retrocaval segment**; preserve blood supply.
- **Laparoscopic or robotic** repair (transperitoneal or retroperitoneal) now preferred: equal success, faster recovery. Nephrectomy if non-functioning.
- **Also know:** retroiliac ureter (L5–S1, vasal anomalies); mimics: spermatic / gonadal or lumbar vein, psoas (iliopsoas) tendon.

**Diagram to draw:** right ureter hooking behind the IVC with fishhook pelvis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Venous anomaly (persistent subcardinal vein); right side; 1 in 1500; male 3:1.
> - Type I fishhook (obstructs); type II sickle often does not.
> - CT urography; repair by anterior transposition and (with) ureteroureterostomy.

## Maldescended (undescended) testes and treatment algorithm
**Asked as:**
- Treatment algorithm of undescended testis. — *Summer 2018 Paper III Q3a (SAQ)*
- Maldescended testes — *Practice Set 5 Paper III Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(algorithm also in 2018-Summer Paper 3, Q3a; bilateral cases in 2024-Winter Paper 1, Q2)

*Taken as any testis not in a stable scrotal position: undescended, ectopic, retractile and acquired.*

### Definitions
| Type | Description |
|---|---|
| **Undescended (cryptorchid)** | Arrested on the normal path: abdominal, canalicular, prescrotal |
| **Ectopic** | Off the normal path: **superficial inguinal pouch** (commonest), perineal, femoral, prepubic, penile, transverse (crossed) |
| **Retractile** | Scrotal testis pulled up by the cremaster; stays down after traction |
| **Acquired (ascending)** | Previously scrotal; presents at 8–11 years |
| Vanished / agenesis | Lost in utero (torsion) / never formed |

### Epidemiology and aetiology
- 1–4% of term boys, more in preterm; most descend by 6 months. 75–80% palpable; 60–70% unilateral.
- Descent needs **INSL3 (insulin-like peptide 3)** and **androgen** acting on the gubernaculum. Risk factors: prematurity, low birth weight, syndromes (prune belly, cerebral palsy).

### Evaluation
- Warm room, supine and cross-legged; sweep from the anterior superior iliac spine to the scrotum to find the lowest position. Sustained traction fatigues the cremaster and separates retractile from undescended.

### Management principles (AUA (American Urological Association) 2014; EAU (European Association of Urology) similar)
- **Observe for 6 months** (corrected age) for spontaneous descent; refer if still undescended.
- **Orchidopexy after (from) 6 months, preferably by 12 months, and by 18 months at the latest.** Reason: germ-cell and Leydig-cell loss starts in infancy; early surgery improves testicular growth and lowers cancer risk.
- **No ultrasound or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)**: inaccurate (ultrasound sensitivity about 45% for non-palpable testes); never changes the operation; delays surgery.
- **No hormonal therapy** (hCG (human chorionic gonadotrophin) or LHRH (luteinising hormone-releasing hormone)): descent in about 20% only (LHRH works in about 20%); hCG may increase germ-cell apoptosis.
- **Bilateral non-palpable testes** (especially with hypospadias or small phallus; bilateral non-palpable or with hypospadias): urgent **karyotype and hormones** (17-OHP (17-hydroxyprogesterone), AMH (anti-Müllerian hormone)) to exclude **CAH (congenital adrenal hyperplasia)** and other DSD (differences of sex development).

### Algorithm
```
Boy with empty scrotum
  ├─ Bilateral non-palpable ± abnormal phallus → DSD work-up (karyotype, hormones, electrolytes)
  └─ Otherwise → re-examine at 6 months corrected age
        Still undescended → refer → examination under anaesthesia (EUA)
          ├─ Retractile → yearly review (risk of ascent)
          ├─ Palpable (undescended or ectopic) → inguinal or scrotal (Bianchi) orchidopexy
          └─ Non-palpable → diagnostic laparoscopy
                ├─ Testis seen → laparoscopic orchidopexy
                │       short vessels → Fowler–Stephens (1 or 2 stage) / Shehata
                ├─ Vessels + vas enter ring → inguinal exploration (excise nubbin)
                └─ Blind-ending vessels → stop (vanished testis)
```
- **Scrotal nubbin with contralateral hypertrophy** may start with scrotal exploration.

### Key operative points
- **Inguinal orchidopexy:** free the cord, ligate the processus, retroperitoneal mobilisation ± **Prentiss manoeuvre**, subdartos pouch.
- **Fowler–Stephens:** divides testicular vessels; testis survives on vasal collaterals; for testes not within 2–4 cm of the ring. Primary orchidopexy, when possible, is more successful.
- **Postpubertal intra-abdominal testis with normal contralateral testis: orchidectomy** (poor function, cancer risk); offer prosthesis.

### Sequelae and follow-up
- Check position and size at 6 months; teach **self-examination** after puberty.
- **Infertility:** paternity about four-fifths after unilateral, one-third after bilateral.
- **Cancer:** 2–5-fold TGCT (testicular germ cell tumour) risk (cancer risk 2–5 times), higher with late surgery; teach self-examination.
- **Also know:** hernia, torsion, trauma.

**Diagram to draw:** laparoscopic findings at the internal ring (testis, vessels entering ring, blind-ending vessels); line of descent with ectopic sites.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ectopic = off the path; commonest site superficial inguinal pouch.
> - Operate (orchidopexy) from 6 months, preferably by 12 months (6–12 months) and by 18 months at the latest.
> - No imaging, no hormones.
> - Non-palpable: EUA then diagnostic laparoscopy.
> - Bilateral non-palpable: exclude CAH/DSD first (DSD work-up first).

## Autosomal recessive polycystic kidney disease.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 3, Q3c

### Definition and genetics
- **ARPKD (autosomal recessive polycystic kidney disease)**: bilateral, symmetrical renal enlargement from **dilated collecting ducts**, **always with congenital hepatic fibrosis**.
- Gene **PKHD1 on chromosome 6p**; protein **fibrocystin (polyductin)** on the **primary cilium** of tubular and bile-duct cells, so it is a **ciliopathy**.
- Incidence about 1 in 10,000–50,000 live births. Siblings have a 25% risk.
- "Infantile" is a misnomer: a **spectrum**; earlier onset means more severe disease.

### Pathology
- Radially arranged dilated collecting ducts (**microcysts**).
- **Liver:** periportal fibrosis, biliary ectasia; **Caroli syndrome** if intrahepatic ducts dilate.

### Clinical features
| Age | Features |
|---|---|
| **Fetus** | Large echogenic kidneys, **oligohydramnios** → Potter facies, limb deformity, **pulmonary hypoplasia** |
| **Neonate** | Huge, smooth, hard flank masses; respiratory distress; rising creatinine; **30–50% die early** |
| **Child** | Hypertension, CKD (chronic kidney disease), growth failure, anaemia, osteodystrophy |
| **Older child** | **Portal hypertension**: varices, splenomegaly, cholangitis; liver function stays normal |

- No association with renal tumours.

### Investigations
- **Ultrasound:** bilateral very large, **homogeneously hyperechoic** kidneys (brighter than liver) from countless microcyst interfaces; discrete cysts appear with age.
- **Parents' ultrasound** normal (excludes ADPKD); **PKHD1 genetic testing**; liver ultrasound.
- **Differential:** neonatal ADPKD (autosomal dominant PKD), bilateral hydronephrosis, MCDK (multicystic dysplastic kidney), glomerulocystic disease, bilateral tumours, renal vein thrombosis.

### Management (no cure)
- **Neonatal:** ventilation, fluid and electrolyte care, antihypertensives (ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitors).
- **CKD care:** nutrition, growth hormone, anaemia and bone treatment; dialysis and **kidney transplantation**.
- **Nephrectomy** (breathing, feeding, blood pressure, room for peritoneal dialysis): cautiously; **very early bilateral nephrectomy** (first 3 months) had the worst neurological outcomes (ARegPKD registry).
- **Liver:** variceal sclerotherapy, portosystemic shunt; **combined liver–kidney transplant** for severe liver disease.
- **Genetic counselling** and prenatal diagnosis.

### ARPKD versus ADPKD
| | ARPKD | ADPKD |
|---|---|---|
| Gene | PKHD1 (chromosome 6) | PKD1 (16), PKD2 (4) |
| Age | Perinatal | 30–50 years |
| Cysts from | Collecting duct | Whole nephron |
| Ultrasound | Symmetric, homogeneous, echogenic | Large discrete cysts, asymmetric |
| Liver | Fibrosis (always) | Cysts |
| Other | None | Berry aneurysm, mitral prolapse |

**Diagram to draw:** cut kidney with radial dilated collecting ducts.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - PKHD1, chromosome 6, fibrocystin; always congenital hepatic fibrosis.
> - Huge homogeneous echogenic kidneys with oligohydramnios.
> - Neonates die of pulmonary hypoplasia; survivors develop portal hypertension.

## Endoscopic correction of VUR (STING procedure): indications and complications
**Asked as:**
- Endoscopic correction of VUR : indications and complications — *Summer 2019 Paper III Q3e (SAQ)*
- Sting procedure. — *Winter 2024 Paper III Q3f (SAQ)* (also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3c)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*Taken as STING (subureteric Teflon injection), the endoscopic treatment of VUR (vesicoureteral reflux).*

### Introduction / Definition and principle
- Endoscopic injection of a **bulking agent** beneath the ureteric orifice. The mound supports the intramural ureter and restores the **flap-valve**.
- A **bulking agent** injected under the ureteric orifice supports and lengthens the intramural tunnel, restoring the flap-valve in VUR (vesicoureteral reflux).
- First done with Teflon (1981). Popularised by **O'Donnell and Puri, 1986**. **Dx/HA (dextranomer/hyaluronic acid, Deflux)** is now standard.

### Bulking agents
| Agent | Particle / Property | Problem |
|---|---|---|
| Teflon (PTFE, polytetrafluoroethylene paste) | 90% <40 µm (particles <40 μm), permanent | **Migration**; never FDA (US Food and Drug Administration) approved |
| **Dx/HA (dextranomer/hyaluronic acid, Deflux)** | Microspheres **80–250 µm**; gel resorbed | Volume loss about 23%; late recurrence; FDA approved 2001; standard agent |
| Polyacrylate (Vantris) | 300 µm, permanent | Late ureteric obstruction |

### Indications
- Persistent primary VUR (grades II–IV) with **breakthrough febrile UTI** (urinary tract infection) on prophylaxis, **new scars**, poor adherence or parental preference.
- Older girls; residual VUR after reimplantation; duplex or secondary VUR (lower success).
- **Relative contraindications:** grade V, **UVJ (ureterovesical junction) obstruction**, untreated BBD (bladder and bowel dysfunction), large diverticulum.

### Technique
1. Antibiotic at induction; offset-lens cystoscope; 3.7–5 Fr needle; bladder partly filled.
2. **STING:** needle enters **2–3 mm below the orifice at 6 o'clock**, bevel up, advanced **4–5 mm** submucosally.
3. Inject slowly; a mound should appear within **0.1–0.2 mL**, else reposition.
4. Aim for a **"volcano" mound** with a slit-like orifice. Do not cauterise bleeding (implant extrudes).

| Technique / Method | Needle entry | Result |
|---|---|---|
| STING (subureteric Teflon injection) | 2–3 mm below orifice, at 6 o'clock | Baseline |
| **HIT** (hydrodistension implantation technique) | Inside the hydrodistended (distended) orifice | Better than STING, all grades |
| **Double HIT** | Two intraureteric sites | About 90% resolution |

### Results / Success
- Low-grade VUR about 90% after one injection.
- **Elder 2006 meta-analysis** (one injection): grade I–II about 79%, III 72%, IV 63%, V 51%; repeat injections raise success.
- **Swedish Reflux Trial:** 20% recurrence at 2 years after initial success.

### Complications
- **Failure or recurrence** (commonest): repeat injection (1–2), then reimplantation (reimplant).
- **New contralateral VUR** after unilateral injection.
- **Ureteric obstruction:** early or **late-onset**, so long-term ultrasound.
- Febrile UTI despite negative VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram).
- **Calcified mound** (over half) mimics a ureteric stone. Teflon migration.

### Follow-up
- Day case; antibiotics 3 months; ultrasound at 2–6 weeks; ultrasound ± VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) at 3 months.

**Diagram to draw:** STING versus HIT needle positions — needle sites: STING below the orifice, HIT and double HIT inside the tunnel, with volcano mound.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - O'Donnell and Puri 1986; needle 2–3 mm below orifice at 6 o'clock.
> - Dx/HA 80–250 µm; HIT better than STING; double HIT about 90%.
> - Elder 2006: grade IV 63% after one injection.
> - Watch for late obstruction and calcified mound; calcified mound mimics a stone.

## Snodgrass (TIP) repair for hypospadias, including extended indications
**Asked as:**
- Snodgrass repair for Hypospadias. — *Winter 2023 Paper III Q3c (SAQ)*
- Extended indications of Shodgrass repair in hypospadias — *Practice Set 5 Paper III Q3c (SAQ)*
- Snodgrass Repair for distal hypos. — *Practice Set 5 Paper III Q3d (SAQ)* (also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 3, Q3c)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*"Shodgrass" is taken as Snodgrass TIP (tubularised incised plate) repair. Technique: see Practice Set 5 Paper 3, Q3d. "Distal hypos." is taken as distal hypospadias.*

### Introduction
- **TIP (tubularised incised plate) urethroplasty**, described by Snodgrass in 1994 (based on Rich's hinging incision, 1989).
- Designed for distal hypospadias, it now covers (is used for) **over 90% of distal and about 80% of midshaft repairs**.
- Distal hypospadias forms 70–85% of cases.

### Principle
- A **deep midline relaxing incision** of the urethral plate widens a narrow plate, so (so that) it can be tubularised without tension.
- The incised raw area **heals by re-epithelialisation** (the incision re-epithelialises) without a graft, giving a **vertical slit-like meatus**.

### Indications, prerequisites (for any extended use) and assessment
- Distal and midshaft hypospadias, and redo cases with a healthy plate, when curvature is mild (<30° after degloving) and corrected by degloving or (dorsal) plication **without dividing the plate**.
- Supple, well-vascularised plate of adequate width and depth; adequate plate and glans. **Glans width <14 mm** raises complications; give preoperative testosterone (to enlarge it) — i.e. **glans width ≥14 mm** needed.
- **POST (plate objective scoring tool):** a low ratio (AB/BC ratio) of neomeatus length to glans fusion length means a poor plate, which favours (favouring) an inlay graft.
- Timing: 6–12 months of age.
- Not for severe curvature needing plate division; proximal TIP is abandoned.

### Extended indications
| Indication | Rationale | Caveat |
|---|---|---|
| **Midshaft hypospadias** | Same principle | Good results |
| **Proximal hypospadias with mild curvature**, good plate | Single stage; no flaps | More fistula, stricture, recurrent curvature; Campbell-Walsh authors now prefer staging |
| **Redo or failed repairs** with preserved plate | Plate often survives | Worse than primary; inlay graft if scarred |
| **MIP (megameatus intact prepuce)** | Wide, deep plate | GAP (glans approximation procedure) alternative |
| **Narrow or shallow plate**: TIP + **dorsal inlay graft** ("Snodgraft") | Preputial or buccal graft fills the incision, so it cannot contract | Less meatal stenosis |
| **Adults, circumcised** boys | No preputial tissue needed | Success similar |

- **Also know:** in redo cases with poor blood supply a dorsal buccal inlay beats onlay or tube; incise-and-graft is also used in adult urethral stricture (Asopa dorsal inlay) and female urethroplasty.

### Contraindications
- Curvature >30° needing plate division; absent or scarred plate; penoscrotal transposition. Use staged repair (Bracka, STAG [staged tubularised autograft], Byars).

### Operative steps
1. Glans stay suture; tourniquet.
2. U-incision around the plate and meatus; circumscribe and deglove; artificial erection.
3. Separate the glans wings deeply from the plate.
4. **Midline incision of the plate** from (inside) the meatus to the end of the plate, down to the corpora.
5. Tubularise over a **6–8 Fr stent** with subepithelial 6-0/7-0 absorbable sutures, from distal to proximal, stopping 2–3 mm short (the first stitch about 3 mm from the plate end leaves an oval meatus) to leave an oval meatus.
6. **Dartos flap** (dorsal preputial) over the neourethra as a barrier.
7. Two-layer glansplasty; mucosal collar; skin closure (Byars flaps) or preputioplasty.
8. Stent 5–7 days; compressive dressing; oxybutynin for spasm.

### Complications and outcomes
- Pfistermuller meta-analysis (2015, 4,675 boys): complications 10.6%, reoperation 4.5%; **fistula 5.7%**, meatal stenosis 3.6%, stricture 1.3%.
- **Meatal stenosis is commoner than after Mathieu**; glans dehiscence also occurs.
- Distal repair success 83–95%.
- **Proximal and redo cases do (did significantly) worse**; a dartos (barrier) layer improves results.

### Modifications
- **Dorsal inlay graft** (inner preputial skin) for a shallow plate; lateral plate incisions to 12 mm width.
- **Also know:** MAGPI (meatal advancement and glanuloplasty incorporated), Mathieu, Thiersch–Duplay, onlay flap.

**Diagram to draw:** cross-sections showing plate incision, inlay graft, tubularisation and dartos cover.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Snodgrass 1994; incise and tubularise; heals by epithelialisation.
> - Slit-like meatus; dartos barrier reduces fistula.
> - Fistula 5.7%, meatal stenosis 3.6% (Pfistermuller 2015).
> - Extended to midshaft, selected proximal, redo, MIP and adults.
> - Key condition: curvature <30° without plate division.
> - Poor plate → dorsal inlay graft ("Snodgraft").
> - Proximal and redo TIP fail more often.

## Ureterocele
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 2 Paper 3, Q3c
(ectopic ureter also answered in 2018-Summer Paper 3, Q1)

### Definition
- Cystic dilatation of the distal ureter, in the bladder or spanning bladder neck and urethra; **never wholly urethral**, never joined to Wolffian structures.
- Commoner in girls (about 4:1); about 80% drain the **upper pole of a duplex kidney** (Weigert–Meyer rule: upper pole orifice caudal-medial); about 10% bilateral.
- **Cause:** persistent **Chwalla membrane**; upper pole often dysplastic.

### Classification
| System | Basis | Types |
|---|---|---|
| **Glassberg (most useful)** | Position | **Intravesical** vs **ectopic (extravesical)**: part at bladder neck or urethra |
| Stephens | Orifice morphology | Stenotic, sphincteric, sphincterostenotic, **cecoureterocele** (cavity extends under urethra), blind, non-obstructed |
| Churchill | Function | Upper pole only / whole kidney / contralateral at risk |

### Clinical features
- Antenatal hydronephrosis (most); febrile UTI (urinary tract infection) or urosepsis; adult single-system type with stone.
- **Prolapse:** smooth interlabial mass in girls; BOO (bladder outlet obstruction).
- **No incontinence when untreated** (unlike female ectopic ureter).

### Evaluation
- **Ultrasound:** thin-walled cyst in bladder, dilated upper pole and ureter.
- **VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram):** lower pole reflux ("drooping lily"), eversion (weak floor), prolapse.
- **DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid):** upper pole function; cystoscopy before incision.

### Management
- **Goals:** preserve function; remove infection, obstruction, reflux; keep continence.
- **CAP (continuous antibiotic prophylaxis)** from birth if hydronephrotic.
- **Observation:** small, non-obstructing, low-grade reflux, non-functioning upper pole.

| Option | Indication / result |
|---|---|
| **TUI (transurethral incision)** | Sepsis, neonates, intravesical type. Low transverse full-thickness incision; relieves obstruction in 78–97%; new reflux 0–50%. Intravesical without reflux: 70–80% cured; ectopic: secondary surgery up to 70% |
| Upper pole heminephrectomy | Non-functioning upper pole, no reflux; secondary surgery 40–50% |
| Ureteroureterostomy / pyeloureterostomy | Functioning upper pole |
| Excision + common sheath reimplant ± bladder neck repair | Persistent reflux, ectopic or cecoureterocele; persisting reflux 5–10% |
| Total reconstruction | Older child, non-functioning upper pole, lower pole reflux |

- **Also know:** "watering-can" laser punctures (less reflux); pseudoureterocele; ureterocele disproportion.

**Diagram to draw:** duplex kidney with upper pole ureterocele and lower pole reflux.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Upper pole of duplex; never wholly urethral; Chwalla membrane.
> - Intravesical vs ectopic predicts TUI success.
> - TUI relieves obstruction in 78–97%; new reflux 0–50%.

## Primary obst. megaureter.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 2 Paper 3, Q3f

*Assumed meaning: primary obstructive megaureter (POM).*

### Definition
- **Megaureter:** ureter diameter **≥7 mm** (normal up to 16 years is 5.0–6.5 mm). It is a descriptive term.
- **Classification:** primary (intrinsic) or secondary (neurogenic bladder, outlet obstruction); and **obstructed, refluxing, non-refluxing non-obstructed, or refluxing and obstructed**.
- **POM:** an **adynamic juxtavesical segment** at the UVJ (ureterovesical junction) blocks peristalsis and causes functional obstruction above.

### Pathogenesis
- Ureteric muscle matures cranially to caudally; the juxtavesical segment matures last, even after birth. This explains spontaneous resolution.
- Excess collagen, thick muscle sleeve, abnormal interstitial cells of Cajal.

### Clinical features
- 10–23% of antenatal upper-tract dilatation. Boys, **left side**; **bilateral 25%**; contralateral dysplasia or obstruction 10–15%.
- Mostly asymptomatic; or UTI (urinary tract infection), pain, haematuria, stones.

### Evaluation
- **Ultrasound:** hydroureteronephrosis.
- **VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram):** excludes reflux and PUV (posterior urethral valves).
- **MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) renogram:** split function; washout T½ unreliable (false positive up to 44%).
- MRU (magnetic resonance urography) for anatomy.

### Management
**Conservative (most)**
- CAP (continuous antibiotic prophylaxis) in infants; serial ultrasound and renograms; most resolve.
- Diameter <10 mm: virtually never needs surgery; ≥10 mm: a substantial minority do.
- Follow to early adulthood.

**Indications for surgery**
- Recurrent febrile UTI; progressive dilatation; **DRF (differential renal function) <40%**; **fall in DRF ≥5%**.

**Infant (small bladder, huge ureter): temporise**
- Cutaneous ureterostomy; refluxing reimplant; nephrostomy; **endoscopic balloon dilatation with double-J** for 2–6 months (70–80% success; complications up to a third).

**Definitive repair (child)**
- Excise stenotic segment → straighten → **taper** → **non-refluxing reimplant at 5:1** (Cohen or Politano–Leadbetter; extravesical for huge ureters). Stent afterwards.

| Tailoring | Method | Use |
|---|---|---|
| Starr plication | Imbricate lateral wall | Moderate (<1.75 cm) |
| Kalicinski folding | Exclude and fold lumen | Moderate |
| **Hendren excision** | Excise lateral wall | Massive; risk of ischaemia |

- Success over 90%.
- **Complications:** obstruction (stent first), VUR (vesicoureteral reflux; observe or inject), persistent dilatation.

**Diagram to draw:** dilated ureter above narrow adynamic segment; tapered reimplant.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Megaureter ≥7 mm; POM = adynamic juxtavesical segment.
> - Surgery: UTIs, progression, DRF <40% or fall ≥5%.
> - Excise, taper, reimplant 5:1; success >90%.

## Paquin and Gil-Vernet techniques of ureteric reimplantation
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 8 Paper 3, Q3f

### Principles of antireflux surgery
- Normal flap valve: intramural ureter compressed against detrusor during filling.
- **Paquin's rule:** tunnel length to ureteric diameter about **5:1** in non-refluxing junctions versus 1.4:1 in refluxing ones.
- Exclude secondary VUR (vesicoureteral reflux); tension-free, straight course.

### Indications for surgery
- Breakthrough febrile UTI (urinary tract infection) on prophylaxis, new scars, persistent high-grade VUR.

### 1. Paquin technique
- **Combined extravesical–intravesical** reimplant described by Paquin (1959), with the 5:1 rule.
- **Steps:**
  1. Ureter mobilised **extravesically** and divided at the bladder.
  2. Bladder opened; a **new hiatus** created higher on the posterior wall (**suprahiatal**).
  3. Long **submucosal tunnel** from the new hiatus down to the original orifice site, length about 5 × diameter.
  4. Ureter drawn through, spatulated, sutured to mucosa and anchored to detrusor.
  5. Old hiatus closed.
- **Advantages:** long tunnel under direct vision; good for **dilated ureters and megaureter** (can combine with tapering).
- **Disadvantages:** kinking or obstruction at the new hiatus.

### 2. Gil-Vernet trigonoplasty
- **Intravesical, no tunnelling and no ureteric mobilisation.**
- **Steps:**
  1. Bladder opened.
  2. **Transverse mucosal incision** across the trigone between the two orifices.
  3. Intramural ureters with their muscle sheath **advanced medially** by one non-absorbable or absorbable stitch across the midline.
  4. Mucosa closed.
- **Effect:** lengthens and medialises the intravesical ureters; corrects both sides at once.
- **Indications:** low to moderate grade (I–III), usually bilateral VUR, with non-dilated ureters.
- **Advantages:** quick, simple, low obstruction risk; easy later retrograde access.
- **Disadvantages:** unsuitable for high grade, dilated ureters, paraureteral diverticulum.

### Comparison
| Feature | Paquin | Gil-Vernet |
|---|---|---|
| Approach | Extra + intravesical | Intravesical |
| Ureter divided | Yes | No |
| Tunnel | New long submucosal | None (advancement) |
| Best for | High grade, dilated | Low grade, bilateral |

- **Also know:** Politano–Leadbetter, Cohen (commonest, >98% success), Glenn–Anderson, Lich–Gregoir. Open success benchmark 95.9% (AUA [American Urological Association] meta-analysis).

**Diagram to draw:** trigone showing Gil-Vernet transverse incision and medial advancement stitch.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Paquin: tunnel 5:1; combined, suprahiatal reimplant.
> - Gil-Vernet: trigonoplasty, medial advancement, no tunnel.
> - Gil-Vernet for low-grade bilateral VUR.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 4 · Paediatric Urology 1

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Management of bilateral VUR: medical treatment, surgical techniques, selection and long-term outcome (LAQ)

- Grade V loses papillary impressions; bilateral grade IV rarely resolves (persists most); bilateral VUR is high risk.
- RIVUR: CAP halves recurrent UTI but not scarring.
- Treat BBD (bladder and bowel dysfunction) and give CAP (continuous antibiotic prophylaxis) first; correct for breakthrough febrile UTI (urinary tract infection), new scars or persistent grade IV–V.
- Endoscopic Dx/HA (dextranomer/hyaluronic acid) by HIT (hydrodistension implantation): least invasive; lower success at higher grades.
- Open reimplantation 95.9%; Cohen cross-trigonal reimplant (over 98%) suits bilateral VUR (vesicoureteral reflux) and does both sides through one cystotomy.
- Bilateral extravesical (Lich–Gregoir) repair risks retention up to 10%.
- 34–38% of scarred children become hypertensive; correction does not lower BP.
- Scars bring 8× pre-eclampsia risk: the scar, not the reflux, matters.

#### Embryology of the penis and management of proximal hypospadias (including Koyanagi repair) (LAQ)

- DHT at 8–12 weeks; opening then closing zipper; 12 o'clock nerve-free zone.
- Hypospadias plus undescended testis → DSD work-up.
- <30° plication; >30° divide plate ± ventral graft and stage.
- STAG/Bracka preferred; fistula commonest complication.
- Koyanagi: one stage; meatus-based parameatal foreskin flap.
- Koyanagi: plate divided; no proximal circular anastomosis.
- Koyanagi: fistula commonest; diverticulum, meatal stenosis.

#### Embryology and management of ectopic ureter. (LAQ)

- Ectopia = orifice at or below the bladder neck; bud too cranial on the wolffian duct.
- Girls: continuous dribbling with normal voiding; boys: never incontinent, epididymitis.
- DMSA decides keep (UU/reimplant) versus remove (heminephroureterectomy).
- Protect the lower-pole blood supply; stent the recipient ureter for UU.

#### Development and clinical anatomy of Vesico-Ureteric junction and management of grade 3 VUR in adult? (LAQ)

- Bud too low → lateral orifice → short tunnel → VUR.
- Adult intramural ureter 1.2–2.5 cm; Paquin 5:1.
- In adults the scar, not the reflux, carries the risk; exclude secondary causes first.
- Correct for recurrent pyelonephritis, new scars or pregnancy plans.

#### Bilateral Wilms tumour: treatment strategies (SAQ)

- Stage V, 5–7%; no upfront nephrectomy or biopsy.
- COG: three drugs for 6 weeks (reassess at 6 weeks), surgery by week 12; SIOP: two drugs.
- AREN0534: 4-year OS about 95%.
- Bilateral NSS to avoid ESRD.

#### Congenital ureteral anomalies (SAQ)

- Upper pole obstructs (ectopia, ureterocele); lower pole refluxes.
- Female ectopic ureter: continuous dribbling with normal voiding; male never incontinent.
- Retrocaval ureter is a venous anomaly; always right-sided.

#### Retrocaval ureter (SAQ)

- Venous anomaly (persistent subcardinal vein); right side; 1 in 1500; male 3:1.
- Type I fishhook (obstructs); type II sickle often does not.
- CT urography; repair by anterior transposition and (with) ureteroureterostomy.

#### Maldescended (undescended) testes and treatment algorithm (SAQ)

- Ectopic = off the path; commonest site superficial inguinal pouch.
- Operate (orchidopexy) from 6 months, preferably by 12 months (6–12 months) and by 18 months at the latest.
- No imaging, no hormones.
- Non-palpable: EUA then diagnostic laparoscopy.
- Bilateral non-palpable: exclude CAH/DSD first (DSD work-up first).

#### Autosomal recessive polycystic kidney disease. (SAQ)

- PKHD1, chromosome 6, fibrocystin; always congenital hepatic fibrosis.
- Huge homogeneous echogenic kidneys with oligohydramnios.
- Neonates die of pulmonary hypoplasia; survivors develop portal hypertension.

#### Endoscopic correction of VUR (STING procedure): indications and complications (SAQ)

- O'Donnell and Puri 1986; needle 2–3 mm below orifice at 6 o'clock.
- Dx/HA 80–250 µm; HIT better than STING; double HIT about 90%.
- Elder 2006: grade IV 63% after one injection.
- Watch for late obstruction and calcified mound; calcified mound mimics a stone.

#### Snodgrass (TIP) repair for hypospadias, including extended indications (SAQ)

- Snodgrass 1994; incise and tubularise; heals by epithelialisation.
- Slit-like meatus; dartos barrier reduces fistula.
- Fistula 5.7%, meatal stenosis 3.6% (Pfistermuller 2015).
- Extended to midshaft, selected proximal, redo, MIP and adults.
- Key condition: curvature <30° without plate division.
- Poor plate → dorsal inlay graft ("Snodgraft").
- Proximal and redo TIP fail more often.

#### Ureterocele (SAQ)

- Upper pole of duplex; never wholly urethral; Chwalla membrane.
- Intravesical vs ectopic predicts TUI success.
- TUI relieves obstruction in 78–97%; new reflux 0–50%.

#### Primary obst. megaureter. (SAQ)

- Megaureter ≥7 mm; POM = adynamic juxtavesical segment.
- Surgery: UTIs, progression, DRF <40% or fall ≥5%.
- Excise, taper, reimplant 5:1; success >90%.

#### Paquin and Gil-Vernet techniques of ureteric reimplantation (SAQ)

- Paquin: tunnel 5:1; combined, suprahiatal reimplant.
- Gil-Vernet: trigonoplasty, medial advancement, no tunnel.
- Gil-Vernet for low-grade bilateral VUR.

# Section 5 · Paediatric Urology 2 — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Embryological development of urinary bladder and management of Hutch Diverticulum?
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2021-Summer Paper 3, Q1
(bladder diverticulum also answered in 2016-Summer Paper 2, Q3c)

### A. Embryology of the bladder
**1. Division of the cloaca (weeks 4–6)**
- The **urorectal septum** (Tourneux and Rathke folds) divides the endodermal cloaca into a dorsal **anorectal canal** and a ventral **UGS (urogenital sinus)**. The cloacal membrane perforates to form the urogenital and anal openings.
- The **Wolffian (mesonephric) duct entry** is the landmark:
  - **Cranial vesicourethral canal** (continuous with the allantois) → bladder and pelvic urethra.
  - **Caudal (definitive) UGS** → phallic urethra in males; distal vagina and vestibule in females. The whole female urethra comes from the pelvic part.

**2. Wall layers**
- **Urothelium:** endoderm of the UGS.
- **Lamina propria, detrusor and adventitia:** surrounding splanchnic mesenchyme. Epithelium and mesenchyme induce each other (epithelial–mesenchymal signalling).

**3. Trigone (weeks 5–6)**
- The **common excretory ducts** (mesonephric duct distal to the ureteric bud) dilate and are absorbed into the UGS. **Apoptosis** separates the ureters from the mesonephric ducts.
- Ureteric orifices migrate **cranially and laterally**; the Wolffian ducts end caudally as ejaculatory ducts at the verumontanum.
- Classic view: trigone muscle is ureteric (Wolffian) tissue. Current view (mouse lineage tracing): **mostly bladder smooth muscle**, minor ureteric contribution.
- **Bud position (Weigert–Meyer):** bud too low → lateral orifice, short tunnel → VUR (vesicoureteral reflux); too high → caudal, medial, ectopic orifice.

**4. Allantois and urachus**
- **Months 4–5:** the bladder descends into the pelvis. The allantoic duct elongates and obliterates into the **urachus** (median umbilical ligament).

**5. Function**
- Urine production starts at **week 10**. Fetal bladder is seen from 10 weeks, almost always by 13 weeks, and empties every 15–20 minutes. After **16 weeks** amniotic fluid is mainly fetal urine.

### B. Anomalies explained by embryology
| Anomaly | Embryological basis |
|---|---|
| Agenesis | Cranial UGS atrophy, or trigone not formed; survival only with ectopic ureters |
| Exstrophy | Cloacal membrane not reinforced by mesoderm |
| Duplication | Complete or incomplete; sagittal or coronal |
| Urachal (patent urachus, sinus, cyst, vesicourachal diverticulum) | Urachus not obliterated at different levels |
| Congenital diverticulum | Focal detrusor deficiency, often at the ureteric hiatus |

### C. Hutch diverticulum: definition
- **Paraureteral diverticulum**: mucosa herniates through the **ureteric hiatus**, between the intravesical ureter and the roof of the hiatus, superolateral to the orifice, sparing the trigone. **Hutch** divided them into primary and secondary.

| | Primary (congenital) | Secondary (acquired) |
|---|---|---|
| Cause | Congenitally deficient detrusor at the hiatus | High pressure: PUV (posterior urethral valves), BOO (bladder outlet obstruction), neurogenic bladder (described in paraplegics), dysfunctional voiding, prior surgery |
| Bladder | Smooth; single, often intermittent | Trabeculated; one of many |

### D. Pathophysiology
- No muscle backing → **VUR**; no muscle in the sac → cannot empty → stasis, UTI (urinary tract infection), stones; tumour in adults.
- An enlarging sac damages **Waldeyer's sheath** and pulls the ureter out → UVJ (ureterovesical junction) dysfunction, reflux, later **obstruction** and renal damage.
- Large sacs compress the bladder neck → retention (vicious circle).

### E. Evaluation
- **US (ultrasound):** sac, residual urine, hydronephrosis.
- **VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram), gold standard:** oblique and post-void films show size, VUR and whether the ureter enters the sac. Repeat if suspicion persists (sac is dynamic).
- **DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid):** scars; **MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) renogram** if obstruction.
- **Urodynamics** if secondary; cystoscopy (orifice, neck); in adults enter the sac, cytology.

### F. Management
**1. Treat the cause first (secondary):** valve ablation, BOO surgery, CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) and antimuscarinics. Remodelling may make excision unnecessary.

**2. Primary diverticulum:**
| Situation | Management |
|---|---|
| Small, asymptomatic, no VUR | Observe |
| VUR, ureter beside the sac | As primary VUR (resolves like VUR of the same grade); many excise if it persists. Dx/HA (dextranomer/hyaluronic acid) injection possible |
| Ureter enters the sac | VUR will not resolve → surgery |
| Breakthrough febrile UTI, new scars, large sac with residual, stones, obstruction, retention | **Diverticulectomy + ipsilateral reimplantation** |

**3. Operative steps (open, laparoscopic or robotic):**
```
Cystoscopy ± ureteric catheter → cystotomy → evert sac or dissect it
from its pseudocapsule (0.5 cm mucosal cuff round the neck)
→ close detrusor defect in two layers (firm backing)
→ reimplant ureter: Cohen cross-trigonal or Politano–Leadbetter
  (tunnel 5:1) → catheter ± suprapubic tube, drain
```
- Extravesical or combined approaches suit selected cases. In adults, endoscopic outlet surgery is done first.

### G. Complications and follow-up
- Ureteric injury, leak, persistent VUR, obstruction, recurrence (syndromic patients).
- US at 6–12 weeks; VCUG for febrile UTI; urodynamics if voiding is abnormal.

**Also know:** posterolateral multiple diverticula (Ehlers–Danlos, Williams, Menkes); hypoplasia; megacystis.

**Diagram to draw:** cloacal division by the urorectal septum; Hutch sac beside the ureteric orifice.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Urorectal septum weeks 4–6; vesicourethral canal → bladder; urachus at months 4–5.
> - Hutch: herniation through the ureteric hiatus → VUR.
> - Ureter entering the sac: VUR will not resolve.
> - Secondary: treat the outlet first; else diverticulectomy + reimplantation.

## Antenatal hydronephrosis (UTD) and fetal uropathy (LUTO)
**Asked as:**
- Define algorithm for evaluation of antenatally diagnosed unilateral HN in male fetus. What are the current rate of prenatal fetal anomalies — *Practice Set 1 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)*
- Antenatal management of Fetal Uropathy. — *Summer 2021 Paper III Q3b (SAQ)*
- ANH — *Practice Set 1 Paper III Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

(antenatal LUTO management also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 3, Q3b)
**See Practice Set 1 Paper 3, Q2 (full answer).**

*Assumed meaning: HN = hydronephrosis, now called UTD (urinary tract dilation). ANH (antenatal hydronephrosis) = UTD.*

### Introduction and rates of prenatal anomalies
- Major congenital anomalies occur in about **2–3% of births**. Urinary tract anomalies are among the commonest detected prenatally (commonly quoted as 20–30% of all prenatal anomalies).
- **UTD is the second commonest prenatal anomaly after cardiac defects, in 1–3% of pregnancies.**
- Mostly **UTD** on the 20-week scan, usually mild and transient. Most is mild: mild 57–88%, moderate 10–30%, severe 1.5–13% of UTD. The serious group is **LUTO (lower urinary tract obstruction)**: PUV (posterior urethral valves), urethral atresia, PBS (prune-belly syndrome).
- Screening US (ultrasound) at 18–20 weeks detects dilation well (sensitivity about 90%), but predicts diagnosis poorly. Fetal urine production begins at about 14 weeks; amniotic fluid is mainly fetal urine after 16 weeks (**after 16 weeks amniotic fluid is fetal urine**); oligohydramnios causes **pulmonary hypoplasia**.

### Causes (SFU [Society for Fetal Urology] consensus)
| Cause | Incidence |
|---|---|
| Transient/physiological dilation | 50–70% |
| UPJ (ureteropelvic junction) obstruction pattern | 10–30% |
| VUR (vesicoureteral reflux) | 10–40% |
| UVJ (ureterovesical junction) obstruction/megaureter | 5–15% |
| MCDK (multicystic dysplastic kidney) | 2–5% |
| Duplex system, ureterocele, ectopic ureter | 5–7% |
| LUTO (lower urinary tract obstruction: PUV [posterior urethral valves], prune-belly) | 0.1–0.5% |
- Chance of postnatal pathology rises with grade: about 12% (mild) to 88% (severe). UPJ obstruction and PUV track grade; **VUR does not** (VUR is not predicted by grade).

### Grading systems
**APD (anteroposterior renal pelvic diameter):** transverse image at the hilum. APD normal <4 mm before 28 weeks, <7 mm after, <10 mm postnatally.
| Grade | Second trimester | Third trimester |
|---|---|---|
| Mild | 4 to <7 mm | 4 to <9 mm |
| Moderate | 7–10 mm | 9–15 mm |
| Severe | >10 mm | >15 mm |

**SFU (postnatal only):** 1 pelvis barely split; 2 pelvis and major calyces; 3 minor calyces uniformly dilated; 4 plus parenchymal thinning.

**UTD consensus 2014 (risk stratification):** six parameters: APD, central/peripheral calyces, parenchymal thickness, parenchymal appearance, ureter, bladder (plus amniotic fluid prenatally).
| Prenatal parameter | A1 low risk | A2–3 increased risk (any one) |
|---|---|---|
| APD 16–27 weeks | 4 to <7 mm | ≥7 mm |
| APD ≥28 weeks | 7 to <10 mm | ≥10 mm |
| Calyces | Central calyces only (central or none) | Peripheral |
| Others: parenchyma, ureter, bladder | Normal (all normal) | Abnormal |
| Amniotic fluid | Normal | Unexplained oligohydramnios |
- **A1:** repeat US after 32 weeks (one scan). **A2–3:** US every 4 weeks to delivery. Both: postnatal US after 48 hours.

### Prenatal algorithm (unilateral UTD, male fetus)
```
Anomaly scan: unilateral UTD
 → Detailed scan: contralateral kidney, ureter, bladder wall and emptying,
   posterior urethra (keyhole), amniotic fluid, other anomalies
 ├─ Bilateral / thick bladder / keyhole / oligohydramnios ─► LUTO pathway
 │    (fetal medicine; karyotype; Ruano staging; possible shunt)
 ├─ A1 ─► repeat US after 32 weeks
 └─ A2–3 ─► US every 4 weeks to delivery
 → Normal delivery at local centre; no fetal intervention for unilateral UTD
 → Postnatal US after 48 hours (earlier if solitary kidney or suspected LUTO)
```
- Repeat scans because the lesion may progress and a second anomaly is more likely. **Fetal MRI** only for complex anatomy.
- Counsel the parents: most cases resolve; prognosis with a normal opposite kidney is excellent.

### Antenatal management of fetal uropathy: suspected LUTO
**Evaluation**
- Male fetus, thick-walled "keyhole" bladder, bilateral hydroureteronephrosis, oligohydramnios.
- Anomaly scan, **fetal echocardiogram, karyotype** (one-fifth of megacystis is aneuploid); fetal MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) if unclear.
- **Serial fetal urine** (3 samples, 48–72 hours apart). Favourable: Na <100 mEq/L, Cl <90 mEq/L, osmolality <200 mOsm/L, β2-microglobulin <6 mg/L; not validated.
- **Ruano staging:** I mild, normal fluid → observe. **II severe, function preserved** (oligohydramnios, favourable urine) → intervene. III cysts, dysplasia, unfavourable urine → for lungs only.

**Options**
| Option | Notes |
|---|---|
| Observation, serial scans | Most cases |
| **VAS (vesicoamniotic shunt)** | Dislodgement, blockage, preterm labour |
| **Fetal cystoscopy + laser valve ablation** | Diagnoses and treats; fistula up to 10% |
| Serial amnioinfusion | Lungs in anhydramnios; under trial |
| Early delivery / termination | Mature lungs, falling fluid / poor prognosis |
- **PLUTO (2013):** shunt vs conservative (31 fetuses): better perinatal survival, **no renal benefit**.

**Counselling and delivery**
- Multidisciplinary team: fetal medicine, paediatric urology, nephrology, neonatology.
- Intervention improves survival, not renal function; complications **over 40%**.
- Deliver where the bladder can be decompressed at once; prophylaxis, then VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram).

### Postnatal algorithm
- **First US after 48 hours**, because neonatal dehydration under-reads dilation. Postnatal normal: APD <10 mm, no calyceal or ureteral dilation.

| | P1 (SFU 1–2) | P2 (SFU 3) | P3 (SFU 4) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Findings | APD 10–15 mm, central calyces | APD ≥15 mm, peripheral calyces or dilated ureter | Abnormal parenchyma or bladder |
| Repeat US | 3–6 months | Individualised | 1 month |
| Antibiotic prophylaxis | No | Discretion; **yes if ureter >7 mm** | Yes |
| VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) | No | Discretion | Yes |
| MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) renogram | No | Discretion | If persistent |

- **Male-specific points:** VCUG confirms a normal posterior urethra. **Circumcision** protects (NNT [number needed to treat] 10). UTI (urinary tract infection) risk: female sex, uncircumcised boys, high grade, dilated ureter.
- Prophylaxis: amoxicillin or a first-generation cephalosporin from birth; nitrofurantoin and trimethoprim only after 8 weeks.
- MAG3: DRF (differential renal function) <40%, T½ >20 minutes, or delayed retention suggest obstruction. MRU (magnetic resonance urography) for anatomy; DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid) for MCDK or duplex function.
- **Surgery indications (UPJ):** rising APD, DRF <40% with poor drainage, falling DRF, symptoms (UTI, stone, pain). Megaureter surgery: DRF <40%, febrile UTI; defer reimplant beyond 1 year.
- Outcome: SFU 1 resolves in nearly all by 2.5 years; SFU 2 about 95% by 4 years.

### Bilateral dilation and PUV: how they differ
- Bilateral hydroureteronephrosis, thick bladder, keyhole sign, oligohydramnios = LUTO until proved otherwise.
- At birth: 5 Fr feeding tube, prophylaxis, creatinine, early VCUG; prenatal intervention improves survival but not renal function (PLUTO).

**Diagram to draw:** UTD A1/A2–3 prenatal and P1–P3 postnatal flowchart; keyhole bladder with bilateral hydroureteronephrosis and shunt.

> **Key points to remember (including SAQ angle):**
> - UTD: 1–3% of pregnancies; second commonest anomaly after cardiac.
> - ANH (antenatal hydronephrosis) = UTD; APD normal <4 mm before 28 weeks, <7 mm after, <10 mm postnatally.
> - Transient dilation 50–70% (commonest cause); UPJ obstruction commonest surgical cause; VUR is not predicted by grade.
> - First postnatal US after 48 hours; A1: one scan after 32 weeks; A2–3 scan every 4 weeks.
> - Risk-based care: P1 no prophylaxis or VCUG; prophylaxis if ureter >7 mm or P3; P3 prophylaxis, VCUG, MAG3.
> - Favourable urine: Na <100, Cl <90, osmolality <200, β2-microglobulin <6.
> - PLUTO: survival up, renal function unchanged.

## Development of the urethra, congenital urethral anomalies and management of epispadias (including isolated penopubic epispadias)
**Asked as:**
- Discuss development of urethra. Classify congenital anomalies and discuss management of epispadias — *Practice Set 6 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)*
- Isolated penopubic Epispadias. — *Summer 2022 Paper III Q3f (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(isolated penopubic epispadias also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q3f)

### Development of the urethra
**1. Division of the cloaca (weeks 5–6)**
- The urorectal septum divides the cloaca into the dorsal anorectal canal and the ventral **UGS (urogenital sinus)**.
- Wolffian duct entry is the landmark. Above it, the **vesicourethral canal** forms the bladder and pelvic urethra. Below it, the **definitive UGS** forms the phallic urethra in males, and the urethra and vestibule in females.
- UGS also forms the prostate (buds at 10 weeks) and Cowper and Littre glands; the **utricle** is Müllerian.
- The epithelium of the **entire male urethra is endodermal** (UGS origin).

**2. Penile urethra: the "double zipper" (androgen-dependent)**
- Indifferent genital tubercle until 8–9 weeks. Testosterone (Leydig cells, 8 weeks) is converted to DHT (dihydrotestosterone) by **type 2 5α-reductase**.
- The **urethral plate** (endoderm) forms in the tubercle.
- **Opening zipper:** the plate canalises proximal to distal into a urethral groove bounded by the urethral folds.
- **Closing zipper:** the folds fuse in the ventral midline, forming the tubular urethra and median raphe.
- **Glanular urethra:** formed by direct canalisation of the plate inside the glans (current endodermal view; Glenister's ectodermal ingrowth theory is outdated).
- **Timetable:** coronal sulcus 11–12 weeks; fusion complete 16 weeks; urethra complete 18 weeks; fetal curvature gone 20 weeks.
- The prepuce forms with the glanular urethra, so hypospadias has a hooded foreskin.
- **Female:** the vestibular plate canalises but the folds do not fuse, giving an open vestibule and labia minora.

### Classification of congenital urethral anomalies

| Mechanism | Anomaly |
|---|---|
| Failed ventral fold fusion | **Hypospadias** (1 in 250 males; commonest at shaft–glans junction); congenital urethral fistula |
| Dorsal defect | **Epispadias** (exstrophy–epispadias complex) |
| Obstruction | **PUV (posterior urethral valves)**, Young type 1 (95%) and type 3; **anterior urethral valves** with diverticulum; urethral atresia or hypoplasia; meatal stenosis |
| Duplication | **Urethral duplication**: sagittal; ventral channel is functional; Effmann type I blind, II complete, III with caudal duplication |
| Dilatation | **Megalourethra**: scaphoid (spongiosum deficient) or fusiform (all erectile tissue deficient, more severe); prune-belly association |
| Cystic or remnant | Cowper's syringocele; enlarged utricle (proximal hypospadias); parameatal cyst |

### Epispadias (including isolated penopubic epispadias): definition, embryology, classification and clinical features
- Part of the **exstrophy–epispadias complex**: a **dorsal urethral defect** (defect of the dorsal urethral wall): the roof is replaced by a broad (dorsal) mucosal strip (urethral plate). Cause (embryology): absent mesodermal reinforcement of the cloacal membrane; the genital tubercles fuse **caudal** to it, giving a dorsal urethral defect.
- **Male (1 in 117,000; incidence of isolated male epispadias about 1 in 117,000), classification by meatus:** glanular, penile, **penopubic** (subsymphyseal) (about 70% of male cases are complete (penopubic), with incontinence). **Female (1 in 484,000), Davis:** vesicular (patulous), subsymphyseal, retrosymphyseal (incontinent).
- **Features (clinical):** penopubic meatus; outlet may admit a finger → **total incontinence**. **Short, broad, upturned penis with dorsal chordee**, like exstrophy; pubic diastasis (less than exstrophy); small bladder from no outlet resistance; **VUR (vesicoureteral/vesicoureteric reflux) 30–40% (male), 30–75% (female)**. Female (Davis retrosymphyseal type): bifid clitoris, depressed mons, incontinence.

### Evaluation
- (Renal) ultrasound; VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram); cystoscopy and capacity under anaesthesia.
- **Key decision:** epispadias or exstrophy pathway. Treat as exstrophy (osteotomy and closure) if pubic diastasis is over 3 cm, the intrasymphyseal bar is poor, sphincter is absent or capacity is tiny (tiny bladder).

### Management of epispadias
- **Aims:** continence, safe upper tracts, a straight functional penis of good length.
- **Preoperative testosterone** 2 mg/kg intramuscularly (intramuscular), 5 and 2 weeks before urethroplasty.

**Stage 1: penile reconstruction and urethroplasty (6–12 months)**
- Release chordee: divide suspensory ligaments and scar; free corpora from the inferior pubic rami.
- **Modified Cantwell–Ransley:** outline an 18-mm plate to the tip; reverse MAGPI (meatal advancement and glanuloplasty) brings the meatus to the tip; free the plate from the corpora (distal 1 cm left on the glans); tubularise over 8 Fr; transpose it (the tubularised plate is moved) **ventral to the corpora**, which are rotated medially ± cavernocavernostomy; close glans wings; Z-plasty at the base; stent 10–12 days.
- **Mitchell–Bägli complete penile disassembly:** reliable chordee correction, but often leaves hypospadias.
- Outlet resistance rises, so capacity grows.

**Stage 2: bladder neck reconstruction (4–5 years, capacity about 100 mL)**
