- **YDL (Young–Dees–Leadbetter)** bladder neck repair + Marshall–Marchetti–Krantz suspension + **ureteric reimplantation**. Osteotomy usually unnecessary.
- **Good capacity:** single-stage repair possible.

**Female epispadias:** at 6–12 months excise the urethral roof, close over 10 Fr, unite clitoral halves and labia minora, monsplasty; YDL at 4–5 years.

### Outcomes and complications
- Continence after bladder neck reconstruction: **males 47–82%, females 70–87%**; capacity is the main predictor.
- Early fistula up to (about) one-fifth (at the penile base); long-term fistula and stricture about 5% (stricture rare); erections and paternity (usually) preserved.
- Better than exstrophy (bladder never exposed or scarred). Failures (failure): bulking injection (injectables), augmentation, continent stoma.

**Diagram to draw:** double zipper (canalisation then fusion); penopubic epispadias (dorsal penopubic meatus and plate).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Whole male urethra is endodermal UGS; fold fusion complete at 16, urethra at 18 weeks.
> - About 70% of male epispadias are penopubic and incontinent; VUR 30–40%.
> - Diastasis over 3 cm (>3 cm) → exstrophy pathway.
> - Cantwell–Ransley at 6–12 months; YDL (Young–Dees–Leadbetter) at about 100 mL capacity.
> - Continence after YDL: males 47–82%, females 70–87%.

## Bladder and ureter development, exstrophy–epispadias complex and its management
**Asked as:**
- Discuss the developmental basis of extrophy epispadias complex. Briefly outline management — *Practice Set 7 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)* (theories also answered in Practice Set 6 Paper 1, Q2; single-stage repair in Practice Set 3 Paper 3, Q3e)
- Describe normal development of bladder and ureter and outline management of a child born with ectopia vesicae. — *Practice Set 8 Paper III Q1 (LAQ)* (bladder embryology and exstrophy theories also answered in Practice Set 6 Paper 1, Q2)
- Single stage repair of Extrophy of UB. — *Practice Set 3 Paper III Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

*Assumed meaning: ectopia vesicae = classic bladder exstrophy (CBE). Single stage repair of Extrophy of UB = single-stage (complete primary) repair of exstrophy of the urinary bladder, mainly CPRE (complete primary repair of exstrophy, Mitchell).*

### Introduction
- **EEC (exstrophy–epispadias complex)** is one spectrum: glanular epispadias → **CBE (classic bladder exstrophy)**, over 50% → cloacal exstrophy.
- CBE (incidence) about 1 in 46,000; male:female 2.3:1. Offspring of an affected parent: 1 in 70.

### A. Development of the ureter
- **Weeks 4–6:** the **ureteric bud** arises from the distal **Wolffian (mesonephric) duct**. GDNF (glial cell line-derived neurotrophic factor) from the metanephric mesenchyme induces a single bud (extra buds → duplication).
- **Reciprocal induction:** the bud invades the metanephric blastema. Bud stalk → ureter; its branches → pelvis, calyces and collecting ducts; blastema → nephrons.
- **Wall:** urothelium, lamina propria, smooth muscle and serosa, the same plan as the bladder.
- **Chwalla membrane** at the future orifice normally ruptures; failure is the classic explanation of ureterocele.

### B. Development of the bladder (normal development)
- The **cloacal membrane** (bilaminar ectoderm–endoderm) occupies the infraumbilical wall. **Mesenchyme grows in** between its layers and forms the lower abdominal muscles and pelvic bones.
- **Cloacal division (weeks 5–6):** the **urorectal septum** divides the cloaca into dorsal **anorectal canal** / rectum (posterior) and ventral **UGS (urogenital sinus)** / bladder (anterior), meets the membrane, which perforates into urogenital and anal openings. Paired genital tubercles fuse **cephalad** to the membrane.
- **Wolffian duct entry** is the landmark: cranial **vesicourethral canal** → bladder and pelvic urethra; caudal **definitive UGS** → phallic urethra (male), vestibule (female).
- **Layers:** urothelium from UGS endoderm; detrusor and adventitia from splanchnic mesenchyme by epithelial–mesenchymal signalling.
- **Urachus:** the bladder descends (months 4–5); the allantois obliterates to the urachus (median umbilical ligament).
- Urine from week 10; after week 16 amniotic fluid is mainly fetal urine.

### C. Trigone and ureterovesical junction
- **Weeks 5–6:** the **common excretory duct** (Wolffian duct distal to the bud) is absorbed into the UGS; **apoptosis** separates ureter from Wolffian duct. The arms rotate (**Mackie–Stephens**): ureteric orifice moves cranially and laterally; Wolffian duct ends caudally as the ejaculatory duct.
- **Trigone muscle:** classic view, ureteric extension; current lineage tracing, mostly bladder smooth muscle.

| Bud origin | Orifice | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Too low (early contact) | High, lateral, short tunnel | VUR (vesicoureteral reflux) |
| Too high (late contact) | Caudal, medial | Ectopic ureter, often obstructed |
| Either | Abnormal bud–blastema contact | Dysplasia (VUR and dysplasia coexist) |

- **Weigert–Meyer:** in duplex systems the upper-pole orifice is caudal and medial.

### D. Developmental basis of EEC / ectopia vesicae
**1. Core defect (cause)**
- The cloacal membrane is **not reinforced by mesodermal ingrowth** (by mesoderm), so it ruptures (**Marshall–Muecke** overdevelopment theory). **Timing and extent of rupture decide the variant:** before septum descent gives cloacal exstrophy; after gives CBE; limited rupture gives epispadias.

**2. Theories**
| Theory | Proposed defect | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| **Cloacal membrane overdevelopment (Marshall–Muecke)** | Abnormally large membrane in week 4 blocks medial mesenchymal migration | Timing of rupture gives the variant |
| Lateral body-wall fold failure | Folds fail to meet in midline | Abdomen and pelvis → cloacal; pelvis alone → classic |
| Caudal genital hillocks | Tubercles fuse **below** the membrane | Dorsal urethral defect (epispadias) |
| Abnormal body-stalk insertion | Caudal insertion stops mesenchyme interposing | Unstable membrane disintegrates |
| Bony pelvis first | Pelvic ring primordium fails to rotate | Bladder herniates |
- All converge on **absent infraumbilical midline mesoderm**.

**3. Aetiology**
- Multifactorial (inconsistent twin concordance). **22q11.2 duplication** commonest variant; **ISL1** susceptibility gene; p63 under-expressed in exstrophic urothelium.
- **IVF (in vitro fertilisation)** about 7.5-fold and early high-dose progesterone about tenfold raise risk; maternal smoking linked to cloacal exstrophy; folate to the mildest form.

**4. Resulting anatomy / defects (why each defect occurs)**
- Exposed (open) bladder plate; epispadias; low umbilicus; anterior anus; hernias.
- **Pelvis:** externally rotated (posterior 12°, anterior 18° each side), **anterior pubic segment 30% short**, **pubic diastasis mean 4.8 cm**.
- **Male genitalia:** anterior corpora about 50% shorter; short, upturned penis with dorsal chordee.
- **Female:** bifid clitoris, short vagina, anterior stenotic introitus; later prolapse.
- **Bladder:** immature, not neuropathic (raised type III collagen falls after closure). Ureters enter with no obliquity, so **VUR (vesicoureteric reflux) in 100%** after closure.
- Anterior anus, wide levator hiatus, inguinal hernias, low umbilicus.

### E. Management (outline)
**1. Prenatal:** prenatal diagnosis only about 25%: ultrasound shows absent (no) bladder filling, low umbilicus, wide pubic rami, small genitalia, enlarging lower abdominal mass. Counsel (counselling); deliver at an exstrophy centre.

**2. At birth**
- Tie cord with silk (no clamp); cover the plate with plastic wrap; saline irrigation at nappy change.
- Renal ultrasound; multidisciplinary team.
- **Assess:** template size and polyps (**≥3 × 3 cm** suits newborn closure), diastasis and pelvic malleability under anaesthesia, urethral plate and penile length, associated anomalies.
- Small template, penoscrotal duplication, hypoplastic bladder: **delayed closure at 6–9 months** (delay closure, not a bad early closure).

**3. Pelvic osteotomy and immobilisation**
- **Indications:** closure **after 72 hours**, diastasis **>4 cm**, stiff (poorly malleable) pelvis, failed closure, or cloacal exstrophy.
- **Combined bilateral anterior innominate + vertical iliac osteotomy** with external fixator and (Buck) traction 4–6 weeks. Under 72 hours with malleable pubes / without osteotomy: modified Bryant traction for 4 weeks. Spica casting fails more often.

**4. MSRE (modern staged repair of exstrophy)**
- Aim of closure: convert exstrophy into incontinent epispadias, with enough outlet resistance for bladder growth but not enough to harm the kidneys.

| Stage | Age | Procedure | Purpose |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Newborn | Bladder, posterior urethra and abdominal wall closure; ± osteotomy | Converts to complete epispadias; outlet resistance stimulates bladder growth |
| 2 | 6–12 months | Epispadias repair (**modified Cantwell-Ransley**), after testosterone | Raises capacity; straightens penis |
| 3 | 5–7 years (another source: 4–7 years) (capacity ≥100 mL) | BNR (bladder neck reconstruction): modified **Young-Dees-Leadbetter (YDL)** + bilateral **Cohen** reimplantation (reimplant) | Continence; needs capacity **≥100 mL** and a motivated child |

- **Closure keys:** **radical division of the urogenital diaphragm fibres** so the bladder / vesicourethral unit sits deep in the pelvis (commonest error if missed); ureteric stents; suprapubic tube 4 weeks; pubic nylon mattress suture.
- **Alternatives:** **CPRE (complete primary repair of exstrophy, Mitchell)** with penile disassembly; Kelly radical soft-tissue mobilisation; Erlangen total repair (single stage); Mainz ureterosigmoidostomy. Most still need an outlet procedure.

**5. Single-stage repair (CPRE and others)**
*Background:* classic bladder exstrophy: absent infraumbilical mesoderm; open bladder, epispadias, pubic diastasis (mean 4.8 cm).
- **MSRE (modern staged repair):** closure at birth, epispadias repair 6–12 months, bladder neck reconstruction 4–7 years. Single-stage repairs aim for fewer operations and continence without bladder neck repair.

*Single-stage options:*

| Repair | Features |
|---|---|
| **CPRE (Mitchell)** | Newborn closure + penile disassembly + bladder neck tailoring |
| Erlangen ("total") | All in one at about 8 weeks, no osteotomy |
| Combined closure + epispadias repair | Delayed or failed closures, boys >6 months |

- **Selection:** template ≥3 × 3 cm, elastic, few polyps; experienced team. Not for penoscrotal duplication, hypoplastic bladder, bilateral hydronephrosis.
- **CPRE technique:**
  1. Combined general and epidural anaesthesia; **osteotomy if >72 hours** (3 days) old or diastasis >4 cm or stiff pelvis.
  2. Incise around plate; free bladder; **divide urogenital diaphragm fibres** to levator hiatus so the unit sits deep.
  3. **Penile disassembly:** right and left hemicorporoglanular bodies plus urethral wedge on spongiosum (now often left attached to glans).
  4. Urethra placed ventrally between corpora; corpora joined; meatus usually **hypospadiac**.
  5. Bladder and neck closed and tapered; ± bilateral ureteric reimplantation (BUR-CPRE).
  6. Pubic No. 2 nylon mattress suture; suprapubic catheter 4 weeks, ureteric stents.
  7. Immobilise: external fixation plus traction (failure 7.5% vs spica 47%).
- **Complications:** same frequency as MSRE but **more serious**: dehiscence, prolapse, vesicocutaneous fistula, **glans or corporal loss** (ischaemia), early reimplantation for VUR (vesicoureteric reflux) or hydronephrosis; retention in girls.

**6. Follow-up**
- Prophylaxis; ultrasound; yearly cystogram and cystoscopy under anaesthesia.

**7. Failures and salvage**
- Dehiscence or prolapse: wait 4–6 months, reclose with osteotomy.
- Failed BNR (no 3-hour dry interval by 2 years): bulking, redo YDL, or **augmentation + bladder neck closure + Mitrofanoff** (continent catheterisable stoma).

**8. Cloacal exstrophy (OEIS: omphalocele, exstrophy, imperforate anus, spinal defects)**
- Stabilise; neurosurgery (spinal defects 85–100%); separate bowel from hemibladders; colostomy; osteotomy always; gender congruent with karyotype; continence mostly by augmentation with CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation).

### F. Outcomes and long-term
- Daytime dryness (day continence) after MSRE about **70–74%** (another source: 70–75%); versus about 56% after CPRE with YDL (Young-Dees-Leadbetter). Most (after CPRE) still need bladder neck surgery.
- **Bladder capacity at neck repair is the main predictor**; each failed closure lowers it. **Best (continence) predictors: template size at birth and a successful first closure.**
- **Also know:** variants (superior vesical fissure, duplicate, pseudoexstrophy); adenocarcinoma risk (cytology, cystoscopy; colonoscopy after ureterosigmoidostomy); fertility (reduced male fertility) and uterine prolapse in adults; female epispadias; psychosocial support.

**Diagram to draw:** cloacal membrane with urorectal septum and failed mesodermal ingrowth; Wolffian duct with ureteric bud and its absorption into the UGS; MSRE timeline; penile disassembly into two hemicorpora and urethral wedge.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bud from Wolffian duct; position decides VUR or ectopia.
> - Failed mesodermal reinforcement of the cloacal membrane; rupture timing decides variant.
> - Diastasis 4.8 cm; anterior pubis 30% short; VUR in 100%.
> - MSRE: (birth) closure → epispadias repair 6–12 months (mo) → YDL / BNR at 5–7 years, at ≥100 mL.
> - Osteotomy if >72 h (closure after 72 h) or diastasis >4 cm.
> - CPRE: closure + penile disassembly in newborn. Risks: glans loss, hypospadias, VUR.
> - Capacity ≥100 mL predicts continence; template size + first successful closure (first closure success) predict continence.

# Section 5 · Paediatric Urology 2 — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## HSG in neonates
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 3, Q3c

*HSG (hysterosalpingography) is not done in neonates, so this is taken as "USG (ultrasonography) in neonates".*

### Introduction
- First-line, radiation-free imaging. For antenatal **UTD (urinary tract dilation)** (1–3% of pregnancies), febrile UTI (urinary tract infection), mass, poor stream.

### Timing and technique
- **First scan after 48 hours**: earlier, relative dehydration under-reads dilatation.
- **Scan early if outlet obstruction is suspected**; catheterise.
- Measure **APD (anteroposterior pelvic diameter)** on a transverse mid-kidney view; assess calyces, parenchyma, ureters, bladder wall (thick if over 3 mm when full).
- Normal: **prominent hypoechoic pyramids** (not hydronephrosis); APD under 10 mm.

### Grading
- **SFU (Society for Fetal Urology)**: 0 none; 1 pelvis only; 2 major calyces; 3 minor calyces too; 4 plus parenchymal thinning.
- **UTD 2014**: prenatal A1 (APD 4 to <7 mm before 28 weeks, 7 to <10 mm after) versus A2–3 (larger, or any other abnormality); postnatal:

| Grade | Findings | Plan |
|---|---|---|
| P1 | APD 10 to <15 mm or central calyces | USG at 3–6 months; no VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) or prophylaxis |
| P2 | APD 15 mm or more, peripheral calyces or dilated ureter | VCUG, prophylaxis optional (give if ureter >7 mm) |
| P3 | Abnormal parenchyma or bladder | USG at 1 month, VCUG, prophylaxis, MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) scan |

### Specific findings
| Condition | USG |
|---|---|
| PUJO (pelviureteric junction obstruction) | Dilated pelvis, normal ureter; pyramids under 3 mm predict pyeloplasty |
| VUR (vesicoureteral reflux) | Normal first scan in up to 25% |
| **PUV (posterior urethral valves)** | Bilateral hydroureteronephrosis, thick bladder, dilated posterior urethra (**keyhole sign**); VCUG before discharge |
| Ureterocele | Thin-walled intravesical cyst |
| MCDK (multicystic dysplastic kidney) | Non-communicating cysts, no central pelvis |
| ARPKD (autosomal recessive polycystic kidney disease) | Bilateral large, bright kidneys |
| Renal vein thrombosis | Large echogenic kidney, no venous flow; haematuria, mass, thrombocytopenia |
| Adrenal haemorrhage | Avascular, evolving suprarenal mass (neuroblastoma is vascular) |
- **Also know:** mesoblastic nephroma; twinkling artefact (stones).

### Recent advances
- ceVUS (contrast-enhanced voiding urosonography).

**Diagram to draw:** SFU grades 0–4.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - First scan after 48 hours, except suspected PUV.
> - UTD: P1 APD 10 to <15 mm; P2 15 mm or more; P3 abnormal parenchyma or bladder.
> - Keyhole sign suggests PUV.

## Prune belly syndrome
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 3, Q3f

### Definition
- **PBS (prune belly syndrome)**, Eagle–Barrett or triad syndrome: **deficient abdominal wall muscles**, **dilated poorly contracting urinary tract**, **bilateral intra-abdominal testes**.
- 1 in 29,000–44,000 births; **95% male**. **Renal dysplasia, not the abdominal wall, decides survival.**

### Pathogenesis (unproven)
- Fetal **urethral obstruction**, though most have none after birth; **lateral plate mesoderm defect**; intrinsic ureteric defect; yolk sac defect. Mostly sporadic; smooth-muscle genes (MYOCD, FLNA) in families.

### Features
- **Kidneys**: dysplasia in about 50%. **Ureters**: tortuous, worse distally; collagen replaces muscle, so peristalsis fails; **VUR (vesicoureteral reflux) about 75%**.
- **Bladder**: large, smooth, urachal diverticulum (urachus patent in 25–30%). **Prostatic hypoplasia**, dilated posterior urethra; atresia or megalourethra.
- **Testes**: intra-abdominal; azoospermia usual.
- **Extra-genitourinary (about 75%)**: pulmonary hypoplasia; cardiac 10%; gastrointestinal at least 30%; orthopaedic up to 65%.

### Woodard classification
| | Features | Outlook |
|---|---|---|
| I | Oligohydramnios, **pulmonary hypoplasia**, dysplasia, urethral atresia | Perinatal death |
| II | Full triad, moderate uropathy, **no pulmonary hypoplasia** | Risk of CKD (chronic kidney disease); debated |
| III | Mild, **stable function** | Good; majority |
- **Also know:** RUBACE severity score (renal, ureter, bladder, abdominal wall, cryptorchidism, extra-genitourinary); "pseudo-prune" presents late.

### Management
- **Neonatal**: lungs and heart first; creatinine trend, ultrasound, DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid) at 4–6 weeks, prophylaxis, circumcision. VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) only if renal insufficiency or obstruction.
- Category III: surveillance. Sepsis or rising creatinine: wide **cutaneous vesicostomy**.
- **Reconstruction** after 3–6 months: ureteric tailoring and reimplantation using the healthier proximal ureter; reduction cystoplasty gives little lasting benefit.
- **Orchidopexy**: bilateral **transabdominal at about 6 months**; staged Fowler–Stephens later.
- **Abdominoplasty** (cosmesis, better cough and emptying): **Randolph** (transverse excision of the worst lower wall); **Ehrlich** (vertical incision, umbilicus kept, vest-over-pants overlap); **Monfort** (umbilicus kept, central fascial strip with lateral flaps overlapped; good exposure for combined surgery).

### Prognosis
- 20% die perinatally; **up to 30% develop chronic renal failure**; nadir creatinine **under 0.7 mg/dL** is favourable.

**Diagram to draw:** VCUG showing large bladder and dilated posterior urethra.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Triad; 95% male; dysplasia decides prognosis.
> - Woodard I–III.
> - Vesicostomy for drainage; orchidopexy at about 6 months.

## Neurogenic bladder: prenatal/neonatal assessment and bladder myoplasty
**Asked as:**
- Prenatal and Neonatal assessment of neurogenic bladder dysfunction — *Summer 2016 Paper III Q3e (SAQ)*
- Bladder myeloplsty — *Summer 2017 Paper III Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Part A: Prenatal and Neonatal assessment of neurogenic bladder dysfunction

#### Introduction
- Commonest cause: **myelomeningocele** (open NTD (neural tube defect)); also occult dysraphism, sacral agenesis, anorectal malformation.
- **Bony or motor level does not predict bladder function**, so every newborn needs testing.

#### Prenatal assessment
- **Maternal serum AFP (alpha-fetoprotein)** raised in open defects; amniotic AFP and acetylcholinesterase confirm.
- **Ultrasound**: spinal defect; "lemon" and "banana" signs of Chiari II; ventriculomegaly; hydronephrosis, thick bladder. Fetal MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) for level.
- Multidisciplinary counselling at a fetal centre.
- **MOMS (Management of Myelomeningocele Study) 2011**: closure **before 26 weeks** reduced shunt need (about 40% vs 82%) and improved motor and cognitive scores, but caused prematurity (34 vs 37 weeks) and uterine thinning. **No proven bladder benefit**: CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) and reconstruction rates unchanged.

#### Neonatal assessment (baseline)
| Test | Timing / purpose |
|---|---|
| Examination | Spine, legs, anal tone, perineal sensation, bladder |
| **Renal and bladder ultrasound** | Before discharge, then 6-monthly for 2 years; hydronephrosis, wall thickness |
| Residual urine | Start CIC if not emptying |
| Creatinine | Within 3 months (cystatin C later) |
| **VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) or video-UDS** | Reflux; dilating VUR (vesicoureteric reflux) in about 7% |
| **UDS (urodynamics)** with EMG (electromyography) | Within 3 months, once stable after closure |
| DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid) | Baseline scarring (EAU (European Association of Urology)) |

#### Urodynamic risk (UMPIRE (Urologic and Renal Protocol for the Newborn and Young Child with Spina Bifida))
| Risk | DLPP (detrusor leak point pressure) or end-fill pressure |
|---|---|
| High (hostile) | 40 cm H2O or more (McGuire 1981) |
| Intermediate | 25–39, or detrusor overactivity |
| Low | Under 25 |
- Poor compliance and DSD (detrusor–sphincter dyssynergia) also predict upper-tract damage.

#### Action
- High risk → **early CIC plus oxybutynin**: hydronephrosis 0–15% versus 18–80% expectant; ESRD (end-stage renal disease) about 30% falls to under 2%.
- Repeat studies: the lesion can change (re-tethering).

**Diagram to draw:** neonatal UDS trace with DLPP over 40 cm H2O.

### Part B: Bladder myeloplsty

*Assumed to mean "bladder myoplasty" (a muscle procedure on the bladder wall).*

#### Definition
- **Detrusor myoplasty**: wrapping a **vascularised, innervated skeletal muscle flap** round the bladder so that voluntary contraction of that muscle empties an **acontractile bladder**.

#### Indications
- **Acontractile or underactive detrusor** with catheter dependence: lower motor neuron lesions (spina bifida, cauda equina injury), myogenic failure.
- Needs good bladder capacity and compliance, and a competent but not obstructing outlet.

#### Latissimus dorsi detrusor myoplasty (LDDM; Stenzl)
- **Free latissimus dorsi flap**: artery and vein joined to the **inferior epigastric vessels**; motor nerve joined to an **intercostal nerve**.
- Muscle wrapped **spirally over more than three-quarters of the bladder** and anchored to pelvic floor fascia and ligaments.
- Patient voids by contracting the transferred muscle (abdominal effort).
- **Results:** most voided with low residual; bladder contractility index rose to the strong range.
- **Complications** in about one-third: thromboembolism, abscess, wound infection; none long term.
- **Rectus abdominis detrusor myoplasty**: simpler, less morbid; small spinal cord injury series with spontaneous voiding.
- **Status: investigational.** Standard care stays **clean intermittent catheterisation**.

#### Other "myoplasty" procedure: detrusor myotomy/myectomy (autoaugmentation)
- Purpose is the opposite: **increase capacity and compliance** in a small, poorly compliant or overactive bladder.
- **Technique (Cartwright and Snow, 1989):** strip and excise the detrusor over the dome, leave the mucosa intact, and anchor the detrusor edges (psoas hitch) so the mucosa bulges as a **wide-mouthed diverticulum**.
- **Pros:** urothelial lining, no mucus, no metabolic change, no bowel.
- **Cons:** seldom adds significant capacity; compliance gain may not last; **not recommended for adult detrusor overactivity**.
- **Also know:** seromuscular enterocystoplasty covers the bulge with demucosalised sigmoid.

#### Alternatives
- **Also know:** sacral neuromodulation, sacral anterior root stimulation, reduction cystoplasty (abandoned), muscle-derived stem cells (research).

**Diagram to draw:** latissimus flap spiralled round the bladder with vascular and nerve anastomoses.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Level does not predict bladder; UDS within 3 months.
> - DLPP 40 cm H2O or more = hostile; early CIC + antimuscarinic.
> - MOMS: fewer shunts, no bladder benefit.
> - LDDM: free latissimus to inferior epigastric vessels and intercostal nerve; for acontractile bladder.
> - Investigational; clean intermittent catheterisation remains standard.
> - Autoaugmentation: detrusor excised, mucosa bulges; little lasting gain.

## DD of frequency and urgency in 3 yr male child and treatment
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Summer Paper 3, Q3c

### Introduction
- **Frequency**: ≥8 voids a day (ICCS, International Children's Continence Society). **Urgency**: sudden, compelling need to void.
- At 3 years toilet training may be ongoing; decide first whether the pattern is abnormal.

### Differential diagnosis
| Group | Causes and clues |
|---|---|
| **Infection** | **UTI (urinary tract infection)**: fever, dysuria; balanoposthitis; viral haemorrhagic cystitis |
| **Functional** | **BBD (bladder and bowel dysfunction)**/OAB (overactive bladder); voiding postponement; **extraordinary daytime urinary frequency** (sudden very frequent small voids, no nocturia or wetting, often after stress; self-limiting) |
| **Bowel** | **Constipation**: rectal mass presses on bladder; commonest associated cause |
| **Obstruction** | **PUV (posterior urethral valves)**, mild late form; **meatal stenosis** (circumcised boys; thin deflected stream); severe phimosis |
| **Neurogenic** | Occult spinal dysraphism, tethered cord (sacral dimple, hair tuft, gait or foot abnormality) |
| **Bladder irritants** | Stone, foreign body, hypercalciuria, pinworms, rarely **rhabdomyosarcoma** |
| **Polyuria** | Diabetes mellitus, diabetes insipidus, chronic kidney disease |
| **Behavioural** | Anxiety, family stress, developmental disorders |

### Evaluation
- **History:** onset, nocturia, wetting, stream, fever, **bowel habit (Bristol chart)**, thirst, stressors.
- **Examination:** abdomen, meatus and foreskin, **lumbosacral spine**, lower-limb neurology.
- **Urinalysis and culture**; glucose; specific gravity >1.005 makes diabetes insipidus unlikely.
- **Ultrasound** of kidneys and bladder: wall thickness, residual, dilation, stones.
- **Bladder and bowel diary**; uroflow if the child cooperates.
- **Selective:** VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) for suspected PUV or febrile UTI with abnormal ultrasound; spinal MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) for neurological signs; urine calcium/creatinine ratio.

### Treatment (cause-directed)
- **UTI:** antibiotics per culture.
- **Constipation first:** disimpaction, then maintenance **polyethylene glycol**, diet and toilet routine.
- **Urotherapy:** timed voiding every 2–3 hours, relaxed posture, adequate fluids, avoid caffeine.
- **OAB persisting after urotherapy:** **oxybutynin** 0.3–0.6 mg/kg/day in 2–3 doses (maximum 15 mg/day); watch for constipation.
- **Extraordinary daytime frequency:** reassurance; resolves in weeks to months.
- **Meatal stenosis:** meatotomy. **PUV:** endoscopic valve ablation. **Tethered cord:** neurosurgical release.

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: urinalysis → ultrasound with residual → targeted tests.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Always ask about bowels; treat constipation first.
> - Rule out UTI, PUV, meatal stenosis and occult dysraphism.
> - Extraordinary daytime frequency: no nocturia, normal tests, self-limiting.

## Embryology and Management of P.U. valve patient.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 3, Q3d

### Embryology
- **Weeks 5–6:** the **urorectal septum** divides the cloaca into rectum and **urogenital sinus**; wolffian ducts migrate to the verumontanum, leaving small folds.

| Young type | Anatomy | Origin |
|---|---|---|
| **I (about 95%)** | Leaflets from the verumontanum, fused anteriorly | Abnormal anterior insertion of the wolffian duct ends |
| II | Folds from verumontanum to bladder neck | Not real (secondary hypertrophy) |
| **III** | Ring diaphragm with central hole | **Persistent urogenital membrane** |

- **COPUM (congenital obstructing posterior urethral membrane):** one membrane, torn by the first catheter into type I leaflets.
- Fetal urine from 10–14 weeks: obstruction causes **renal dysplasia**, oligohydramnios, pulmonary hypoplasia.

### Antenatal
- Bilateral hydroureteronephrosis, thick bladder, **keyhole sign**, oligohydramnios. **Vesicoamniotic shunt** helps survival (lungs), not kidneys (PLUTO trial, 2013).

### Neonatal management
1. **Drain the bladder:** 5–7 Fr feeding tube (coudé if it curls); minimal balloon inflation.
2. **Stabilise:** ventilation; correct **post-obstructive diuresis**, hyponatraemia, acidosis, hyperkalaemia; antibiotics.
3. **Confirm:** VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram), renal ultrasound, MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) at 2–3 months.
4. **Primary endoscopic valve ablation** (preferred): laser, Bugbee or hook at 5 and 7 o'clock ± 12; VCUG within 1 month.
5. **Vesicostomy** if the urethra is too small or drainage fails. **Upper-tract diversion** rarely: no proven renal benefit.
6. **Circumcision:** cuts febrile UTI (urinary tract infection) sharply.
- **VUR (vesicoureteral reflux)** (50–80%) is secondary; treat the bladder, not the ureter.

### Prognosis
| Nadir creatinine (first year) | ESRD (end-stage renal disease) risk |
|---|---|
| < 0.4 mg/dL | Nil |
| 0.4–0.69 | 2% |
| 0.7–0.99 | 27% |
| > 1.0 | Up to 100% |

- Lifetime ESRD risk 20–50%.

### Long-term care
- **Valve bladder** (Mitchell): polyuria, poor sensation and residual urine keep the upper tracts dilated. Timed and double voiding, anticholinergic or α-blocker, **CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation)**, **overnight drainage**, Mitrofanoff, rarely augmentation.
- **CKD (chronic kidney disease) care; transplantation** after urodynamics and bladder optimisation; graft survival similar.

**Diagram to draw:** type I leaflets versus type III membrane, with keyhole VCUG.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Type I (95%): wolffian duct insertion; type III: persistent urogenital membrane.
> - Catheter, stabilise, then primary valve ablation; vesicostomy if not possible.
> - Nadir creatinine below 0.7 mg/dL predicts good renal outcome.
> - Valve bladder: overnight drainage and CIC.

## CSIC.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 3, Q3e

*Taken to mean **CISC – clean intermittent self-catheterisation**.*

### Definition and principle
- Regular, **clean (not sterile)** passage of a catheter by the patient (or carer) to empty the bladder, then removal.
- **Lapides (1971–72)**: infection comes from **stasis and overdistension** (poor bladder-wall blood flow), not from bacteria alone. Regular complete emptying protects the bladder wall and upper tracts, even though bacteriuria develops.
- Gold standard for chronic emptying failure.

### Indications
- **Neurogenic bladder:** spina bifida, spinal cord injury, multiple sclerosis, cauda equina, diabetic cystopathy.
- **Underactive or acontractile detrusor**; chronic retention; post-operative retention.
- **Detrusor–sphincter dyssynergia**: CISC bypasses the obstructed sphincter.
- After **augmentation or continent diversion**; **valve bladder**; high residual after botulinum toxin.
- **Contraindications:** poor hand function or cognition without a carer, urethral false passage or stricture, unwillingness.

### Technique
- Wash hands → clean meatus with water → lubricated catheter (or pre-lubricated **hydrophilic** catheter) → pass until urine flows → drain fully → withdraw slowly.
- Sizes: adults about 12–14 Fr; children by age (8–10 Fr).
- **Frequency:** 4–6 times a day, keeping each volume **below about 400–500 mL** (child: below expected bladder capacity).
- Combine with **antimuscarinics** for overactivity; bladder diary.

### Catheters
| Type | Note |
|---|---|
| Plain PVC (polyvinyl chloride), reusable after washing | Cheap; common in India |
| **Hydrophilic-coated** single-use | Less urethral trauma; longer time to first UTI (urinary tract infection) |
| Compact or closed-system | Convenience, outdoor use |

### Children
- Start **early (infancy)** in spina bifida with hostile bladder; families adapt best; child involved from 3–5 years.
- **Mitrofanoff appendicovesicostomy** (1980) if urethral catheterisation is difficult (wheelchair, sensate urethra, girls).

### Complications
- **Asymptomatic bacteriuria** (common): **do not treat or give prophylaxis**; treat only symptomatic UTI.
- Urethral trauma, bleeding, **false passage**, stricture, epididymitis, bladder stones (hair, mucus), rarely lost catheter.

### Outcome
- Less pyelonephritis, epididymo-orchitis and urosepsis than indwelling catheters; preserves the upper tracts; with drugs, continence in many.

**Diagram to draw:** neurogenic bladder management ladder: CISC + antimuscarinic → botulinum toxin → augmentation + channel.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Lapides: overdistension, not bacteria, causes infection; clean is enough.
> - 4–6 times daily, under about 500 mL each.
> - Do not treat asymptomatic bacteriuria.
> - Mitrofanoff when urethral access is hard.

## Enuresis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 3, Q3e

### Definition (ICCS [International Children's Continence Society])
- Intermittent urinary incontinence **during sleep** in a child **aged 5 years or more**.
- **Primary:** never dry. **Secondary:** wetting after at least **6 months** dry (look for stressors, diabetes, UTI [urinary tract infection], neurological disease).
- **MNE (monosymptomatic):** no daytime LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms). **NMNE (non-monosymptomatic):** with daytime LUTS.

### Epidemiology
- About **5–10% at 7 years**; spontaneous resolution about 15% a year; about 2% of adults; familial.

### Pathophysiology: three-way mismatch
1. **Nocturnal polyuria** (reduced night-time ADH [antidiuretic hormone] rise).
2. **Reduced nocturnal bladder capacity** or DO (detrusor overactivity).
3. **Failure to arouse** from sleep.
- Comorbidities: constipation, sleep-disordered breathing (atrial natriuretic peptide raises urine output), ADHD (attention deficit hyperactivity disorder).

### Evaluation
- History: day symptoms, bowels, snoring, fluids, family history.
- **Bladder diary**: daytime voided volumes against EBC (expected bladder capacity) = (age + 2) × 30 mL in the chapter (ICCS uses [age + 1] × 30); night urine output by weighing nappies.
- Examination: spine, genitalia, abdomen for faecal loading; urinalysis.
- Uroflowmetry, PVR (post-void residual) and ultrasound only for NMNE or before adding anticholinergics. No invasive tests in MNE.

### Management
- **Treat daytime LUTS and constipation first** in NMNE.
- Education removes blame; standard urotherapy. Waking, fluid restriction alone and punishment do not work.

| | **Alarm** | **Desmopressin** |
|---|---|---|
| Target | Arousal (conditioning) | Night urine output |
| Use | Nightly for months; parent wakes child; continue to 14 dry nights | 0.2–0.4 mg tablet or 120–240 µg melt, 1 hour before bed |
| Response | 50–70%; durable | Rule of thirds; immediate but relapses |
| Caution | Needs motivated family | Restrict evening fluids (hyponatraemia) |
- Overlearning (extra evening fluid once dry) reduces alarm relapse.
- **Failure:** switch or combine. **Second line:** add an anticholinergic (oxybutynin) after checking PVR and bowels. **Resistant:** imipramine (cardiotoxic; ECG [electrocardiogram]), off-label β3-agonists; treat sleep apnoea (adenotonsillectomy).

**Diagram to draw:** triad diagram: nocturnal polyuria, small bladder capacity, arousal failure.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ≥5 years; secondary = after 6 months dry.
> - Alarm (durable) and desmopressin (rule of thirds) are first line.
> - Treat daytime symptoms and constipation first.

## Discuss diagnostics and operative pediatric laparoscopy in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 3, Q3a

### Evolution
- Diagnostic (non-palpable testis, 1976) → nephrectomy (1994) → pyeloplasty (1995) → robotic (2002).

### Diagnostic laparoscopy
- **Non-palpable testis** (procedure of choice, after examination under anaesthesia): note vessels, vas, testis and internal ring.

| Finding | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Blind-ending vessels and vas, closed ring | Vanishing testis: stop |
| Vessels and vas entering ring | Explore groin; excise remnant |
| Intra-abdominal testis | Orchidopexy (primary or Fowler-Stephens) |
- **DSD (differences of sex development):** gonadal inspection and biopsy, Müllerian structures.

### Operative laparoscopy
- **Testis:** primary orchidopexy (>90% success); Fowler-Stephens one- or two-stage; gonadectomy in DSD.
- **Kidney:** nephrectomy, heminephrectomy, **pyeloplasty** (commonest MIS (minimally invasive surgery) procedure).
- **Ureter and bladder:** extravesical or vesicoscopic (cross-trigonal) reimplantation, Mitrofanoff, augmentation, bladder neck reconstruction, utricle and urogenital sinus.
- **Also know:** RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection) for paratesticular rhabdomyosarcoma.

### Technical principles
- **Access:** open Hasson or Veress at umbilicus (Palmer's point if scarred); equal safety.
- 3–5 mm ports; robots use 8 mm. Transperitoneal (most space), retroperitoneal (spares peritoneum, useful in peritoneal dialysis), vesicoscopic.
- **Pressures:** CO₂ absorption, reduced venous return, raised airway pressure; reversible oliguria (no fluid bolus).

| Age | 0–1 month | 2–12 months | 1–2 years | 2–8 years |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Max IAP (intra-abdominal pressure) | ≤6 mmHg | ≤8 | ≤10 | ≤12 |
- **Positioning:** modified flank for kidney; supine steep Trendelenburg for pelvis. Infants: minimise inhalational anaesthetic, rectal tube to reduce bowel distension.
- **Only absolute contraindication:** inability to tolerate pneumoperitoneum.

### Complications
- Right common iliac vessel injury (behind umbilicus; commonest major), inferior epigastric (commonest minor), bowel injury, subcutaneous emphysema, **port-site hernia 0.5–3%** (close fascia).

### Pros and cons
- Shorter stay, better cosmesis, deep pelvic access; pain benefit unproven. Robot: cost, learning curve; infant working space only about 1 L.

**Diagram to draw:** laparoscopic view of internal ring with blind-ending vessels.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Diagnostic laparoscopy is the procedure of choice for non-palpable testis.
> - Pressure ceiling 6–12 mmHg by age.
> - Right common iliac at Veress entry; close port fascia.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 5 · Paediatric Urology 2

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Embryological development of urinary bladder and management of Hutch Diverticulum? (LAQ)

- Urorectal septum weeks 4–6; vesicourethral canal → bladder; urachus at months 4–5.
- Hutch: herniation through the ureteric hiatus → VUR.
- Ureter entering the sac: VUR will not resolve.
- Secondary: treat the outlet first; else diverticulectomy + reimplantation.

#### Antenatal hydronephrosis (UTD) and fetal uropathy (LUTO) (LAQ)

- UTD: 1–3% of pregnancies; second commonest anomaly after cardiac.
- ANH (antenatal hydronephrosis) = UTD; APD normal <4 mm before 28 weeks, <7 mm after, <10 mm postnatally.
- Transient dilation 50–70% (commonest cause); UPJ obstruction commonest surgical cause; VUR is not predicted by grade.
- First postnatal US after 48 hours; A1: one scan after 32 weeks; A2–3 scan every 4 weeks.
- Risk-based care: P1 no prophylaxis or VCUG; prophylaxis if ureter >7 mm or P3; P3 prophylaxis, VCUG, MAG3.
- Favourable urine: Na <100, Cl <90, osmolality <200, β2-microglobulin <6.
- PLUTO: survival up, renal function unchanged.

#### Development of the urethra, congenital urethral anomalies and management of epispadias (including isolated penopubic epispadias) (LAQ)

- Whole male urethra is endodermal UGS; fold fusion complete at 16, urethra at 18 weeks.
- About 70% of male epispadias are penopubic and incontinent; VUR 30–40%.
- Diastasis over 3 cm (>3 cm) → exstrophy pathway.
- Cantwell–Ransley at 6–12 months; YDL (Young–Dees–Leadbetter) at about 100 mL capacity.
- Continence after YDL: males 47–82%, females 70–87%.

#### Bladder and ureter development, exstrophy–epispadias complex and its management (LAQ)

- Bud from Wolffian duct; position decides VUR or ectopia.
- Failed mesodermal reinforcement of the cloacal membrane; rupture timing decides variant.
- Diastasis 4.8 cm; anterior pubis 30% short; VUR in 100%.
- MSRE: (birth) closure → epispadias repair 6–12 months (mo) → YDL / BNR at 5–7 years, at ≥100 mL.
- Osteotomy if >72 h (closure after 72 h) or diastasis >4 cm.
- CPRE: closure + penile disassembly in newborn. Risks: glans loss, hypospadias, VUR.
- Capacity ≥100 mL predicts continence; template size + first successful closure (first closure success) predict continence.

#### HSG in neonates (SAQ)

- First scan after 48 hours, except suspected PUV.
- UTD: P1 APD 10 to <15 mm; P2 15 mm or more; P3 abnormal parenchyma or bladder.
- Keyhole sign suggests PUV.

#### Prune belly syndrome (SAQ)

- Triad; 95% male; dysplasia decides prognosis.
- Woodard I–III.
- Vesicostomy for drainage; orchidopexy at about 6 months.

#### Neurogenic bladder: prenatal/neonatal assessment and bladder myoplasty (SAQ)

- Level does not predict bladder; UDS within 3 months.
- DLPP 40 cm H2O or more = hostile; early CIC + antimuscarinic.
- MOMS: fewer shunts, no bladder benefit.
- LDDM: free latissimus to inferior epigastric vessels and intercostal nerve; for acontractile bladder.
- Investigational; clean intermittent catheterisation remains standard.
- Autoaugmentation: detrusor excised, mucosa bulges; little lasting gain.

#### DD of frequency and urgency in 3 yr male child and treatment (SAQ)

- Always ask about bowels; treat constipation first.
- Rule out UTI, PUV, meatal stenosis and occult dysraphism.
- Extraordinary daytime frequency: no nocturia, normal tests, self-limiting.

#### Embryology and Management of P.U. valve patient. (SAQ)

- Type I (95%): wolffian duct insertion; type III: persistent urogenital membrane.
- Catheter, stabilise, then primary valve ablation; vesicostomy if not possible.
- Nadir creatinine below 0.7 mg/dL predicts good renal outcome.
- Valve bladder: overnight drainage and CIC.

#### CSIC. (SAQ)

- Lapides: overdistension, not bacteria, causes infection; clean is enough.
- 4–6 times daily, under about 500 mL each.
- Do not treat asymptomatic bacteriuria.
- Mitrofanoff when urethral access is hard.

#### Enuresis (SAQ)

- ≥5 years; secondary = after 6 months dry.
- Alarm (durable) and desmopressin (rule of thirds) are first line.
- Treat daytime symptoms and constipation first.

#### Discuss diagnostics and operative pediatric laparoscopy in urology (SAQ)

- Diagnostic laparoscopy is the procedure of choice for non-palpable testis.
- Pressure ceiling 6–12 mmHg by age.
- Right common iliac at Veress entry; close port fascia.

# Section 6 · Female Urology — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Urological complications in OBS-GYN and uretero-vaginal fistula
**Asked as:**
- Mention in brief, urological complications in OBS-GYN. Describe in detail treatment of ureterovaginal fistula. — *Practice Set 7 Paper III Q1 (LAQ)* (OBGY complications also answered in Practice Set 3 Paper 3, Q1; ureterovaginal fistula in 2015-Summer Paper 3, Q3e and 2017-Summer Paper 3, Q3b)
- Uretero-vaginal fistula — *Summer 2015 Paper III Q3e (SAQ)*
- Pathogenesis and management of uretero-vaginal fistula — *Summer 2017 Paper III Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

*Assumed meaning: OBS-GYN = obstetric and gynaecological practice and surgery.*

### Part A: Urological complications in OBS-GYN
- The bladder, distal ureter and urethra lie against the cervix, uterine vessels and anterior vaginal wall. Risk rises with distorted anatomy: fibroids, endometriosis, cancer, previous caesarean, radiation, heavy bleeding.

| Setting | Complications |
|---|---|
| **Obstetric** | **Obstructed labour** (fetal head impacted for days): pressure necrosis, so large VVF (vesicovaginal fistula), urethral loss, ureteric involvement; over 90% of VVF in the developing world. **Caesarean**: bladder injury (about 0.3%), ureteric injury, **vesicouterine fistula** (Youssef syndrome: cyclical haematuria, amenorrhoea, continence preserved). Post-partum or post-epidural **retention** |
| **Gynaecological surgery** | **Bladder injury** (commonest organ; hysterectomy about 0.3–0.75%). **Ureteric injury** (hysterectomy 0.2–2.4%; over 90% distal third; ligation commonest). **Fistulae**: VVF (over 75% follow hysterectomy in the developed world), UVF (ureterovaginal fistula), urethrovaginal fistula |
| **Radical hysterectomy** | Pelvic plexus injury: retention, poor compliance; manage with CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) |
| **Prolapse and continence surgery** | Ureteric kinking at apical suspension; mesh or sling exposure, perforation, outlet obstruction, pain |
| **Radiotherapy** | Radiation cystitis, VVF, ureteric stricture |

- **Prevention:** know the four ureteric danger sites (know the danger sites); cystoscopy after (at) hysterectomy where indicated (recognise injury intraoperatively); prophylactic stents only selectively (selective prophylactic stents), only if high risk (they aid recognition but do not prevent injury).

### Part B: Ureterovaginal fistula (UVF)
**Definition**
- **UVF (uretero-vaginal fistula)**: abnormal communication between the ureter and vagina (ureter–vagina communication), causing continuous leakage.

**Aetiology / pathogenesis and presentation (brief)**
- Usually **unrecognised injury to the distal third of the ureter** (distal ureteric injury) during pelvic surgery. About **three-quarters follow an uneventful abdominal hysterectomy** (often uneventful; ureteric injury in 0.2–2.4%): an unrecognised distal ureteric injury (ligation, crush, laceration, thermal, devascularisation / ischaemia) necroses and drains (leaks) into the vaginal cuff, which is the weakest point. Diagnosis is delayed in 18–88%; **delayed detection (recognition) raises fistula risk about fourfold**.
- Others: obstetric, cancer, radiation, endometriosis, foreign body.
- **Sites of injury (at risk):** pelvic brim (near the infundibulopelvic ligament); **under the uterine artery** in the cardinal ligament; lateral to (beside) the uterosacral ligament; anterolateral vaginal fornix near the UVJ (ureterovesical junction). Most in the **distal 6 cm**.
- **Mechanisms:** by ligation, crush, laceration, thermal injury, **devascularisation** or ischaemia. The injured segment necroses and leaks into the vaginal cuff.
**Clinical features**
- **Continuous leakage with normal voiding** (the other kidney fills the bladder), usually (appears) **within 4 weeks**, often with fever, flank pain or ileus, as thermal or ischaemic injury necroses.

| | VVF (vesicovaginal fistula) | UVF |
|---|---|---|
| Voiding | Reduced | Normal |
| Upper tract | Normal | Hydronephrosis common |
| First line | Catheter, repair | Stent |

**Confirming the diagnosis before treatment (investigations)**
| Test | Method and reading |
|---|---|
| **Double dye test** | Oral phenazopyridine 100–200 mg (orange urine); after 1 hour, blue dye instilled into the bladder; tampon; walk about. **Orange = UVF**; blue = VVF; both = both |
| **Three-swab test** | Blue dye in the bladder; three swabs stacked in the vagina. Blue upper swab = VVF; **wet, unstained swab = UVF** |
| **CTU (computed tomography urography) / CT (computed tomography) urography, delayed phase** | Hydronephrosis, extravasation; MR (magnetic resonance) urography if contrast is contraindicated |
| **Cystoscopy + RGP (retrograde pyelogram)** | Abrupt cut-off or contrast leak; stent (stenting) at the same sitting |
- **Exclude a coexisting VVF** (up to 25% of VVF have a UVF) and bilateral injury.

### Treatment (management)
**Goals:** dryness and preservation of renal function. Management has moved from nephrectomy, through reimplantation, to endourology.

**1. Endourological (first line): stent first**
- **Retrograde double-J stent as soon as possible** (if a wire passes); otherwise PCN and antegrade stent. Stenting **within 2 weeks** of detection works best: succeeds far more often than at 2–6 weeks, because the ureter heals over the stent before fibrosis.
- Wire will not pass: **PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy)** with antegrade stent, or rendezvous.
- Stent for **4–6 weeks**, then remove and re-image. Most stentable fistulae close; a stricture may follow.

**2. Surgical repair (stent fails or impossible)**
- **Timing:** no consensus. Early repair equals delayed: early repair took less time and blood loss with equal success; a systematic review found equal success before and after 6 weeks. Delay only if tissue is still inflamed.
- **Ureteroneocystostomy** (distal third, so usually feasible):
  1. Transperitoneal or extraperitoneal approach; identify ureter above the fistula.
  2. Divide above the diseased segment; dissect as distally as possible for length; **preserve periureteric adventitia**, especially laterally where vessels enter.
  3. Mobilise the bladder; spatulate; tension-free mucosa-to-mucosa anastomosis over a double-J stent; refluxing or tunnelled.
  4. Drain; catheter 7–14 days.
- **Adding length:**

| Technique | Extra reach |
|---|---|
| **Psoas hitch** (sutures parallel to psoas fibres, spare genitofemoral nerve) | Up to about 5 cm |
| **Boari flap** (base ≥4 cm, length:base ≤3:1) | 10–15 cm (up to 15 cm) |
| Downward nephropexy; ileal ureter | Long (longer) defects / gaps |
- Open, **laparoscopic and robotic repair give equal results**; vaginal reimplantation reported. **Success ≥90% (90% or more).**
- The vaginal opening is left to heal; no separate vaginal repair is needed.

```
Suspected UVF / Leak + normal voiding -> exclude VVF (dye test, cystoscopy) -> CTU (CT urography) / RGP
  -> stent passes (wire passes)? Yes -> double-J 4-6 wk -> re-image -> healed / persistent
                   No  -> PCN + antegrade stent -> fails
  -> ureteroneocystostomy + psoas hitch / Boari flap
```

### Complications and follow-up
- Hydronephrosis, infection, ischaemic stricture.
- Follow-up ultrasound 1–2 weeks after stent removal; some add a renal scan at 6 weeks. Stricture usually appears within a year but may appear **up to 30 months** later, so follow beyond this.

**Diagram to draw:** ureter passing under the uterine artery; double dye test; ureteroneocystostomy with psoas hitch.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bladder is the commonest organ injured; obstructed labour causes most fistulae worldwide.
> - Distal ureteric injury at hysterectomy; leak within 4 weeks, normal voiding (continuous leak with normal voiding).
> - UVF: orange tampon = UVF on double dye test; exclude coexisting VVF.
> - Stent early (within 2 weeks); remove at 4–6 weeks.
> - Reimplant with psoas hitch or Boari flap if it fails; success ≥90%; follow 30 months.

## Female pelvic floor, continence mechanism, classification and management of SUI
**Asked as:**
- Discuss continence mechanism in females . classify SUI and principles of different surgical options — *Summer 2017 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe anatomy of pelvic floor in females and describe surgeries for SUI in females. — *Winter 2017 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)*
- Management of stress urinary incontinence. — *Winter 2024 Paper III Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

### Introduction (definition and mechanism)
- **SUI (stress urinary incontinence)**: involuntary leakage on effort, coughing or sneezing (ICS, International Continence Society).
- Continence needs **urethral pressure greater than bladder pressure** at rest and during stress.
- Support comes from the **levator ani, endopelvic fascia and ligaments, perineal membrane and perineal body**.
- Two mechanisms: **urethral hypermobility** (loss of the suburethral "hammock") and **ISD (intrinsic sphincter deficiency)**.

### A. Anatomy of the female pelvic floor
**1. Pelvic diaphragm**
| Muscle | Attachment | Function |
|---|---|---|
| Puborectalis | Back of pubis, loops behind anorectal junction | Anorectal angle |
| Pubococcygeus (pubovaginal) | Pubis, ATFP (arcus tendineus fasciae pelvis) → viscera, raphe | Slings urethra and vagina |
| Iliococcygeus | Arcus tendineus levator ani, ischial spine → coccyx | Horizontal **levator plate** |
| Coccygeus (not levator) | Ischial spine → sacrum, coccyx | Lies on sacrospinous ligament |
- Nerve to levator ani **(S3–5)** on its pelvic surface. Resting tone closes the **urogenital hiatus**; widening of the hiatus or tilting of the plate causes prolapse. Muscle fails first, fascia then carries the load.

**2. Perineal membrane and perineal body**
- The perineal membrane spans the pubic arch; urethra and vagina pierce it. Above it lies the **striated urogenital sphincter**: sphincter urethrae (mid-urethra), **compressor urethrae** and **urethrovaginal sphincter**. The perineal body anchors the distal vagina.

**3. Endopelvic fascia and ligaments**
- **Pubocervical fascia** attaches laterally to the **ATFP** (pubis to ischial spine); **rectovaginal fascia** posteriorly; **cardinal–uterosacral complex** at the apex; **pubourethral ligaments** anchor the mid-urethra.

**4. DeLancey levels (1992)**
| Level | Structures | Defect |
|---|---|---|
| I suspension | Cardinal–uterosacral | Apical prolapse, enterocele |
| II attachment | ATFP, levator fascia | Cystocele (central or paravaginal), rectocele |
| III fusion | Perineal membrane, perineal body | **Urethral hypermobility**, perineal descent |

### B. Continence mechanism in the female
**1. Intrinsic urethral factors** (smooth and striated sphincters, vascular submucosa)
- **Mucosal seal**: folded urothelium coapts. The **oestrogen-sensitive (oestrogen-dependent) submucosal vascular plexus** (oestrogen-dependent vascular submucosa) provides up to **30%** of closure force (much of closure pressure).
- **Smooth muscle** (internal sphincter, bladder neck and proximal urethra): involuntary tone. The female bladder neck is weak and often open in continent women.
- **Striated urogenital sphincter**: the rhabdosphincter, mostly **slow-twitch** fibres, gives tonic mid-urethral closure. Distal parts are the **compressor urethrae** and **urethrovaginal sphincter**.
- The **continence zone is the proximal and mid-urethra.**

**2. Support (extrinsic)**
- The urethra lies on the **anterior vaginal wall and endopelvic fascia**, attached laterally to the **ATFP (arcus tendineus fasciae pelvis)** and levator ani.
- **Pubourethral ligaments** anchor the mid-urethra; **levator ani** (pubococcygeus) tone keeps the backing firm.

**3. Neural**
- **Pudendal nerve (S2–4)** drives the sphincter (**guarding reflex**). Urethral pressure rises **just before** a cough reaches it, so closure is active, not just transmitted.

**4. Theories of continence and SUI**
| Theory | Mechanism | Surgical implication (answer) |
|---|---|---|
| **Pressure transmission (Enhorning 1961)** | Hypermobile urethra drops out of (leaves) the abdominal pressure zone | Restore position (Burch) |
| **Hammock (DeLancey 1994)** | Abdominal pressure compresses the urethra against a firm suburethral layer (urethra compressed against it); a lax layer fails | Restore backing |
| **Integral theory (Petros & Ulmsten 1990)** | Pubourethral ligament laxity at the mid-urethra | Tension-free MUS (mid-urethral sling) |
| **ISD (intrinsic sphincter deficiency; McGuire & Lytton 1978)** | Urethra itself fails to close (VLPP (Valsalva leak point pressure) <60, MUCP (maximum urethral closure pressure) <20 cmH₂O) | Sling, bulking, sphincter (AUS (artificial urinary sphincter)) |
- Most SUI has some ISD. **Hypermobility alone never causes SUI.**

### C. Classification of SUI
**1. Blaivas & Olsson (1988), on video-urodynamics**
| Type | Bladder neck at rest | On stress |
|---|---|---|
| **0** | Closed, above lower border of symphysis | Descends and opens, but no leak seen (history of SUI) |
| **I** | Closed, above symphysis | Descends **<2 cm**; leaks; little or no cystocele |
| **IIA** | Closed, above symphysis | Descends **>2 cm** with cystourethrocele; leaks |
| **IIB** | Closed but **at or below** lower border of symphysis | Proximal urethra opens; leaks with or without further descent |
| **III** | **Open at rest** without detrusor contraction | Leaks with minimal stress = **ISD** |

**2. Functional: hypermobility versus ISD**
| | Urethral hypermobility | ISD |
|---|---|---|
| Q-tip test | Angle change **>30°** | Often fixed urethra |
| VLPP (Valsalva leak point pressure) | **≥90 cmH₂O** | **<60 cmH₂O** (60–90 equivocal) |
| MUCP (maximum urethral closure pressure) | Normal | **≤20 cmH₂O** |
| Cause | Childbirth, ageing, obesity | Previous surgery, radiation, neuropathy, ageing |

**3. Other**
- **Severity (Stamey):** grade 1 leak on cough or sneeze; grade 2 on minimal effort (walking, rising); grade 3 at rest.
- **Index (uncomplicated)** versus **complicated** SUI (recurrent, prolapse, neurogenic, high PVR (post-void residual), radiation); pure versus mixed incontinence.

### D. Evaluation (before surgery)
- Bladder diary, pad test, **cough stress test**, PVR (post-void residual), urinalysis; Q-tip >30° = hypermobility.
- **Urodynamics not needed** in the uncomplicated (index) patient (VALUE trial); use if SUI not demonstrated, or complicated: mixed, recurrent, neurogenic or with prolapse.

### E. Conservative (first line)
- **PFMT (pelvic floor muscle training)**, weight loss, pessary first.
- Weight loss, fluid and caffeine adjustment.
- **Supervised PFMT** for at least 3 months (Cochrane: more cure than no treatment).
- **Pessary**; vaginal oestrogen for atrophy.
- **Duloxetine** (serotonin–noradrenaline reuptake inhibitor): increases Onuf's nucleus drive to the sphincter; approved in Europe, not USA.

### F. Principles of surgical options (surgeries for female SUI)
| Operation | Principle (how it works) | Best for | Key facts |
|---|---|---|---|
| **MUS (mid-urethral sling)**: retropubic (TVT, tension-free vaginal tape, Ulmsten 1995), transobturator (TOT; Delorme 2001; inside-out de Leval), single-incision | Loose (tension-free) type I polypropylene tape under the **mid-urethra** acts as a new pubourethral ligament; urethra kinks against it on (during) stress | First-line index SUI | **First line** (AUA/SUFU (American Urological Association/Society of Urodynamics, Female Pelvic Medicine and Urogenital Reconstruction) strong recommendation). **TOMUS**: retropubic 80.8% vs transobturator 77.7% cure (equal, equivalent); retropubic often preferred in ISD. Retropubic: more bladder perforation; transobturator: more groin pain |
| **SIMS (single-incision mini-sling)** | Short tape, vaginal only | Option | Non-inferior to transobturator (TOT) for subjective cure (in most studies); conditional recommendation (2023) |
| **Autologous pubovaginal sling (PVS)** (rectus fascia or fascia lata) | At the **bladder neck**, minimal tension; restores the hammock and compresses (bladder-neck support and compression) | **All types, including ISD**, recurrent SUI, neurogenic SUI, failed MUS, after mesh removal, mesh-averse | Gold standard. **SISTEr**: sling beat Burch (47% vs 38%) but more voiding dysfunction and UTI (urinary tract infection) |
| **Burch colposuspension** (open, laparoscopic or robotic) | Paravaginal fascia beside the bladder neck (at mid-urethra and bladder neck) sutured to **Cooper's ligament**; lifts the bladder neck back into the pressure zone (restores bladder neck position) and gives a stable backing | Hypermobility; women declining mesh; at abdominal surgery | 85–90% at 1 year, about 70% at 5 years. Needs a mobile urethra; poor in ISD |
| Paravaginal repair, VOS (vagino-obturator shelf) | Vagina to ATFP or obturator fascia | | Flatter, less obstructive |
| **MMK (Marshall–Marchetti–Krantz)** | Bladder neck to pubic (symphysis) periosteum | Historical | Osteitis pubis 0.7–2.5%; abandoned |
| **(Urethral) bulking agents** | Submucosal injection at bladder neck or proximal urethra improves coaptation | ISD with fixed urethra; frail (patients); minimal surgery | Low durability (less durable); repeat injections |
| **AUS (artificial urinary sphincter)** | Cuff round the bladder neck (bladder-neck cuff); constant circumferential closure | Severe recurrent (refractory) ISD, fixed urethra | Second-line; erosion, infection, revision |
- **Also know:** needle suspensions abandoned (sutures pull through; pull-through); anterior colporrhaphy is not an SUI operation; mesh complications (exposure, erosion into bladder or urethra, pain) need excision; FDA (Food and Drug Administration) 2019 stopped transvaginal mesh for prolapse, not slings.

**Burch steps:** Pfannenstiel → space of Retzius → stay lateral to urethra → 2–4 sutures each side (mid-urethra, then bladder neck) → Cooper's ligament → tie loosely (two fingers behind pubis) → **cystoscopy** for ureteric efflux.

**Retropubic vs transobturator MUS**
| | Retropubic | Transobturator |
|---|---|---|
| Bladder perforation | 4.5% | 0.6% |
| Voiding dysfunction | More (TOMUS surgery 2.7% vs 0%) | Less |
| Groin pain | 1.3% | 6.4% |
| ISD, long-term durability | Often preferred | Possibly more repeat surgery |

### G. Complications
- **MUS complications:** perforation, retention, de novo urgency, mesh exposure (~2%), pain.
- Bladder trocar injury: re-pass and continue (cystoscopy after every pass); **urethral injury: abandon mesh**.
- Voiding dysfunction (sling too tight or proximal) → urethrolysis or sling incision; de novo urgency; UTI; haematoma.
- **Mesh complications** (IUGA/ICS (International Urogynecological Association/International Continence Society) terms): exposure, extrusion, perforation into bladder or urethra, pain → excision. FDA (Food and Drug Administration) 2019 stopped transvaginal mesh for prolapse, not slings.
- **Also know:** CARE (prophylactic Burch at sacrocolpopexy); Ward–Hilton (TVT = colposuspension).

### H. Male SUI (brief)
- Usually after radical prostatectomy. PFMT first; no surgery for 6–12 months, as continence keeps improving.
- **AUS** for moderate–severe or irradiated men (gold standard, ~80% continent); **male sling** for mild–moderate leakage without radiotherapy. Bulking agents not advised.

**Diagram to draw:** DeLancey levels and hammock cross-section; sites of Burch sutures, bladder-neck sling and mid-urethral tape; sagittal pelvis showing retropubic and transobturator tape paths and Burch sutures.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Levator ani = puborectalis, pubococcygeus, iliococcygeus; S3–5.
> - DeLancey: I suspension, II attachment (ATFP), III fusion.
> - Continence zone is the mid-urethra; submucosal plexus gives up to 30% of closure.
> - Blaivas types 0, I, IIA, IIB, III; type III = ISD (VLPP <60, MUCP ≤20 cmH₂O).
> - Burch corrects position; slings and AUS correct function.
> - VALUE: no urodynamics in index SUI.
> - PFMT first; MUS first-line surgery.
> - SISTEr (fascial sling > Burch); TOMUS (retropubic = transobturator).
> - PVS for ISD; urethral injury → no mesh.
> - Men: AUS gold standard; sling for mild, non-irradiated.

## SUI: evaluation in females, role of UDS, and surgical therapy in males
**Asked as:**
- Describe evaluation of SUI in females. Discuss surgical therapy of SUI in Males. — *Summer 2019 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)*
- Role of UDS in SUI — *Practice Set 1 Paper III Q3b (SAQ)* (SUI evaluation also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 3, Q2)

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

### Part A: Evaluation of SUI (stress urinary incontinence) in women
- **Definition (ICS [International Continence Society]):** involuntary leakage on effort, exertion, sneezing or coughing.
- **Mechanisms:** urethral **hypermobility** (loss of suburethral support, DeLancey hammock) and **ISD (intrinsic sphincter deficiency)**. Most women have some ISD.

**History**
- Leakage with cough or activity means SUI; after urgency, UUI (urgency urinary incontinence); both, MUI (mixed). A positive SUI history predicts urodynamic SUI in about three-quarters.
- Severity (pads/day), bother; voiding symptoms, prolapse, obstetric history, prior surgery or radiation, neurological disease, drugs.
- **Questionnaires:** ICIQ-UI SF (International Consultation on Incontinence Questionnaire–UI Short Form); UDI-6 (Urogenital Distress Inventory: bother); IIQ-7 (Incontinence Impact Questionnaire: impact). **Bladder diary** (3 days).

**Examination (comfortably full bladder)**
- Neurological (S2–S4 sensation, anal tone, bulbocavernosus reflex); atrophy; prolapse by **POP-Q (Pelvic Organ Prolapse Quantification)**.
- **Cough stress test:** cough supine, then standing if negative. Leakage synchronous with cough confirms SUI. Reduce prolapse to unmask **occult SUI**.
- **Q-tip test:** swab to bladder neck, Valsalva; rotation **>30°** = hypermobility. Visual mobility examination is now routine.

**Tests**
| Test | Method and use |
|---|---|
| Urinalysis | Required; exclude infection, haematuria |
| PVR (post-void residual) | Required before surgery; ultrasound; repeat if high (no agreed cut-off) |
| 1-hour pad test (ICS) | 500 mL fluid, standard exercises; >1.4 g positive; 1–10 g mild, 11–50 g moderate, >50 g severe |
| 24-hour pad test | Normal activities; more reproducible |
| Cystoscopy | Haematuria, irritative symptoms, previous mesh |

### Role of urodynamics (UDS) in SUI
- **Introduction:** UDS (urodynamic studies) assess storage and voiding. In SUI the key question is: **will the result change the operation?** Pure, demonstrable SUI gains little. **Urodynamics (UDS): not routine.**

**What UDS shows in SUI**
| Parameter | Method and meaning |
|---|---|
| USI (urodynamic stress incontinence) | Leak with raised abdominal pressure, no detrusor contraction |
| ALPP/VLPP (abdominal/Valsalva leak point pressure) | Standing, from 150 mL, Valsalva or cough; **<60 cmH2O = ISD (intrinsic sphincter deficiency)**, 60–90 equivocal, ≥90 = little ISD (McGuire 1993) |
| MUCP (maximum urethral closure pressure) | Urethral pressure profile; a low MUCP (<20 / ≤20 cmH2O) also suggests ISD; does not diagnose or grade SUI |
| Filling cystometry | DO (detrusor overactivity), compliance, capacity; cough-induced DO mimics SUI |
| Pressure–flow study | Voiding dysfunction, poor contractility (retention risk after sling) |
| Prolapse reduction | Occult SUI |
- If no leak with the catheter, repeat with it removed.

**Evidence**
- **VALUE / ValUE trial (2012), 630 women** with uncomplicated SUI: office evaluation alone was **non-inferior** to office evaluation plus UDS before surgery (success 77.2% vs 76.9%).
- **Dutch VUE study:** immediate midurethral sling was non-inferior to UDS-tailored treatment.
- **SISTEr:** UDS did not predict outcome.

**Guidelines**
- **AUA [American Urological Association]/SUFU [Society of Urodynamics, Female Pelvic Medicine and Urogenital Reconstruction] 2023:** UDS may be omitted in the index patient with demonstrable SUI.
- **Indications / UDS advised in non-index patients (AUA/SUFU 2023):** unclear diagnosis or SUI not shown (not demonstrated); mismatch between symptoms and findings; urgency-predominant MUI (mixed incontinence); neurogenic bladder; high (raised) PVR or voiding dysfunction; prolapse stage ≥3 without demonstrable SUI; failed previous surgery (previous anti-incontinence or prolapse surgery).
- **EAU (European Association of Urology):** no routine UDS for uncomplicated SUI; do not use leak point or urethral pressure to grade severity.

**Men**
- Before AUS (artificial urinary sphincter) or sling for post-prostatectomy incontinence: confirms USI, finds DO, poor compliance or weak contractility. MASTER trial recruited men with USI.

### Part B: Surgical therapy of male SUI
- **Aetiology:** commonest after **radical prostatectomy (RP)**, which removes the internal sphincter, leaving continence to the rhabdosphincter. Also TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate), radiation, pelvic fracture.
- **Work-up:** 24-h pad weight (mild <150 g, moderate <400 g, severe above); standing cough test; **cystoscopy** (anastomotic stricture, sphincter coaptation); UDS if mixed or poor contractility.
- **Timing:** PFMT (pelvic floor muscle training) first; continence improves for up to 2 years, but do not delay surgery beyond 6 months for severe leak.

**Devices**
| Device | Mechanism | Use and results |
|---|---|---|
| **Transobturator sling (AdVance XP)** | Repositions the bulb 3–4 cm proximally; needs residual sphincter | Mild–moderate, no radiation; cure about 60% |
| Quadratic sling (Virtue) | Compression plus elevation | Mild; limited data |
| Adjustable sling (ATOMS, adjustable transobturator male system) | Cushion adjusted via scrotal port | Mild–moderate; about two-thirds dry |
| ProACT balloons | Bladder-neck compression | More erosion |
| Bulking agents | Submucosal bulk | Small leaks, unfit men; rarely durable |
| **AUS (artificial urinary sphincter)** | Circumferential compression | **Gold standard**; all severities, after radiation |

**AMS 800 AUS**
- **Components:** urethral **cuff** (bulbar, usually 4.0–4.5 cm), **PRB (pressure-regulating balloon) 61–70 cmH2O** (51–60 high risk; 71–80 bladder neck) in a preperitoneal pocket, scrotal **pump**. Filled with 23 mL.
- **Technique:** perineal incision; cuff on proximal bulbar urethra; **deactivate for 4–6 weeks**.
- **Needs** dexterity and cognition. Social continence (0–1 pad) 55–77%; satisfaction about 90%; lifespan 7–10 years.
- **Complications:** infection 1–3% (explant; reimplant after 3–6 months); **erosion** 2–15% (explant, catheter about 3 weeks); urethral **atrophy** (late recurrent leak); mechanical failure; retention.
- **Erosion risks:** radiation, prior erosion, urethroplasty; use **transcorporal cuff** (tunica adds bulk) or lower PRB. Atrophy: smaller or tandem cuff.

**Algorithm (AUA/SUFU 2019)**
```
Severe SUI or any radiotherapy -> AUS
Moderate, no radiotherapy      -> AUS or male sling
Mild, no radiotherapy          -> sling (or PFMT, AUS)
Sling failure                  -> AUS
```
- **MASTER RCT (randomised controlled trial):** sling was **non-inferior** to AUS for continence at 12 months; AUS remains preferred for heavy leak (>250 g/day) or radiation.

**Diagram to draw:** AMS 800 (cuff, pump, PRB) and transobturator sling path; cystometrogram trace with abdominal pressure spike and leak (ALPP).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Q-tip >30° = hypermobility; VLPP / ALPP <60 cmH2O = ISD; MUCP ≤20 cmH2O.
> - VALUE (ValUE) trial: UDS not needed in uncomplicated SUI.
> - Use UDS when it changes treatment: mixed, failed surgery, voiding dysfunction.
> - AUS PRB 61–70 cmH2O; deactivate 4–6 weeks.
> - Radiation or severe SUI: AUS first; MASTER: sling non-inferior.

## Recto-urethral (recto-prostatic) fistula: etiopathogenesis, diagnosis and management
**Asked as:**
- Etio-pathogenesis, diagnosis and management of Recto-urethral fistula? — *Summer 2020 Paper III Q1 (LAQ)*
- Etiopathogenesis, diagnosis and management of Recto-Prostatic fistula — *Winter 2025 Paper III Q1 (LAQ)* (also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 3, Q1, as recto-urethral fistula)

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)

*Assumed meaning (recto-prostatic): rectourethral fistula at the level of the prostatic urethra or the vesicourethral anastomosis.*

### Definition
- **RUF (rectourethral fistula):** a communication between the posterior urethra / prostatic urethra (or the vesicourethral anastomosis after prostatectomy) and the distal rectum.
- Rare but very morbid. Almost never seen in females, because the vagina lies between urethra and rectum.

### Aetiology
| Group | Causes |
|---|---|
| Congenital | Anorectal malformation in boys (rectobulbar or rectoprostatic fistula) |
| Surgical (commonest acquired) | **RP (radical prostatectomy)**; historically TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate) and open (simple) prostatectomy |
| Radiation and energy | **EBRT (external beam radiotherapy) plus brachytherapy** (commonest), then brachytherapy alone, then pelvic radiotherapy; cryotherapy; HIFU (high-intensity focused ultrasound): low after primary, up to about 9% after salvage HIFU |
| Others | Trauma (penetrating injury, pelvic fracture); Crohn disease; advanced rectal or prostate cancer |

### Pathogenesis
- **Surgical:** the rectum is torn during apical or posterior dissection behind Denonvilliers fascia, especially with small glands and salvage surgery. An unrecognised tear (near the apex) leaks urine (urine leaks into it), an abscess forms and the tract epithelialises.
  - Rectal injury at RP: laparoscopic about 1%, open and robotic under 0.5%, salvage RP about 7%. (Another source: rectal injury is commonest with retropubic and laparoscopic RP, lowest with robotic RP, and was over 15% in historical open salvage series.)
- **Radiation or ablation:** obliterative endarteritis makes the tissue hypoxic and fibrotic. Necrosis follows months to years later. These fistulae are large and heal poorly.
- **Congenital:** abnormal cloacal development leaves the rectum ending in the posterior urethra.

### Classification: Martini (after prostate cancer treatment)
| Axis | Category | Definition |
|---|---|---|
| Stage (size on CT (computed tomography) urography or cystography) | I / II / III | ≤1.5 cm / >1.5 cm / any size with sphincter damage |
| Grade (tissue) | 0 | Surgical injury, no prior non-surgical treatment |
| | 1 | Prior radiation, brachytherapy, HIFU or other energy |
| | 2 | Salvage RP or salvage ablation |
- **Tissue quality (grade), not size, chiefly decides the repair.**

### Clinical features
- **Faecaluria and pneumaturia** (most typical); urine per rectum on voiding.
- Recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection), dysuria, urosepsis; coexisting SUI (stress urinary incontinence).

### Diagnosis
- **DRE (digital rectal examination):** may feel the defect on the anterior rectal wall.
- **Cystoscopy and proctosigmoidoscopy:** show the tract and tissue vitality; **biopsy** to exclude recurrent cancer.
- **VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) or RUG (retrograde urethrogram):** usually conclusive; gives size and site for planning.
- Upper-tract imaging to exclude ureteric injury; pelvic MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) for complex cases.
- **UDS (urodynamics)** with rectal packing: fistula repair alone will not cure severe SUI; an AUS (artificial urinary sphincter) may be needed later; a later AUS erodes more often.

### Prevention
- At RP: a recognised tear without gross spillage → two-layer (multilayer) closure, air test, **omental or peritoneal interposition**, drain, antibiotics; colostomy if (previously) irradiated.
- Radiotherapy: a **hydrogel rectal spacer** lowers rectal dose and late rectal toxicity (randomised trial: late rectal toxicity 2.0% vs 7.0%).

### Management
- **Principles:** control sepsis; divert urine ± faeces; let inflammation settle (about 12 weeks); exclude cancer; then excise the tract and close urethra and rectum separately in **tension-free, non-overlapping layers with vascularised (interposition) tissue interposed**.
- **Conservative:** no sepsis and no faecaluria → catheter (urethral or suprapubic), low-residue diet ± colostomy. A simple fistula may close in about 12 weeks; irradiated or complex ones rarely do.
- **Surgery:** no single accepted operation; choice depends on site, radiation and prior repair. **A diverting colostomy usually comes first.**

| Approach | Essentials | Strengths | Weaknesses |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Perineal** (Young; Goodwin) | Separate and close urethra and rectum separately; **gracilis flap** between (or dartos; buccal graft) | High closure; most stomas reversed | Urinary incontinence common |
| **Transsphincteric (York-Mason)** | Prone jackknife; sphincter divided in tagged layers; through anterior rectal wall; three-layer closure | Avoids scarred plane; spares sexual function | Poor membranous exposure; no flap; faecal incontinence up to 20% |
| **Abdominal** (transvesical, extravesical, O'Conor-like) / robotic | Omental flap | Large or irradiated fistulae, extensive resection | Morbid; poor deep-pelvic view; small robotic series |
| **Laparoscopic / robotic** | Open pouch of Douglas; close rectum in two layers; omental or peritoneal flap; can combine with perineal gracilis | Deep-pelvic vision | Small series only |
| **Salvage** | Cystoprostatectomy, ileal conduit ± permanent colostomy | Unreconstructable irradiated field | Major surgery |

```
RUF → sepsis control, catheter ± colostomy, biopsy
  small, non-irradiated, no faecaluria → conservative ~12 weeks
  persistent, grade 0 → perineal (gracilis) or York-Mason
  irradiated or large → abdominal/robotic (omentum) or perineal (gracilis)
  unreconstructable → urinary + faecal diversion
Congenital → newborn colostomy → posterior sagittal anorectoplasty
```
- **PSARP (posterior sagittal anorectoplasty)** separates the rectum from the urethra and places it within (in) the sphincter complex.

### Complications
- **Recurrence** (the greatest challenge), urinary or faecal incontinence, urethral stricture, ED (erectile dysfunction), wound infection, permanent stoma.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal pelvis showing (with) the fistula and a gracilis flap between urethra and rectum.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Most acquired RUFs follow prostate cancer treatment.
> - Martini: stage = size; grade = tissue (surgery, radiation, salvage).
> - VCUG or RUG plus endoscopic biopsy to exclude cancer.
> - Divert first; perineal (gracilis) or York-Mason for surgical fistulae; irradiated ones need imported tissue or diversion.

## Urological complications of OBGY surgery and urethrovaginal fistula
**Asked as:**
- Describe in brief urological complications of OBGY surgery. Describe in detail, management of urethrovaginal fistula — *Practice Set 3 Paper III Q1 (LAQ)*
- Urethrovaginal fistula — *Winter 2025 Paper III Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

(urethrovaginal fistula also answered in 2025-Winter Paper 3, Q3a; iatrogenic ureteric injury in Practice Set 2 Paper 3, Q1)

*Assumed meaning: OBGY = obstetric and gynaecological surgery.*

### Part A: Urological complications of OBGY surgery
- The bladder, distal ureter and urethra lie against the cervix, uterine vessels and anterior vaginal wall. Risk rises with distorted anatomy: fibroids, endometriosis, cancer, previous caesarean, radiation, heavy bleeding, prolapse or mesh surgery.

| Complication | Key facts |
|---|---|
| **Bladder injury** (commonest organ injured) | Caesarean 0.27–0.3%; hysterectomy about 0.3–0.75% (laparoscopic highest); retropubic tape 3–9%. Repair: debride, 2-layer closure, fill test, catheter 7–14 days; check ureters if trigonal |
| **Ureteric injury** | Gynaecology causes over half of iatrogenic injuries; over 90% distal third. Hysterectomy: vaginal 0.02–0.5%, abdominal 0.03–2%, minimally invasive 0.13–6%; prolapse surgery 1.7–4.3%. **Ligation** is the commonest gynaecological mechanism |
| **Fistulae** | VVF (vesicovaginal fistula), about 0.8 per 1000 hysterectomies; UVF (ureterovaginal fistula); vesicouterine fistula after caesarean (Youssef syndrome); urethrovaginal fistula |
