| **Voiding dysfunction** | Radical hysterectomy (pelvic plexus injury) 16–80% historically: retention, poor compliance; CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) and wait. Post-partum or post-epidural overdistension |
| **Mesh and sling** | Exposure, erosion into bladder or urethra, outlet obstruction, pain, dyspareunia |
| **Radiation** | Radiation cystitis; VVF in 0.6–2% after cervical cancer radiotherapy; ureteric stricture |

- **Ureteric danger sites:** pelvic brim (infundibulopelvic ligament), under the uterine artery, beside the uterosacral ligament, anterolateral vaginal fornix.
- **Prevention and recognition:** haemostasis and anatomy; prophylactic stents only selectively (they aid recognition but do not prevent injury); cystoscopy with dye (IV indigo carmine or oral phenazopyridine) after minimally invasive hysterectomy and prolapse or continence surgery.
- Only about one-third of ureteric injuries are recognised at open surgery. Missed injury presents with fever, ileus, flank pain, urinoma or leakage; do CTU (computed tomography urography) with delayed films, then retrograde stenting. Distal repair: ureteroneocystostomy ± psoas hitch or Boari flap (see Q3d).

### Part B: Urethrovaginal fistula
**Aetiology**
- Developing world: **obstructed labour**, often with a VVF (vesicovaginal fistula); also trauma, radiation, catheter injury.
- Developed world (iatrogenic): **urethral diverticulectomy** (fistula in 1–8%; causes 20–25% of urethrovaginal fistulae (UVFs)), slings, bulking agents, anterior repair or mesh, indwelling catheters (catheter pressure necrosis), pessaries, radiation, trauma; also gender-affirming and paediatric genital surgery.

**Clinical features**
- Depend on position (site) relative to the sphincter. **Distal** fistula: often asymptomatic, split stream, vaginal voiding, post-void dribble. **Proximal** (bladder neck): continuous leakage or SUI (stress urinary incontinence).
- Dyspareunia, recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection), perineal irritation; partner discomfort with exposed mesh.

**Evaluation (diagnosis)**
- **Speculum examination** usually suffices; **methylene blue test** if rugae hide it (distal stain on tampon). Assess prolapse, SUI, atrophy.
- **VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram), lateral view;** VUDS (videourodynamics) for coexisting (associated) SUI/incontinence or detrusor overactivity.
- **Cystourethroscopy** (flexible is better, outflow at the lens; flexible scope distends the urethra better): size, site, bladder neck, exposed mesh, other fistulae. **About 20% have a separate VVF.** Image the upper tract.
- Goh location/classification (distance from external meatus): type 1 >3.5 cm, 2: 2.5–3.5, 3: 1.5–2.5, 4: <1.5 cm from meatus. Closer means worse closure and continence.
- Optimise: treat UTI, vaginal oestrogen, nutrition; biopsy if previous cancer.

### Management
- Asymptomatic distal fistula: may need no repair. Timing: early if tissue healthy; otherwise after inflammation settles.
- **Standard: transvaginal rotational-flap repair:**

| Step | Details |
|---|---|
| 1. Exposure | Lithotomy (prone gives better anterior view/exposure); cystourethroscopy, cannulate tract; suprapubic catheter (Lowsley tractor), urethral catheter (catheter ± suprapubic) |
| 2. Incision | Hydrodissect; circumscribe tract with 1–2 mm cuff, minimal excision; inverted **J or U** (J- or U-shaped vaginal flap) incision |
| 3. Flaps | Raise vaginal flap; mobilise periurethral tissue widely to bladder neck; open retropubic space if sling planned |
| 4. Closure | **Close urethra** over a urethral sound: **4-0 absorbable** sutures, transverse or longitudinal; **second layer perpendicular**; buccal graft or flap onlay for large defects; leak test |
| 5. Interposition | **Martius flap** (labial fat pad, external pudendal pedicle) most often; omentum, gracilis, rectus fascia occasionally |
| 6. Cover | Advance vaginal flap beyond closure (2-0) |

- **Autologous pubovaginal sling** for (if) SUI or bladder-neck involvement; **never synthetic** (erodes ischaemic urethra). Remove all (any) mesh in the field.
- **Near-total urethral loss:** tubularised anterior bladder flap neourethra. Irreparable cases: diversion.
- **After repair:** urethral catheter out at 2–3 days; suprapubic catheter ≥14 days; lateral VCUG before removal; nothing per vagina for 6 weeks.

### Outcomes and complications
- Closure **90–95%**, but continence only **50–80%**; postoperative SUI in up to half. Sling then or staged later.
- Urethral stricture (may follow), recurrence, dyspareunia.

**Diagram to draw:** inverted-U vaginal flap, two perpendicular urethral layers (layered closure), Martius flap.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bladder is the commonest organ injured; ureteric injury is mostly distal and missed.
> - Diverticulectomy is the commonest Western cause; obstetric in developing countries.
> - Look for a coexisting VVF (about 20%).
> - Layered closure plus Martius flap; autologous sling only.
> - Closure 90–95% (closure is easy); continence only 50–80% (continence is hard).

## Vesicovaginal fistula (VVF): management options and early vs delayed repair
**Asked as:**
- Discuss management options of VVF — *Practice Set 3 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)*
- Early vs. delayed repair in VVF — *Practice Set 5 Paper III Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(timing in 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q3b; recurrent VVF in 2025-Winter Paper 2, Q3f; early vs delayed repair also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q3b)

### Introduction
- VVF (vesicovaginal fistula): an epithelialised bladder–vagina tract causing continuous leakage; commonest acquired urinary fistula (>75% of genitourinary fistulae).
- **Developing world (low-resource settings):** obstetric (obstructed labour) (>90%); fetal head impacted for days causes pressure necrosis. **Developed world (high-resource settings):** iatrogenic (>75% after hysterectomy; mainly hysterectomy), usually supratrigonal at the vaginal cuff. Also radiation, malignancy, mesh, foreign body.
- Mechanism (common mechanism): **ischaemic necrosis**; continuous urine flow keeps the tract open until it epithelialises.

### Classification
| Goh (distance from meatus) | Size | Tissue |
|---|---|---|
| 1: >3.5 cm; 2: 2.5–3.5; 3: 1.5–2.5; 4: <1.5 cm | A <1.5 cm; B 1.5–3 cm; C >3 cm | i mild fibrosis; ii moderate/severe; iii radiation, ureteric, circumferential, previous repair |
- **Also know:** Waaldijk (closing mechanism involvement); WHO (World Health Organization) simple vs complex.

### Evaluation (guides choice)
- Speculum; fluid creatinine. **Dye tests:** single (blue backfill, tampon); **double** (oral phenazopyridine plus blue): orange = UVF (ureterovaginal fistula), blue = VVF.
- **Cystoscopy and vaginoscopy:** number, size, ureteric orifices, bladder neck, mesh; **biopsy if previous cancer.**
- **Upper tract (CTU, computed tomography urography) is mandatory:** coexisting ureteric fistula (up to 12–25%).
- Urodynamics: capacity, compliance, SUI (stress urinary incontinence) in 10–15%.
- Prepare: vaginal oestrogen for atrophy; counsel about residual incontinence, dyspareunia and possible reoperation.

### Principles
- Optimise nutrition, treat infection, drain continuously, relieve obstruction, exclude malignancy.
- Surgery: tension-free, multilayer, **non-overlapping**, watertight closure; remove foreign material; consider interposition; drain afterwards. **The first repair has the best chance.**
- At repair: treat infection; ureteric stents if within 1 cm of a ureter; vaginal or abdominal route (open, laparoscopic, robotic); **interposition flap** (Martius, omentum, peritoneum) if irradiated or recurrent; catheter drainage for about 2 weeks.

### Option 1: Conservative and endoscopic (conservative trial while waiting)
- **Catheter drainage** cures about 15%. Favourable (best if): small, <1 cm (best <3 mm), fresh, started (catheterised) within **3 weeks**, non-irradiated. Unlikely after 6 weeks (once epithelialised, ≥6 weeks, closure is unlikely). EAU (European Association of Urology): if tried, ≥12 weeks (at least 12 weeks).
- **Fulguration** plus 2 weeks' drainage for tiny tracts <3.5 mm.
- **Sealants** (fibrin glue, cyanoacrylate, collagen): tiny series; not recommended by EAU.

### Timing: early vs delayed repair (the debate)
| | Early repair | Delayed repair |
|---|---|---|
| Definition (varies) | Days, <6 weeks or <3 months | Traditionally ≥3 months |
| Rationale | Clean iatrogenic tissue heals well; ends wetness and distress early | Inflammation settles, necrosis sloughs, margins become clear |
| Evidence | **Comparable closure** in most series; Waaldijk: immediate obstetric repair gave very high first-attempt closure | One small series: more recurrence when repaired <6 weeks |
| Drawback | Friable tissue if inflamed | Prolonged distress, no better outcome |

- Some authors avoid the **4–12 week** window (active healing).
- **EAU (European Association of Urology) consensus:** no minimum delay if there is **no active infection and no recent irradiation (radiation)**.
- **Timing by cause:**
  - **Clean iatrogenic / post-hysterectomy:** early repair.
  - **Obstetric with necrosis (necrotic edges):** catheter trial, then repair at about 3 months.
  - **Radiation:** wait **≥12 months** after radiotherapy; biopsy to exclude recurrence; interposition flap; consider diversion if the bladder is poor.

### Option 2: Surgical route
| Vaginal | Abdominal (open, laparoscopic, robotic) |
|---|---|
| Low, trigonal, accessible apex | Supratrigonal, narrow vagina, no descent |
| Ureters distant | Ureter involved, reimplant needed |
| Less pain, blood loss, stay | Augmentation, Tanagho flap, diversion possible |
| Risk: vaginal shortening | Large (≥4 cm primary, ≥2 cm recurrent), radiation |
- First-attempt closure about **90% either route**; choose the route the surgeon knows best.

**Vaginal techniques**
- **Flap-splitting (Raz):** ureteric stents if within 1 cm; Foley through tract for traction; inverted J/U incision; raise flaps; layer 1 3-0/4-0 including tract edge; layer 2 perpendicular; fill test 200–300 mL; Martius or peritoneal flap; advance opposite flap ≥2 cm beyond.
- **Latzko (high partial colpocleisis):** denude vagina 1–2 cm around tract and close vaginal walls over it; bladder not opened. ≥90% in non-irradiated VVF; favoured for radiation VVF and tracts near the ureters; vaginal shortening.
- **Also know:** vaginal cuff excision technique.

**Abdominal techniques**
- **O'Conor (1951):** extraperitoneal; bivalve bladder from dome down to fistula; excise tract; separate bladder from vagina 2–3 cm beyond; close vagina; **omental or peritoneal flap**; close bladder; suprapubic catheter.
- **Transvesical** (limited cystotomy) and **extravesical** (no cystotomy; fewer LUTS [lower urinary tract symptoms]).
- **Laparoscopic (1994) and robotic (2005):** same closure (85–100%) with less blood loss and shorter stay; vaginal stent, backfill, ICG (indocyanine green) perfusion check.

### Interposition flaps (for radiation, recurrent, ischaemic or large VVF)
| Flap | Supply | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **Martius** (labial fat pad) | Internal and external pudendal | Mid–distal VVF; not apex |
| Peritoneal (vaginal) | Pouch of Douglas fold | Apical post-hysterectomy |
| **Omentum (J-flap)** | Gastroepiploic arteries | Abdominal; radiation, large |
| Gracilis, Singapore, rectus | Myocutaneous | Refractory, tissue loss |
- RCT (randomised controlled trial): Martius added nothing in simple VVF, with donor-site pain in nearly a third.

### Postoperative care
- Catheter ≥10–14 days (urethral and/or suprapubic); antimuscarinic or β3-agonist for spasm; cystogram before removal; stents out at 4–6 weeks.

### Option 3: Urinary diversion
- Multiple failures, severe radiation, massive tissue loss: conduit or continent reservoir. Unfit: ureteric occlusion with nephrostomies. Resource-poor: ureterosigmoidostomy as last resort.

### Outcomes
- Residual urgency or incontinence despite closure (Goh 3–4 predicts); retention (vertical bladder closure); dyspareunia (narrow vagina, fistula ≥3 cm). ICS (International Continence Society): pelvic floor physiotherapy.

**Diagram to draw:** flap-splitting repair: perpendicular bladder layers, Martius flap, advanced vaginal flap. For timing: timeline showing early window, 4–12 week zone, 3 months (obstetric) and 12 months (radiation).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Image upper tracts; exclude ureteric fistula and cancer.
> - Catheter trial only if small, fresh (<3 weeks), non-irradiated; works only if started within 3 weeks.
> - Vaginal and abdominal routes both close about 90%.
> - Early repair for clean iatrogenic VVF; closure comparable to delayed.
> - Delay ~3 months for obstetric, ≥12 months for radiation fistula.
> - Interposition for complex VVF; radiation repair after ≥12 months.

# Section 6 · Female Urology — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Female sexual dysfunction
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 3, Q3f

### Definition and prevalence
- FSD (female sexual dysfunction): persistent desire, arousal, orgasm or pain problems causing **personal distress**.
- About **40%** of women in their lifetime; **HSDD (hypoactive sexual desire disorder) commonest**. Almost half of women with UI (urinary incontinence) have FSD.

### Physiology and response models
- Arousal: pudendal afferents, pelvic parasympathetic efferents → **NO (nitric oxide)–cGMP (cyclic guanosine monophosphate)** → engorgement and lubrication. Oestrogen maintains tissues; testosterone drives desire.
- **Masters and Johnson** (linear): excitement, plateau, orgasm, resolution; Kaplan added desire. **Basson** (circular): desire is often responsive.

### Classification
| DSM-5 (Diagnostic and Statistical Manual, 2013) | ICSM (International Consultation on Sexual Medicine)/ISSWSH (International Society for the Study of Women's Sexual Health) |
|---|---|
| Female sexual interest/arousal disorder (≥3 of 6 criteria) | HSDD and arousal disorder kept separate (cognitive vs genital arousal) |
| Female orgasmic disorder | Orgasm disorders |
| Genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder | Genital-pelvic pain dysfunction |
| – | PGAD (persistent genital arousal disorder) |
- ≥6 months with distress; lifelong or acquired; generalised or situational.

### Causes
- Hormonal (menopause, GSM [genitourinary syndrome of menopause]), drugs (SSRIs [selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors], antihypertensives), diabetes, depression, relationship factors.
- Urological: incontinence, prolapse, sling dyspareunia, cystectomy nerve or clitoral injury.

### Evaluation
- Clinician must ask. **PLISSIT** (permission, limited information, specific suggestions, intensive therapy); BSSC-W (Brief Sexual Symptom Checklist for Women) screen; **FSFI (Female Sexual Function Index, 19 items, six domains)**.
- Examination: atrophy, lichen sclerosus, cotton-swab test, pelvic floor tone. Laboratory tests rarely help.

### Management
- Treat causes; education; sex therapy (sensate focus, mindfulness).
- **HSDD (premenopausal, FDA [US Food and Drug Administration] approved):** **flibanserin** 100 mg at bedtime (5-HT1A agonist/5-HT2A antagonist; alcohol caution); **bremelanotide** 1.75 mg subcutaneously on demand (melanocortin agonist). Postmenopausal: transdermal testosterone (off-label).
- **Arousal:** testosterone; PDE5 (phosphodiesterase type 5) inhibitors only with neurovascular disease.
- **Orgasm:** education, directed masturbation.
- **Pain/vestibulodynia:** pelvic floor physiotherapy, dilators, biofeedback, topical lidocaine, vaginal oestrogen; vestibulectomy if refractory.
- **Also know:** ospemifene, intravaginal prasterone (menopausal dyspareunia).

**Diagram to draw:** Basson circular sexual response model.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - About 40% prevalence; HSDD commonest; distress required.
> - DSM-5 merges desire and arousal, dyspareunia and vaginismus.
> - Flibanserin and bremelanotide: only FDA-approved HSDD drugs (premenopausal).

## Duloxetin
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 4 Paper 3, Q3e

*Read as: duloxetine, mainly its role in stress urinary incontinence.*

### Introduction
- **Duloxetine** is an SNRI (serotonin–noradrenaline reuptake inhibitor), also an antidepressant.
- Licensed in Europe (not USA) for **moderate to severe SUI (stress urinary incontinence) in women**; off-label in men.

### Mechanism
- During storage, bladder afferents drive glutamate excitation of pudendal motor neurons in **Onuf's nucleus (S2–S4)**, which keep the striated urethral sphincter closed.
- Serotonin (5-HT2) and noradrenaline (α1) amplify this glutamate drive. Duloxetine blocks their reuptake, so **sphincter tone and the sneeze-induced continence reflex increase**.
- In voiding, glutamate drive switches off, so retention does not occur.
- Central effects also reduce bladder activity.

### Dose
- **40 mg twice daily**; starting at 20 mg twice daily for 2 weeks reduces nausea. Taper on stopping.

### Evidence
- Phase III RCTs (randomised controlled trials) in women: about 50% median fall in incontinence episodes versus about 30–40% with placebo; better quality of life; **cure rare**.
- Benefit is temporary and lasts only while taken.
- Male PPI (post-prostatectomy incontinence): small, short-term benefit.

### Guideline position
- **EAU (European Association of Urology):** may be offered to women for **temporary** improvement, not cure; warn about side effects. Men: offer for SUI (weak), and **inform of off-label use and adverse effects** (strong).
- **NICE (National Institute for Health and Care Excellence):** not first line; not routinely second line; only if surgery is unwanted or unsuitable.
- Adding PFMT (pelvic floor muscle training): evidence conflicting.

### Adverse effects
- **Nausea** (commonest, early), dry mouth, dizziness, insomnia, constipation; **high discontinuation**.
- Raised blood pressure, hyponatraemia (elderly), suicidal ideation, discontinuation syndrome.

### Contraindications and interactions
- MAOIs (monoamine oxidase inhibitors), including **methylene blue** (serotonin toxicity); CYP1A2 inhibitors (fluvoxamine, ciprofloxacin); hepatic impairment; severe renal impairment; uncontrolled hypertension or narrow-angle glaucoma.

**Diagram to draw:** Onuf's nucleus with 5-HT and noradrenaline terminals driving the pudendal nerve.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - SNRI acting on Onuf's nucleus to increase sphincter tone.
> - 40 mg twice daily; titrate from 20 mg to reduce nausea.
> - Temporary benefit only; approved in Europe, not USA.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 6 · Female Urology

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Urological complications in OBS-GYN and uretero-vaginal fistula (LAQ)

- Bladder is the commonest organ injured; obstructed labour causes most fistulae worldwide.
- Distal ureteric injury at hysterectomy; leak within 4 weeks, normal voiding (continuous leak with normal voiding).
- UVF: orange tampon = UVF on double dye test; exclude coexisting VVF.
- Stent early (within 2 weeks); remove at 4–6 weeks.
- Reimplant with psoas hitch or Boari flap if it fails; success ≥90%; follow 30 months.

#### Female pelvic floor, continence mechanism, classification and management of SUI (LAQ)

- Levator ani = puborectalis, pubococcygeus, iliococcygeus; S3–5.
- DeLancey: I suspension, II attachment (ATFP), III fusion.
- Continence zone is the mid-urethra; submucosal plexus gives up to 30% of closure.
- Blaivas types 0, I, IIA, IIB, III; type III = ISD (VLPP <60, MUCP ≤20 cmH₂O).
- Burch corrects position; slings and AUS correct function.
- VALUE: no urodynamics in index SUI.
- PFMT first; MUS first-line surgery.
- SISTEr (fascial sling > Burch); TOMUS (retropubic = transobturator).
- PVS for ISD; urethral injury → no mesh.
- Men: AUS gold standard; sling for mild, non-irradiated.

#### SUI: evaluation in females, role of UDS, and surgical therapy in males (LAQ)

- Q-tip >30° = hypermobility; VLPP / ALPP <60 cmH2O = ISD; MUCP ≤20 cmH2O.
- VALUE (ValUE) trial: UDS not needed in uncomplicated SUI.
- Use UDS when it changes treatment: mixed, failed surgery, voiding dysfunction.
- AUS PRB 61–70 cmH2O; deactivate 4–6 weeks.
- Radiation or severe SUI: AUS first; MASTER: sling non-inferior.

#### Recto-urethral (recto-prostatic) fistula: etiopathogenesis, diagnosis and management (LAQ)

- Most acquired RUFs follow prostate cancer treatment.
- Martini: stage = size; grade = tissue (surgery, radiation, salvage).
- VCUG or RUG plus endoscopic biopsy to exclude cancer.
- Divert first; perineal (gracilis) or York-Mason for surgical fistulae; irradiated ones need imported tissue or diversion.

#### Urological complications of OBGY surgery and urethrovaginal fistula (LAQ)

- Bladder is the commonest organ injured; ureteric injury is mostly distal and missed.
- Diverticulectomy is the commonest Western cause; obstetric in developing countries.
- Look for a coexisting VVF (about 20%).
- Layered closure plus Martius flap; autologous sling only.
- Closure 90–95% (closure is easy); continence only 50–80% (continence is hard).

#### Vesicovaginal fistula (VVF): management options and early vs delayed repair (LAQ)

- Image upper tracts; exclude ureteric fistula and cancer.
- Catheter trial only if small, fresh (<3 weeks), non-irradiated; works only if started within 3 weeks.
- Vaginal and abdominal routes both close about 90%.
- Early repair for clean iatrogenic VVF; closure comparable to delayed.
- Delay ~3 months for obstetric, ≥12 months for radiation fistula.
- Interposition for complex VVF; radiation repair after ≥12 months.

#### Female sexual dysfunction (SAQ)

- About 40% prevalence; HSDD commonest; distress required.
- DSM-5 merges desire and arousal, dyspareunia and vaginismus.
- Flibanserin and bremelanotide: only FDA-approved HSDD drugs (premenopausal).

#### Duloxetin (SAQ)

- SNRI acting on Onuf's nucleus to increase sphincter tone.
- 40 mg twice daily; titrate from 20 mg to reduce nausea.
- Temporary benefit only; approved in Europe, not USA.

# Section 7 · Voiding Dysfunction — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Sacral neuromodulation
**Asked as:**
- Sacral neuromodulation — *Summer 2015 Paper III Q3a (SAQ)*
- Sacral Neuromodulation. — *Winter 2017 Paper III Q3a (SAQ)*
- Sacral Neuromodulation — *Winter 2025 Paper III Q3e (SAQ)* (also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q3a)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Definition and mechanism
- **SNM (sacral neuromodulation)** (Tanagho and Schmidt): continuous low-amplitude **S3** stimulation through (via) a tined lead and IPG (implantable pulse generator).
- It acts on **afferents** to the forebrain (not motor nerves), which modulate forebrain awareness. In OAB (overactive bladder) this damps the PMC (pontine micturition centre) firing; in retention it restores filling awareness (sensation), so the PMC switches on and the sphincter relaxes. Hence it treats **both**.

### Indications (third line after behavioural and drug therapy; AUA (American Urological Association) and EAU (European Association of Urology))
| FDA (Food and Drug Administration) approved | Not approved |
|---|---|
| Refractory UUI (urgency urinary incontinence) 1997 | Bladder pain syndrome |
| Urgency–frequency 1999 | Neurogenic bladder (small studies positive; low-level evidence) |
| **Non-obstructive urinary retention** (e.g. Fowler's syndrome; Fowler syndrome responds well) 1999 | Pelvic pain |
| Faecal incontinence 2011 | |
- **Fowler's syndrome**: young women in retention with abnormal sphincter EMG (electromyography) activity; responds well to SNM.
- Retention needs some detrusor function; success falls with age.

### Technique
| Test phase | PNE (percutaneous nerve evaluation) | Staged implant (staged tined lead) |
|---|---|---|
| Lead | Temporary, office, local anaesthesia (dislodges) | Permanent tined lead, theatre, fluoroscopy |
| Duration | 5–7 days | Up to 4 weeks |
| Use | OAB | More reliable; higher conversion; used for retention |

- Prone; needle into S3 about **9 cm above the coccyx tip, 1–2 cm lateral**.

| Root | Pelvic response | Foot response |
|---|---|---|
| S2 | — | Calf and foot (external) rotation |
| **S3 (target)** | **Bellows**, perineal sensation | **Great-toe flexion** |
| S4 | Bellows/perineal only | None |

- **Implant if ≥50% (at least 50%) improvement** in a diary parameter (retention: ≥50% fewer catheterisations); IPG in the buttock.
- **Also know:** 10–16 Hz at sensory threshold; bilateral no better.

### Outcomes
- **InSite**: success 61% vs 42% with standard medical therapy at 6 months; about 70–80% success at 5 years (another source: about half keep benefit at ≥5 years).
- **ROSETTA**: onabotulinumtoxinA 200 U slightly better for UUI (−3.9 vs −3.3 episodes/day) and higher satisfaction, but more UTI (urinary tract infection) (24% vs 10%) and self-catheterisation; SNM removal 9% at 2 years.

### Complications and contraindications
- Pain at IPG site, lead migration, loss of efficacy (effect), infection; **revision over 30% at 5 years**; costly (costliest option); battery replacement.
- Contraindications: failed test, inability to use the programmer, sacral anomaly.

### Recent advances
- Rechargeable devices / IPGs (≥15 years); **full-body (whole-body) MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)-conditional** systems; bilateral stimulation no better. **Also know:** PTNS (percutaneous tibial nerve stimulation) and pudendal stimulation as alternatives (PTNS alone as alternative in another source).

**Diagram to draw:** lead in S3 foramen with IPG in the buttock.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Afferent modulation treats both OAB and retention.
> - S3: bellows plus great-toe flexion.
> - Implant if test improves ≥50% (at least 50%); revision >30% at 5 years.

## Urinary bladder compliance
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Summer Paper 3, Q3e

### Definition
- **Compliance** is the change in volume for a change in detrusor pressure: **C = ΔV / ΔPdet**, in **mL/cmH₂O**.
- It reflects **low-pressure storage** during filling.

### Physiological basis
- **Viscoelastic wall**: smooth muscle, collagen (about 50% of the wall) and elastin (about 2%). Coiled collagen straightens as the bladder fills.
- More collagen or less elastin makes the wall stiff.

### Measurement (filling cystometry)
- ICS (International Continence Society) points: Pdet at **start of filling** and at **cystometric capacity** (or before a leak-causing contraction), **excluding detrusor contractions**.
- **No agreed normal value**; compliance depends on capacity. Below **20 mL/cmH₂O** (some use 10–20) suggests poor accommodation. In children, normal is often taken as **>15 mL/cmH₂O**.

### Pitfalls
| Pitfall | Effect | Fix |
|---|---|---|
| Fast filling | False low compliance | Slow or stop filling; pressure falls |
| Sustained low-amplitude detrusor contraction | Mimics low compliance | Stop filling: pressure returns to baseline |
| **Pop-off**: VUR (vesicoureteric reflux), diverticulum, leaking outlet | Falsely good compliance | Video-urodynamics; occlude outlet |
- A small bladder can give a low number without danger (**compliance paradox**). **Absolute storage pressure matters more than the ratio.**

### Causes of low compliance
- **Neurogenic**: spina bifida, spinal cord injury, lower motor neuron and decentralisation injury (radical pelvic surgery).
- **Outlet obstruction**: PUV (posterior urethral valves), long-standing BPO (benign prostatic obstruction).
- **Structural**: radiation cystitis, tuberculosis, schistosomiasis, multiple bladder operations.

### Consequences and DLPP
- **DLPP (detrusor leak point pressure)**: lowest Pdet at which leakage occurs without contraction or strain.
- **McGuire (1981)**: DLPP **>40 cmH₂O** in myelodysplastic children led to hydronephrosis or VUR in 85%. Not a validated safe limit (20 cmH₂O is more sensitive); aim as low as possible.
- A strong outlet keeps the patient dry but transmits high pressure to the kidneys.

### Management
- **Clean intermittent catheterisation + antimuscarinics** (first line).
- **Intradetrusor botulinum toxin**.
- **Augmentation cystoplasty** if refractory; vesicostomy in infants.
- Repeat urodynamics to confirm safety.

**Diagram to draw:** cystometrogram: normal flat versus steep rising filling curve.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - C = ΔV/ΔPdet; <20 mL/cmH₂O suggests poor compliance.
> - McGuire: DLPP >40 cmH₂O, 85% upper-tract damage.
> - Pop-offs (VUR, diverticulum) hide true pressure; use video-urodynamics.

## Botulinum toxin in urology
**Asked as:**
- Botulinum toxin — *Winter 2017 Paper III Q3b (SAQ)*
- Botox in uro — *Practice Set 4 Paper III Q3a (SAQ)* (also answered in 2025-Winter Paper 2, Q3d); *Read as: botulinum toxin in urology.*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Introduction
- *Clostridium botulinum* neurotoxin, serotypes A–G. Urology uses **onabotulinumtoxinA (BoNT-A)**; type B is short-acting.
- 150 kDa: heavy chain (100 kDa; binding, entry) plus zinc-dependent light chain (50 kDa).

### Mechanism
1. Heavy chain binds the cholinergic terminal; toxin is internalised.
2. Light chain **cleaves SNAP-25 (synaptosomal-associated protein 25)**, a SNARE (vesicle-docking) protein, so vesicles cannot fuse and **acetylcholine release stops**.
- **Afferent action**: less ATP (adenosine triphosphate), substance P and CGRP (calcitonin gene-related peptide) release; fewer TRPV1 (capsaicin) and P2X3 (purinergic) receptors, so urgency falls.
- Effect wanes as nerve terminals sprout (6–9 months); repeat injections work.

### Technique
- Cystoscopic injection into detrusor at **20 sites (100 U) or 30 sites (200 U)**, usually sparing trigone (trigone usually spared); sterile urine, antibiotic cover; check PVR (post-void residual) at 2 weeks.

### Urological uses
| Indication | Dose and evidence |
|---|---|
| **Idiopathic OAB (overactive bladder)**, third line | **100 U** (EMBARK): incontinence −59%, about 25% dry. **ABC** trial: equal to antimuscarinic, more complete dryness. **ROSETTA**: 200 U ≥ SNM (sacral neuromodulation) (another source: slightly better than SNM), more UTI (urinary tract infection) |
| **NDO (neurogenic detrusor overactivity)**: SCI (spinal cord injury), MS (multiple sclerosis) | **200 U** (DIGNITY): less incontinence, lower detrusor pressure, larger capacity |
| **Paediatric NDO** (spina bifida) | About **10 U/kg (maximum 360 U)** in Campbell series; FDA (Food and Drug Administration) label (2021, age ≥5) is 6 U/kg, maximum 200 U; continence 65–87%, most avoid augmentation |
| **DSD (detrusor–sphincter dyssynergia)**, underactive detrusor | Intrasphincteric injection 50–100 U; men unable to do CISC (clean intermittent self-catheterisation) |
| **IC/BPS (interstitial cystitis/bladder pain syndrome)** | 100 U, often trigonal; AUA (American Urological Association) option, off-label |
| **BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia)** | Intraprostatic: early small trial positive, but larger (large) placebo-controlled RCTs (randomised controlled trials) showed **no benefit over placebo** (no better than placebo); not recommended |
- **Also know:** paediatric dysfunctional voiding (sphincter); pelvic floor spasm; chronic pelvic pain; ED (erectile dysfunction) trials. Units of different toxin products are not interchangeable.

### Complications and contraindications
- **Retention needing CISC**: 0–12% in OAB trials; real-world about 20% women, up to 35% men; higher (commoner) in NDO. Each 10 mL rise in baseline PVR raises risk about 27%. **Patient must accept CISC beforehand.**
- **UTI** about 20%; haematuria; rarely generalised weakness.
- Contraindications: active UTI, myasthenia gravis, aminoglycosides, pregnancy. Repeat injections stay effective.

**Diagram to draw:** cholinergic synapse (nerve terminal) showing SNAP-25 cleavage.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Cleaves SNAP-25; efferent and afferent action (acts on efferent and afferent nerves).
> - 100 U idiopathic OAB; 200 U NDO; children 10 U/kg (max 360 U) in series, FDA label 6 U/kg (max 200 U).
> - Main risks: retention needing CISC, UTI.

## OAB Pharmacotherapy: Darifenacin vs Solifenacin vs Tolterodine, and Mirabegron
**Asked as:**
- Darifenacin vs. solifenacin vs. tolterodine — *Practice Set 2 Paper III Q3e (SAQ)*
- Mirabegron in over active bladder. — *Winter 2024 Paper III Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(also answered in Practice Set 1 Paper 1, Q3e)

### Introduction
- OAB (overactive bladder: urgency, with or without urgency incontinence, usually with frequency and nocturia).
- Antimuscarinics treat OAB after behavioural therapy. Acetylcholine on **M3** receptors contracts the detrusor; M2 (commoner) adds to it. Blockade reduces involuntary contractions and afferent urgency signals.
- Side effects: **M1** (brain) cognition; **M2** heart rate; **M3** (glands, gut) dry mouth, constipation.
- Selectivity is by **receptor subtype** (M3) or **tissue** (bladder over salivary gland); tissue selectivity matters more.
- **Mirabegron** is the first **β3-adrenoceptor agonist** for OAB. FDA (Food and Drug Administration) approval 2012.

### Antimuscarinics: comparison
| Feature | Darifenacin | Solifenacin | Tolterodine |
|---|---|---|---|
| Selectivity | **Most M3-selective (about 30-fold)**; not tissue-selective | M3 about 10-fold over M2; bladder-selective | Non-subtype-selective; **bladder-selective** |
| Dose | 7.5–15 mg daily (ER (extended-release)) | 5–10 mg daily | 2 mg twice daily (IR (immediate-release)); 4 mg daily (ER) |
| Half-life | Moderate (ER once daily) | **Long** (about 2–3 days): once daily | Short; active metabolite 5-HMT (5-hydroxymethyl tolterodine) |
| Metabolism | CYP3A4 (a liver enzyme) and CYP2D6 | CYP3A4 | CYP2D6 (and 3A4) |
| Dry mouth | Dose-related | Dose-related | Lower with ER |
| Constipation | **Highest** (gut M3) | Moderate–high | Lower |
| CNS (central nervous system) | Spares M1: **preferred in elderly** | Better persistence | High dose: QT prolongation |
| Dose limit | Lower dose with CYP3A4 inhibitors or liver disease | 5 mg in severe renal or moderate liver impairment | Halve with renal or liver impairment |

#### Common points
- **Efficacy is similar**; response is individual, so switch agent or dose.
- **STAR trial:** flexible solifenacin beat tolterodine ER for urgency and incontinence.
- **Contraindications:** urinary or gastric retention, uncontrolled narrow-angle glaucoma.
- Men: add to α-blocker; retention up to 3%; check PVR (postvoid residual).

### Mirabegron (β3 agonist)
#### Mechanism of action
- **β3 receptors** are the most highly expressed adrenoceptor in human detrusor.
- Activation → adenylyl cyclase → **↑ cAMP (cyclic adenosine monophosphate)** → detrusor relaxation during **storage**.
- Reduces non-voiding contractions and afferent firing. The voiding contraction (muscarinic M3) is spared, so PVR (post-void residual) barely rises.

#### Pharmacology and dose
- Oral, extended release, once daily: start **25 mg**, increase to **50 mg** after 4–8 weeks.
- **25 mg maximum** in severe renal impairment (eGFR [estimated glomerular filtration rate] 15–29) or moderate hepatic impairment; avoid in end-stage renal disease and severe hepatic impairment.
- **Moderate CYP2D6 inhibitor:** raises levels of metoprolol, desipramine, flecainide; monitor digoxin.

#### Efficacy
- Phase III trials (SCORPIO, ARIES, CAPRICORN): fewer incontinence episodes and micturitions than placebo. **TAURUS** (12 months): efficacy similar to tolterodine, with less dry mouth.
- **Combination with solifenacin** (SYNERGY, SYNERGY II, BESIDE): solifenacin plus mirabegron better than either alone (beats monotherapy), without extra cardiovascular effects.

#### Adverse effects and contraindications
- Hypertension, tachycardia, headache, nasopharyngitis, UTI (urinary tract infection).
- **Dry mouth and constipation near placebo rates**, far below antimuscarinics.
- **Contraindicated:** severe uncontrolled hypertension (≥180/110 mmHg). Caution with arrhythmia and BOO (bladder outlet obstruction) with antimuscarinics (retention).

### Place in therapy
- After behavioural therapy, as first-line pharmacotherapy alongside antimuscarinics (AUA/SUFU [American Urological Association/Society of Urodynamics, Female Pelvic Medicine and Urogenital Reconstruction]).
- **Dementia:** antimuscarinic use >3 months raises risk 10–65% (is linked to 10–65% more new-onset dementia; SUFU white paper). Prefer **β3 agonist** (mirabegron **preferred in the elderly and cognitive impairment:** no anticholinergic burden), or ER trospium, darifenacin or fesoterodine, in the elderly. Beers criteria: avoid in dementia.
- Mirabegron: add-on to α-blocker in men with BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia); neurogenic detrusor overactivity.
- **Also know:** vibegron 75 mg (no CYP2D6 interaction).

**Diagram to draw:** muscarinic subtypes M1 (brain), M2 (heart), M3 (bladder, gland, gut) with each drug's selectivity; detrusor cell: noradrenaline → β3 → cAMP → relaxation, versus acetylcholine → M3 → contraction.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Darifenacin most M3-selective; tolterodine bladder-selective; solifenacin longest half-life.
> - Similar efficacy; STAR: solifenacin beat tolterodine ER.
> - β3 → cAMP → storage-phase relaxation; voiding spared.
> - Mirabegron 25 mg → 50 mg; avoid if blood pressure ≥180/110; CYP2D6 interaction (metoprolol).
> - Elderly: darifenacin, trospium or β3 agonist; mirabegron preferred over antimuscarinics in the elderly (dementia risk 10–65% with >3 months' use).
> - Add-on to solifenacin (SYNERGY) works.

## Uroflowmetry
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 2 Paper 3, Q3a
(also answered in 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q3f)

### Definition
- Non-invasive urodynamic test recording **voided volume per unit time** (mL/s). It shows detrusor contraction and outlet resistance together.

### Method
- Comfortably full bladder; private void in the preferred position. Ask whether the void was typical; compare with the frequency–volume chart.
- **Voided volume ≥150 mL** (Qmax unreliable below 125–150 mL). Repeat; then measure **PVR (postvoid residual)** by ultrasound.
- Flowmeters: weight transducer (load cell), rotating disc, capacitance dipstick.

### Parameters
| Parameter | Note |
|---|---|
| **Qmax** (maximum flow) | Key value; read from smoothed curve, ignoring spikes |
| Qave (average flow) | Less accurate for obstruction |
| Voided volume | ≥150 mL |
| Flow time vs voiding time | Voiding time includes interruptions |
| Time to Qmax | Normally early in the void |
| Voiding efficiency | VV ÷ (VV + PVR) × 100 |

### Curve patterns (ICCS (International Children's Continence Society))
| Pattern | Suggests |
|---|---|
| **Bell** | Normal |
| Tower (high, short) | Overactive bladder |
| **Plateau** (flat, prolonged) | Fixed obstruction: stricture, PUV (posterior urethral valves) |
| Staccato (never reaches zero) | Sphincter overactivity, dysfunctional voiding |
| **Interrupted** (reaches zero) | Detrusor underactivity with straining, or DSD (detrusor-sphincter dyssynergia) |

### Interpretation
- **Qmax <10 mL/s:** obstruction likely (more specific). **<15 mL/s:** more sensitive, less specific.
- **Volume correction:** corrected Qmax = Qmax ÷ √VV; **Siroky** (men) and **Liverpool** (men and women) nomograms.
- **Key limitation:** cannot separate BOO (bladder outlet obstruction) from DUA (detrusor underactivity); only a pressure–flow study can.
- Children: valid if voided volume ≥50% of expected capacity; Qmax only if the peak lasts ≥2 s.

### Uses
- BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia): before active treatment and to monitor outcome.
- After urethroplasty or urethrotomy (stricture recurrence).
- Children: BBD (bladder and bowel dysfunction), after valve ablation; with patch EMG (electromyography).
- Women with voiding dysfunction.
- **AUA (American Urological Association):** pressure–flow study may be done before BPH surgery if Qmax >10 mL/s.

**Diagram to draw:** bell, plateau, tower and interrupted curves.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Voided volume ≥150 mL; Qmax is the key value.
> - Plateau = fixed obstruction; staccato = sphincter; interrupted = weak detrusor.
> - Cannot distinguish BOO from DUA.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 7 · Voiding Dysfunction

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Sacral neuromodulation (SAQ)

- Afferent modulation treats both OAB and retention.
- S3: bellows plus great-toe flexion.
- Implant if test improves ≥50% (at least 50%); revision >30% at 5 years.

#### Urinary bladder compliance (SAQ)

- C = ΔV/ΔPdet; <20 mL/cmH₂O suggests poor compliance.
- McGuire: DLPP >40 cmH₂O, 85% upper-tract damage.
- Pop-offs (VUR, diverticulum) hide true pressure; use video-urodynamics.

#### Botulinum toxin in urology (SAQ)

- Cleaves SNAP-25; efferent and afferent action (acts on efferent and afferent nerves).
- 100 U idiopathic OAB; 200 U NDO; children 10 U/kg (max 360 U) in series, FDA label 6 U/kg (max 200 U).
- Main risks: retention needing CISC, UTI.

#### OAB Pharmacotherapy: Darifenacin vs Solifenacin vs Tolterodine, and Mirabegron (SAQ)

- Darifenacin most M3-selective; tolterodine bladder-selective; solifenacin longest half-life.
- Similar efficacy; STAR: solifenacin beat tolterodine ER.
- β3 → cAMP → storage-phase relaxation; voiding spared.
- Mirabegron 25 mg → 50 mg; avoid if blood pressure ≥180/110; CYP2D6 interaction (metoprolol).
- Elderly: darifenacin, trospium or β3 agonist; mirabegron preferred over antimuscarinics in the elderly (dementia risk 10–65% with >3 months' use).
- Add-on to solifenacin (SYNERGY) works.

#### Uroflowmetry (SAQ)

- Voided volume ≥150 mL; Qmax is the key value.
- Plateau = fixed obstruction; staccato = sphincter; interrupted = weak detrusor.
- Cannot distinguish BOO from DUA.

# Section 8 · Nocturia & Male Incontinence — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Desmopressin (DDAVP)
**Asked as:**
- Desmopressin. — *Winter 2023 Paper III Q3b (SAQ)*
- DDAVP — *Practice Set 3 Paper III Q3c (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 3, Q3b)

### Pharmacology
- DDAVP (**1-deamino-8-D-arginine vasopressin**, desmopressin), a synthetic analogue of AVP (arginine vasopressin/antidiuretic hormone).
- Deamination and D-arginine substitution prolong action (give longer action) and make it a **selective V2 agonist (selective V2 agonism) without pressor (V1) effect**.
- **Mechanism:** V2 receptor on collecting-duct cells raises cAMP (cyclic adenosine monophosphate), inserting **aquaporin-2**; water is reabsorbed, so urine is less and concentrated (V2 receptor → cAMP → aquaporin-2 insertion → water reabsorption → less, concentrated urine).
- Endothelial V2 action releases von Willebrand factor and factor VIII (haemostatic use).

### Formulations

| Form | Dose |
|---|---|
| Tablet | 0.2–0.4 mg at bedtime, about 1 h before bed (enuresis) |
| Oral lyophilisate (melt) | 120–240 µg, about 1 h before bed |
| Low-dose nasal spray (nocturia) | 0.83 or 1.66 µg, 30 min before bed |
| Sublingual ODT (orally disintegrating tablet) | 27.7 µg women, 55.3 µg men |
| IV / subcutaneous | 0.3 µg/kg (bleeding) |

### Uses (in urology)
1. **Monosymptomatic nocturnal enuresis:** first line with the alarm. **Rule of thirds**: one-third full, one-third partial, one-third no response. Acts (works) at once but relapses on stopping. Restrict fluids in the evening (avoid evening fluid); add oxybutynin if partial.
2. **Nocturia from nocturnal polyuria** (NPi [nocturnal polyuria index] >20–33%, age-dependent). EAU (European Association of Urology): offer below (under) 65; low dose above (over) 65 with ≥2 voids. Add to α-blocker for refractory nocturia in BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia).
3. **Neurogenic nocturia** (multiple sclerosis, MSA [multiple system atrophy]).
4. **Bleeding:** uraemic platelet dysfunction, von Willebrand disease (including acquired, in Wilms tumour). Tachyphylaxis after about 3 (repeated) doses.
5. **Diagnosis:** central versus nephrogenic DI (diabetes insipidus).

### Adverse effects
- **Hyponatraemia** (most important): headache, nausea, confusion, seizures.
- Risk factors: **age ≥65**, baseline sodium <135 mmol/L, CrCl (creatinine clearance) <60 mL/min, higher dose; women are more sensitive.
- Most events occur in the **first 2–3 weeks**.

### Monitoring and contraindications
- Sodium at baseline, then within 1 week and at 1 month (EAU adds day 3), and after dose change.
- **Avoid:** hyponatraemia, polydipsia, heart failure, SIADH (syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone), significant renal impairment, **loop diuretics**.

**Diagram to draw:** V2 receptor–cAMP–aquaporin-2 pathway in the collecting duct.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Selective V2 agonist; no pressor effect.
> - Enuresis: 0.2–0.4 mg tablet or 120–240 µg melt; rule of thirds.
> - Hyponatraemia: age ≥65, low sodium; check sodium early.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 8 · Nocturia & Male Incontinence

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Desmopressin (DDAVP) (SAQ)

- Selective V2 agonist; no pressor effect.
- Enuresis: 0.2–0.4 mg tablet or 120–240 µg melt; rule of thirds.
- Hyponatraemia: age ≥65, low sodium; check sodium early.

# Section 9 · Andrology & Sexual Medicine — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Priapism: Diagnosis and Management
**Asked as:**
- Diagnosis and Management of Priapism — *Winter 2025 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)*
- Priapism — *Practice Set 4 Paper III Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

(also answered as an SAQ in 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q3a; full LAQ answer in 2025-Winter Paper 3, Q2)

### Definition
- Full or partial erection lasting **≥4 hours** beyond sexual stimulation, or unrelated to it (AUA/SMSNA (American Urological Association/Sexual Medicine Society of North America) 2021–2022).

### Classification (types)
| | Ischaemic (low flow) | Stuttering (recurrent ischaemic) | Non-ischaemic (high flow) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Share | **95%** | Subset of ischaemic | <5% |
| Lesion | Veno-occlusion | Repeated (recurrent) ischaemic episodes | Cavernosal artery–sinusoid fistula (arterio-sinusoidal fistula) |
| Pain, rigidity (penis) | Painful, fully rigid | Painful, recurrent, often nocturnal | Painless, partial |
| Typical cause | ICI (intracavernosal injection), drugs, SCD (sickle cell disease) | SCD | Perineal or needle trauma |
| Urgency | **Emergency** | Emergency per episode + prophylaxis | Not an emergency (not urgent) |

### Aetiology
- **Ischaemic:** ICI (intracavernosal injection: papaverine, phentolamine, PGE1 (prostaglandin E1)), including recreational use; α-blockers, trazodone, antipsychotics, cocaine; heparin or warfarin withdrawal; **SCD** (29–42% of affected men in their lifetime; 63% of paediatric ischaemic priapism); leukaemia (1–5%, high white cell count); penile metastases; scorpion and spider venom.
- **Non-ischaemic:** straddle or coital injury, pelvic fracture, needle injury. Onset is often delayed, when the clot over the arterial tear lyses.

### Pathophysiology
- **Ischaemic:** failed outflow acts like a compartment syndrome. Hypoxia, hypercarbia and acidosis cause sinusoidal thrombosis, smooth-muscle necrosis and TGF-β (transforming growth factor beta)-driven fibrosis, so ED (erectile dysfunction) follows (hence ED). Irreversible damage starts within hours.
- **Stuttering (molecular):** low basal NO (nitric oxide) from eNOS (endothelial NO synthase) dysfunction downregulates PDE5 (phosphodiesterase 5). When an erection starts, cGMP (cyclic guanosine monophosphate) is not broken down, so relaxation is unopposed (cGMP rises unopposed). Low Rho-kinase and high adenosine add to this. This explains why daily PDE5 inhibitors prevent episodes.
- **Non-ischaemic:** unregulated, well-oxygenated arterial inflow, so fibrosis is unusual.

### Diagnosis
- **History:** duration, pain, drugs, trauma, SCD or blood disease, prior episodes, baseline erectile function.
- **Examination:** rigid tender corpora with a soft glans and spongiosum (ischaemic); partial, non-tender (high flow); perineal bruising; vital signs before phenylephrine.
- **Corporal blood gas** (cornerstone); high flow resembles arterial blood:

| | Colour | pO2 (mmHg) | pCO2 (mmHg) | pH |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ischaemic | Dark red | **<30** | **>60** | **<7.25** |
| Flaccid (mixed venous) | Medium red | 40 | 50 | 7.35 |
| Non-ischaemic | Bright red | >90 | <40 | 7.40 |

- **PDUS (penile duplex ultrasound)** when the gas is equivocal: ischaemic = absent cavernosal flow or PSV (peak systolic velocity) <50 cm/s with no diastolic flow; high flow = PSV >50 cm/s, may show the fistula (high flow with fistula). Angiography only for embolisation.
- Blood count, reticulocytes, haemoglobin electrophoresis, coagulation, toxicology: in parallel, never delaying treatment (without delaying treatment).

### Management of ischaemic priapism (AUA/SMSNA 2021)
```
Counsel on ED risk → dorsal penile block
<36 h:
  aspiration (19 G, penoscrotal junction, 3 or 9 o'clock) ± saline irrigation
  + phenylephrine 100–500 µg/mL, 1 mL every 3–5 min, up to 1 h
    (monitor blood pressure, pulse: reflex bradycardia)
  → fails → distal shunt (Winter, T-shunt, Al-Ghorab) ± tunnelling
  → proximal shunt (Quackels) or penoscrotal decompression
>36 h (beyond 36 h) or refractory → early penile prosthesis (or analgesia and counselling)
```
- Aspiration alone succeeds in under 1 in 4. Cold (10 °C) saline irrigation was better than body temperature in an RCT (randomised controlled trial); avoid chilled saline in SCD.
- Ice, oral agents and IV (intravenous) fluids are adjuncts only; in SCD, intracavernosal treatment still comes first.

| Shunt | Method |
|---|---|
| **Winter** (distal) | Tru-Cut needle through glans; least effective |
| **Ebbehoj / T-shunt** (distal) | Scalpel through glans; T-shunt rotates blade 90° |
| **Al-Ghorab** (distal, open) | Dorsal glans incision; excise corporal tips |
| **Snake tunnelling** | 7/8 Hegar dilator passed proximally through distal shunt |
| **Quackels / Sacher** (proximal) | Cavernosum to spongiosum at bulb; stagger ≥1 cm |
| **Grayhack / Barry** | Cavernosum to saphenous / dorsal vein |
| **Penoscrotal decompression** | Proximal corporotomy, clot suction; allows prosthesis |

- **ED by duration** (Bennett and Mulhall, SCD series): ≤12 h 0%; 12–24 h 22%; 24–36 h 56%; ≥36 h 100%.
- **Early prosthesis** (Zacharakis 2014): less shortening and fewer revisions than delayed insertion — shortening 3% vs 40%, revision 9% vs 27%, satisfaction 96% vs 60% compared with delayed insertion.

### Stuttering priapism
- An episode lasting ≥4 hours is treated as ischaemic; home self-injection of phenylephrine.
- **Prophylaxis:** daily PDE5 inhibitor (Burnett 2014 RCT: primary endpoint missed, but promising), pseudoephedrine, etilefrine. **Also know:** GnRH (gonadotropin-releasing hormone) agonists, antiandrogens, finasteride (cost: libido); digoxin; baclofen.

### Non-ischaemic priapism (AUA/SMSNA 2022)
- Observation, perineal compression and ice; about 60% resolve. Reassess at 4 weeks.
- Persistent → PDUS → **selective cavernosal artery embolisation** (selective arterial embolisation; resolution ≥75%, recurrence 30–40%). For recurrence, repeat embolisation with non-resorbable PVA (polyvinyl alcohol), not surgery. Surgical ligation is a last resort (ED about 50%).

### Complications
- ED, corporal fibrosis, penile shortening; shunt-related oedema, haematoma, urethral injury or fistula, skin necrosis, infection.

**Diagram to draw:** distal (glans) and proximal shunt sites on a penile outline.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Blood gas decides the type: ischaemic pO2 <30, pCO2 >60, pH <7.25.
> - Aspiration plus phenylephrine first; distal shunt ± tunnelling next.
> - ED risk 0% ≤12 h, 100% ≥36 h (beyond 36 h); consider early prosthesis beyond 36 h.
> - High flow: observe, then embolise; repeat embolisation rather than surgery.

# Section 9 · Andrology & Sexual Medicine — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Types of penile prosthesis. What are biofilms in penile prosthesis?
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 3, Q3d

### Introduction
- For ED (erectile dysfunction) failing less invasive treatment; satisfaction **80–90%**. Repairs: about 15% at 5 years, 30% at 10.

### Types
| Type | Examples | Features |
|---|---|---|
| **Malleable** | Tactra (nitinol), Genesis | Simple, cheap, no mechanical failure; always rigid |
| **2-piece inflatable** | Ambicor (discontinued) | No reservoir; poor flaccidity |
| **3-piece inflatable** | AMS 700, Titan | **Best rigidity and true flaccidity**; cylinders, scrotal pump, reservoir |
- Penoscrotal or infrapubic approach; reservoir in Retzius or **ectopic submuscular** after pelvic surgery.
- **Also know:** erosion, pump migration, glans hypermobility, cylinder aneurysm; modelling in Peyronie's disease.

### Biofilm
- **Definition**: a structured microbial community in a self-made **polysaccharide matrix**, attached to the implant.
- **Stages**: (1) conditioning film of host proteins coats the device within minutes; (2) reversible, then irreversible bacterial adhesion; (3) microcolonies; (4) maturation into a 3D structure with water channels and quorum sensing; (5) dispersal of free bacteria, causing clinical infection.
- **Organisms**: coagulase-negative staphylococci commonest; also S. aureus, Gram-negatives, Candida.
- **Why antibiotics fail**: the matrix blocks penetration, bacteria are slow-growing (persisters), and they survive up to **1000–1500 times** normal killing levels.
- **Relevance**: organisms from the first operation lie dormant; revising an uninfected device gives about 10% infection, cut to about 3% by **antiseptic washout**.

### Prevention (infection 1–3%)
- Glucose under 200 mg/dL; treat skin and urine; decolonise MRSA (methicillin-resistant S. aureus) carriers.
- **Chlorhexidine-alcohol** skin preparation; vancomycin or cephalosporin plus gentamicin for up to 24 hours.
- **Coatings halve infection**: InhibiZone (rifampin–minocycline); hydrophilic coating dipped in antibiotic.
- **No-touch technique**: under 0.5%.

### Infected implant
- Pus, fixed pump or exposed parts: remove **all** components.
- **Mulcahy salvage**: remove all parts; wash with dilute povidone-iodine (3 minutes) then saline or antibiotic; change gloves, drapes and instruments; insert a new device at once. Success **84%**; fails with early onset, aggressive organisms, cellulitis.

**Diagram to draw:** 3-piece device; biofilm life cycle.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 3-piece inflatable gives best rigidity and flaccidity.
> - Biofilm: microbes in a matrix; antibiotics fail.
> - Infection 1–3%; Mulcahy salvage 84%.

## Oligospermia and oligoasthenozoospermia
**Asked as:**
- Oligoanaesthospermia. — *Summer 2016 Paper III Q3a (SAQ)*
- Oligospermia — *Practice Set 4 Paper III Q3c (SAQ)* (also answered in 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q3a)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*Assumed meaning (oligoanaesthospermia): oligoasthenozoospermia (low sperm count with poor motility).*

### Definition (WHO (World Health Organization) 2021, 6th edition, 5th centiles)
- **Oligozoospermia:** sperm concentration below the lower reference limit: **WHO 2021 (6th edition) 16 million/mL** (5th centile; total count 39 million). The legacy cut-off was 20 million/mL.

| Parameter (other WHO 2021 5th centiles) | Lower reference (value) |
|---|---|
| Concentration | 16 million/mL (total 39 million) |
| Progressive motility | 30% |
| Total motility | 42% |
| Volume | 1.4 mL |
| Normal forms (strict) | 4% |
| Vitality | 54% |
- These are reference values (these centiles come) from fertile men, not pass/fail cut-offs; they are a reference, not a fertility test. Define oligospermia in context (testis size, FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone), repeated values).
- **Severe oligozoospermia: <5 million/mL (under 5 million/mL)**; cryptozoospermia: sperm only in a centrifuged pellet.

### Causes
- **Varicocele** (commonest correctable; heat and oxidative stress) and **idiopathic** (largest group).
- **Pre-testicular / Hormonal:** hypogonadotropism (hypogonadotropic hypogonadism), **exogenous testosterone or anabolic steroids**, obesity (high / raised oestradiol), hyperprolactinaemia, opioids.
- **Testicular:** **varicocele**, cryptorchidism, mumps orchitis, torsion, chemotherapy or radiation, gonadotoxins; genetic (Klinefelter, Klinefelter mosaic, AZF (azoospermia factor) c deletion / **AZFc (azoospermia factor c) deletion**).
- **Post-testicular / Motility:** infection or **leucocytospermia** (over 1 million/mL), antisperm antibodies, partial ejaculatory duct obstruction (EDO; low volume), PCD (primary ciliary dyskinesia).
- Lifestyle: smoking, alcohol, cannabis, heat, gonadotoxins.

### Evaluation
- **At least two semen analyses, 2–3 weeks apart**, same abstinence (WHO 2–7 days abstinence; parameters peak at 1–2 days); history and examination (testis size / volume, varicocele, vasa).
- Morning **testosterone, LH (luteinising hormone), FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone)**; oestradiol and prolactin if testosterone is low.
- Under 5 million/mL (especially with raised FSH or small testes): **karyotype and Y-microdeletion** (AUA/ASRM (American Urological Association/American Society for Reproductive Medicine) 2020 guideline).
- Selected: vitality test (eosin or hypo-osmotic swelling), antisperm antibodies, semen culture, scrotal Doppler, TRUS (transrectal ultrasonography / ultrasound) if volume low (<1.0 mL), DNA fragmentation.

### Management
- **General**: stop smoking, weight loss, avoid heat; **stop exogenous testosterone** and gonadotoxins; treat infection.
- **Varicocelectomy** (microsurgical subinguinal) for a **palpable** varicocele with abnormal semen; improves parameters and pregnancy. Not (never) for subclinical ones.
- **Medical**: hypogonadotropic → hCG (human chorionic gonadotropin) with or without (±) FSH. Idiopathic with low testosterone → off-label clomiphene 25 mg daily, or anastrozole 1 mg daily if testosterone:oestradiol ratio under 10 (<10). Antioxidants: weak evidence.
- **ART (assisted reproductive technology)**: mild → IUI (intrauterine insemination); severe, or failed IUI → **IVF/ICSI (in vitro fertilisation/intracytoplasmic sperm injection)**. Bank sperm in AZFc deletion.
- Spermatogenesis takes about 64 days, so judge response (reassess) after 3–6 months.

**Diagram to draw:** hypothalamic–pituitary–testis axis with sites of treatment (treatment sites).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - WHO 2021: concentration 16 million/mL, progressive motility 30%; severe <5 million/mL.
> - Karyotype and Y-microdeletion if under 5 million/mL.
> - Repair palpable varicoceles only; never give testosterone.

## Sperm retrieval techniques
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 3, Q3b

### Indications
- **OA (obstructive azoospermia)**: CBAVD (congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens), unreconstructable obstruction, failed or declined vasectomy reversal, or at the time of reconstruction (back-up).
- **NOA (non-obstructive azoospermia)**: testicular failure; only testicular retrieval works.
- Anejaculation (spinal cord injury): vibratory stimulation or electroejaculation first.
- All need **ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection)**; cryopreserve spare sperm.

### Techniques
| Technique | Method | Best for | Drawback |
|---|---|---|---|
| **PESA (percutaneous epididymal sperm aspiration)** | Needle into caput under local | OA | Few sperm, may obstruct epididymis |
| **MESA (microsurgical epididymal sperm aspiration)** | Single tubule opened at 16–25×; micropipette by capillary action | OA (best) | Microsurgical skill |
| **TESA (testicular sperm aspiration)** | 23 G needle, suction, many passes | OA | Immotile sperm, small yield; poor in NOA |
| **TESE (testicular sperm extraction)** | Open biopsy, one or several sites | OA, NOA | Blind sampling |
| **Micro-TESE** | Testis bivalved; larger opaque tubules picked under the microscope | **NOA (best)** | Long operation |

- **MESA points**: move proximally until motile sperm appear (most motile in efferent ductules); 4–12 pipettes, frozen in aliquots.
- **Micro-TESE logic (Schlegel 1999)**: spermatogenesis in NOA is **focal**; tubules with sperm are larger and plumper than thin white Sertoli-cell-only tubules. Incision through an avascular plane, then the other testis if negative. It removes the least tissue for the most sperm.

### Results
- OA: retrieval almost always succeeds.
- NOA micro-TESE: sperm in **about half**; better than conventional TESE.

| Condition | Retrieval |
|---|---|
| Post-orchidopexy | 74% |
| AZF (azoospermia factor) c deletion | 70% |
| Klinefelter | 68% |
| Post-chemotherapy | 53% |
| Maturation arrest | 40% |
| Sertoli-cell-only | 30% |
| **AZFa or AZFb deletion** | **0% (do not operate)** |

- NOA pregnancies have more miscarriage (sperm aneuploidy and DNA damage).

### Complications
- Haematoma, infection, epididymal obstruction (PESA), testicular atrophy or transient fall in testosterone (TESE).

**Also know:** testicular sperm may beat ejaculated sperm when DNA fragmentation is high; postmortem retrieval only with prior consent; microfluidic sperm sorting.

**Diagram to draw:** micro-TESE with dilated tubules beside thin Sertoli-cell-only tubules.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - OA: epididymal (MESA); NOA: micro-TESE.
> - Micro-TESE finds sperm in about half of NOA.
> - AZFa/AZFb deletion: no sperm, do not operate.

## Genetic causes of male infertility. Enumerate and classify.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Summer Paper 3, Q3d

### Introduction
- Genetic defects underlie many cases of **azoospermia** and **severe oligozoospermia**. They decide **sperm retrieval odds** and **transmission to offspring** through ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection).

### Classification
| Group | Condition | Key features |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Chromosomal, numerical** | **Klinefelter syndrome (47,XXY)** | **Commonest genetic cause**; 1 in 500–1000; small testes, high FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone), low testosterone, usually azoospermia. Non-mosaic in 80–90%. Testicular sperm in about 50% (micro-TESE, microdissection testicular sperm extraction) |
| | 47,XYY; **46,XX male** (SRY gene translocated to X) | XX male: azoospermia, no sperm retrievable |
| **2. Chromosomal, structural** | **Robertsonian and reciprocal translocations**, inversions, ring Y | Oligozoospermia; recurrent miscarriage |
| **3. Y chromosome microdeletions (Yq, AZF (azoospermia factor))** | **AZFa** (USP9Y, DDX3Y), **AZFb**, **AZFc** (DAZ genes) | Complete AZFa or AZFb deletion: **no sperm at retrieval**. AZFc: sperm often in ejaculate or testis; sons inherit the deletion |
| **4. Single-gene: excurrent ducts** | **CFTR (cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator) mutations**: CBAVD (congenital bilateral absence of vas deferens); idiopathic ejaculatory duct obstruction | Low-volume azoospermia, normal spermatogenesis; **test both partners** (ΔF508) |
| **5. Single-gene: hypothalamic–pituitary** | **Kallmann syndrome** (KAL1, anosmia); GnRH (gonadotropin-releasing hormone) receptor mutations | Low LH (luteinising hormone) and FSH; treat with hCG ± FSH |
| **6. Androgen action** | **Androgen receptor** mutation (long CAG repeat); 5α-reductase type 2 deficiency | High testosterone and LH, poor sperm |
| **7. Sperm structure and motility** | **Globozoospermia** (SPATA16, DPY19L2: no acrosome); macrocephalic sperm (AURKC); **PCD (primary ciliary dyskinesia)**/Kartagener (DNAH5, DNAI1: immotile sperm) | ICSI; macrocephalic sperm: ICSI not advised (aneuploidy) |

### Genetic testing indications
- **Karyotype and Y microdeletion:** non-obstructive azoospermia and severe oligozoospermia (**<5 million/mL**, ASRM/AUA (American Society for Reproductive Medicine/American Urological Association) 2021; EAU (European Association of Urology) uses <10 million/mL for karyotype).
- **CFTR:** CBAVD, idiopathic obstructive azoospermia; screen the partner; counsel before ICSI.
- **Also know:** Noonan syndrome, myotonic dystrophy.

**Diagram to draw:** Y chromosome long arm showing AZFa, b, c.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Klinefelter is the commonest genetic cause; micro-TESE finds sperm in about half.
> - AZFa or AZFb complete deletion: do not attempt retrieval.
> - CBAVD = genital form of cystic fibrosis; test both partners.

## Premature ejaculation.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 3, Q3a

### Definition
- **ISSM (International Society for Sexual Medicine) unified definition (2014):** all three needed:
  1. Ejaculation always or nearly always within **about 1 minute** of vaginal penetration (lifelong), or a clinically significant, bothersome fall in latency, often to **≤3 minutes** (acquired).
  2. Inability to delay ejaculation on all or nearly all penetrations.
  3. Negative personal consequences: distress, frustration, avoidance of intimacy.
- **AUA/SMSNA (American Urological Association/Sexual Medicine Society of North America) 2020:** lifelong PE (premature ejaculation) within about **2 minutes**.
- **Types:** lifelong, acquired, variable and subjective (normal latency).

### Aetiology
- **Lifelong:** neurobiological and genetic; 5-HT2C hyposensitivity and/or 5-HT1A hypersensitivity.
- **Acquired:** performance anxiety, relationship problems, **ED (erectile dysfunction)**, chronic prostatitis, hyperthyroidism, drug withdrawal.

### Evaluation
- Diagnosis is by **history**: latency, control, bother; lifelong or acquired.
- **IELT (intravaginal ejaculatory latency time):** self-estimate in clinic; stopwatch in trials. Population median 5.4 minutes.
- **PEDT (Premature Ejaculation Diagnostic Tool):** 5 items; score ≥11 suggests PE, 9–10 probable PE. Also PEP (Premature Ejaculation Profile) and IPE (Index of Premature Ejaculation).
- IIEF-5 (International Index of Erectile Function) for ED; examination; TSH (thyroid-stimulating hormone) only if suggested.

### Management
- **Psychosexual education** for all; behavioural therapy (stop–start, squeeze).
- Treat the cause in acquired PE (ED, prostatitis, thyroid).

| Drug | Dose and timing |
|---|---|
| **Dapoxetine** (short-acting SSRI [selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor]; only licensed oral drug) | 30–60 mg, 1–3 h before intercourse |
| Paroxetine (strongest delay), sertraline, fluoxetine, citalopram | Daily, off-label; full effect 2–3 weeks; do not stop abruptly |
| Clomipramine | 12.5–50 mg daily or 3–4 h before |
| **Topical lidocaine–prilocaine** (spray licensed in Europe 2014) | 5–30 min before; condom avoids partner numbness |
| PDE5Is (phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitors) | With ED, alone or with SSRI |
| Tramadol | Low evidence; addiction risk |

- SSRIs raise synaptic serotonin, which delays ejaculation. Avoid in bipolar disorder.

**Diagram to draw:** office algorithm (confirm PE, then treat cause or classify lifelong/acquired).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ISSM: about 1 min (lifelong), about 3 min (acquired) plus poor control plus distress.
> - Lifelong is neurobiological; acquired is usually secondary.
> - Dapoxetine 30–60 mg on demand; paroxetine gives the greatest delay.

## Spermatogenesis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 3, Q3d
(also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 1, Q2)

### Definition
- Production of haploid spermatozoa from diploid spermatogonia in the seminiferous tubules: about **64 days** (42–76); about 123 million sperm a day.

### Hormonal control: HPG (hypothalamic–pituitary–gonadal) axis
- Pulsatile **GnRH (gonadotropin-releasing hormone)** → **LH (luteinising hormone)** → Leydig cells → testosterone. **FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone)** → Sertoli cells.
- Feedback: testosterone on LH; **inhibin B** (Sertoli) on FSH.
- **Intratesticular testosterone** is about 50 times serum, held by ABP (androgen-binding protein). Exogenous testosterone suppresses LH and so stops spermatogenesis.

### Sertoli cells and blood–testis barrier
- Nurse cells: nourish germ cells; secrete ABP, inhibin, fluid; phagocytose residual bodies.
- **Sertoli–Sertoli tight junctions** form a **basal** compartment (spermatogonia) and an **adluminal** compartment (spermatocytes, spermatids). This protects immunologically "foreign" post-meiotic cells.

### Phases
| Phase | Events |
|---|---|
| 1. Proliferation | **Ad (dark type A)** stem cells self-renew (GDNF [glial cell line–derived neurotrophic factor] niche); **Ap (pale type A)** divide every 16 days into type B → primary spermatocytes. Daughters remain joined by cytoplasmic bridges |
| 2. Meiosis | Primary spermatocytes cross into the adluminal compartment. Meiosis I (reduction division; synapsis and crossing-over) → secondary spermatocytes → meiosis II → 4 haploid round spermatids |
| 3. Spermiogenesis | No division: acrosome from Golgi, flagellum from centriole, nucleus compacts to 10%, mitochondria form the midpiece, cytoplasm shed |
| 4. Spermiation | Sertoli cell releases sperm into the lumen |
- **Cycle, stage, wave:** six stages in humans; cycles arranged as a helical wave, so production is continuous.

### Post-testicular maturation
- Epididymal transit 2–12 days: sperm gain **progressive motility** and fertilising ability; stored in the cauda. **Capacitation** and acrosome reaction occur in the female tract.

### Clinical relevance
- A semen sample reflects events about 3 months earlier; reassess 3 months after treatment.
- Disruptors: heat (testis normally 2–4 °C below core; varicocele, cryptorchidism), exogenous testosterone, chemotherapy, radiation.
- Defective meiotic recombination causes maturation arrest.

**Diagram to draw:** seminiferous tubule cross-section with basal and adluminal compartments.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - About 64 days; Ap divide every 16 days; six stages.
> - Intratesticular testosterone 50 × serum; FSH → Sertoli, LH → Leydig.
> - Tight junctions between Sertoli cells form the blood–testis barrier.

## Ejacultory duct obstruction
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 2 Paper 3, Q3b

*Assumed meaning: ejaculatory duct obstruction (EDO).*

### Anatomy
- Intraprostatic continuations of the seminal vesicles, joined by the vasal ampullae; they lack muscle and open at the **verumontanum**. Seminal vesicles give about 60% of the ejaculate and its fructose.

### Aetiology
- **Congenital (commonest):** midline (Müllerian/utricular) cyst, Wolffian duct cyst, atresia. Idiopathic EDO carries CFTR (cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator) mutations in about half.
- **Acquired:** infection, instrumentation, duct stones, compression.
- **Zinner syndrome:** seminal vesicle cyst, ipsilateral renal agenesis, EDO.
- Complete bilateral EDO causes under 5% of azoospermia. Partial EDO gives oligoasthenospermia.

### Clinical features
- Infertility, painful ejaculation, haematospermia, perineal pain.

### Diagnosis
**Semen (complete bilateral EDO)**
- Volume **<1.0 mL**, **acidic pH**, low or absent **fructose**, azoospermia.
- Palpable vas, normal FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone) and testes.
- Exclude retrograde ejaculation and CBAVD (congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens).

**Investigations**
| Test | Method / finding |
|---|---|
| **TRUS (transrectal ultrasound)** | Seminal vesicle AP diameter **>1.5 cm**, duct >2.3 mm, midline cyst. Low specificity |
| **Seminal vesicle aspiration** | **≥3 sperm per high-power field** confirms obstruction |
| Vesiculography | Contrast into vesicles; invasive |
| Chromotubation | Indigo carmine into vesicles; dye at the veru on cystoscopy |

### Management
**TURED (transurethral resection of the ejaculatory ducts)**
- For azoospermic or severely oligoasthenospermic men, after the couple declines IVF (in vitro fertilisation).
- **Steps:** TRUS-guided aspiration (cryopreserve sperm) → instil indigo carmine with contrast → 24-Fr resectoscope, finger in rectum → resect the veru until dye effluxes. Spare bladder neck, sphincter and rectum. Catheter overnight.
- **Results:** volume rises in most; sperm appear in about half. Cold-knife incision alone re-obstructs.

**Complications**
- **Urine reflux** into the ducts, **epididymitis**, retrograde ejaculation, rectal injury, re-obstruction.

**Alternatives**
- **Seminal vesiculoscopy** (4–6 Fr via utricle): avoids reflux; restenosis unknown. Sperm retrieval with ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection).

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal prostate with midline cyst compressing the ducts at the veru.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Low volume (<1 mL), acidic, fructose-negative azoospermia with palpable vasa.
> - TRUS: vesicle >1.5 cm; aspiration ≥3 sperm per high-power field confirms.
> - TURED: sperm return in about half; reflux and epididymitis are its price.

## Tadalafil
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 3, Q3b

### Pharmacology
- Selective **PDE5 (phosphodiesterase type 5) inhibitor**; FDA (Food and Drug Administration) approved 2003.
- **Mechanism:** sexual stimulation releases NO (nitric oxide), raising cGMP (cyclic guanosine monophosphate), which relaxes cavernosal smooth muscle. PDE5 breaks down cGMP; tadalafil blocks this. It **augments, not initiates,** erection.
- Structurally distinct from sildenafil; cross-reacts with **PDE11**, little with PDE6 (retina).

| Property | Tadalafil (vs sildenafil) |
|---|---|
| Time to peak | 2 h (0.8 h) |
| Half-life | **17.5 h** (3–5 h) |
| Fatty food | No effect (reduces absorption) |
| Window | Widest (up to 36 h) |

### Uses and doses
1. **ED (erectile dysfunction):** 10–20 mg on demand, or 2.5–5 mg once daily. Intercourse success about 70%; 40–50% in diabetes and after prostatectomy.
2. **BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) with LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms), with or without ED:** **5 mg daily**, the only PDE5 inhibitor licensed. IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) falls about 6 points (placebo 3.6) **without Qmax (peak flow rate) change**; mechanism is NO–cGMP relaxation, perfusion and reduced afferent activity, not de-obstruction. Equal to tamsulosin for IPSS. EAU (European Association of Urology) 2022 and AUA (American Urological Association) 2021: option for moderate–severe LUTS.
3. **Also know:** fixed finasteride–tadalafil (FDA 2021); penile rehabilitation (benefit unproven); prevention of stuttering priapism (PDE5I paradox); pulmonary arterial hypertension.

### Adverse effects
- Headache, dyspepsia, **back pain and myalgia** (characteristic), flushing, nasal congestion. Visual effects rare.
- NAION (non-arteritic anterior ischaemic optic neuropathy): no proven link; stop if sudden visual loss.

### Contraindications and interactions
- **Nitrates in any form: absolute.** After tadalafil, no nitrates for **48 h** (24 h for sildenafil).
- Caution: α-blockers (start lowest dose of the added drug), aortic stenosis, hypotension. CYP3A4 inhibitors (ketoconazole, ritonavir) raise levels.
- Not studied: MI (myocardial infarction) or stroke within 6 months, unstable angina, BP <90/50 or >170/100 mmHg, retinitis pigmentosa.

**Diagram to draw:** NO–cGMP–PDE5 pathway in cavernosal smooth muscle.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Half-life 17.5 h; food-independent; back pain.
> - 5 mg daily for LUTS: IPSS improves, Qmax does not.
> - No nitrates for 48 h after a dose.

## Retrograde ejaculation
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 3, Q3f

### Definition
- RE (retrograde ejaculation): semen passes backwards into the bladder at orgasm because the **bladder neck fails to close**; orgasm is preserved, ejaculate absent or low volume.

### Physiology
| Phase | Event | Control |
|---|---|---|
| Emission | Vas, seminal vesicles, prostate deliver semen to posterior urethra | Sympathetic T10–L2 |
| Ejection | **Bladder neck closes**, external sphincter relaxes, bulbospongiosus contracts | Somatic S2–S4 + sympathetic reflex |
| Orgasm | Cerebral perception | Cortical |

### Causes
- **Anatomical:** **TURP (transurethral resection of prostate)** 42–100%, TUIP (incision) 5–45%; most other resections and enucleations; previous bladder neck reconstruction. Ejaculation-sparing techniques, PUL (prostatic urethral lift) and Aquablation preserve ejaculation.
- **Neurogenic:** **diabetic autonomic neuropathy** (commonest medical), RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection; less with nerve sparing), colorectal surgery, sympathectomy, aortic surgery, spinal cord injury, multiple sclerosis.
- **Drugs:** α-blockers (silodosin, tamsulosin), antipsychotics, antidepressants, thiazides.
- **Congenital:** Müllerian duct cyst, Wolffian duct anomaly, prune belly syndrome.

### Evaluation
- Low-volume or absent ejaculate with orgasm; infertility.
- **Post-ejaculatory urinalysis:** void, ejaculate, void again; centrifuge. **>5–10 sperm per HPF (high-power field)** confirms RE; significant if urinary sperm approach or exceed antegrade sperm.
- Sperm and fructose in urine separate RE from **failure of emission**; TRUS (transrectal ultrasound) if EDO (ejaculatory duct obstruction) suspected.

### Treatment
- Reassure if fertility not desired; stop offending drug.
- **Sympathomimetics** (close bladder neck): **pseudoephedrine** 60–120 mg (120 mg 6-hourly the day before and twice on the day); midodrine, ephedrine, phenylpropanolamine. Side effects: dry mouth, hypertension.
- **Imipramine** 25 mg twice daily; antihistamines (brompheniramine) add to sympathomimetics.
- **Bladder neck reconstruction:** poor results; collagen injection experimental.
- **Sperm retrieval:** alkalinise urine (sodium bicarbonate), void or catheterise after orgasm, wash sperm in buffer → IUI (intrauterine insemination), IVF (in vitro fertilisation) or ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection).
- **Prevention:** nerve-sparing RPLND, ejaculation-preserving procedures for BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia).

**Diagram to draw:** bladder neck open at ejaculation with semen entering the bladder.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - TURP and diabetic neuropathy are the commonest causes.
> - Post-ejaculatory urine: sperm and fructose confirm RE.
> - Pseudoephedrine first; else urinary sperm retrieval for assisted reproduction.

## Immunobead test for semen
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 4 Paper 3, Q3d

### Introduction
- The IBT (immunobead test) detects **antisperm antibodies (ASA)** bound to sperm (direct) or free in body fluids (indirect).
- **Pathogenesis:** Sertoli tight junctions form the blood–testis barrier. If it is breached, sperm antigens meet the immune system. ASA cause **agglutination, poor motility, poor cervical-mucus penetration and impaired fertilisation**.
- **Causes:** vasectomy and other obstruction; orchitis, prostatitis, STI (sexually transmitted infection); varicocele, cryptorchidism, heat; trauma, torsion; genetic (HLA-B28).

### Indications
- Sperm agglutination or isolated asthenozoospermia; unexplained infertility; after vasectomy reversal.

### Principle and method
- Polyacrylamide beads coated with rabbit anti-human **IgG, IgA or IgM**.

| Test | Method | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **Direct IBT** | Patient's motile sperm washed free of seminal plasma; mixed with beads; motile sperm with attached beads counted | Sperm-bound antibody, class and site |
| **Indirect IBT** | Antibody-free donor sperm incubated with heat-inactivated patient serum, seminal plasma or female serum/cervical mucus; then direct IBT done | Free antibody; patient sperm too few or immotile; female partner |
| **MAR (mixed antiglobulin reaction)** | Fresh unwashed semen + IgG (or IgA)-coated latex beads + anti-IgG; mixed sperm–bead clumps counted | Quick, cheap screen; IgG/IgA only |
