- Report: **% of motile sperm with beads**, immunoglobulin class, and **site** (head, midpiece, tail). Tail-tip binding is ignored.
- **WHO 2010 (5th edition) threshold: ≥50% of motile sperm bound is clinically significant.**
- **Head binding** and **IgA** matter most (IgA is produced locally and blocks mucus penetration; IgM is too large to enter semen).
- Both direct tests need motile sperm. **Also know:** ELISA (enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay) and flow cytometry; serum tests correlate poorly with sperm-bound antibody.

### Management
- Condoms and sperm washing lack good evidence (washing removes unbound, not bound, antibody).
- Corticosteroids: two controlled trials conflict.
- IUI for mild binding; **IVF with ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection)** is the usual treatment, bypassing antibody effects.

**Diagram to draw:** motile sperm with beads attached at head and tail.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Direct IBT = sperm-bound antibody; indirect = antibody in serum, seminal plasma or mucus.
> - ≥50% motile sperm bound is significant; head-bound IgA matters most.
> - Treatment of choice: ICSI.

## CBAVD
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 4 Paper 3, Q3f

### Definition
- **CBAVD (congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens):** both vasa absent, causing **obstructive azoospermia** with normal spermatogenesis.
- Often a **genital form of CF (cystic fibrosis)**: nearly all men with CF lack both vasa.

### Genetics
- **CFTR (cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator)**: chloride channel gene; more than 1600 mutations.
- Severe mutation on both alleles: CF. **Mild mutation on one or both alleles: CBAVD.** Commonest severe mutation **ΔF508**; the **5T variant** (intron 8) is a common mild allele.
- A minority have other mesonephric duct gene defects instead, with more **renal agenesis**.

### Clinical features
- **Vasa not palpable** (Meacham: a presumed vas slipping away three times is absent).
- Caput epididymis present and distended; body and tail often absent.
- Normal testis size, FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone) and testosterone.
- Seminal vesicles absent or hypoplastic: **semen volume <1.0 mL, acidic, fructose-negative**, azoospermic.
- Sinus or chest symptoms may reveal mild CF.

### Evaluation
- Semen analysis; FSH; **TRUS (transrectal ultrasound)** for seminal vesicles.
- **Renal ultrasound** (renal agenesis in about 1 in 10).
- **CFTR testing of the man and his female partner** (carrier rate about 1 in 20 in northern Europeans). Panels cover 25–40 mutations, so a negative panel leaves residual risk.
- Testis biopsy unnecessary when FSH and testis size are normal.

### Management
- **Genetic counselling**: if the partner carries a mutation, offspring may have CF; PGT (preimplantation genetic testing) or donor sperm can be offered.
- Reconstruction is impossible, so **surgical sperm retrieval + ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection)**:

| Technique | Points |
|---|---|
| **MESA (microsurgical epididymal sperm aspiration)** | Best: many motile sperm; cryopreserve several vials |
| PESA (percutaneous epididymal aspiration) | Local anaesthetic; fewer sperm |
| TESA/TESE (testicular aspiration/extraction) | If epididymal retrieval fails |
- ICSI results are good; one study reported more miscarriage and stillbirth.
- **Also know:** CUAVD (unilateral absence) is a Wolffian duct defect with ipsilateral renal agenesis.

**Diagram to draw:** absent vas and seminal vesicle with intact testis and caput epididymis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - CBAVD = mild CFTR mutation; test both partners.
> - Low-volume, acidic, fructose-negative azoospermia; normal FSH.
> - Renal ultrasound; MESA + ICSI.

## Sertoli cell only syndrome
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 8 Paper 3, Q3a
(non-obstructive azoospermia also answered in 2025-Winter Paper 2, Q3a)

### Definition
- SCOS (Sertoli cell only syndrome), **germ cell aplasia** (Del Castillo syndrome): seminiferous tubules lined only by Sertoli cells, with **complete loss of spermatogonia**. Leydig cells usually normal.
- The worst histological pattern of NOA (non-obstructive azoospermia).

### Types
| Type | Feature |
|---|---|
| Pure (complete, congenital) | All tubules lack germ cells |
| Mixed (focal, acquired) | Some tubules keep foci of spermatogenesis, the basis of sperm retrieval |

### Aetiology
- **Developmental:** primordial germ cells fail to migrate into the testis.
- **Genetic:** **complete AZFa (azoospermia factor a) deletion** gives pure SCOS; **AZFc** may give SCOS with foci; Klinefelter (47,XXY).
- **Acquired:** cryptorchidism, chemotherapy or radiation, mumps orchitis, torsion; idiopathic.

### Clinical features
- Normal virilisation; **azoospermia** with normal ejaculate volume; small soft testes.

### Evaluation
- Semen: azoospermia on two centrifuged samples.
- **FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone) high**, **inhibin B low** (Sertoli marker); LH (luteinising hormone) normal or high; testosterone normal or low.
- **FSH >7.6 IU/L and testis long axis ≤4.6 cm** suggest NOA.
- **Karyotype and Y microdeletion** before surgery.
- **Histology** confirms, usually at sperm retrieval.

| Pattern | Meaning | Micro-TESE retrieval |
|---|---|---|
| Hypospermatogenesis | All stages, fewer | Best |
| Maturation arrest | Stops at one stage | About 40% |
| **SCOS** | No germ cells | **About 30%** |

### Management
- **Counsel** on genetic findings.
- **Complete AZFa or AZFb deletion:** retrieval essentially nil; do not operate; donor sperm or adoption.
- **Micro-TESE (microdissection testicular sperm extraction):** at ×20–25, **larger, opaque, yellow tubules** (foci of spermatogenesis) are selected; SCOS tubules are thin, white and stringy. Live births are reported even in SCOS.
- Sperm used for **ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection)**.
- Optimise first: stop exogenous testosterone; hormonal stimulation evidence weak.
- **Also know:** spermatogonial stem cell transplantation and in-vitro spermatogenesis are experimental.

**Diagram to draw:** tubule cross-section: normal layered epithelium versus Sertoli cells only.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Complete loss of spermatogonia; high FSH, low inhibin B.
> - Complete AZFa deletion: pure SCOS, no retrieval.
> - Micro-TESE retrieval about 30%: worst histology, not hopeless.

## Oxidative stress and male infertility
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 8 Paper 3, Q3b

### Definition
- **Oxidative stress (OS):** imbalance where ROS (reactive oxygen species) exceed the antioxidant capacity of semen. The term **MOSI (male oxidative stress infertility)** has been proposed.
- Low ROS levels are **physiological**: needed for capacitation, hyperactivation, acrosome reaction and sperm–oocyte fusion.

### Sources of ROS
| Source | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Leucocytes** (main) | Infection, prostatitis, epididymitis, leucocytospermia |
| **Immature sperm** | Excess residual cytoplasm |
| Varicocele | Heat and hypoxia |
| Lifestyle | **Smoking**, alcohol, obesity, heat |
| Others | Ageing, diabetes, pollutants, radiation |

### Why sperm are vulnerable
- Membrane rich in **polyunsaturated fatty acids** → lipid peroxidation.
- Little cytoplasm, so few antioxidant enzymes; limited DNA repair.
- **Seminal plasma antioxidants:** superoxide dismutase, catalase, glutathione peroxidase; taurine, vitamins C and E, zinc.

### Effects
- Lipid peroxidation → loss of **motility** and membrane fluidity.
- **Sperm DNA fragmentation** → poor embryo quality, miscarriage, lower ART (assisted reproductive technology) success.
- Mitochondrial damage and apoptosis.

### Evaluation
- Semen analysis: peroxidase-positive WBC (white blood cells) **>1 million/mL** = leucocytospermia.
- **Direct ROS:** chemiluminescence (luminol).
- **Indirect:** total antioxidant capacity, malondialdehyde (lipid peroxidation), ORP (oxidation-reduction potential).
- **DNA fragmentation tests:** TUNEL (terminal deoxynucleotidyl transferase dUTP nick-end labelling), SCSA (sperm chromatin structure assay), SCD (sperm chromatin dispersion), Comet.
- Use selectively (unexplained infertility, recurrent miscarriage, failed ART); not routine.

### Management
- **Lifestyle:** stop smoking, reduce weight and alcohol, avoid heat.
- **Treat cause:** antibiotics for infection; **varicocelectomy** for clinical varicocele (reduces DNA fragmentation).
- **Antioxidants** (vitamins C and E, CoQ10 [coenzyme Q10], zinc, selenium, carnitine): Cochrane review, low-quality evidence; **MOXI trial (2020)** showed no gain in semen parameters, DNA fragmentation or live birth. Avoid excess: **reductive stress** also harms.
- High DNA fragmentation with failed ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection): **testicular sperm** has less fragmentation.

**Diagram to draw:** balance of ROS sources against seminal antioxidants, with effects on sperm.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Leucocytes and immature sperm are main ROS sources.
> - Lipid peroxidation and DNA fragmentation are the key injuries.
> - Leucocytospermia >1 million/mL; antioxidant benefit unproven (MOXI).

## Dhat syndrome
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 8 Paper 3, Q3c

### Definition
- **Dhat syndrome:** a **culture-bound syndrome** of South Asia. The man believes he is losing **semen ("dhat")** in urine or nocturnal emissions, and attributes weakness, fatigue and sexual problems to it.
- Term coined by **Wig** (India); listed in ICD-10 (International Classification of Diseases, 10th revision) and in the DSM-5 (Diagnostic and Statistical Manual, 5th edition) glossary of cultural concepts of distress.
- Related syndromes: **Shen-kui** (China), **Koro** (genital retraction).

### Cultural basis
- Traditional belief that semen is a **precious vital fluid** made from many drops of blood; its loss is felt to drain body and mind.

### Epidemiology
- Young men, often unmarried or newly married, rural, lower education; common in urology and sexual medicine clinics.

### Clinical features
- **Somatic:** fatigue, weakness, aches, poor appetite.
- **Psychological:** anxiety, low mood, guilt (about masturbation), poor concentration, sleep loss.
- **Sexual:** fear of ED (erectile dysfunction) or PE (premature ejaculation), worry about penis size and fertility.
- Whitish urine is usually phosphaturia, oxaluria or normal secretions.
- **Comorbidity:** **depression** and **anxiety** in many; somatisation.

### Evaluation
- Non-judgmental sexual history and belief system.
- Examination of genitalia; urine microscopy (phosphates, oxalates, sperm, pus cells).
- Exclude organic disease: **UTI (urinary tract infection), prostatitis, STI (sexually transmitted infection), diabetes**, true spermatorrhoea.
- Screen for depression and anxiety.

### Management
**1. Counselling (main)**
- **Sex education:** semen is continuously produced; nocturnal emission and masturbation are normal and harmless.
- Reassure without dismissing.

**2. Psychological therapy:** CBT (cognitive behavioural therapy) for misbeliefs and anxiety.

**3. Drugs**
- **SSRIs (selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors)** for depression, anxiety or associated PE; short anxiolytic course if needed.
- Treat genuine ED or PE.

**4. Avoid harm**
- Avoid unnecessary "tonics", antibiotics and repeated tests; they reinforce the belief.
- Refer to psychiatry when depression is significant.

### Prognosis
- Good with counselling; drop-out is common.

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: complaint of semen loss → exclude organic cause → counsel → treat depression or anxiety.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Culture-bound semen-loss anxiety of South Asia.
> - Depression and anxiety common; exclude UTI, prostatitis, STI.
> - Counselling and sex education first; SSRIs for comorbidity.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 9 · Andrology & Sexual Medicine

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Priapism: Diagnosis and Management (LAQ)

- 36 h (beyond 36 h) or refractory → early penile prosthesis (or analgesia and counselling)
- Blood gas decides the type: ischaemic pO2 <30, pCO2 >60, pH <7.25.
- Aspiration plus phenylephrine first; distal shunt ± tunnelling next.
- ED risk 0% ≤12 h, 100% ≥36 h (beyond 36 h); consider early prosthesis beyond 36 h.
- High flow: observe, then embolise; repeat embolisation rather than surgery.

#### Types of penile prosthesis. What are biofilms in penile prosthesis? (SAQ)

- 3-piece inflatable gives best rigidity and flaccidity.
- Biofilm: microbes in a matrix; antibiotics fail.
- Infection 1–3%; Mulcahy salvage 84%.

#### Oligospermia and oligoasthenozoospermia (SAQ)

- WHO 2021: concentration 16 million/mL, progressive motility 30%; severe <5 million/mL.
- Karyotype and Y-microdeletion if under 5 million/mL.
- Repair palpable varicoceles only; never give testosterone.

#### Sperm retrieval techniques (SAQ)

- OA: epididymal (MESA); NOA: micro-TESE.
- Micro-TESE finds sperm in about half of NOA.
- AZFa/AZFb deletion: no sperm, do not operate.

#### Genetic causes of male infertility. Enumerate and classify. (SAQ)

- Klinefelter is the commonest genetic cause; micro-TESE finds sperm in about half.
- AZFa or AZFb complete deletion: do not attempt retrieval.
- CBAVD = genital form of cystic fibrosis; test both partners.

#### Premature ejaculation. (SAQ)

- ISSM: about 1 min (lifelong), about 3 min (acquired) plus poor control plus distress.
- Lifelong is neurobiological; acquired is usually secondary.
- Dapoxetine 30–60 mg on demand; paroxetine gives the greatest delay.

#### Spermatogenesis (SAQ)

- About 64 days; Ap divide every 16 days; six stages.
- Intratesticular testosterone 50 × serum; FSH → Sertoli, LH → Leydig.
- Tight junctions between Sertoli cells form the blood–testis barrier.

#### Ejacultory duct obstruction (SAQ)

- Low volume (<1 mL), acidic, fructose-negative azoospermia with palpable vasa.
- TRUS: vesicle >1.5 cm; aspiration ≥3 sperm per high-power field confirms.
- TURED: sperm return in about half; reflux and epididymitis are its price.

#### Tadalafil (SAQ)

- Half-life 17.5 h; food-independent; back pain.
- 5 mg daily for LUTS: IPSS improves, Qmax does not.
- No nitrates for 48 h after a dose.

#### Retrograde ejaculation (SAQ)

- TURP and diabetic neuropathy are the commonest causes.
- Post-ejaculatory urine: sperm and fructose confirm RE.
- Pseudoephedrine first; else urinary sperm retrieval for assisted reproduction.

#### Immunobead test for semen (SAQ)

- Direct IBT = sperm-bound antibody; indirect = antibody in serum, seminal plasma or mucus.
- ≥50% motile sperm bound is significant; head-bound IgA matters most.
- Treatment of choice: ICSI.

#### CBAVD (SAQ)

- CBAVD = mild CFTR mutation; test both partners.
- Low-volume, acidic, fructose-negative azoospermia; normal FSH.
- Renal ultrasound; MESA + ICSI.

#### Sertoli cell only syndrome (SAQ)

- Complete loss of spermatogonia; high FSH, low inhibin B.
- Complete AZFa deletion: pure SCOS, no retrieval.
- Micro-TESE retrieval about 30%: worst histology, not hopeless.

#### Oxidative stress and male infertility (SAQ)

- Leucocytes and immature sperm are main ROS sources.
- Lipid peroxidation and DNA fragmentation are the key injuries.
- Leucocytospermia >1 million/mL; antioxidant benefit unproven (MOXI).

#### Dhat syndrome (SAQ)

- Culture-bound semen-loss anxiety of South Asia.
- Depression and anxiety common; exclude UTI, prostatitis, STI.
- Counselling and sex education first; SSRIs for comorbidity.

# Section 10 · Testis, Penis & Urethra — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Urethral stricture: classification, DVIU and surgery after failed VIU
**Asked as:**
- Classify urethral strictures. Discuss in brief, surgical management of stricture after failed VIU — *Summer 2017 Paper III Q1 (LAQ)*
- DVIU (Direct Visual Internal Urethrotomy). — *Winter 2017 Paper III Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

### Definition
- A **urethral stricture** is a fixed narrowing of the **anterior** urethra (inside the corpus spongiosum) that will not accept instruments without tearing the mucosa. It is an epithelial scar with variable **spongiofibrosis**.
- **VIU (visual internal urethrotomy)**, also called DVIU (direct vision internal urethrotomy), cuts (incision of) the stricture scar into healthy tissue under vision (Sachse cold-knife urethrotome), so the lumen heals enlarged, and lets it heal (healing is) by **secondary intention**: a **race between epithelialisation and wound contraction**. It fails (the stricture recurs) when contraction wins, which is usual when spongiofibrosis is deep. Deep spongiofibrosis cannot be cured endoscopically.

### Classification
**1. By site (WHO (World Health Organization) 2004 / ICUD (International Consultation on Urological Diseases) 2010 terminology)**
| Segment | Term |
|---|---|
| Anterior: meatus, fossa navicularis, penile (pendulous), bulbar | **Stricture** |
| Membranous urethra after pelvic fracture | **PFUI (pelvic fracture urethral injury)** / distraction defect |
| Posterior urethra after prostatectomy or radiotherapy; bladder neck | **Stenosis or contracture** |

**2. By aetiology**
| Category | Examples |
|---|---|
| Idiopathic (commonest, with iatrogenic) | No cause found |
| Iatrogenic | Catheters, TURP (transurethral resection of prostate), cystoscopy, hypospadias repair, radiotherapy |
| Traumatic | Straddle injury (bulbar); pelvic fracture (PFUI) |
| Inflammatory | **Lichen sclerosus (LS)**; gonorrhoea (now rare) |
| Congenital | Rarest; infants only |

**3. By length:** short (up to about 2 cm), long, multifocal, **panurethral** (whole anterior urethra).

**4. By depth (Jordan):** (A) mucosal fold, (B) iris constriction, (C) full-thickness with minimal spongiofibrosis, (D) full-thickness spongiofibrosis, (E) fibrosis beyond the spongiosum, (F) complex stricture with fistula or abscess.

**5. By complexity:** simple (short, bulbar, first treatment) versus complex (long, multiple, LS, radiation, failed previous treatment, fistula).
- **PFUI** (Ch 125): major distraction, minor distraction, partial injury.
- **Also know:** **LSE system** (Lumen calibre, Segment involved, Etiology) for standard reporting.

### DVIU: indications (favourable)
| Favourable | Unfavourable |
|---|---|
| **Bulbar** | Penile, fossa navicularis, any non-bulbar |
| **Short (<1.5–2 cm)** | Longer |
| Superficial, no dense spongiofibrosis | Dense scar (straddle, LS (lichen sclerosus)) |
| **First procedure** | Repeat; luminal narrowing >74% |

### DVIU: technique
1. RUG (retrograde urethrogram) and VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) define length and site.
2. Saline irrigation; **guidewire** through the stricture.
3. **Cold-knife incision traditionally at 12 o'clock** through scar to healthy spongiosum. Campbell notes the spongiosum is thinnest from 10 to 2 o'clock; deep cuts enter the corpora (ED (erectile dysfunction), fistula).
4. Catheter **3–7 days**; longer catheterisation adds no benefit.
- **Laser** (holmium, Nd:YAG (neodymium:yttrium–aluminium–garnet), others): **no advantage over cold knife**.

### DVIU: adjuncts and complications
- **CISC (clean intermittent self-catheterisation)/self-dilation**, tapering over 3–6 months: delays recurrence, which returns when stopped.
- Mitomycin C, steroid injection (limited data); UroLume withdrawn.
- **Recurrence** (commonest); bleeding (often with erections); **irrigant extravasation**; UTI (urinary tract infection), sepsis; ED; spongio-cavernous fistula; incontinence if sphincter incised.

### Why repeat VIU is futile after failure (outcomes)
- VIU succeeds only in **bulbar strictures <1.5 cm without dense spongiofibrosis, at the first attempt**. Recurrence shows deep spongiofibrosis, which an incision cannot remove.
- **Steenkamp 1997, Heyns 1998 (randomised trials):** dilation = urethrotomy; a second urethrotomy helps few, a third none. **A second urethrotomy adds virtually nothing.**
- **Santucci & Eisenberg (2010):** about **8% stricture-free** long term after a first DVIU; repeat procedures almost always fail.
- Each (repeat) dilation or urethrotomy lengthens the scar and lowers later urethroplasty success; urethroplasty is more **cost-effective** after one failure. The "reconstructive ladder" is abandoned.
- Guideline position: AUA (American Urological Association) guideline (2016): DVIU or dilation may be offered for **short (<2 cm) bulbar** strictures; offer **urethroplasty, not repeated endoscopy**, after failed dilation or DVIU; no permanent stents.

### Pre-operative evaluation
- Define the **4 Ls: location, length, depth, density**.
- **Dynamic RUG (retrograde urethrogram) + VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram)**, flexible urethroscopy, sonourethrography; MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) for PFUI.
- **Urethral rest**: suprapubic catheter for 6–8 weeks lets the true length show. Avoid instrumentation before surgery.
- Treat infection; look for LS at the meatus; examine the mouth; record erectile function.

### Surgical options after failed VIU
| Stricture | Operation | Principle |
|---|---|---|
| **Bulbar, short (1–2 cm, up to 3–5 cm proximally)** | **EPA (excision and primary anastomosis)**: gold standard, about 90–95% success | Excise all scar, spatulate both ends widely, tension-free anastomosis. **Non-transecting** or **vessel-sparing** variants keep bulbar arteries |
| **Bulbar, longer** | **BMG (buccal mucosal graft) onlay**: dorsal (**Barbagli**), ventral, dorsal inlay (**Asopa**); Kulkarni one-sided | Graft spread-fixed on a well-vascular bed (corpora or spongiosum) |
| Bulbar with an obliterated segment | **Augmented anastomosis** | Excise the narrowest part, anastomose the floor, graft the roof |
| **Penile** | One-stage BMG inlay or onlay; **penile skin flap** (Orandi, Quartey, circular) | Avoid EPA here (chordee) |
| **LS, failed hypospadias, infection** | **Staged repair** (Johanson): graft, then tubularise after about 6 months | Oral mucosa, not genital skin, in LS |
| **Panurethral** | Kulkarni one-stage dorsal graft, or staged | — |
| **Elderly, unfit, multiple failures** | **Perineal urethrostomy** (BMG-augmented) | Simple, durable bypass |
| **PFUI after failed VIU** | Delayed **perineal anastomotic urethroplasty** | Bulbar mobilisation, then intracrural separation, inferior pubectomy, rerouting |

- **Onlay is better than tube.** Graft and flap results are equal; grafts are simpler.
- **Not fit or declines surgery:** **Optilume** paclitaxel-coated balloon (FDA (Food and Drug Administration) 2023) (**ROBUST III**: stricture-free 74.6% vs 26.8% at 1 year after repeated failed endoscopy); intermittent self-dilation; long-term suprapubic catheter.
- **Urethral stents** (UroLume) are withdrawn.

### Complications
- Recurrence, chordee, ED (erectile dysfunction; similar to circumcision overall), post-void dribble, graft-site pain and numbness.

### Follow-up
- Voiding cystourethrogram at catheter removal (about 3 weeks); then uroflow, PVR (post-void residual) and symptom score; urethroscopy if flow falls. EPA recurrences occur mostly in the first 6 months; graft results decline with time.

### Recent advances (recent advance)
- Tissue-engineered oral mucosa; robotic posterior repair.
- **Optilume** paclitaxel-coated balloon: **ROBUST III** stricture-free 74.6% vs 26.8% at 1 year after failed endoscopy.

**Diagram to draw:** dorsal versus ventral onlay cross-section; EPA with spatulated ends; urethrotome cutting at 12 o'clock over guidewire.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - VIU (DVIU) cures only (is best for) short bulbar strictures (<1.5–2 cm) at first attempt; do not repeat it.
> - Santucci: ~8% long-term success; repeat DVIU futile.
> - AUA: urethroplasty after failure; laser = cold knife.
> - EPA is the gold standard (about 90–95%); BMG onlay for longer bulbar strictures.
> - Staged oral mucosa for LS; perineal urethrostomy for the unfit or multiply failed.
> - ROBUST III: Optilume 74.6% vs 26.8% stricture-free at 1 year.

## Classify testicular tumors and describe management of seminoma
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 3, Q1
(stage I seminoma also answered in 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q2)

### Introduction
- Over 95% of testicular tumours are GCTs (germ cell tumours). Seminoma is 52–56% of GCNIS (germ cell neoplasia in situ)-derived GCTs, peaks in the fourth–fifth decade.
- **Risk factors:** cryptorchidism, previous contralateral GCT (twelvefold), family history, GCNIS, White race, infertility.
- All postpubertal GCTs except spermatocytic tumour carry **isochromosome 12p**.

### WHO (World Health Organization) classification (2016, retained 2022)
| Group | Tumours |
|---|---|
| **1. GCNIS-derived GCTs** | GCNIS; **seminoma**; **NSGCT (non-seminomatous GCT)**: embryonal carcinoma, postpubertal yolk sac tumour, choriocarcinoma and other trophoblastic tumours, postpubertal teratoma, teratoma with somatic-type malignancy; mixed GCT |
| **2. Non-GCNIS-derived GCTs** | **Spermatocytic tumour** (formerly spermatocytic seminoma); prepubertal teratoma, dermoid and epidermoid cyst; prepubertal yolk sac tumour |
| **3. Sex cord–stromal tumours** (<5%) | Leydig cell, Sertoli cell (including large cell calcifying), granulosa cell (adult, juvenile), fibroma–thecoma, mixed |
| **4. Mixed germ cell–sex cord stromal** | Gonadoblastoma (DSD [difference of sex development]) |
| **5. Others** | Lymphoma (commonest testicular tumour in older men), rete testis and collecting duct tumours, adnexal and paratesticular tumours, metastases |

- Any NSGCT element, however small, makes the tumour an NSGCT and is treated as such.
- **Seminoma pathology:** sheets of clear cells with lymphocyte-rich fibrovascular septa. Immunohistochemistry: CD117 and PLAP (placental alkaline phosphatase) positive, CD30 negative. Syncytiotrophoblasts (hCG [human chorionic gonadotropin]) in about 15%. "Anaplastic seminoma" is no longer recognised.
- **Spermatocytic tumour** (<1%): older men, no GCNIS, no i(12p), benign unless sarcomatous.

### Evaluation and staging
- Scrotal ultrasound; AFP (alpha-fetoprotein), β-hCG and LDH (lactate dehydrogenase) before and after orchidectomy. **A raised AFP means NSGCT**, whatever the histology.
- **Radical inguinal orchidectomy** with early cord control at the internal ring; never trans-scrotal. Offer sperm banking and prosthesis.
- CT (computed tomography) of chest, abdomen and pelvis; TNMS (tumour, node, metastasis, serum markers) staging.
- About 80% of seminomas are clinical stage (CS) I; 15–20% CS II (nodes IIA ≤2 cm, IIB 2–5 cm, IIC >5 cm).

### Management of seminoma by stage
**CS I** (cure by orchidectomy alone 80–85%; cancer-specific survival about 100% with any option)

| | Surveillance (**preferred**) | Carboplatin | Radiotherapy |
|---|---|---|---|
| Method | Examination, markers, CT for at least 5 years | 1 cycle **AUC (area under the curve) 7**, dose = 7 × (GFR [glomerular filtration rate] + 25) mg | **20 Gy** para-aortic strip |
| Relapse | 14–20% | About 5% (1 cycle) | 3–5% |
| Drawback | Late relapses (10–20% after 4 years) | Underdosing; late effects unknown | Second cancers, GI (gastrointestinal) toxicity |

- **Relapse risk factors:** tumour **>4 cm** and **rete testis invasion** (relapse 12%, 16%, 32% with 0, 1, 2 factors). LVI (lymphovascular invasion) is not a factor in seminoma.
- **Trials:** MRC TE19/EORTC (Medical Research Council/European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer) 30982: one cycle of carboplatin equals radiotherapy (5-year relapse-free 94.7% vs 96%). MRC TE18: 20 Gy equals 30 Gy.
- Surveillance relapses are 84–100% retroperitoneal and almost all salvaged.

**CS IIA–B**
- **Non-bulky (≤3 cm):** dog-leg radiotherapy with nodal boost (25–30 Gy in the chapter; EAU [European Association of Urology] uses 30 Gy for IIA and 36 Gy for IIB), or chemotherapy, or **primary RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection)** (SEMS trial).
- **Bulky (>3 cm) or multiple nodes:** BEP (bleomycin, etoposide, cisplatin) ×3 or EP (etoposide, cisplatin) ×4.

**CS IIC–III: chemotherapy by IGCCCG (International Germ Cell Cancer Collaborative Group)**
| Risk | Seminoma definition | 5-year survival | Regimen |
|---|---|---|---|
| Good (90%) | Any primary, **no NPVM (non-pulmonary visceral metastases)**, normal AFP, any hCG/LDH | 86% | BEP ×3 or EP ×4 |
| Intermediate (10%) | NPVM present | 72% | BEP ×4 |
| Poor | **None for seminoma** | – | – |
- Carboplatin alone is inferior in advanced disease.

### Post-chemotherapy residual mass
```
Residual mass after chemotherapy
 ├─ <3 cm ─────────────► observe (necrosis about 90%)
 └─ >3 cm ─► FDG-PET (fluorodeoxyglucose positron emission tomography) at ≥6 weeks
               ├─ negative ► observe
               └─ positive ► resection (full template PC-RPLND) or salvage
```
- Surgery is difficult because of desmoplasia; complete resection only 58–74%.

### Relapse and follow-up
- After surveillance or radiotherapy: radiotherapy for small retroperitoneal relapse, otherwise first-line BEP. After chemotherapy: VeIP (vinblastine, ifosfamide, cisplatin) or high-dose chemotherapy.
- Surveillance schedule: review every 2–4 months (years 1–3), 6-monthly (years 4–7), then yearly. CT detects >85% of relapses.

### Recent advances
- Serum **miR-371a-3p** microRNA: sensitive marker of residual disease and relapse.
- MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) for surveillance to cut radiation; primary RPLND for low-volume stage II.

**Diagram to draw:** WHO classification tree (GCNIS-derived vs non-GCNIS-derived vs sex cord–stromal) and the residual-mass flowchart.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - WHO 2016: GCNIS-derived vs non-GCNIS-derived; spermatocytic tumour is separate and benign.
> - CS I risk factors: >4 cm and rete testis invasion; surveillance preferred.
> - Carboplatin AUC 7 ×1 equals radiotherapy (TE19); 20 Gy equals 30 Gy (TE18).
> - No poor-risk seminoma; residual >3 cm → PET at ≥6 weeks.

# Section 10 · Testis, Penis & Urethra — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Uses of buccal mucosa in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 3, Q3b

### Introduction
- **BMG (buccal mucosal graft)** is the workhorse graft for urethral reconstruction. **Humby 1941** (hypospadias); **Bürger 1992** and **El-Kasaby 1993** (stricture).

### Why it works
- Thick, **non-keratinised**, hairless epithelium used to a wet environment.
- **Thin, highly vascular lamina propria**: rapid imbibition and inosculation, so take is excellent. Little contraction; low donor morbidity.
- A graft lives on its bed, so it does best on thick vascular beds.

### Harvest
- Nasotracheal intubation; about 4–6 × 2–2.5 cm per cheek, **1 cm from the commissure**, **avoiding Stensen's duct** (opposite the upper second molar).
- Defat, cut about 10% oversize, fenestrate, quilt to the bed. Alternatives: **lingual**, **lip** (thinner, for the glans).
- Donor problems: pain, numbness, mouth tightness.

### Uses
| Technique | How | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **Dorsal onlay** (Barbagli) | Urethra rotated, dorsal urethrotomy; graft spread-fixed on the corpora | Bulbar; reliable bed |
| **Ventral onlay** | Ventral urethrotomy; graft covered by spongioplasty | Bulbar with healthy spongiosum |
| **Dorsal inlay** (Asopa) | Ventral urethrotomy, dorsal plate incised, graft inlaid | Bulbar and penile, no mobilisation |
| **Kulkarni** | One-sided dissection keeps ventral blood supply; dorsal onlay | Panurethral, one stage |
| **Augmented anastomosis** | Excise the narrowest part, anastomose the floor, graft the roof | Long dense bulbar stricture |
| **Staged** (Johanson, Bracka) | Stage 1 lay open and graft; stage 2 tubularise after 6 months or more | **Lichen sclerosus**, failed hypospadias |
- **Others**: redo hypospadias, BMG perineal urethrostomy, ureteroplasty, bladder neck contracture, female urethral stricture.

### Outcomes
- Bulbar BMG urethroplasty about 85–90%; dorsal equals ventral; graft equals flap; onlay beats tube.
- Results fall with time and in penile, redo and lichen sclerosus cases. **Avoid skin in lichen sclerosus** (it recurs in skin).
- **Also know:** other grafts: full-thickness skin, bladder mucosa (meatal prolapse), meshed split skin (staged only).

### Recent advances
- Tissue-engineered oral mucosa.

**Diagram to draw:** bulbar cross-section with dorsal onlay, ventral onlay and Asopa inlay.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Thin vascular lamina propria gives excellent take.
> - Staged BMG for lichen sclerosus.
> - Bulbar success about 85–90%.

## Varicocele: controversies and present status of treatment
**Asked as:**
- Controversies in management of varicocele. — *Summer 2018 Paper III Q3f (SAQ)*
- Present status of varicocele treatment — *Practice Set 6 Paper III Q3e (SAQ)* (related: 2018-Summer Paper 3, Q3f)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Background
- Dilated pampiniform veins: about **15% of men, 35% with primary and up to 80% (another source: 75–81%) with secondary infertility**; mostly left-sided (taller venous column to the left renal vein; vein enters renal vein 8–10 cm higher; valve failure / valve incompetence).
- Isolated right or non-decompressing varicocele → scan (image) for renal tumour (a mass).
- Harm / **Injury:** **heat** (lost countercurrent exchange), oxidative stress, **sperm DNA fragmentation**; progressive, duration-dependent atrophy; lower testosterone.
- **Grades:** subclinical (Doppler only), I (palpable with Valsalva), II (palpable), III (visible).

### The controversies / current indications
| Question / Situation | Evidence | Current (present) position |
|---|---|---|
| **Does repair improve fertility?** Infertile couple, **palpable** varicocele, abnormal semen | Madgar RCT (randomised controlled trial, 1995): pregnancy 44% versus (vs) 10% at 1 year; meta-analyses mixed | **Repair** **clinical** varicocele with **abnormal semen** in an infertile couple with normal female partner (AUA/ASRM (American Urological Association / American Society for Reproductive Medicine)) |
| **Subclinical varicocele?** | No benefit for semen or pregnancy | **Do not treat** |
| **Normal semen?** | No proven benefit | Not for fertility |
| **Adolescent varicocele** | Testis volume is a surrogate; link to adult fertility unproven | Observe; repair for **testis asymmetry above 20%** (testicular hypotrophy over 20%), pain, or abnormal semen (Tanner 5 or 18 years) |
| **NOA (non-obstructive azoospermia)** | Small series: sperm returns to ejaculate in some | No definitive evidence; may be considered before micro-TESE (microsurgical testicular sperm extraction) |
| **Before IVF/ICSI** (in vitro fertilisation / intracytoplasmic sperm injection) | Repair may improve live-birth rate (improves outcomes) or allow less invasive ART (assisted reproduction / assisted reproductive technology) | Discuss, especially with oligozoospermia |
| **High DNA fragmentation** | Repair lowers fragmentation | Reasonable indication |
| **Hypogonadism** (low testosterone) | Repair raises testosterone in hypogonadal men (repair improves both) | Emerging indication |
| **Pain** | Dull ache on standing; relief in most after microsurgery | Repair after other causes excluded |

### Choice of technique (Techniques)
| Technique | Recurrence | Hydrocele | Comment / Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Microsurgical subinguinal/inguinal** | Lowest (about 1%; **0.5–1%**) | Near 0 (**About 0**) | **Best / Preferred**: artery and lymphatics preserved |
| Retroperitoneal (Palomo) | High with artery spared (15–25%) | High with mass ligation (about 7%) | Children (artery ligation acceptable) |
| Laparoscopic | 3–15% | Up to 12% | Bilateral; serious morbidity possible |
| Embolisation / sclerotherapy | 4–25% (another source: 15–25%) | Nil (0) | Outpatient; technical failure |

- **Microsurgical steps:** micro-Doppler maps the artery; ligate all veins except vasal; preserve artery and lymphatics; deliver testis to ligate gubernacular and external spermatic veins.
- **Complications:** hydrocele (lymphatic ligation, commonest after non-microsurgical repair); artery injury (atrophy); recurrence (diagnose by upright colour Doppler).
- **Predictors of poor result:** recurrence, artery ligation, hydrocele. Earlier repair recovers more.
- **Also know:** isolated right or non-decompressing varicocele → scan for renal tumour; recurrence diagnosed by upright colour Doppler.

**Diagram to draw:** cord cross-section at microsurgery: artery, lymphatics and vas preserved, veins ligated.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Treat (repair) only palpable varicocele with abnormal semen; never subclinical.
> - Madgar 1995: 44% versus (vs) 10% pregnancy.
> - Adolescents: observe; repair for asymmetry above (over) 20%.
> - Microsurgical subinguinal: lowest recurrence (under 1%) and hydrocele (nil).

## Inguinal Block Dissection?
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2020-Summer Paper 3, Q3f

*Taken as ILND (inguinal lymph node dissection), mainly for penile cancer. For VEIL see 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q3d; for nodal strategy see 2017-Summer Paper 2, Q1.*

### Indications
- Penile SCC (squamous cell carcinoma): **proven cN+**; **cN0 high risk** (pT1b or more, G3) if DSNB (dynamic sentinel node biopsy) is positive or unavailable. Also vulval cancer, melanoma.
- **Within 3 months**; aim for **at least 8 nodes per side**. Early ILND: 3-year disease-specific survival about 84% vs 35% if delayed (Kroon).
- Bulky or fixed nodes: chemotherapy first.

### Surgical anatomy
- **Femoral triangle:** inguinal ligament, sartorius (lateral), adductor longus (medial); floor fascia lata.
- **Superficial nodes** (4–25) in **Daseler's five zones**; sentinel nodes mostly **superomedial**.
- **Deep nodes** (1–3) medial to femoral vein; **Cloquet's node** highest. Spread is stepwise, without skips.

### Templates
| Template | Extent |
|---|---|
| **Radical** | Superficial + deep; saphenous often ligated; sartorius transposed |
| **Modified (Catalona)** | Nothing lateral to femoral artery or below fossa ovalis; saphenous spared; positive frozen section → radical |
| Superficial | Above fascia lata only |

### Technique (open radical)
- Frog-leg; incision **2 cm below and parallel** to the ligament.
- Flaps **deep to Camper's fascia** (skin capillaries).
- Divide saphenous at apex; open fascia lata; clear along femoral vessels; **never lateral to the femoral artery** (femoral nerve).
- **Sartorius transposition**; suction drains until <30 mL/day.
- Pelvic dissection if more than 2 inguinal nodes or ENE (extranodal extension).

### Complications
- **Wound infection, flap necrosis**, **lymphocele**, **lymphoedema**, DVT (deep vein thrombosis).
- Reduced by correct flap plane, modified template, saphenous sparing and **VEIL (video-endoscopic inguinal lymphadenectomy)**.

**Diagram to draw:** femoral triangle with Daseler's zones.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ligament, sartorius, adductor longus.
> - Flaps deep to Camper's; stay medial to femoral artery.
> - Within 3 months; at least 8 nodes per side.

## Minimal invasive retroperitoneal lymph node dissection
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2025-Winter Paper 3, Q3f
(cross-referenced from 2025-Winter Paper 4, Q3e)

### Introduction
- **MI-RPLND (minimally invasive retroperitoneal lymph node dissection)** for testicular GCT (germ cell tumour): laparoscopic (1992) or robotic (2006). **It must duplicate open RPLND exactly.**

### Indications
- **Primary:** marker-negative stage I NSGCT (non-seminomatous GCT), especially high-risk IB; small-volume stage II NSGCT or seminoma (1–2 nodes ≤3 cm in the landing zone).
- **Postchemotherapy:** only a small solitary residual mass with little desmoplasia; **open** for large masses or vessel involvement.

### Templates and nerve sparing
- Boundaries: renal hila, ureters, ureter crossing the common iliacs; plus spermatic cord.
- **Primary:** extended **unilateral modified template** with nerve sparing (right: paracaval, precaval, interaortocaval; left: para-aortic, preaortic; both take nodes above the IMA (inferior mesenteric artery)). Enlarged nodes → bilateral.
- **Postchemotherapy:** bilateral full template.
- **Nerve sparing:** identify the sympathetic chains and L1–L4 postganglionic fibres to the hypogastric plexus below the IMA.

### Steps (robotic)
1. Positioning: **modified lateral decubitus** (unilateral) or **supine steep Trendelenburg with peritoneal hammock** (any template).
2. Mobilise colon; Kocherise duodenum (right); divide gonadal vein.
3. **Split-and-roll:** split over the great vessel from common iliac to renal vein; roll packets off.
4. **Divide lumbar vessels** to clear retrocaval and retroaortic tissue.
5. Clip lymphatics (left renal vein); bag each packet.

| Feature | Laparoscopic | Robotic |
|---|---|---|
| Retraction | Assistant | Fourth arm |
| Instruments | Straight | Wristed |
| Suturing, vessel control | Difficult | Easier |

### Outcomes
- Stay 1–3 days, blood loss mostly ≤200 mL; conversion about 5%; **antegrade ejaculation ≥90%**; chylous ascites as open. Node yields approach open (about 30).
- **Major bleeding:** pressure, raise pneumoperitoneum, convert if uncontrolled (open set ready).
- Concerns: staging-only dissections, frequent adjuvant chemotherapy, unindicated surgery, catastrophic bleeding, follow-up <5 years; no randomised trials.
- **Guidelines:** AUA (American Urological Association): experienced surgeons may offer it; EAU (European Association of Urology): specialist centres only.

**Also know:** serum miR-371a-3p for selection; RPLND for stage II seminoma (SEMS, PRIMETEST).

**Diagram to draw:** right and left modified templates.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Same template, split-and-roll, lumbar division and nerve sparing as open.
> - Best for chemotherapy-naive low-volume disease.
> - Conversion about 5%; ejaculation preserved ≥90%.

## Eponymous urethroplasty techniques and perineal urethrostomy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 8 Paper 3, Q3d
(perineal urethrostomy also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q3c)

### Introduction
- Options: **EPA (excision and primary anastomosis)**, the gold standard for short bulbar strictures; **graft or flap onlay**; **staged repair**.

### Eponymous techniques
| Eponym | Technique | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **Johanson** | Two-stage: stricture laid open, edges sutured to skin (urethrostomy); tubularised later | LS (lichen sclerosus), failed hypospadias, infection |
| **Blandy** | Inverted-U scrotal or perineal skin flap into a ventral urethrotomy | Bulbar strictures; classic perineal urethrostomy (PU) |
| **Turner-Warwick** | Two-stage scrotal inlay; transpubic approach, omental wrap | Complex, posterior |
| **Webster** | Progressive perineal approach: mobilisation, crural separation, inferior pubectomy, rerouting | PFUI (pelvic fracture urethral injury) |
| **Barbagli** | **Dorsal onlay** BMG (buccal mucosal graft) spread-fixed to corpora | Bulbar |
| **Asopa** | **Dorsal inlay** graft through a ventral urethrotomy, no mobilisation | Penile and bulbar |
| **Kulkarni** | One-sided dorsolateral onlay; panurethral repair via perineum with penile invagination | Panurethral (LS) |
| **Orandi** | Ventral longitudinal penile skin island flap on dartos | Pendulous |
| **Quartey** | Showed skin island is a passenger on a fascial (dartos) flap | Basis of all genital skin flaps |
| **McAninch** | Circular fasciocutaneous penile skin flap | Long anterior strictures (up to 15 cm) |
| **Jordan** | Vessel-sparing EPA; ventral transverse island for fossa | Proximal bulbar; fossa navicularis |

- **Also know:** Monseur dorsal urethrotomy; Andrich–Mundy non-transecting anastomosis; Bracka two-stage BMG.
- **Principles:** grafts need a vascular bed; onlay beats tube; graft equals flap.

### Perineal urethrostomy (PU)
- **Definition:** bulbar urethra opened onto perineal skin; the patient voids sitting. It **bypasses** a diseased anterior urethra.
- **Indications:** LS panurethral disease, failed urethroplasties, elderly or high-risk patients, after penectomy.
- **Techniques:**
  - **Classic (Blandy):** inverted-U perineal flap into the opened bulbar urethra.
  - **BMG PU:** dorsal urethrotomy to healthy mucosa; ventral urethra to skin; calibrated to **30 Fr**; BMG on corpora completes the dorsal wall, preventing circumferential scarring.
- **Complications:** stenosis (main), spraying, perineal wetness.

**Diagram to draw:** bulbar cross-section showing dorsal onlay (Barbagli), dorsal inlay (Asopa) and ventral onlay.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Barbagli dorsal onlay; Asopa dorsal inlay; Kulkarni one-sided.
> - Orandi and McAninch flaps; Quartey flap anatomy.
> - Johanson staged; Blandy inverted-U PU; BMG PU at 30 Fr.

## Prognostic index score in carcinoma penis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 8 Paper 3, Q3e

*Assumed meaning: prognostic factors, risk-group systems and nomograms in penile SCC (squamous cell carcinoma). No single universally used "prognostic index score" exists.*

### Introduction
- Prognosis rests on primary histology and stage and, above all, **inguinal node status**.

### 1. Nodal status (most important factor)
| Nodal finding | 5-year survival |
|---|---|
| Node negative | About 73% |
| ≤2 nodes, unilateral, no ENE (extranodal extension) | About 80% |
| >2 nodes | 0–50% |
| **ENE** or **pelvic nodes** | Poor (pelvic about 14%) |

- TNM (tumour-node-metastasis) 8th edition: pN1 (1–2 unilateral nodes) about 90% 3-year DSS (disease-specific survival); pN3 (ENE or pelvic) about one-third.

### 2. Primary tumour factors (predict occult nodes in cN0)
- **Depth:** corporal invasion → about 60% node positive.
- **Grade:** any grade III component = grade III.
- **LVI (lymphovascular invasion)** and **PNI (perineural invasion)**: strongest predictors; define pT1b.
- **Subtype:** verrucous and PeIN (penile intraepithelial neoplasia) do not metastasise; basaloid and sarcomatoid aggressive.

### 3. Risk-group systems
| System | Low | Intermediate | High |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Earlier EAU (European Association of Urology), after Solsona** | Tis, Ta, T1 G1 | T1 G2 | T2 or more, or G3 |
| **Current EAU–ASCO (American Society of Clinical Oncology) / TNM** | PeIN, Ta, **T1a** (nodes 0–18%) | — | **T1b or more** (one-third to over half) |

- Low risk → groin surveillance; high risk → **DSNB (dynamic sentinel node biopsy)** or ILND (inguinal lymph node dissection).

### 4. Nomograms and indices
- **Ficarra nomogram:** predicts nodal involvement (thickness, growth pattern, grade, LVI, corporal invasion, clinical node status).
- **Kattan nomogram:** predicts cancer-specific survival.
- A pathological **prognostic index** (grade + anatomical level of invasion + PNI; Cubilla group) has been proposed.
- **Status:** none externally validated; not used routinely.

### 5. Molecular markers
- **HPV (human papillomavirus)/p16 positive:** better DSS (not node status).
- Adverse: p53, PD-L1 (programmed death-ligand 1), Ki-67, high **NLR** (neutrophil-to-lymphocyte ratio), raised CRP (C-reactive protein).

### 6. Other
- Inguinal recurrence after observation: only about one-third survive 5 years. Size >5 cm worse.

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: primary pathology → low or high risk → surveillance or DSNB/ILND.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Node status is the strongest prognostic factor.
> - LVI, PNI, grade III define high-risk T1b.
> - Nomograms (Ficarra, Kattan) unvalidated; p16 favourable.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 10 · Testis, Penis & Urethra

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Urethral stricture: classification, DVIU and surgery after failed VIU (LAQ)

- VIU (DVIU) cures only (is best for) short bulbar strictures (<1.5–2 cm) at first attempt; do not repeat it.
- Santucci: ~8% long-term success; repeat DVIU futile.
- AUA: urethroplasty after failure; laser = cold knife.
- EPA is the gold standard (about 90–95%); BMG onlay for longer bulbar strictures.
- Staged oral mucosa for LS; perineal urethrostomy for the unfit or multiply failed.
- ROBUST III: Optilume 74.6% vs 26.8% stricture-free at 1 year.

#### Classify testicular tumors and describe management of seminoma (LAQ)

- WHO 2016: GCNIS-derived vs non-GCNIS-derived; spermatocytic tumour is separate and benign.
- CS I risk factors: >4 cm and rete testis invasion; surveillance preferred.
- Carboplatin AUC 7 ×1 equals radiotherapy (TE19); 20 Gy equals 30 Gy (TE18).
- No poor-risk seminoma; residual >3 cm → PET at ≥6 weeks.

#### Uses of buccal mucosa in urology (SAQ)

- Thin vascular lamina propria gives excellent take.
- Staged BMG for lichen sclerosus.
- Bulbar success about 85–90%.

#### Varicocele: controversies and present status of treatment (SAQ)

- Treat (repair) only palpable varicocele with abnormal semen; never subclinical.
- Madgar 1995: 44% versus (vs) 10% pregnancy.
- Adolescents: observe; repair for asymmetry above (over) 20%.
- Microsurgical subinguinal: lowest recurrence (under 1%) and hydrocele (nil).

#### Inguinal Block Dissection? (SAQ)

- Ligament, sartorius, adductor longus.
- Flaps deep to Camper's; stay medial to femoral artery.
- Within 3 months; at least 8 nodes per side.

#### Minimal invasive retroperitoneal lymph node dissection (SAQ)

- Same template, split-and-roll, lumbar division and nerve sparing as open.
- Best for chemotherapy-naive low-volume disease.
- Conversion about 5%; ejaculation preserved ≥90%.

#### Eponymous urethroplasty techniques and perineal urethrostomy (SAQ)

- Barbagli dorsal onlay; Asopa dorsal inlay; Kulkarni one-sided.
- Orandi and McAninch flaps; Quartey flap anatomy.
- Johanson staged; Blandy inverted-U PU; BMG PU at 30 Fr.

#### Prognostic index score in carcinoma penis (SAQ)

- Node status is the strongest prognostic factor.
- LVI, PNI, grade III define high-risk T1b.
- Nomograms (Ficarra, Kattan) unvalidated; p16 favourable.

# Section 11 · Stones, Obstruction & Trauma — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Discuss various ureteric stents. Discuss ureteroscopy with or without stenting.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 3, Q1

### Introduction
- A stent keeps the upper tract draining past obstruction, injury or an anastomosis. **Zimskind 1967** placed the first indwelling splint; **Finney 1978** introduced the self-retaining **double-J (double-pigtail)** stent.

### Types and materials
| Feature | Details |
|---|---|
| Size | 3–8.5 Fr (adult usually 6 Fr); adults 20–30 cm by height, children about age + 10 cm |
| Polymers | Polyurethane, silicone (least encrusting, soft); hydrocoated silicone causes less pain |
| **Metallic (Resonance)** | Resists extrinsic compression: malignant obstruction, retroperitoneal fibrosis |
| Others | Hybrid metal-reinforced, thermo-expandable nitinol (Memokath), biodegradable (experimental) |
| Designs | Single-pigtail diversion stent; open-ended catheter to identify the ureter; modified bladder ends (Polaris loop: no benefit) |

**Also know:** anatomy: three narrowings (PUJ (pelviureteric junction), iliac vessels, intramural ureter, the narrowest at 3–4 mm). Placement: retrograde pyelogram first, stent over a wire under fluoroscopy; antegrade route if retrograde fails.

**Diagram to draw:** double-J stent in situ with both coils and side holes.

### Indications
- Obstruction: infected obstructed kidney (stent equals PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy)), malignancy, stricture, pregnancy.
- After URS (ureteroscopy), pyeloplasty, reimplantation, transplant; injury, leak, fistula; pre-stenting a tight ureter.

### Stent symptoms and complications
- About **4 in 5** have urgency, frequency, haematuria or flank pain, measured by the **USSQ (Ureteral Stent Symptom Questionnaire)**. Cause: **intravesical material irritating the bladder and reflux up the stent**. Diameter and length matter little.
- Relief: **alpha-blockers and antimuscarinics**; intraureteral placement; shortest dwell; opioid-free ERAS (enhanced recovery after surgery) analgesia.
- **Misplacement** (submucosal) and **migration** (too short).
- **Stent failure** from compression, debris or encrustation: worst with malignant or irradiated ureter, bilateral obstruction; then PCN.
- **Encrustation** rises with dwell time, infection and stone-forming urine. **Forgotten stent** causes sepsis and renal loss; keep a register.
- **FECal (Forgotten, Encrusted, Calcified) grading**: I minimal linear encrustation; II circular encrustation; III partial encrustation of one or both coils; IV complete encrustation of one coil; V both coils.
- **Encrusted stent**: never pull by force; NCCT (non-contrast computed tomography); clear the **bladder coil**, then **ureter**, then **renal coil** (flexible URS or PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy)).

### Ureteroscopy
| | Semirigid | Flexible |
|---|---|---|
| Tip | 7 Fr or less | 4.5–7.5 Fr; 3.6 Fr channel |
| Use | Distal and mid ureter | Upper ureter, kidney |
| Deflection | None | Over 270° |

- Segura's dictum: always a safety wire. Tight ureter: stent and return after **2–4 weeks**; routine pre-stenting is unnecessary.
- **UAS (ureteral access sheath)**, usually 12/14 Fr: lowers intrarenal pressure and eases repeated passes, but **severe injury in about 1 in 8**; less after pre-stenting.
- **Ho:YAG (holmium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet, 2120 nm)** or **TFL (thulium fibre laser, about 1940 nm)**. **Dusting** (low energy, high frequency) is faster but leaves fines; **fragment and basket** confirms stone clearance and gives stone for analysis. Keep pressure and temperature low with outflow.

**Steps of URS**: (1) sterile urine, antibiotic dose; (2) cystoscopy and **safety guidewire**; (3) semirigid inspection, dilate only if needed; (4) access sheath over a working wire under fluoroscopy (for flexible URS); (5) low-pressure irrigation, laser, basket; (6) inspect the ureter on withdrawal; (7) decide on stent.
- **Also know:** anti-retropulsion devices; digital and single-use scopes; URS suits patients who cannot stop anticoagulants.
- Complications: sepsis about 5% (treat positive urine culture first), stricture about 3%, bleeding under 1%, perforation (stop and stent), avulsion under 1% (PCN, delayed repair).

### Stent after URS?
- **Denstedt 2001** randomised trial and a Cochrane review: URS is safe without a stent.
- **AUA (American Urological Association)**: stent may be omitted when anatomy and renal function are normal, there is no ureteric injury and no second stage is planned. **EAU (European Association of Urology)** agrees.
- **Stent needed**: perforation, stricture or tight ureter, infection, planned second stage, solitary kidney, impacted stone.
- Removal at 3 days gave more unplanned visits than at 7 days; a string allows home removal. Stenting at SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) does not improve stone passage.

```
Uncomplicated URS -> no stent (or short string stent)
Complicated       -> double-J: injury 2-6 wk; second stage until re-look
```

### Recent advances
- Suction sheaths, intrarenal pressure sensors, biodegradable stents.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Finney 1978 double-J; symptoms from intravesical stent and reflux.
> - Encrusted stent: bladder coil, ureter, then kidney.
> - Denstedt 2001: no routine stent after uncomplicated URS.

## Laparoscopic pyeloplasty.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 3, Q2

### Introduction
- **PUJO (pelviureteric junction obstruction)** is impaired flow from pelvis to ureter that, if untreated, causes progressive renal damage (Koff).
- Causes: **intrinsic** adynamic segment (abnormal circular muscle), **extrinsic** crossing lower-pole vessel, high insertion; secondary (failed repair, stone).
- **Anderson–Hynes (1949) dismembered pyeloplasty** is the gold standard by any route. First laparoscopic pyeloplasty **1993 (Schuessler)**; robotic **2002**. Success **over 90–95%**, equal to open, with less morbidity.

### Indications
- Symptoms (pain, Dietl's crisis), infection, stones, hypertension (rare).
- Impaired split function or **fall of 10% or more** on serial renography; rising APD (anteroposterior diameter); obstructed solitary kidney or bilateral disease.
- Children: most antenatal hydronephrosis resolves; about one-third need surgery.
- **Contraindications**: uncorrected coagulopathy, untreated UTI (urinary tract infection), cardiopulmonary disease barring pneumoperitoneum.
- **Nephrectomy instead** if split function under 15% in adults with a normal other kidney.

### Preoperative evaluation
- **USG (ultrasonography)**: large pelvis, small calyces, ureter not seen.
- **MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) diuretic renogram**: split function and an obstructive curve that does not wash out after furosemide. Usual teaching: washout half-time under 10 minutes normal, 10–20 minutes equivocal, over 20 minutes obstructed (Ch 93 quotes over 40 minutes). Split function under 40% supports repair.
- **CT (computed tomography) angiography**: anatomy, **crossing vessels**, stones.
- **Retrograde pyelogram** at surgery: confirms the level, excludes ureteric stricture.
- **Whitaker test** if equivocal: perfusion 10 mL/min; pelvic pressure over 15–20 cm H2O means obstruction.
- Sterile urine. Drain beforehand (stent or nephrostomy) only for infection, azotaemia or severe pain.

### Approaches
| | Transperitoneal | Retroperitoneal |
|---|---|---|
| Space | Large, familiar landmarks | Small, created by balloon |
| Bowel | Colon reflected (left: **transmesocolic** window) | Not entered; leak stays extraperitoneal |
| Use | Commonest; easier suturing | Avoids bowel; harder orientation |
- One paediatric randomised trial favoured retroperitoneal (shorter stay); overall outcomes similar.

### Steps (transperitoneal Anderson–Hynes)
1. Cystoscopy, retrograde pyelogram, catheter.
2. 45–60° flank; pneumoperitoneum 12–15 mmHg in adults (under 12 mmHg in children); 3–4 ports, camera at umbilicus.
3. Reflect colon medially, or open the mesocolon over the pelvis.
4. Find the ureter; trace it to the PUJ with its periureteric tissue; minimal diathermy. Look for a **crossing vessel**.
5. Hitch stitch on the pelvis; transect the pelvis above the PUJ; **reduce** a redundant pelvis if needed.
6. **Spatulate the ureter laterally** into healthy ureter; keep the narrow segment as a handle (no-touch).
7. **Transpose** the PUJ in front of any crossing vessel.
8. Anastomose the spatulation apex to the most dependent pelvis with 4-0 absorbable (5-0/6-0 in infants): back wall, stent, front wall, then close the pelvis. Watertight, tension-free, funnel-shaped.
9. **Antegrade double-J** over a wire; clamp the catheter: urine from the stent side holes confirms the bladder coil.
10. Excise the narrow segment; drain near (not on) the suture line.

### Crossing vessel
- It lowers endopyelotomy success (42% vs 86%), so pyeloplasty is preferred. **Never divide** a lower-pole vessel (segmental infarction).
- **Vascular hitch (Hellström)**: vessel moved up onto the pelvis, only if the PUJ is intrinsically normal and peristalsing.

### Stenting and postoperative care
- Double-J for **4–6 weeks** (children 1–4 weeks: no difference). Externalised stent in infants avoids a second anaesthetic.
- Foley for 24–48 hours (bladder pressure refluxes up the stent); drain out when dry. If drain output rises after Foley removal, replace the Foley.
- **MAG3 renogram** after stent removal, then ultrasound; follow for years.

### Robotic pyeloplasty
- 3D vision, wristed suturing, tremor filtration, **shorter learning curve**. Now first-line at major centres.
- Success 94.7–100%, the same as laparoscopy; advantage is ergonomic, at higher cost.

### Outcomes
- Laparoscopic: operating time 2–4 hours; complications 2–16%; conversion 0–5.5%; stay about 2.7 vs 4.2 days (open); success 87–99%.
- **Up to 30% of failures appear after 2 years**, so follow-up must be long.

### Complications
- Urine leak and urinoma, stent symptoms or migration, UTI, haematuria, ileus, bowel or vessel injury, port-site hernia, recurrent obstruction.

### Management of failure
```
Early (clot, oedema)        -> nephrostomy, wait
Persistent obstruction      -> CT angiography (missed vessel?)
  short, no vessel          -> endopyelotomy (about 70%)
  otherwise                 -> redo laparoscopic/robotic pyeloplasty
                               (about 89% after failed open; buccal graft onlay)
  small intrarenal pelvis   -> ureterocalicostomy
  poor function             -> nephrectomy
```

**Also know:** Y-V plasty (high insertion); Culp spiral flap (long narrowing); stones removed through the pyelotomy with a flexible scope; safe in horseshoe and pelvic kidneys.

### Recent advances
- Single-port robot (2018); indocyanine green fluorescence for redo cases; buccal mucosa ureteroplasty.

**Diagram to draw:** Anderson–Hynes steps: transection, lateral spatulation, transposition over the crossing vessel, dependent anastomosis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Anderson–Hynes dismembered pyeloplasty is gold standard by any route; laparoscopic success over 90%.
> - Transpose over a crossing vessel; never divide it.
> - Double-J 4–6 weeks; MAG3 follow-up; up to 30% of failures are late.
> - Robotic equals laparoscopic in outcome, with easier suturing.

## Discuss the pathology, etiology, Clinical features diagnostic evaluation and treatment of retroperitoneal fibrosis.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 3, Q1
(medical management also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q3e)

### Introduction
- RPF (retroperitoneal fibrosis) is a rare inflammatory fibrosis encasing the aorta, IVC (inferior vena cava) and ureters.
- Incidence about 1 per 200,000–500,000 a year; age 40–60; male:female 2–3:1.

### Etiology

| Idiopathic (about 70%) | Secondary (about 30%) |
|---|---|
| Part of **chronic periaortitis**, a large-vessel vasculitis | **Drugs** (commonest identifiable cause): methysergide and other ergots, β-blockers, phenacetin |
| Proposed antigen: **ceroid** (oxidised lipid in plaque) | **Malignancy:** lymphoma (commonest), myeloma, carcinoid, pancreas, prostate, sarcoma |
| Probably autoimmune: membranous glomerulonephritis, ankylosing spondylitis, granulomatosis with polyangiitis; ANA (antinuclear antibody) positivity predicts relapse | Radiotherapy; asbestos |
| Part of multifocal fibrosclerosis: sclerosing mediastinitis and cholangitis, orbital pseudotumour, Riedel thyroiditis | Infection: tuberculosis, actinomyces, gonorrhoea, schistosomiasis |

- Many idiopathic cases are now classed as **IgG4 (immunoglobulin G4)-related disease**.

### Pathology
- **Gross:** flat, tan, dense plaque centred on the **distal aorta at L4–L5**, wrapping the ureters; may invade ureter or psoas.
- **Histology changes with stage:**
  - Early (active): collagen with capillary proliferation and lymphocytes, plasma cells and fibroblasts.
  - Late (burnt-out): acellular, avascular sheets of collagen.
- **Trap:** malignant RPF looks identical except for small islands of tumour cells, so take **deep, multiple biopsies**.
- **Mechanism of obstruction:** extrinsic compression and loss of ureteric peristalsis; the lumen stays patent.

### Clinical features
- **Dull lower back or flank pain** (commonest); weight loss, anorexia, nausea, malaise, fever, hypertension.
- About half present with **uraemia** or oliguria/anuria; symptoms last 4–6 months before diagnosis.
- **Extension:** IVC (leg oedema, DVT [deep venous thrombosis]); renal vein (gross haematuria); spermatic cord and scrotum. Examination is usually unrevealing.

### Diagnostic evaluation
**Laboratory**
- Raised ESR (erythrocyte sedimentation rate) and CRP (C-reactive protein) in half to two-thirds; leucocytosis; anaemia; raised creatinine; ANA; serum IgG4.

**Imaging**

| Test | Findings and role |
|---|---|
| CT (computed tomography) / CT urography | Homogeneous soft-tissue mass enveloping aorta, IVC and ureters; **medial deviation** of proximal and mid-ureters with smooth tapering |
| Retrograde pyelography | When renal function is poor |
| MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) | Low T1; **high T2 signal means active disease**; enhancement falls with treatment |
| **PET-CT (positron emission tomography)** | **Most sensitive test of activity**; guides biopsy and follow-up |
| Renography | Split function (repair or nephrectomy) |

- **Biopsy** (CT-guided or at ureterolysis) if malignancy is suspected; may be omitted with no malignancy history, no lymphadenopathy and typical imaging.

### Treatment

```
RPF with hydronephrosis
  -> Relieve obstruction (stents; nephrostomy if anuric or septic)
  -> Treat post-obstructive diuresis
  -> Stop culprit drug; exclude malignancy
  -> Active disease: steroids (+/- second-line drugs)
  -> Failure or burnt-out fibrosis: ureterolysis
```

**1. Initial management**
- Emergent **stents or PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy)**; nephrostomy if critically ill with little urine. Replace fluid during **post-obstructive diuresis**.

**2. Medical therapy**

| Agent | Role and regimen |
|---|---|
| **Prednisone** (first line) | 60 mg daily (about 1 mg/kg), tapered to 5 mg, up to 2 years. About 80% respond; relapse on tapering 25–50% |
| Azathioprine, mycophenolate mofetil, cyclophosphamide, ciclosporin, colchicine | Second line or steroid-sparing |
| Tamoxifen; medroxyprogesterone | Thought to inhibit fibroblasts |
| **Rituximab** | Refractory or IgG4-related disease |

- Steroids work on the cellular, active phase: pain settles in days and the mass shrinks.
- **Vaglio RCT (randomised controlled trial; Lancet 2011):** relapse 17% with prednisone vs 50% with tamoxifen.
- Drainage plus medical therapy clears obstruction in about two-thirds; only about **15% need ureterolysis**.

**3. Surgical ureterolysis**
- For failed medical therapy, burnt-out fibrosis or tissue diagnosis. **Bilateral**, even if hydronephrosis is unilateral; open, laparoscopic or robotic.
- Steps:
  1. Pre-place stents to identify the ureters.
  2. Mobilise the colon; take deep biopsies for frozen section.
  3. Start at the distal, non-dilated ureter.
  4. "Split and roll": right-angle clamp between ureter and plaque; incise the fibrosis over it.
  5. Close any ureterotomy with absorbable suture.
  6. **Separate the ureter from the fibrotic bed so it cannot be re-encased.**

| Protection method | How |
|---|---|
| Intraperitonealisation | Ureter lateralised; peritoneum closed behind it |
| Lateral retroperitoneal placement | Ureter displaced laterally (equivalent results) |
| **Omental wrap** | Omentum split on the gastroepiploic arteries, wrapped round each ureter; adds blood supply; best for extensive disease |

- Postoperative steroids may reduce recurrence.
- **Outcomes:** open about 94% stent-free at 1 year; laparoscopic about 90%; robotic with omental wrap about 86%.

**4. Other options:** long-term metallic stents (unfit); autotransplantation if ureterolysis is impossible; nephrectomy for an unrecoverable kidney.

### Follow-up
- Symptoms, ESR/CRP, creatinine, ultrasound; MRI or PET-CT for activity; remove stents once ureters drain; watch steroid side effects.

**Diagram to draw:** periaortic plaque at L4–L5 drawing both ureters medially, with an omental wrap.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Idiopathic 70% (periaortitis); methysergide and lymphoma classic secondary causes.
> - Medially deviated ureters; PET-CT best shows activity; biopsy deep.
> - Drain first, then prednisone (about 80% respond; Vaglio 2011 beat tamoxifen).
> - About 15% need bilateral ureterolysis with intraperitonealisation or omental wrap.

## Ureteric injury: iatrogenic (gynaecological) and upper ureteric injury
**Asked as:**
- Iatrogenic ureteric injury — *Practice Set 2 Paper III Q1 (LAQ)* (upper ureteric injury also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 3, Q2)
- Discuss management of ureteric injuries occurring during gynec surgeries — *Practice Set 4 Paper III Q1 (LAQ)* (also answered in Practice Set 2 Paper 3, Q1)
- Discuss Etiology, Diagnosis and Management of Upper ureteric injury. — *Winter 2023 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)

*Assumed scope: surgical (iatrogenic) injury at any level of the ureter, in gynaecological, colorectal, vascular and urological surgery.*
*Assumed meaning (upper ureteric injury): injury to the upper (proximal) ureter, from the PUJ (pelviureteric junction) to the pelvic brim, from external trauma or surgery.*

### Introduction
- Ureteric trauma is uncommon: 1–2.5% of genitourinary trauma. **Iatrogenic injury is commoner overall** than external trauma.
- **The proximal ureter is the commonest site of external injury.** **The distal ureter is the commonest (usual) iatrogenic site** in non-urological surgery.
- Gynaecological surgery has historically caused about half of all iatrogenic ureteric injuries; hysterectomy is the main culprit.
- It is mainly a problem of **recognition** (the core problem): thermal, ligation and devascularisation injuries are invisible at operation and declare themselves days to weeks later. Injury is often **missed** and declares itself later as urinoma, sepsis, stricture or fistula.
- Late diagnosis brings urinoma, sepsis, AKI (acute kidney injury), fistula and renal loss, and needs nephrostomy about ten times more often.

### Aetiology and incidence (EAU (European Association of Urology) trauma guideline data)
**1. External trauma**
- **Penetrating** (most): gunshot wounds up to about 90%. Blast damages the intramural blood supply up to 2 cm from the wound.
- **Blunt** (up to about 40%): rapid deceleration avulses the ureter at the **PUJ**; linked to lumbar transverse process fractures.
- **Over 90% have associated injuries** (small bowel commonest, colon, vessels, kidney 10–28%). Mortality 6–9% reflects these.

**2. Iatrogenic**

| Source | Key facts (upper-ureter relevance) |
|---|---|
| **Gynaecological** | Historically about half. Hysterectomy: vaginal 0.02–0.5%, abdominal 0.03–2%, minimally invasive 0.13–6%; prolapse surgery highest (1.7–4.3%). Endometriosis and placenta accreta raise risk |
| Colorectal | 0.15–10%; laparoscopic equals open |
| Colorectal, spinal, retroperitoneal surgery | Ligation, transection, cautery |
| **Urological / Ureteroscopy** | Ureteroscopy is the commonest (iatrogenic) cause in some series: abrasion, perforation 0.2–2%, avulsion under 0.5% (another source: <0.3%) (proximal third most at risk); stricture about 3% (another source: 0.3–3%). Risk: proximal stone, impacted stone, long operation, large sheath, inexperience. Also radical prostatectomy, partial nephrectomy or ablation (late proximal stricture) |
| Partial nephrectomy, thermal ablation | Proximal stricture months later (lower-pole and hilar tumours) |
| Vascular (aortic) surgery | Aorto-iliac bypass: transient hydronephrosis 12–20%; up to 85% unrecognised (missed initially). Devascularisation. Rare ureteroarterial fistula (chronic stent plus pelvic surgery or radiation) |

**Gynaecological incidence (EAU trauma guideline data)**

| Procedure | Ureteric injury (%) |
|---|---|
| Caesarean section (emergency) | 0.01–0.06 |
| Vaginal hysterectomy | 0.02–0.5 |
| Abdominal hysterectomy | 0.03–2.0 |
| Minimally invasive hysterectomy | 0.13–6.0 (laparoscopic = robotic, about 0.9) |
| Prolapse surgery | **1.7–4.3 (highest)** |

- **Danger sites in the pelvis (applied anatomy):** pelvic brim near (at) the infundibulopelvic ligament; **under the uterine artery** ("water under the bridge"); beside the uterosacral ligament; vaginal fornix / vaginal cuff near the UVJ (ureterovesical junction).
- Up to a third are recognised during open surgery, fewer during laparoscopy; the uterosacral region is hardest to see.
- **Mechanisms:** ligation, crush, laceration, transection, avulsion, thermal injury, **devascularisation**. About 10% of women get a large share of distal ureteric blood from the uterine artery (the uterine artery supplies much of the distal ureter), so some (ischaemic) injuries are unavoidable.
- **Risk factors:** previous surgery, adhesions, malignancy, radiotherapy, heavy bleeding, long operation, learning curve; endometriosis (encasement, adhesions, medial deviation), placenta accreta.
- **URS (ureteroscopy) risks:** long operation, 12/14-Fr sheath, impacted or proximal stone, inexperience.

### Classification: AAST (American Association for the Surgery of Trauma) ureter scale

| Grade | Injury (description) |
|---|---|
| I | Haematoma or contusion, no devascularisation (without devascularisation) |
| II | Laceration <50% of circumference |
| III | Laceration ≥50% |
| IV | Complete transection, <2 cm devascularised (with <2 cm devascularisation) |
| V | Avulsion, >2 cm devascularised (with >2 cm devascularisation) |

- Advance one grade for bilateral injury, up to grade III.

### Prevention
- See (identify) the ureter at the **pelvic brim** (crossing the common iliac artery) and trace it before ligating pedicles. Good haemostasis and exposure.
- **Prophylactic stents** (or lighted stents) help recognition in high-risk (complex) cases, but do not clearly reduce (prevent) injury. **ICG (indocyanine green)** instilled into the ureter (intraureteric) makes it fluoresce (fluoresces) for hours.
- **Cystoscopy** after minimally invasive hysterectomy and prolapse surgery, with oral phenazopyridine or IV (intravenous) dye to show efflux.
- URS: safety wire; ≤14-Fr sheath in an unstented ureter; fragment before basketing; "stop, stent, stage".

### Diagnosis and recognition
**Suspicion (trauma)**
- Penetrating flank or abdominal wound; high-energy deceleration.
- **Haematuria is absent in 25–45%**, so its absence does not exclude injury.

**Intraoperative (best outcome)**
- Inspect the ureter (at laparotomy); IV (intravenous) indigo carmine or methylene blue; cystoscopy for efflux (jets), with retrograde (on-table) pyelogram or stent. Check the other ureter and bladder. About 11% are missed at trauma laparotomy.
- Methylene blue is an MAO (monoamine oxidase) inhibitor: avoid with SSRIs/SNRIs (selective serotonin / serotonin–noradrenaline reuptake inhibitors; serotonin toxicity), G6PD (glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase) deficiency and pregnancy.

**Delayed presentation (most cases) / postoperative**
- Mean diagnosis about day 10: fever, ileus, flank pain, leucocytosis, rising creatinine, rising drain output, drain or wound urine leak, urinoma, anuria (bilateral), or **ureterovaginal fistula** (UVF): normal voiding plus constant leak, because the other kidney fills the bladder, usually within 4 weeks.

**Imaging and tests**

| Test | Role |
|---|---|
| **CT (computed tomography) urography with 10-minute delayed images** (both ureters) | Best test in stable patients. Signs: extravasation, non-opacified distal ureter, delayed nephrogram, urinoma |
| **Retrograde pyelography** | Most sensitive; shows site; allows stenting at once |
| Antegrade nephrostogram | When retrograde fails or gap is wide |
| One-shot IVU (intravenous urography) | Non-diagnostic in 33–100%; never rely on it |

- **Drain-fluid (or vaginal fluid) creatinine** above (far above) serum creatinine confirms urine.
- **Double-dye test** separates VVF (vesicovaginal fistula) from ureterovaginal fistula: oral phenazopyridine plus intravesical methylene blue. Orange pad = UVF; blue = VVF; both = both.

### Management
**Principles**
- **Stability decides timing; level and length decide the operation.**
- Repair: mobilise sparing (preserve) adventitia; debride to bleeding tissue (edges); **spatulated, tension-free, watertight, stented** repair (anastomosis) with fine absorbable suture; drain; retroperitonealise; omentum (omental wrap) for thermal, radiation, blast or vascular injury; non-tunnelled reimplant.
- **No bowel interposition or autotransplantation acutely.**

**By timing**
```
Recognised at surgery -> repair at once (stable patient)
Unstable -> ligate + later nephrostomy, or exteriorised stent
Recognised late -> try retrograde stent (or nephrostomy + antegrade stent)
   |-- succeeds -> stent 4-8 weeks, then image
   |-- fails -> nephrostomy; early repair if <=7 days,
               otherwise delayed repair after about 6 weeks
Uncontrolled sepsis / collections -> drain; repair regardless of timing
```
```
Ureteric injury suspected
 |-- Unstable -> damage control: ligate + later PCN,
 |               or exteriorised stent / cutaneous ureterostomy
 |-- Stable, found at surgery -> immediate repair over stent
 |-- Found late / postoperatively
       -> Attempt retrograde stent (+/- PCN, drain)
       -> Fails -> PCN; delayed definitive repair
```
```
Suspected late injury
 -> retrograde stent (or nephrostomy + antegrade stent)
     |-- succeeds -> stent 4-8 weeks, then image
     |-- fails -> nephrostomy
          |-- found <=7 days -> early repair
          |-- later -> repair after about 6 weeks
Sepsis / uncontrolled collections -> drain; repair regardless of timing
```

**At surgery, by injury**

| Injury | Action |
|---|---|
| Suture through wall, minor thermal | Remove suture; stent |
| Partial tear | Close transversely (transverse closure, 4-0/5-0) over stent, 4–8 weeks |
| Ligation | Remove ligature (deligate); stent if viable, excise and repair if not |
| Transection, lower ureter (usual) | **Ureteroneocystostomy ± psoas hitch** (95–100% success) |
| Long distal gap | **Boari flap** + hitch (gaps up to 12 cm) |
| Transection above pelvic brim | Ureteroureterostomy |
| Unstable patient | Ligate + later nephrostomy, or exteriorised stent |

**Repair by grade**
- **Grade I–II (contusion, partial):** double-J stent for about 6 weeks; or close a laceration transversely over a stent. Excise non-viable contused ureter.
- **Grade III–V:** formal reconstruction.

**By level (complete injury)**

| Level | Repair |
|---|---|
| Upper | Ureteroureterostomy; ureteropyelostomy (PUJ (pelviureteric junction) avulsion); ureterocalicostomy |
| Mid | Ureteroureterostomy ± downward nephropexy; TUU (transureteroureterostomy) when delayed |
| Lower (commonest iatrogenic) | **Ureteroneocystostomy, non-tunnelled ± psoas hitch** (95–100% success); **Boari flap** for gaps up to 12 cm |
| Long loss (delayed only) | Ileal ureter or Yang–Monti; buccal graft ureteroplasty; autotransplantation (last resort) |

**Options for the upper ureter**

| Procedure | Key points |
|---|---|
| **Ureteroureterostomy** | Commonest acute repair; short defects (2–3 cm); spatulate 5–6 mm, 180° apart; 5-0 absorbable over double-J; up to 90% success; leak 10–24%, stricture up to 12% |
| **Ureteropyelostomy** (reimplant into pelvis) | PUJ avulsion; spatulated |
| **Ureterocalicostomy** | Pelvis or PUJ destroyed; lower-pole parenchyma resected |
| **Renal descensus** | Mobilise kidney downwards; gains up to 8 cm |
| **TUU (transureteroureterostomy)** | Delayed; long loss when the bladder cannot reach; 90–97% success. Avoid with stones, RPF (retroperitoneal fibrosis), TB (tuberculosis), urothelial cancer or radiation |
| **Ileal ureter / Yang–Monti** | Long or panureteric loss; 81–100% success. Avoid if creatinine >2 mg/dL, bladder dysfunction, inflammatory bowel disease, radiation enteritis. Acidosis about 20% |
| Appendiceal interposition | Right side; about 92% short-term success |
| **Buccal mucosa graft ureteroplasty** | Delayed long strictures; onlay with omental wrap, often robotic; about 87% success |
| **Autotransplantation** | Last resort before nephrectomy; 5–10% renal units lost |
| Nephrectomy | Rarely; unstable with unreconstructable kidney, or poor function |

**Iatrogenic endoscopic injury**
- **URS perforation:** stop the procedure and place a **stent** about 4 weeks (± nephrostomy), image later; later reconstruction if stricture develops. **Avulsion:** nephrostomy, delayed reconstruction; avoid nephrectomy.

**Fistulae**
