- **Ureterovaginal fistula (UVF):** stent first (best / ideally within 2 weeks; most heal); if stenting fails, reimplant with psoas hitch or Boari flap (success ≥90%). Early and delayed repair do equally well.
- **Ureteroarterial fistula:** endovascular covered stent.
- **Also know:** TUU (transureteroureterostomy), ileal ureter, buccal graft ureteroplasty and autotransplantation for long delayed defects; robotic reconstruction (repair) for delayed repair matches open; avoid nephrectomy.

### Complications and follow-up
- Urinoma, abscess, leak, fistula, stricture, loss of renal function.
- Persistent leak: check stent position, take drain off suction, catheterise the bladder, then nephrostomy.
- **Follow-up:** ultrasound or diuretic renogram at 4–12 weeks and (at) 1 year; strictures (late stenosis) can be silent and may appear up to 30 months.

### Medicolegal
- Disclose and document; malpractice claims most often follow gynaecological surgery.

**Diagram to draw:** psoas hitch with non-tunnelled reimplant; ureter's relation to the uterine artery (ureter passing under the uterine artery); spatulated ureteroureterostomy over a double-J stent.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Distal ureter (under the uterine artery is the classic site) commonest iatrogenic site; most injuries present late.
> - Proximal ureter commonest site of external injury; haematuria absent in 25–45%.
> - AAST: 50% circumference (II vs III) and 2 cm devascularisation (IV vs V).
> - CT with 10-minute delays; retrograde pyelogram is most sensitive and allows stenting.
> - Late: stent first; early repair (repair) ≤7 days, otherwise delayed after 6 weeks (or after 6 weeks).
> - Lower ureter: psoas hitch 95–100%; Boari flap (bridges) up to 12 cm.
> - Stable: immediate stented ureteroureterostomy; unstable: damage control; bowel and autotransplant only delayed.

## Describe early and late management of PFUD injuries
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 3, Q1

*PFUD is taken as pelvic fracture urethral distraction defect, i.e. PFUI (pelvic fracture urethral injury).*

### Introduction and mechanism
- PFUI occurs in about 10% of male and up to 6% of female pelvic fractures. Highest risk: straddle fracture of all four pubic rami, unstable fractures, symphyseal diastasis.
- The posterior urethra is fixed to the pubis (puboprostatic ligaments, perineal membrane), so displacement shears it, usually at the **bulbomembranous junction**. In prepubertal boys the tear may extend into the prostate and bladder neck.
- The sphincter is avulsed but usually functional, so most men stay continent after repair.
- The gap fills with scar: an obliterative **distraction defect**, not a true stricture.

### Classification
| System | Types |
|---|---|
| Campbell-Walsh | **Partial**; **minor distraction** (a cystoscope passes retrogradely into the bladder); **major distraction** (it cannot). Need for urethroplasty rises from partial to major |
| Goldman | I stretched, intact; II tear above the perineal membrane; III tear of membranous urethra extending below the membrane into the bulb (commonest); IV bladder neck tear into the urethra; IVa bladder base injury mimicking IV; V pure anterior injury |

### Diagnosis
- Triad: **blood at the meatus, inability to void, palpable bladder**. A "high-riding" prostate is unreliable.
- **Blood at the meatus mandates RUG (retrograde urethrogram)** before catheterisation (oblique, 20 mL contrast). Extravasation with bladder filling means partial injury; without filling, complete.
- Females: vaginal examination and urethroscopy. Cystogram or CT (computed tomography) for bladder injury.

### Early management
1. ATLS (Advanced Trauma Life Support) resuscitation; associated injuries take priority.
2. **SPC (suprapubic cystostomy): the standard of care.** Percutaneous (peel-away sheath, 16 Fr Foley; avoid small "punch" tubes) or open through a lower midline incision, which allows bladder inspection.
   - SPC is safe with pelvic plating (AUA [American Urological Association] Urotrauma guideline); tunnel it high, away from hardware.
3. **Primary realignment (PR):** a brief attempt in a stable patient, within days, by antegrade plus retrograde flexible cystoscopy over a guidewire. Catheter out at 4–6 weeks; keep the SPC until voiding is good.
4. Partial tear: one gentle catheter attempt allowed.
5. **Immediate open exploration** only for bladder neck or rectal injury, or laparotomy for other reasons.
- **Also know:** female urethral injuries are usually repaired early.

### SPC versus primary realignment
| | SPC + delayed urethroplasty | Primary realignment |
|---|---|---|
| Advantage | Simple; haematoma untouched | Partial tears may heal; aligned ends ease repair |
| Drawback | Complete injuries need urethroplasty | Most still form a 1–2 cm stenosis |
| Evidence | Prospective data: **no significant difference** in need for urethroplasty | Modern PR does not add ED (erectile dysfunction) or incontinence |

### Late management
**Timing:** after **3 months** (scar stable), once the patient is ambulant.

**Evaluation**
- **"Up-and-down-o-gram":** cystogram plus RUG while the patient tries to void, to measure the gap; antegrade flexible cystoscopy via the SPC tract if the bladder neck stays closed. Bladder neck appearance does **not** predict its later function.
- **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging):** gap length, prostatic displacement (cephalad displacement predicts pubectomy).

**Endoscopic:** DVIU (direct vision internal urethrotomy) only for short, incomplete stenosis. "Cut-to-the-light" for gaps ≥1 cm fails (false passages).

**Perineal anastomotic urethroplasty (treatment of choice)**
1. Exaggerated lithotomy (under 5 hours); sounds via meatus and SPC tract.
2. Midline or lambda incision; mobilise the bulbar urethra up to the penoscrotal junction (not beyond: chordee).
3. Divide the urethra at the scar; excise **all** fibrosis until healthy prostatic mucosa appears over the sound.
4. Spatulate both ends to 30 Fr; 8–10 interrupted 4-0 absorbable sutures; tension-free mucosa-to-mucosa anastomosis over a silicone catheter.

**Progressive perineal approach** (each step shortens the path to bridge a longer gap):
| Step | Manoeuvre |
|---|---|
| 1 | Bulbar urethral mobilisation (enough for most) |
| 2 | Separation of the corporal bodies (intracrural space) |
| 3 | Inferior wedge pubectomy (Kerrison rongeur) |
| 4 | Supracrural rerouting of the urethra around one corpus |

- **Abdominoperineal or transpubic approach:** urethrorectal fistula, failed repair, bladder neck injury, children.
- **Postoperative:** voiding study at 21–28 days; cystoscopy at about 6 months.

### Outcomes and complications
- Success above 95%. Restenosis 5–15%; often DVIU-treatable because the scar is already excised.
- **ED** up to 82%, mostly from the injury (cavernous nerves, pudendal arteries). **Incontinence <4%** (bladder neck). Follow ≥1 year.
- **Also know:** chordee, penile shortening, ejaculatory dysfunction, lithotomy neuropathy, penile Doppler before repair in impotent men.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal pelvis showing the bulbomembranous gap and the four progressive perineal steps.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Blood at meatus → RUG; SPC is the standard initial step.
> - Primary realignment: brief, endoscopic; no difference in final urethroplasty rates.
> - Delayed perineal anastomotic urethroplasty after 3 months; bulbar mobilisation → crural separation → pubectomy → rerouting.
> - ED mostly from the injury; incontinence <4%.

# Section 11 · Stones, Obstruction & Trauma — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Accessories for RIRS
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2019-Summer Paper 3, Q3c

### Introduction
- RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery) is flexible ureteroscopy in the kidney. Its single **3.6 Fr channel** is shared by accessories and irrigation.

### Guidewires (0.018–0.038 inch, 145–260 cm)
| Type | Construction | Use | Weakness |
|---|---|---|---|
| Hydrophilic | Nitinol core, slippery when wet | Bypassing impacted stones, tortuous ureter | Displaces easily |
| PTFE (polytetrafluoroethylene)-coated | Steel or nitinol | Safety and working wire | Damages scope channel on backloading |
| **Hybrid** | Floppy hydrophilic tip, stiff PTFE shaft | Both roles | — |

- **Safety wire** keeps access and allows stenting after injury (AUA [American Urological Association], EAU [European Association of Urology]).
- **Also know:** open-ended or dual-lumen catheter (pyelogram, second wire); stiff wire for the sheath; balloon dilators.

### UAS (ureteral access sheath)
- Hydrophilic sheath with tapered obturator; usually **12/14 Fr** (inner/outer); tip below the PUJ (pelviureteric junction).
- **Pros:** lower intrarenal pressure and temperature; repeated passes; less scope wear.
- **Cons:** ureteric injury in nearly half, severe in about **1 in 8** (less if pre-stented); no proven fall in infection.
- **Suction sheaths and FANS (flexible and navigable suction access sheath):** aspirate dust and keep pressure low.

### Retrieval devices
- **Nitinol tipless baskets** (about 1.5–2.4 Fr): kink-resistant, open flush in calyces; relocate lower-pole stones. Small baskets allow more flow but are fragile. Graspers release easily.

### Laser and fibres
- **Ho:YAG (holmium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet)** or **thulium fibre laser**; small-core fibres (about 200 μm) keep deflection and flow; **≤10 W in the ureter**.

### Irrigation and antiretropulsion
- Gravity, pressure bag, hand or foot pumps (hand pumps: more fever); pressure sensors emerging.
- Stone Cone, NTrap, BackStop gel: mainly ureteric stones.

### Stents
- **Double-J**; AUA: may omit after uncomplicated URS (ureteroscopy).

**Diagram to draw:** sheath over wire, tipless basket in lower calyx.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Hydrophilic wire passes; PTFE wire stays.
> - 12/14 Fr sheath lowers pressure; severe injury about 1 in 8.
> - Nitinol tipless basket for calyces; ≤10 W in ureter.

## Management of lower ureteric stone of size less than 1 cm in pregnancy?
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2020-Summer Paper 3, Q3a

### Background
- About 1 in 200 to 1 in 1500 pregnancies; **renal colic is the commonest non-obstetric cause of admission**. Mostly second or third trimester; mainly **calcium phosphate** stones. Linked with preterm delivery and pre-eclampsia.
- Physiological hydronephrosis (up to 90% by third trimester) mimics obstruction.

### Diagnosis
- Urine culture always; blood count, creatinine; obstetric review.
- **US (ultrasound) first line**; **transvaginal US** for the distal ureter; Doppler RI (resistive index) rises in acute obstruction.
- Second line: **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)**, stone as a filling defect; **low-dose CT (computed tomography)**. Total fetal dose under **50 mGy**.

### Management
```
Lower ureteric stone <1 cm
 ├─ sepsis / solitary kidney / renal failure → urgent stent or nephrostomy
 ├─ pain controlled, no infection → observation (50–80% pass)
 └─ failed observation → ureteroscopy + laser
                         or stent/nephrostomy until delivery
```
- **Conservative:** hydration, paracetamol, opioids. **Avoid NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs)** (ductus arteriosus closure in third trimester). **MET (medical expulsive therapy)** with tamsulosin is off-label.
- **URS (ureteroscopy) with holmium laser:** first-line intervention for a non-complex stone in any trimester; ideal for a distal stone. Neuraxial anaesthesia, lateral tilt, minimal fluoroscopy (US guidance, shield under the patient), fetal monitoring. Third-trimester URS may raise preterm labour risk.
- **Stent or PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy):** temporising. Encrustation is rapid: change stents every **4–6 weeks**, nephrostomy about every 3 weeks; definitive treatment after delivery.
- **SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) contraindicated**. Second trimester is the safest window.

**Diagram to draw:** the flowchart above.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - US first; MRI or low-dose CT second.
> - Observation first; avoid NSAIDs.
> - URS with laser is definitive; SWL contraindicated.
> - Stents encrust: change every 4–6 weeks.

## Type 2 Renal tubular acidosis?
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2020-Summer Paper 3, Q3b

### Definition
- **Proximal RTA (renal tubular acidosis):** failure of the proximal tubule to reclaim bicarbonate, causing **hyperchloraemic, normal anion gap metabolic acidosis**.

### Pathophysiology
- The proximal tubule reclaims **85–90% of filtered bicarbonate** (luminal carbonic anhydrase; exit by the basolateral Na–HCO₃ cotransporter).
- **Self-limiting:** bicarbonate wasting → plasma HCO₃⁻ falls → filtered load drops below the lowered threshold → the intact distal tubule copes → **steady state at about 14–18 mEq/L with urine pH <5.5**.
- **Hypokalaemia** from distal sodium bicarbonate delivery; worsens on alkali.

### Causes
| Isolated | Generalised (Fanconi syndrome) |
|---|---|
| Carbonic anhydrase inhibitors (acetazolamide, **topiramate**) | **Cystinosis** (commonest in children), Lowe, Wilson, galactosaemia |
| Carbonic anhydrase II deficiency (mixed) | **Ifosfamide, cisplatin**, aminoglycosides, valproate |
| | Myeloma, amyloidosis, lead |
- **Fanconi:** glycosuria, aminoaciduria, phosphaturia, uricosuria.

### Clinical features
- Children: growth failure, **rickets**; adults: osteomalacia, weakness.
- **Stones uncommon**: urinary citrate stays near normal and urine is acid at steady state. Topiramate and acetazolamide are exceptions (alkaline urine, calcium phosphate stones).

### Diagnosis
- Normal anion gap acidosis, low potassium; urine can acidify below 5.5 (unlike distal RTA).
- **Bicarbonate loading:** urine pH rises; **fractional bicarbonate excretion >15%**.
- Look for Fanconi features; drug history.

### Treatment
- Treat the cause.
- **Large alkali doses (about 10–15 mEq/kg/day)** as potassium citrate or mixed citrate, replacing potassium.
- Thiazide; phosphate and vitamin D for rickets.

| | Type 1 (distal) | Type 2 (proximal) | Type 4 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Urine pH in acidosis | Always >6 | <5.5 at steady state | Low |
| Potassium | Low | Low | High |
| Stones | Common (up to 70%) | Uncommon | Uncommon |

**Diagram to draw:** proximal tubular cell with carbonic anhydrase.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bicarbonate wasting; HCO₃⁻ 14–18 mEq/L; urine pH <5.5 at steady state.
> - Usually Fanconi; ifosfamide, cisplatin.
> - Fractional HCO₃⁻ excretion >15%; stones uncommon.

## Renal Autotransplantation
**Asked as:**
- Auto-transplantation of Kidney? — *Summer 2020 Paper III Q3c (SAQ)*
- Role of auto transplant of kidney in Urology. — *Summer 2021 Paper III Q3e (SAQ)*
- Renal Autotransplantation — *Winter 2025 Paper III Q3c (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 3, Q3c and 2021-Summer Paper 3, Q3e)

### Definition and principle
- Removing a kidney and re-implanting it, usually in the **ipsilateral iliac fossa** on the iliac vessels, with **bench (ex vivo) surgery** (bench (ex vivo) repair) if needed. First done (first reported) in **1963** for proximal ureteric injury.
- **Principle:** bringing the kidney to the bladder replaces the ureter without bowel; the bench gives a cold, bloodless field.

### Role (indications)
| Group | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Ureteric loss (commonest)** | Long-segment loss or stricture; long iatrogenic or traumatic loss; repeated failed repairs (**delayed setting only, never acute**); **RPF (retroperitoneal fibrosis)** when ureterolysis fails |
| **RPF (retroperitoneal fibrosis)** | When ureterolysis is impossible and function is satisfactory |
| **Tumour** | Total ureterectomy for UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma) in a solitary kidney; bench partial nephrectomy in a solitary kidney; left RCC (renal cell carcinoma) needing suprarenal caval resection (new drainage for the right kidney) |
| **Vascular** | Complex renal artery aneurysm or branch disease; complex branch renal artery aneurysm, stenosis or AVM (arteriovenous malformation) unsuitable for endovascular repair; nutcracker syndrome |
| **Pain** | Selected loin pain–haematuria syndrome |
- **Chosen over ileal ureter** (preferred over ileal ureter) when the contralateral kidney (the other kidney) is absent or poor, or ileal ureter / bowel is unsuitable (creatinine >2 mg/dL, bowel disease: inflammatory bowel disease, radiation enteritis).
- **Contraindications:** poor function (poor renal function), severe iliac atherosclerosis, unfit patient.

### Procedure
```
CT (computed tomography) angiography, renogram, ureteric study
→ harvest as for live donor nephrectomy / live donation (open/laparoscopic/robotic):
  maximal vessel length, preserve ureteric blood supply
→ cold perfusion on bench ± ex vivo repair (bench repair)
→ Gibson incision, extraperitoneal iliac fossa
→ renal vein to external iliac vein (end-to-side);
  renal artery to external iliac (end-to-side) or internal iliac (end-to-end)
→ drainage: pyelovesicostomy (± Boari flap), ureteroneocystostomy,
  or native ureter left in continuity
→ stent, catheter, drain
```
- Laparoscopic harvest plus Gibson implantation: less analgesia, faster recovery; **robotic autotransplantation** emerging.

### Complications and outcome
- Arterial or venous **thrombosis**, bleeding, urine leak, stricture, lymphocele, infection.
- **Renal unit loss 5–10%** (lower in transplant centres); major (Clavien–Dindo ≥III) complications about 15–20%.
- **Long-term success about 90%.** Doppler US (ultrasound) early; renogram and blood pressure later.

**Also know:** alternatives: Boari flap, TUU (transureteroureterostomy), ileal ureter, Yang–Monti, buccal ureteroplasty, renal descensus.

**Diagram to draw:** kidney in the iliac fossa with vascular anastomoses and pyelovesicostomy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - For long ureteric loss when other repairs fail and the other kidney is poor or bowel unsuitable.
> - Delayed setting only; bench surgery for complex vascular or tumour cases.
> - Iliac vessels; pyelovesicostomy or ureteroneocystostomy.
> - Unit loss 5–10%; success about 90%.

## Indication, procedure and complications of Uretero-Calycostomy?
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2020-Summer Paper 3, Q3d

### Definition
- End-to-side anastomosis of the spatulated proximal ureter to a dilated **lower-pole calyx**, after **resecting lower-pole parenchyma**, bypassing the pelvis and PUJ (pelviureteric junction).

### Indications
- **Failed pyeloplasty** with a scarred pelvis and short ureter (commonest).
- **Small intrarenal pelvis** with PUJ obstruction.
- **Rotation or fusion anomalies** (horseshoe kidney).
- Proximal ureteric or pelvic loss: trauma, iatrogenic, **TB (tuberculosis) PUJ stricture**.
- **Prerequisites:** dilated lower calyx, ureter reaching it without tension, worthwhile function. Cortex <5 mm with poor function predicts failure.

### Procedure (open, laparoscopic or robotic)
```
Flank position; pre-placed double-J stent
→ mobilise ureter with generous periureteral tissue
→ mobilise kidney; partial lower-pole nephrectomy
   to expose the calyx widely (not simple nephrotomy)
→ close the pelvic outlet; spatulate ureter laterally
→ apex stitch to lateral calyx wall; second at 180°
→ interrupted sutures, all placed then tied
→ capsule closed over cut surface, away from anastomosis
→ cover with perinephric fat, peritoneum or omentum; drain
```
- **Why resect:** parenchyma or capsule left round the anastomosis contracts and causes stenosis.
- Stent out at 4–6 weeks; renogram later. Later endoscopy needs **antegrade upper-pole access**.

### Complications
- **Anastomotic stricture** (main failure; mostly within the first year).
- Urine leak, urinoma, bleeding from the lower pole, infection, renal unit loss.
- Success about 70% in the largest (mostly redo) series; better in small paediatric series.

**Also know:** alternatives are redo pyeloplasty, Davis intubated ureterotomy, ileal ureter, buccal graft, autotransplantation, nephrectomy.

**Diagram to draw:** kidney with resected lower pole and ureter sewn to the calyx.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Salvage for failed pyeloplasty, intrarenal pelvis, horseshoe kidney.
> - Requires a dilated lower calyx and lower-pole resection.
> - Stricture is the main failure; success about 70% in redo series.

## Post traumatic bladder neck injury: acute and delayed management?
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2021-Summer Paper 3, Q3c

### Introduction
- The bladder neck is the **internal sphincter**. It is torn mainly in **pelvic fracture**, often with prostatic urethral, rectal or vaginal injury; **commoner in children**.
- Missed injury causes **incontinence, fistula and stricture**.

### Diagnosis
- Pelvic fracture with gross haematuria, retention or blood at the meatus; **vaginal examination** in females.
- **RUG (retrograde urethrogram)**, then **retrograde CT or plain cystography** (350 mL).
- Grading: AAST (American Association for the Surgery of Trauma) bladder **grade V** = laceration into the bladder neck or ureteric orifice; Goldman type IV = bladder rupture extending into the proximal urethra.

### Acute management
- Resuscitate; if unstable, damage control (drainage), repair later.
- **Bladder neck injury needs immediate repair** (AUA (American Urological Association) and EAU (European Association of Urology)), unlike isolated posterior urethral distraction (suprapubic tube, delayed repair).
```
Lower midline incision → anterior cystotomy
→ identify ureteric orifices (stent if near)
→ minimal debridement; re-approximate neck to prostatic urethra,
  layered absorbable sutures over a 22 Fr catheter
→ rectal or vaginal tear: separate layers, no overlapping suture
  lines, interpose omentum (colostomy for rectum)
→ remove bone spicules; drain ± suprapubic tube
```
- Catheter 2–3 weeks; cystogram before removal.

### Delayed management (3–6 months)
| Problem | Management |
|---|---|
| Associated urethral distraction | Cystogram + RUG + endoscopy; **anastomotic urethroplasty first**, not combined with bladder neck surgery |
| Bladder neck contracture | Endoscopic incision or resection; open Y-V plasty if refractory |
| **Incontinence** (both sphincters damaged) | Urodynamics; **AUS (artificial urinary sphincter)**; bladder neck reconstruction (Young–Dees–Leadbetter) or fascial sling; last resort **bladder neck closure + Mitrofanoff** ± augmentation |
| Fistula | Layered repair with tissue interposition |
- Bladder neck appearance on imaging **does not predict** continence; restore continuity first, then treat incontinence.
- Continence is poor whenever the bladder neck is involved (paediatric series: incontinence up to 20% overall).

**Diagram to draw:** pelvic fracture with bladder neck tear and repair over a catheter.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bladder neck injury = immediate repair.
> - Posterior urethral distraction alone = suprapubic tube and delayed urethroplasty.
> - Late incontinence: AUS, bladder neck reconstruction, or closure + Mitrofanoff.

## Mini Perc.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q3c

**See 2022-Summer Paper 1, Q1 (full answer).**

> **Key points to remember (SAQ angle):**
> - Definition by sheath size: mini-PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) 15–22 Fr; ultra-mini 11–14 Fr; micro-perc <10 Fr (standard 24–34 Fr).
> - Main advantage: less bleeding and fewer transfusions, shorter stay, with stone-free rates non-inferior to standard PCNL.
> - Main limitation: longer operating time and higher intrarenal pressure (fever, sepsis); the advantage disappears against a 24 Fr tract.

## Boari flap
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 3, Q3d

### Definition
- A **tubularised flap of anterior bladder wall**, based posterolaterally on the side of the lesion, swung up to bridge a distal or mid-ureteric gap. Described in dogs 1894, humans 1947; laparoscopic 2001, robotic 2008.

### Reconstructive ladder (distal ureter)
| Option | Gap bridged |
|---|---|
| Ureteroneocystostomy | Distal 4–5 cm |
| + Psoas hitch | Adds up to 5 cm (total 6–10 cm) |
| **Boari flap (+ hitch)** | **10–15 cm**; spiral flap may reach pelvis (right) |
| Downward nephropexy | Adds up to 5–8 cm |
- Beyond: TUU (transureteroureterostomy), ileal ureter, autotransplantation.

### Indications
- Long distal or mid-ureteric loss: gynaecological injury (commonest), stricture, failed reimplantation, distal TCC (transitional cell carcinoma) ureter, tuberculosis.

### Contraindications
- **Small, contracted or poorly compliant bladder** (radiation, tuberculosis); uncorrected outlet obstruction or neurogenic bladder. Not usually for acute unstable trauma.
- Preoperative: urodynamics or cystogram for capacity; antegrade and retrograde studies for length.

### Technique
1. Mobilise bladder; divide contralateral superior vesical pedicle if needed.
2. Mark flap on the **non-distended** bladder: **base ≥4 cm, apex ≥3 cm, length:base ≤3:1**; length = gap + 3 cm if tunnelled.
3. Raise flap; **psoas hitch** its base (sutures superficial, parallel to fibres: spare genitofemoral and femoral nerves).
4. Anastomose spatulated ureter: **non-refluxing** 3 cm submucosal tunnel, or **refluxing** direct (apex about 3 times ureteric diameter).
5. Stent (double-J); tubularise in two layers 2-0 absorbable; drain, catheter 7–14 days.
- Laparoscopic and robotic: transperitoneal, refluxing anastomosis, results excellent.

### Complications
- **Recurrent stricture** (commonest; ischaemia or tension), reduced capacity with frequency and urgency, leak, nerve palsy, iliac vessel injury, pseudodiverticulum.
- eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) fell in 44% within 2 years after reimplantation: counsel.
- **Alternative:** bladder advancement flap, similar reach and simpler.

**Diagram to draw:** bladder outline with oblique flap (4 cm base, 3 cm apex), hitched and tubularised to ureter.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bridges 10–15 cm; combine with psoas hitch.
> - Base ≥4 cm, length:base ≤3:1, measure bladder empty.
> - Contraindicated in small contracted bladder.

## Newer methods (techniques) of stone analysis
**Asked as:**
- Newer methods of stone analysis — *Practice Set 6 Paper III Q3c (SAQ)*
- Newer techniques of stone analysis — *Practice Set 7 Paper III Q3d (SAQ)*
- Newer methods of stone analysis — *Practice Set 6 Paper III Q3c (earlier version) (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2024-Winter Paper 4, Q3a. Earlier version: **See Practice Set 6 Paper 4, Q3a (full answer).**)

### Why analyse stones
- AUA (American Urological Association) and EAU (European Association of Urology): analyse a stone at least once in every former (analyse every first stone); **repeat if stones recur on treatment** (evolving metabolic condition) — on recurrence despite treatment or early recurrence after clearance.
- Composition points to cause: **uric acid** suggests metabolic disease, **cystine** a genetic one, **struvite** infection. Cystine, struvite, 2,8-DHA (2,8-dihydroxyadenine) and xanthine are diagnostic in themselves.
- Composition can change over time (e.g. calcium oxalate converting to brushite), so re-analyse recurrent stones.

### Why wet chemical analysis is obsolete (limits)
- Detects ions, not **crystal phases**: cannot separate COM (calcium oxalate monohydrate) from COD (dihydrate), or apatite from brushite. High error rates; misses rare stones; needs a large sample.

### Newer methods
| Method | Principle | Comment |
|---|---|---|
| **FTIR (Fourier-transform infrared) spectroscopy** | Each compound absorbs a characteristic infrared spectrum | Commonest reference method; tiny (small) sample; detects proteins, drugs |
| **XRD (X-ray diffraction)** | Crystal lattice diffracts X-rays in a unique pattern (gives a unique diffraction pattern) | Gold standard for crystals; misses amorphous material |
| Polarising microscopy | Crystal birefringence | Cheap; needs experience; observer-dependent |
| **Morpho-constitutional analysis (Daudon)** | Stereomicroscopy of surface and section, plus FTIR | Subtypes point to cause, e.g. Ic suggests primary hyperoxaluria |
| SEM (scanning electron microscopy) ± EDX (energy-dispersive X-ray) | Surface (structure) and elements | Research |
| Micro-CT (micro-computed tomography) | 3-D structure, non-destructive | Research |
| Raman spectroscopy | Scattered light (spectrum) | Research |

### In vivo prediction
- **DECT (dual-energy computed tomography):** two (tube) energies (e.g. 80 and 140 kVp) separate **uric acid from non–uric acid** stones with near-perfect accuracy.
- **Hounsfield units:** SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) works best below about 900–1000 HU; >1000 HU predicts SWL failure; cystine and brushite are dense.
- **Endoscopic recognition** of morphology (lost when stones are dusted). Dusting loses the specimen: collect fragments.

### Significance in management
- **Uric acid:** oral dissolution by alkalinisation (potassium citrate).
- **Struvite:** complete clearance, culture-directed antibiotics.
- **Cystine:** hydration, alkalinisation, tiopronin; avoid SWL.
- **COM, brushite, cystine:** hard, SWL-resistant; prefer endoscopy. Brushite can convert from calcium oxalate after SWL or infection.
- **Brushite:** avoid over-alkalinisation.
- **2,8-DHA (dihydroxyadenine):** APRT (adenine phosphoribosyltransferase) deficiency; allopurinol or febuxostat. **Drug stones** (indinavir, triamterene): stop the drug.

**Diagram to draw:** FTIR spectrum outline; COM (dumbbell, dumb-bell) vs COD (envelope) crystals.

> **Key points to remember (including SAQ angle):**
> - FTIR (Fourier transform infrared spectroscopy) and XRD (X-ray diffraction) are the reference methods; wet chemical analysis (wet chemistry) is obsolete.
> - DECT (dual-energy computed tomography) separates/identifies uric acid stones in vivo; it estimates composition in vivo, mainly uric acid versus calcium stones.
> - Composition guides dissolution, SWL choice and prevention.
> - Stone analysis guides prevention: uric acid points to a metabolic cause, cystine to a genetic one, struvite to infection.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 11 · Stones, Obstruction & Trauma

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Discuss various ureteric stents. Discuss ureteroscopy with or without stenting. (LAQ)

- Finney 1978 double-J; symptoms from intravesical stent and reflux.
- Encrusted stent: bladder coil, ureter, then kidney.
- Denstedt 2001: no routine stent after uncomplicated URS.

#### Laparoscopic pyeloplasty. (LAQ)

- Anderson–Hynes dismembered pyeloplasty is gold standard by any route; laparoscopic success over 90%.
- Transpose over a crossing vessel; never divide it.
- Double-J 4–6 weeks; MAG3 follow-up; up to 30% of failures are late.
- Robotic equals laparoscopic in outcome, with easier suturing.

#### Discuss the pathology, etiology, Clinical features diagnostic evaluation and treatment of retroperitoneal fibrosis. (LAQ)

- Idiopathic 70% (periaortitis); methysergide and lymphoma classic secondary causes.
- Medially deviated ureters; PET-CT best shows activity; biopsy deep.
- Drain first, then prednisone (about 80% respond; Vaglio 2011 beat tamoxifen).
- About 15% need bilateral ureterolysis with intraperitonealisation or omental wrap.

#### Ureteric injury: iatrogenic (gynaecological) and upper ureteric injury (LAQ)

- Distal ureter (under the uterine artery is the classic site) commonest iatrogenic site; most injuries present late.
- Proximal ureter commonest site of external injury; haematuria absent in 25–45%.
- AAST: 50% circumference (II vs III) and 2 cm devascularisation (IV vs V).
- CT with 10-minute delays; retrograde pyelogram is most sensitive and allows stenting.
- Late: stent first; early repair (repair) ≤7 days, otherwise delayed after 6 weeks (or after 6 weeks).
- Lower ureter: psoas hitch 95–100%; Boari flap (bridges) up to 12 cm.
- Stable: immediate stented ureteroureterostomy; unstable: damage control; bowel and autotransplant only delayed.

#### Describe early and late management of PFUD injuries (LAQ)

- Blood at meatus → RUG; SPC is the standard initial step.
- Primary realignment: brief, endoscopic; no difference in final urethroplasty rates.
- Delayed perineal anastomotic urethroplasty after 3 months; bulbar mobilisation → crural separation → pubectomy → rerouting.
- ED mostly from the injury; incontinence <4%.

#### Accessories for RIRS (SAQ)

- Hydrophilic wire passes; PTFE wire stays.
- 12/14 Fr sheath lowers pressure; severe injury about 1 in 8.
- Nitinol tipless basket for calyces; ≤10 W in ureter.

#### Management of lower ureteric stone of size less than 1 cm in pregnancy? (SAQ)

- US first; MRI or low-dose CT second.
- Observation first; avoid NSAIDs.
- URS with laser is definitive; SWL contraindicated.
- Stents encrust: change every 4–6 weeks.

#### Type 2 Renal tubular acidosis? (SAQ)

- Bicarbonate wasting; HCO₃⁻ 14–18 mEq/L; urine pH <5.5 at steady state.
- Usually Fanconi; ifosfamide, cisplatin.
- Fractional HCO₃⁻ excretion >15%; stones uncommon.

#### Renal Autotransplantation (SAQ)

- For long ureteric loss when other repairs fail and the other kidney is poor or bowel unsuitable.
- Delayed setting only; bench surgery for complex vascular or tumour cases.
- Iliac vessels; pyelovesicostomy or ureteroneocystostomy.
- Unit loss 5–10%; success about 90%.

#### Indication, procedure and complications of Uretero-Calycostomy? (SAQ)

- Salvage for failed pyeloplasty, intrarenal pelvis, horseshoe kidney.
- Requires a dilated lower calyx and lower-pole resection.
- Stricture is the main failure; success about 70% in redo series.

#### Post traumatic bladder neck injury: acute and delayed management? (SAQ)

- Bladder neck injury = immediate repair.
- Posterior urethral distraction alone = suprapubic tube and delayed urethroplasty.
- Late incontinence: AUS, bladder neck reconstruction, or closure + Mitrofanoff.

#### Mini Perc. (SAQ)

- Definition by sheath size: mini-PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) 15–22 Fr; ultra-mini 11–14 Fr; micro-perc <10 Fr (standard 24–34 Fr).
- Main advantage: less bleeding and fewer transfusions, shorter stay, with stone-free rates non-inferior to standard PCNL.
- Main limitation: longer operating time and higher intrarenal pressure (fever, sepsis); the advantage disappears against a 24 Fr tract.

#### Boari flap (SAQ)

- Bridges 10–15 cm; combine with psoas hitch.
- Base ≥4 cm, length:base ≤3:1, measure bladder empty.
- Contraindicated in small contracted bladder.

#### Newer methods (techniques) of stone analysis (SAQ)

- FTIR (Fourier transform infrared spectroscopy) and XRD (X-ray diffraction) are the reference methods; wet chemical analysis (wet chemistry) is obsolete.
- DECT (dual-energy computed tomography) separates/identifies uric acid stones in vivo; it estimates composition in vivo, mainly uric acid versus calcium stones.
- Composition guides dissolution, SWL choice and prevention.
- Stone analysis guides prevention: uric acid points to a metabolic cause, cystine to a genetic one, struvite to infection.

# Section 12 · Bladder Cancer & Diversion — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Immediate post TURBT intravesical Therapy for TCC
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2019-Summer Paper 3, Q3d

### Introduction
- After TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour), NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer) recurs in 40–60%. Many early recurrences come from **floating cells implanting** on the raw bed.
- A single chemotherapy instillation (SI) kills them before implantation. TCC (transitional cell carcinoma) is now called urothelial carcinoma.

### Rationale and timing
- Implantation occurs within hours, so instil **as early as possible, ideally within 2–6 h** (theatre or recovery). Guidelines allow up to 24 h, but MMC given at 24 h lost its benefit.

### Agents
| Agent | Dose | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **MMC (mitomycin C)** | 40 mg in 20–40 mL | Alkylating; skin rash |
| **Gemcitabine** | 2 g in 100 mL, 1 h | Standard in USA; mild toxicity |
| Epirubicin | 50 mg | Chemical cystitis |
- **Also know:** pirarubicin works; thiotepa does not.

### Technique
- Exclude perforation; instil via catheter; retain 1–2 h; drain.

### Evidence
- **Sylvester 2016 individual-patient meta-analysis:** about 35% relative reduction in recurrence; **no benefit** with >1 recurrence/year or EORTC (European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer) recurrence score ≥5.
- **SWOG (Southwest Oncology Group) S0337:** gemcitabine versus saline in suspected low-grade NMIBC: 4-year recurrence 35% vs 47%; no excess toxicity.
- No effect on progression.

### Who benefits
- **Low risk** (primary solitary low-grade Ta ≤3 cm): SI alone; greatest benefit.
- Intermediate risk: SI then adjuvant course. High risk: BCG (bacille Calmette-Guérin) follows.

### Contraindications
- **Suspected perforation**, extensive resection, bleeding needing irrigation.
- **Never BCG immediately**: intravasation of live bacteria causes sepsis and death.

### Complications
- Irritative symptoms (commonest), chemical cystitis, skin desquamation (MMC), contracted bladder; serious harm mainly after perforation.
- **Also know:** continuous saline irrigation may also reduce recurrence.

**Diagram to draw:** floating cells implanting on resection bed → killed by instillation.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Instil within 6 h (at most 24 h); never BCG.
> - SWOG S0337: 35% vs 47% recurrence with gemcitabine 2 g.
> - Withhold if perforation; best for low-risk solitary tumours.

## Management of Haemorrhagic cystitis.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q3b

### Definition
- Diffuse inflammation and bleeding of the bladder mucosa, after excluding other sources (cystoscopy, cytology, upper tract imaging).
- **Exclude bladder cancer first:** cyclophosphamide and radiation are also risk factors for it.

### Causes and mechanism

| Cause | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| **Cyclophosphamide, ifosfamide** | Liver makes **acrolein**, excreted in urine, which sloughs urothelium; onset within 48 hours |
| **Pelvic radiation** (5%, 6 months to 10 years later) | **Obliterative endarteritis** → ischaemia, fibrosis, fragile neovessels |
| **Viral** | **BK polyomavirus**, adenovirus 11 and 35; after bone marrow or renal transplant |
| Others | Bacterial infection; busulfan, thiotepa; penicillins; amyloidosis |

### Prevention
- **Mesna** binds and inactivates acrolein; hyperhydration with forced diuresis, CBI (continuous bladder irrigation).

### Management (escalate by severity; stability decides the branch)
```
Resuscitate, transfuse; correct coagulopathy; treat infection
→ large-bore catheter, CBI
→ cystoscopy under anaesthesia: clot evacuation, fulguration, biopsy
→ intravesical alum
→ bleeding after 48 h → hyperbaric oxygen (+ nephrostomies if bleeding continues)
→ formalin 1% → 4% (cystogram first)
→ unstable: internal iliac embolisation
→ cystectomy + conduit
```

| Intravesical agent | Mechanism and method | Caution |
|---|---|---|
| **Alum 1%** (first line) | Protein precipitation, vasoconstriction; 200–300 mL/h; no anaesthesia | Aluminium toxicity in renal failure |
| Aminocaproic acid | Antifibrinolytic CBI, 200 mg/L | Only in a clot-free bladder; thromboembolism |
| Prostaglandins (carboprost), silver nitrate, hyaluronate | Vasoconstriction, coagulation, GAG (glycosaminoglycan) repair | Silver nitrate: cystogram |
| **Formalin 1–4%** | Protein fixation; **80–90% success** | Anaesthesia; **cystogram to exclude reflux**; fibrosis, small bladder, ureteric stricture |

- **HBOT (hyperbaric oxygen therapy):** 100% oxygen at 2–3 atmospheres, 30–40 sessions; neovascularisation; 80–90% response, but many relapse. **RICH-ART** trial: better urinary symptom score in radiation cystitis.
- **Angioembolisation:** anterior division of internal iliac; spare the posterior division (gluteal pain).
- **Nephrostomies:** divert urokinase-rich urine.
- **Cystectomy with diversion:** last resort; remove the bladder (pyocystis risk).
- **Viral:** supportive; cidofovir (intravenous or intravesical).

**Also know:** tranexamic acid and laser ablation are unproven; oral pentosan polysulfate in children.

**Diagram to draw:** stepwise escalation ladder.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Acrolein (prevent with mesna); radiation endarteritis; BK virus.
> - Alum first; formalin only after cystogram.
> - Hyperbaric oxygen for radiation cystitis (RICH-ART); embolise if unstable.

## Methods of preoperative bowel preparation.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q3d

### Rationale
- Bowel used in urinary diversion is opened into a sterile urinary tract. Faecal spillage causes wound infection, abscess, leak and sepsis.
- Flora: aerobes (*E. coli*, *Enterococcus faecalis*); anaerobes (*Bacteroides*, *Clostridium*).

| Segment | Organisms per gram |
|---|---|
| Jejunum | 10–10⁵ |
| Distal ileum | 10⁵–10⁷ |
| Ascending colon | 10⁶–10⁸ |
| Descending colon | 10¹⁰–10¹² |

### Methods

**1. Dietary:** clear liquids the day before; clear drinks until 2 hours before surgery.

**2. Mechanical bowel preparation (MBP)** reduces faecal **amount**, so total bacteria, not concentration.
- **PEG (polyethylene glycol)–electrolyte** lavage: non-absorbable; preferred in elderly, renal or heart failure, cirrhosis.
- Sodium phosphate, magnesium citrate; enemas.
- Historical: 3-day purgation, whole-gut irrigation by nasogastric tube (9–12 L saline), mannitol (feeds bacteria).
- Harms: dehydration, electrolyte loss; liquid stool may spill more.

**3. Oral antibiotic preparation** reduces bacterial **concentration**.
- **Neomycin + erythromycin base** (Nichols–Condon, 1977: septic complications 43% with MBP alone vs 9% with added antibiotics); neomycin + metronidazole; kanamycin.
- Harms: diarrhoea, *C. difficile* colitis, candidiasis; useless in obstruction.

**4. Intravenous prophylaxis (most important):** anaerobic plus aerobic cover (e.g. cephalosporin + metronidazole) within 1–2 hours before incision; it must be in tissue before contamination.

**5. ERAS (enhanced recovery after surgery):** **no routine MBP**; prehabilitation, multimodal opioid-sparing analgesia, chewing gum, **alvimopan** (peripheral µ-opioid antagonist, 12 mg before surgery), early feeding and mobilisation.

### Evidence
- Colorectal meta-analyses and Cochrane: MBP does not reduce leak or abscess; may raise SSI (surgical site infection).
- Oral antibiotics added to MBP reduce SSI and leaks in large colectomy series.
- Small cystectomy trials: no difference without preparation.

### Who still needs full preparation
- Salvage cystectomy after radiation, or cystectomy for benign disease (radiation cystitis, fistula, neurogenic bladder); immunosuppression.

**Also know:** wound protectors reduce SSI; enema before transrectal biopsy.

**Diagram to draw:** bacterial load along the gut.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Mechanical lowers amount; antibiotics lower concentration.
> - Nichols–Condon: neomycin + erythromycin.
> - Intravenous antibiotics before incision are essential; ERAS omits routine MBP.

## Pathogenesis, Clinical importance and Management of Carcinoma in situ urinary bladder.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 3, Q3e

### Definition
- **CIS (carcinoma in situ, Tis):** flat, non-invasive, **high-grade** urothelial carcinoma. It is not "premalignant".
- Histology: severe atypia, loss of polarity, non-cohesive pleomorphic cells, **loss of umbrella cells** (dysplasia keeps them).
- **Types:** primary (isolated, about 10%), secondary or concurrent (with papillary tumour, about 90%).

### Pathogenesis
- **Flat pathway:** dysplasia → CIS → high-grade invasive cancer.
- Driven by **TP53, RB and PTEN** loss; **no FGFR3** mutation (unlike low-grade papillary tumours).
- **Field change:** multifocal; spreads to prostatic urethra, distal ureters and upper tract.

### Clinical importance
- Untreated, **40–83% progress** to muscle invasion. Primary CIS progresses in >50%.
- "CIS alone" hides invasion at cystectomy in up to 20%.
- **Second strongest prognostic factor after grade**; any CIS makes NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer) **high risk**.
- About 80% have **irritative symptoms**; it mimics cystitis.

### Diagnosis
- **Urine cytology:** most useful in CIS, because the cells are poorly cohesive. Positive cytology with normal cystoscopy needs mapping.
- Cystoscopy: red velvety patch or normal-looking mucosa; **blue-light cystoscopy** (hexaminolevulinate) detects about one-third more CIS; NBI (narrow-band imaging).
- **Biopsies:** cold-cup from suspicious and random sites; prostatic urethra; upper tract imaging.

### Management
- **BCG (bacillus Calmette–Guérin) is first line:**
  - Induction: 6 weekly instillations.
  - **Maintenance** (SWOG [Southwest Oncology Group] 2000: 3 weekly doses at 3 and 6 months, then 6-monthly for 3 years): median recurrence-free survival 76.8 vs 35.7 months.
  - Complete response up to 84%; durable in about half.
- **Persistent CIS at 3 months:** second BCG course; 30–50% more respond.
- **BCG-unresponsive** (persistent or recurrent CIS within 12 months of adequate BCG): **radical cystectomy**.
- Unfit or unwilling: **pembrolizumab** (KEYNOTE-057: complete response 41%), nadofaragene firadenovec, gemcitabine–docetaxel.
- Prostatic urethral CIS: TURP plus BCG; ductal involvement needs cystoprostatectomy.

### Follow-up
- Cystoscopy and cytology 3-monthly for 2 years, then 6-monthly; upper-tract imaging.

**Diagram to draw:** flat versus papillary pathways of bladder cancer.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Flat, high-grade; TP53/RB; no FGFR3.
> - Untreated 40–83% progress; always high risk.
> - BCG with maintenance; cystectomy if BCG-unresponsive.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 12 · Bladder Cancer & Diversion

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Immediate post TURBT intravesical Therapy for TCC (SAQ)

- Instil within 6 h (at most 24 h); never BCG.
- SWOG S0337: 35% vs 47% recurrence with gemcitabine 2 g.
- Withhold if perforation; best for low-risk solitary tumours.

#### Management of Haemorrhagic cystitis. (SAQ)

- Acrolein (prevent with mesna); radiation endarteritis; BK virus.
- Alum first; formalin only after cystogram.
- Hyperbaric oxygen for radiation cystitis (RICH-ART); embolise if unstable.

#### Methods of preoperative bowel preparation. (SAQ)

- Mechanical lowers amount; antibiotics lower concentration.
- Nichols–Condon: neomycin + erythromycin.
- Intravenous antibiotics before incision are essential; ERAS omits routine MBP.

#### Pathogenesis, Clinical importance and Management of Carcinoma in situ urinary bladder. (SAQ)

- Flat, high-grade; TP53/RB; no FGFR3.
- Untreated 40–83% progress; always high risk.
- BCG with maintenance; cystectomy if BCG-unresponsive.

# Section 13 · Kidney & UTUC — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Upper Tract Urothelial Carcinoma (including TCC of Ureter): Diagnosis, Staging, Minimally Invasive, Endoscopic and Radical Management
**Asked as:**
- Discuss minimally invasive and endoscopic management of upper urinary tract malignancy. — *Summer 2016 Paper III Q1 (LAQ)*
- Discuss the diagnosis, staging and management of renal upper tract Urothelial tumor. — *Winter 2024 Paper III Q1 (LAQ)*
- Describe in detail TCC of ureter. — *Practice Set 5 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)

(also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 2, Q1, with emphasis on aetiology; also answered for the whole upper tract in 2023-Winter Paper 2, Q1 and 2024-Winter Paper 3, Q1)

*TCC (transitional cell carcinoma) of the ureter is taken as ureteric UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma).*

### Introduction and epidemiology
- **UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma)** is 5–10% of urothelial cancers. Pelvicalyceal tumours are about twice as common as ureteric ones. Incidence is rising, **driven by ureteric tumours**.
- Peak age 70–90; male:female 2:1.
- About 60% are invasive at diagnosis, because the upper tract has a thin muscle wall (the upper tract wall is thin). Concurrent bladder cancer in 17%.
- Bladder recurrence follows in 22–47%, mostly from downstream seeding.
- **Site (ureter): distal ureter 70%, mid 25%, proximal 5%**, explained by downstream implantation.
- Standard is **RNU (radical nephroureterectomy) with bladder cuff**; kidney-sparing versus radical surgery is chosen by **risk**.
- URS (ureteroscopy) biopsy is small and shallow, so it **cannot stage**. Stage is inferred from grade, endoscopic appearance, imaging and cytology together.

### Aetiology and pathogenesis
| Factor | Key point |
|---|---|
| **Tobacco** | Commonest; risk 2.5–7-fold (aromatic amines) |
| **Aristolochic acid** | Balkan and Chinese herb nephropathy; A:T → T:A transversions in TP53; often bilateral |
| **Lynch syndrome** | Mismatch repair genes (MSH2 highest risk); screen if young or family history |
| Occupational | Aniline dyes, benzidine, β-naphthylamine |
| Phenacetin | Papillary necrosis (history in 11% of ureteric tumours) |
- **Also know:** arsenic, cyclophosphamide, chronic stones (squamous carcinoma).
- **Intraluminal seeding** explains distal predominance and bladder recurrence (22–47%); **field change** explains multifocality.

### Pathology and spread
- 85% papillary, 15% sessile; WHO (World Health Organization) 2004/2016 low or high grade. Ureteric tumours are low grade and low stage in 55–75%.
- **Why ureteric tumours do worse:** the renal parenchyma is a barrier for pelvic tumours, but periureteric fat holds a rich lymphovascular plexus. At equal stage, ureteric tumours have more local and distant failure.
- Spread: direct, lymphatic (**mid and distal ureter → pelvic nodes**; upper ureter → hilar and retroperitoneal), blood (lung, liver, bone).

### Clinical features
- **Haematuria** (56–98%), **flank pain** (20–32%, dull from hydronephrosis/obstruction or colic from clots); lumbar mass about 10%; incidental about 15%.
- Weight loss, bone pain or a mass suggest advanced disease.

### Diagnosis (investigations)
**1. Cystoscopy:** always, to exclude coexisting bladder cancer (concurrent bladder cancer 17%). Positive cytology with a normal bladder and urethra means the upper tracts must be examined.

**2. Imaging**
| Test | Role and key points |
|---|---|
| **CTU (computed tomography urography)** | First line; best test; highest accuracy (sensitivity ~92%, specificity ~95%). Radiolucent **filling defect**, hydronephrosis, ureteric wall thickening, non-visualisation. Tumour ~46 HU (Hounsfield units) versus uric acid stone ~100 HU. Misses flat lesions (CIS, carcinoma in situ) |
| MRU (magnetic resonance urography) | If contrast or radiation is contraindicated; sensitivity ~75% for tumours <2 cm |
| Retrograde pyelography | Poorly filled calyces; accuracy ~75%; done with ureteroscopy. **Goblet (champagne-glass) sign**, the ureter dilated just below the tumour; **Bergman's sign**, the catheter coiling below it |
| FDG-PET/CT (fluorodeoxyglucose positron emission tomography) | Nodes and metastases when contrast is contraindicated |
| Renogram | Split function; contralateral kidney must be assessed |
- **Hydronephrosis** predicts invasive disease (invasive ureteric tumour in up to 80%).

**3. Urine cytology**
- Sensitivity ~65%, grade-dependent (grade 1 ~20%, grade 3 ~75%). **Selective** ureteric washings (selective ureteric cytology) are more accurate than voided urine. Take it before contrast. FISH (fluorescence in situ hybridisation) is optional for surveillance.

**4. Flexible URS (ureteroscopy) with biopsy:** the key test
- Adds accuracy (75–85% → ~90%). Biopsy gives **grade in ~90%** (grade correct in ~90%), but **stage cannot be assessed** (shallow samples; a Ta biopsy is often T3 at surgery).
- Indications: diagnostic doubt, planned kidney-sparing treatment, solitary kidney.
- Diagnostic URS before RNU (radical nephroureterectomy / nephroureterectomy) raises bladder recurrence, without changing survival.

### Staging (TNM [tumour, node, metastasis] 8th edition, 2017)
| T | Definition | Ureter |
|---|---|---|
| Ta / Tis | Non-invasive papillary / carcinoma in situ | Papillary non-invasive / carcinoma in situ |
| T1 | Subepithelial connective tissue | Subepithelial connective tissue |
| T2 | Muscularis | Muscularis |
| T3 | Pelvis: **peripelvic fat or renal parenchyma**; ureter: periureteric fat | Periureteric fat |
| T4 | Adjacent organs, or **through the kidney into perinephric fat** | Adjacent organs |
- **N1** single node ≤2 cm; **N2** node >2 cm or multiple. **M1** distant metastasis.
- **AJCC (American Joint Committee on Cancer) grouping:** stage I = T1, II = T2, III = T3; **any N+, T4 or M1 = stage IV**.
- **Grade:** WHO (World Health Organization) 2004/2016: PUNLMP (papillary urothelial neoplasm of low malignant potential), low grade, high grade.
- Regional nodes: pelvis and upper ureter → hilar and retroperitoneal; lower ureter → pelvic.

### Risk stratification
**EAU (European Association of Urology)**
| Low risk (all present / all needed) | High risk (any one) |
|---|---|
| Unifocal, under 2 cm (<2 cm) | Multifocal or 2 cm or more (another source: >2 cm; ≥2 cm) |
| Low-grade cytology and biopsy | High-grade cytology or biopsy |
| No invasion on CTU (computed tomography urography) | Hydronephrosis; invasion on CT / imaging |
| — | Prior (previous) radical cystectomy; variant histology |

- **AUA/SUO (American Urological Association/Society of Urologic Oncology) 2023** splits each group into favourable and unfavourable (obstruction, multifocality, lower-tract involvement). Ablation is **preferred** in low-risk favourable, **offered** in low-risk unfavourable, **rare** in high-risk favourable, **palliative** in high-risk unfavourable. Endoscopic therapy suits tumours **under 1.5 cm**.
- **Prognostic factors:** stage (strongest), grade, LVI (lymphovascular invasion), nodes, sessile architecture, necrosis >10%, ureteric site, surgical delay >12 weeks.

### Management
#### A. Kidney-sparing (minimally invasive and endoscopic) treatment
**Indications**
- **Elective**: low-risk disease, whatever the other kidney.
- **Imperative**: solitary kidney, bilateral tumours (bilateral disease), CKD (chronic kidney disease), unfit for RNU (unfit patients), after shared decision.
- The patient must accept frequent recurrence and **lifelong endoscopic surveillance**.

**Ureteroscopic (retrograde) management**
- Closed system, lowest morbidity; for small ureteric and renal tumours.
- **Steps**: cystoscopy → selective cytology and saline barbotage → no-touch semirigid then flexible URS → map all lesions → biopsy (3 Fr forceps or basket) into fixative → ablate the base with laser (**ureteroscopic laser ablation**, holmium or thulium, after biopsy) → stent optional.

| Laser | Penetration | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **Ho:YAG (holmium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet)** | Under 0.5 mm | Safe in thin ureter |
| **Nd:YAG (neodymium:YAG)** | Up to 5–6 mm | Coagulative necrosis; transmural injury risk |
| **Thulium** | Under 0.2 mm | Strong haemostasis |

- Avoid circumferential fulguration / ablation (stricture); stage large tumours.
- Results: upper-tract recurrence 23–90%, bladder recurrence 15–45%, progression 0–20%. **Grade is the strongest predictor.**

**Percutaneous (antegrade) management**
- Percutaneous resection for large pelvic tumours, lower-pole or inaccessible calyces and urinary diversions.
- **Steps**: retrograde catheter → puncture a calyx away from the tumour (upper or middle calyx for pelvic tumours) → dilate to 30 Fr → cold-cup, bipolar resection or laser; base sent separately → nephrostomy left → **second-look nephroscopy**.
- Removes more tumour and can **stage**; the tract allows topical therapy. But it is an **open system**: tract seeding about 0.3% (tract seeding risk), bleeding.

**Segmental ureterectomy**
- **Distal ureterectomy with bladder cuff and reimplantation** (psoas hitch or Boari flap) for distal tumours, including high-grade ones when the kidney must be saved. It gives a staging specimen; add pelvic lymphadenectomy (pelvic node dissection) if high grade.
- First examine the whole tract above for multifocality. Lower-ureteric tumours with unfavourable features need resection, not endoscopy.

| Site | Operation |
|---|---|
| **Distal ureter** | Distal ureterectomy with bladder cuff and reimplantation; psoas hitch (~5 cm extra) or Boari flap (10–15 cm) |
| Mid or proximal | Mid-ureteric resection: excision with 1–2 cm margins (frozen section margins) and ureteroureterostomy (rare) |
| Whole ureter | Ileal ureter or autotransplantation |
- Refluxing reimplant eases surveillance; non-refluxing limits seeding (debated).
- Laparoscopic or robotic routes are feasible; outcomes equal RNU in selected patients.
- Adjuvant upper-tract BCG (bacillus Calmette–Guérin) or MMC (mitomycin C); UGN-101 mitomycin gel for low grade. Lifelong endoscopic surveillance.

#### B. RNU (radical nephroureterectomy) with bladder cuff: standard for high risk
- **Principles**: kidney outside Gerota's fascia; clip the ureter early; never open the collecting system (avoid opening the tract); remove the **whole intramural ureter and cuff** (stump recurrence 30–75% if left); bag extraction intact; watertight bladder closure; template lymphadenectomy for high risk.
- **Laparoscopic and robotic RNU steps**: modified flank, 3–4 ports → reflect colon → clip ureter → artery then vein → kidney outside Gerota's fascia (adrenal spared) → ureter traced under the superior vesical artery → cuff excised (extravesical or open) → two-layer closure.
- Abandoned (higher recurrence): transurethral "pluck", stripping, laparoscopic staple ("pluck" and stapling techniques are abandoned).
- Open, laparoscopic and robotic routes are equivalent: laparoscopic oncologically **equivalent to open** (AUA/SUO), with less blood loss and shorter stay; less favoured in bulky T3/T4. Robotic yields more nodes.
- **Template lymphadenectomy** for high risk (hilar plus paracaval or para-aortic); pelvic nodes for lower ureteric tumours.

#### C. Adjuvant topical therapy
- **Single postoperative intravesical dose / chemotherapy** (AUA/SUO strong): **ODMIT-C (2011)** MMC (mitomycin C) 40 mg cut bladder recurrence 28% → 16% at 1 year; **Ito (2013)** pirarubicin, 42% → 17% at 2 years.
- **Upper-tract instillation** via nephrostomy (low pressure, 25 cm H2O), ureteric catheter or reflux up a double-J. **BCG (bacillus Calmette–Guérin)** (or MMC): 6-week course for upper-tract CIS (carcinoma in situ), and after complete ablation of high-risk favourable Ta/T1. Risks: sepsis, stricture.
- **UGN-101 (Jelmyto)**: mitomycin reverse-thermal gel, **FDA (Food and Drug Administration) 2020** for low-grade UTUC not manageable endoscopically. **OLYMPUS**: complete response 59%, 56% disease-free at 12 months; ureteric stricture about 44%.

#### D. Perioperative (systemic) chemotherapy
- **POUT (2020)**: adjuvant platinum after RNU for pT2–T4 or N+ improved disease-free survival: 3-year disease-free survival 71% vs 46%.
- Neoadjuvant platinum (chemotherapy) is preferred if renal function is borderline / when post-RNU renal function would exclude cisplatin (RNU removes cisplatin eligibility).

#### E. Metastatic
- Enfortumab vedotin + pembrolizumab (EV-302) first line; gemcitabine–platinum with avelumab maintenance; erdafitinib for FGFR3 alterations.

### Prognosis
- 5-year disease-specific survival: localised ~89%, regional ~63%, distant ~17%. Stage is the strongest factor.

### Surveillance and follow-up (AUA/SUO)
- **Kidney-sparing**: cystoscopy and URS within 3 months, URS again at 6 and 12 months; imaging 3–6-monthly (high risk) or 6–9-monthly (low risk), then yearly to 5 years (URS and CTU at closer intervals).
- **After RNU**: cystoscopy and cytology from 3 months; CT / CTU yearly (more often if pT2 or more).

### Recent advances
- Lynch syndrome screening; thulium fibre laser; single-use digital ureteroscopes.

**Diagram to draw:** ureteroscopic laser ablation of a pelvic tumour beside RNU with bladder cuff; TNM levels of a renal pelvic tumour (T3 parenchyma versus T4 perinephric fat), beside the diagnostic flowchart CTU → cystoscopy → URS biopsy → risk group; distal ureterectomy with psoas-hitch reimplantation, beside the goblet sign.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - CTU is the best test; URS biopsy gives grade, not stage.
> - Ureteroscopy is closed but cannot stage; percutaneous stages but can seed the tract.
> - Pelvic tumour into parenchyma is T3; through kidney to perinephric fat is T4; any N+ is stage IV.
> - EAU low risk needs all favourable features (unifocal, under 2 cm, low grade, no invasion); any single adverse feature makes it high risk.
> - Ureteric tumours: 70% distal; hydronephrosis predicts invasion (up to 80%).
> - Ureteric T3 does worse than pelvic T3 (no parenchymal barrier).
> - Distal ureterectomy for selected distal tumours.
> - RNU + cuff + single postoperative intravesical MMC: bladder recurrence 28% to 16% (ODMIT-C); adjuvant platinum (POUT).

## Discuss differential diagnosis of small renal tumours. Describe techniques to achieve hemostasis during laparoscopic partial nephrectomy.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2019-Summer Paper 3, Q1

### Definition and biology
- **SRM (small renal mass):** an enhancing solid or complex cystic renal mass **≤4 cm (cT1a)**, usually found incidentally.
- **Benign share falls with size:** 20–30% of masses ≤4 cm and about 40% of masses ≤2 cm are benign. At ≥7 cm few are benign and about half are high grade.
- Male sex triples the risk of malignancy. Under 2% of small cancers metastasise.

### Differential diagnosis
| Group | Entities | Pointers |
|---|---|---|
| RCC (renal cell carcinoma) | **Clear cell** (commonest), papillary (10–15%), chromophobe (3–5%), cystic RCC, rare molecular subtypes | Clear cell enhances strongly; papillary enhances weakly |
| Other malignant | Urothelial carcinoma, lymphoma, metastasis, sarcoma, epithelioid AML (angiomyolipoma) | Infiltrative growth |
| Benign solid | Oncocytoma, fat-poor AML, papillary adenoma, metanephric adenoma | See below |
| Cystic | Haemorrhagic or complex cyst, cystic nephroma | Bosniak category |
| Inflammatory, vascular | Abscess, focal pyelonephritis, tuberculosis; aneurysm, AVM (arteriovenous malformation), infarct, pseudotumour | Clinical picture |

- **Oncocytoma:** commonest benign enhancing SRM (up to half of benign SRMs). Central stellate scar and segmental enhancement inversion suggest it but are not specific. A core cannot separate it from chromophobe RCC, which has oncocytic areas. Sestamibi SPECT (single-photon emission computed tomography) is under evaluation.
- **Fat-poor AML:** no visible fat (fat below −20 HU [Hounsfield units] diagnoses classic AML); homogeneous, T2-dark, mimics RCC; biopsy HMB-45 and Melan-A positive.
- **Also know:** metanephric adenoma (hypovascular, polycythaemia, WT1 positive); papillary adenoma (≤15 mm).

### Narrowing the differential
- **Multiphase CT (computed tomography):** enhancement over 15–20 HU means solid tissue. MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) helps below 2 cm.
- **RMB (renal mass biopsy)** if it will change management (AUA [American Urological Association]); seeding almost unreported; non-diagnostic about 14%.
- Then surveillance, ablation or **PN (partial nephrectomy)**, preferred for cT1a.

### Haemostasis in LPN (laparoscopic partial nephrectomy)
**Principle:** a clear field for excision, then secure closure of the bed. Plan with R.E.N.A.L. score and 3D (three-dimensional) CT; intraoperative ultrasound.

**1. Vascular control**
| Technique | Method | Comment |
|---|---|---|
| Main artery ± vein clamp | Bulldog clamps, or laparoscopic Satinsky en bloc | Artery-only equals artery–vein. Aim warm ischaemia ≤25 min (trifecta) |
| Selective or super-selective | Clamp the tumour's segmental branch; 3D CT, NIRF (near-infrared fluorescence) | Avoids global ischaemia; no functional gain in an RCT (randomised controlled trial) |
| Off-clamp ("zero ischaemia") | No clamping; small exophytic tumours | More blood loss; same long-term eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) |
| Early unclamping | Release after the inner suture layer | Shorter ischaemia; possibly fewer pseudoaneurysms |
| Cold ischaemia | Ice slush, cold saline via ureteric catheter, cold arterial perfusion | Solitary kidney, CKD (chronic kidney disease), long repair; benefit unproven |

- Function depends mainly on parenchyma preserved. Mannitol: no benefit in an RCT. **Also know:** parenchymal clamp for peripheral tumours.

**2. Excision:** score the capsule, cut with cold scissors, clip visible vessels. Enucleation along the pseudocapsule bleeds less.

**3. Renorrhaphy (most reliable step)**
- **Inner layer:** running 3-0 or 2-0 absorbable suture closes cut vessels and collecting system (opened in about 60%).
- **Outer layer, sliding-clip technique:** mattress sutures anchored by a locking clip; clips slid down to the capsule give tension without knots. Pledgets prevent capsular tears; oxidised cellulose bolster fills the defect.
- **Barbed suture:** holds tension without knots, so ischaemia is shorter.
- Single layer gave better function and fewer pseudoaneurysms than two layers.

**4. Energy:** argon beam or bipolar coagulation of the bed. Argon can destroy collecting-system sutures and adds gas, so vent the ports.

**5. Haemostatic agents (adjuncts, no proven benefit in RCTs):** gelatin–thrombin matrix; fibrin sealant; oxidised cellulose; PEG (polyethylene glycol) hydrogel; cyanoacrylate. Sutureless closure only for selected small tumours.

**6. Final check:** unclamp, inspect, **lower pneumoperitoneum** to unmask venous bleeding; drain.

### Postoperative bleeding
- Early (hypotension, falling haemoglobin): resuscitate, CT, angioembolisation.
- **Delayed haematuria (about day 12):** pseudoaneurysm or arteriovenous fistula; selective angioembolisation preserves function. Endophytic and posterior tumours carry higher risk.

**Diagram to draw:** sliding-clip renorrhaphy over a bolster, with bulldog clamp on the artery.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - SRM ≤4 cm: 20–30% benign; ≤2 cm about 40%.
> - Oncocytoma is the commonest benign mimic; biopsy cannot exclude chromophobe RCC.
> - Trifecta: negative margin, no major complication, warm ischaemia ≤25 min.
> - Delayed haematuria after PN means pseudoaneurysm: angioembolise.

## Nephron-sparing surgery for renal masses: methods, recent advances and neoadjuvant therapy
**Asked as:**
- Describe various methods of Nephron sparing surgery in relation to Renal masses? — *Summer 2022 Paper III Q1 (LAQ)* (also answered in 2017-Summer Paper 1, Q1 and 2017-Winter Paper 4, Q3f)
- Recent advances in nephron sparing surgery. Role of neoadjuvant therapy. — *Practice Set 6 Paper III Q3f (SAQ)* (NSS also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q1)

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

### Definition and rationale (background)
- NSS (nephron-sparing surgery)/PN (partial nephrectomy): complete removal of the tumour with maximal preservation of functioning parenchyma.
- RN (radical nephrectomy) causes CKD (chronic kidney disease), which raises cardiovascular events and death. PN (partial nephrectomy) gives equal cancer control with better function.
- Function after PN depends mainly on the **parenchyma preserved**, less on ischaemia time. **Concept:** preserved parenchymal volume, not warm ischaemia time, predicts function.

### Indications

| Category | Situations |
|---|---|
| Imperative (absolute) | Solitary kidney; bilateral tumours; RN would mean dialysis |
| Relative | Contralateral kidney threatened (stones, renal artery stenosis, diabetes, hypertension); hereditary RCC (renal cell carcinoma) |
| Elective | cT1a with a normal contralateral kidney: **standard of care** |

- Now extended to cT1b–T2 when feasible (better function).
- **AUA (American Urological Association) 2021:** RN only if all three: high complexity; no CKD or proteinuria; normal contralateral kidney with eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) likely >45 after RN.
- **Relative contraindications:** <20% of nephron mass left; pedicle encasement; venous tumour thrombus; cT4; cN1.

### Preoperative planning
- Multiphase CT (computed tomography) or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging); chest imaging; eGFR, proteinuria.
- **R.E.N.A.L. score** (Radius, Exophytic, Nearness to collecting system, Anterior/posterior, Location to polar lines): 4–6 low, 7–9 moderate, 10–12 high. It predicts complications, ischaemia time and conversion. PADUA (Preoperative Aspects and Dimensions Used for an Anatomical) is an alternative.
- RMB (renal mass biopsy) when it changes management, and before (or at) ablation. 3D models shorten ischaemia (randomised trial).

### Methods

| Group | Methods |
|---|---|
| Surgical PN | Open, laparoscopic, robot-assisted (RAPN) |
| Excision technique | Standard (wedge) resection with a rim; enucleation; polar resection or heminephrectomy (≥30% of parenchyma) |
| Thermal ablation | Cryoablation, RFA (radiofrequency ablation), MWA (microwave ablation) |
| Developmental | SBRT (stereotactic body radiotherapy), HIFU (high-intensity focused ultrasound), irreversible electroporation |

### 1. Open PN: steps
1. Flank extraperitoneal or transperitoneal approach.
2. **Early hilar control** of artery, vein and accessory vessels, even if off-clamp is planned.
3. Defat kidney; **intraoperative ultrasound** maps borders and depth.
4. Clamp; **ice slush** if ischaemia is expected ≥25–30 minutes (solitary kidney, CKD).
5. Excise with a thin rim; a negative margin is the priority, its width is not.
6. **Two-layer renorrhaphy:** inner layer closes vessels and collecting system; outer capsular sutures over a haemostatic bolster.
7. Unclamp, check haemostasis, close Gerota fascia, drain.
- Suits complex tumours in a solitary kidney.

### 2. Laparoscopic and robotic PN
- First laparoscopic PN 1993 (Winfield). **Transperitoneal** for anterior tumours (more space); **retroperitoneal** for posterior tumours (direct hilum, no bowel mobilisation).
- **Robotic vs laparoscopic:** shorter warm ischaemia, fewer conversions. **Robotic vs open:** less blood loss, shorter stay, fewer complications; same margins and eGFR.
- Haemostasis: sliding-clip renorrhaphy, barbed sutures, sealants.
- **Trifecta:** negative margin + no major complication + warm ischaemia ≤25 minutes.

### 3. Enucleation vs standard resection

| | Standard PN | Enucleation |
|---|---|---|
| Plane | Through normal parenchyma (thin rim) | Along tumour pseudocapsule |
| Pros | Fewer positive margins | Less bleeding, maximal parenchyma, eases zero ischaemia |
| Use | Default | Hereditary RCC (e.g. VHL [von Hippel–Lindau]), multifocal, solitary kidney, severe CKD |

### 4. Vascular control strategies

| Strategy | Verdict |
|---|---|
| En bloc or artery-only clamp | Equivalent; traditional warm ischaemia <25–30 minutes |
| Early unclamping (after inner layer) | May reduce pseudoaneurysm |
| Selective or super-selective segmental clamping | Avoids global ischaemia; no functional gain in a randomised trial |
| Off-clamp (zero ischaemia) | Small exophytic tumours; more blood loss; no long-term eGFR benefit in randomised trials |
| Parenchymal compression | Peripheral tumours |
| Hypothermia, mannitol | No benefit with a normal contralateral kidney |

### 5. Thermal ablation
- Mostly **percutaneous** (CT or ultrasound guided). AUA 2021: alternative for **cT1a <3 cm**, especially elderly, comorbid, solitary kidney or VHL.
- **Cryoablation:** double freeze–thaw; cell death at ≤−40 °C; iceball 5–10 mm beyond the tumour. **RFA/MWA:** heat causes coagulative necrosis.
- Success = loss of enhancement on CT/MRI. Local recurrence is higher than PN but equal after repeat ablation.

### Recent advances

| Area | Advance and evidence |
|---|---|
| **Planning** | R.E.N.A.L. (Radius, Exophytic, Nearness, Anterior/posterior, Location) and PADUA (Preoperative Aspects and Dimensions Used for an Anatomical) scores; **3D (three-dimensional) models**: randomised trial showed shorter operation and ischaemia, less blood loss |
| **Platform** | **RAPN (robot-assisted PN):** shorter warm ischaemia than laparoscopic; less blood loss than open. **Single-port robot:** shorter stay, less opioid |
| **Vascular control** | Off-clamp, selective or super-selective clamping (guided by **NIRF (near-infrared fluorescence)** with indocyanine green), early unclamping. **Randomised trials: no long-term eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) gain** |
| **Excision** | **Enucleation** along the pseudocapsule: less blood loss; hereditary, multifocal, solitary kidney |
| **Reconstruction** | Sliding-clip and **barbed suture** renorrhaphy; single-layer gives better function, fewer pseudoaneurysms; sutureless with sealants in selected |
| Outcome reporting | **Trifecta:** negative margin + no major complication + warm ischaemia ≤25 minutes |

### Role of neoadjuvant therapy
- **Aim:** downsize a tumour so that PN becomes possible, mainly an **imperative indication** (solitary kidney, bilateral tumours) where radical nephrectomy means dialysis.
- **TKIs (tyrosine kinase inhibitors):** pazopanib, sunitinib, axitinib. Tumour volume falls **10–30%**; resection becomes possible in **40–45%**, including PN candidates. Best in clear cell RCC (renal cell carcinoma).
- **Toxicity:** serious adverse events about 20%, mainly **wound healing**. Stop TKIs several half-lives before and after surgery.
- **Checkpoint inhibitors:** nivolumab is safe but shrinks little; trials ongoing.
- **Status:** not standard (trials only); **upfront surgery whenever feasible**. Adjuvant pembrolizumab (KEYNOTE-564) suits high-risk resected disease.

### Complications of PN
- Urine leak (drain, stent); haemorrhage, **pseudoaneurysm** or AV (arteriovenous) fistula (angioembolisation); acute kidney injury.
- Positive margin 0–7%: surveillance. Local recurrence 1–2% (elective), 3–5% (imperative).
- Hyperfiltration proteinuria when >50% of a solitary kidney is lost: ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitors.

### Evidence
- **EORTC (European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer) 30904 (2011):** RN vs PN, ≤5 cm: PN better function, similar oncology; 10-year overall survival 81% RN vs 76% PN; trial flawed.
- **Also know:** hereditary RCC treated at 3 cm; surveillance for frail patients.

**Diagram to draw:** clamped renal artery, tumour excised with rim, two-layer renorrhaphy; RAPN with selective segmental clamp and renorrhaphy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Imperative, relative, elective; elective PN is standard for cT1a.
> - Preserved parenchyma, not clamp time, governs function; trifecta warm ischaemia ≤25 min (minutes).
> - Enucleation for hereditary/multifocal; ablation for cT1a <3 cm after biopsy.
> - Off-clamp and super-selective clamping: no long-term eGFR gain in randomised trials.
> - 3D models: randomised benefit.
> - EORTC 30904: PN better function, similar cancer control.
> - Neoadjuvant TKI shrinks tumours 10–30%; for imperative PN only.

# Section 13 · Kidney & UTUC — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Management of AML.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2021-Summer Paper 3, Q3d

*Assumed meaning: AML = renal angiomyolipoma (not acute myeloid leukaemia).*

### Introduction
- Benign **PEComa (perivascular epithelioid cell tumour)** of dysmorphic **vessels, smooth muscle and fat**. Female predominance; fourth–fifth decades.
- **Sporadic** (solitary, small) or with **TSC (tuberous sclerosis complex**; AML in 55–90%: younger, bilateral, multifocal, larger) or LAM (lymphangioleiomyomatosis).
- **Mechanism:** loss of **TSC2** (tuberin, 16p13) or TSC1 (hamartin, 9q34) → unchecked **mTOR (mammalian target of rapamycin)** → growth and angiogenesis.
- **Epithelioid AML**: little fat; aggressive (venous extension, metastasis) → treat as RCC (renal cell carcinoma).

### Clinical features
- Mostly incidental. Large tumours bleed: **Wunderlich syndrome** (spontaneous retroperitoneal haemorrhage; Lenk triad: flank pain, mass, shock). CKD (chronic kidney disease) in TSC.

### Diagnosis
- **CT (computed tomography): macroscopic fat (about −15 to −20 HU) on non-contrast images is diagnostic.**
- US (ultrasound) hyperechoic (so are some RCCs); MRI (magnetic resonance imaging): fat-suppression signal loss, India-ink artefact.
- Fat-poor lesion → MRI ± biopsy (HMB-45 (human melanoma black-45), Melan-A, smooth muscle actin positive).
- **Aneurysm ≥5 mm** on CT angiography predicts bleeding.

### Management algorithm (Restrepo 2022)
| Situation | Management |
|---|---|
| Acute haemorrhage | Resuscitate → **SAE (selective arterial embolisation)** → nephrectomy if SAE fails or unavailable |
| Symptomatic (pain, haematuria) or poor access to care | SAE, NSS (nephron-sparing surgery), ablation in selected cases |
| Asymptomatic sporadic with **>6 cm, aneurysm >5 mm, growth >0.25 cm/year, childbearing age or pregnancy** | Active treatment |
| None of these | **Surveillance**: US or CT at 6 and 12 months, then yearly |
| TSC, asymptomatic, **>3 cm** | **Everolimus**; MRI every 1–3 years |
- The old **4 cm** cut-off overtreats; EAU (European Association of Urology) 2023 favours surveillance for most.

### Treatments
- **SAE:** first line; **post-embolisation syndrome** (fever, pain, leucocytosis) common; abscess, infarction.
- **Partial nephrectomy:** preferred surgery, essential in TSC (multifocal, recurrent).
- **Thermal ablation** (radiofrequency, cryo): limited data.
- **Everolimus (EXIST-2):** ≥50% shrinkage in **42% vs 0%** (placebo); stomatitis, acne, hyperlipidaemia.

**Diagram to draw:** management algorithm by bleeding, symptoms, size and TSC.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Macroscopic fat on non-contrast CT is diagnostic.
> - Treat if >6 cm, aneurysm >5 mm, growth, pregnancy or symptoms.
> - SAE first for bleeding; partial nephrectomy preferred; everolimus for TSC >3 cm.

## Discuss the management of Metastatic Renal Cell Carcinoma.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 3, Q3b
(also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 2, Q1; immunotherapy in detail in 2024-Winter Paper 2, Q3e)

### Assessment
- CT chest, abdomen and pelvis; bone scan or brain MRI if symptomatic. **Biopsy** for histology.
- **IMDC (International Metastatic RCC Database Consortium) risk factors:** Karnofsky <80%; haemoglobin < normal; calcium > ULN (upper limit of normal); neutrophils > ULN; platelets > ULN; <1 year from diagnosis to systemic therapy.
- Favourable 0, intermediate 1–2, poor ≥3; median survival 43.2, 22.5 and 7.8 months.

### First-line systemic therapy (clear cell RCC; NCCN [National Comprehensive Cancer Network] and EAU [European Association of Urology])
| IMDC risk | Preferred | If IO (immuno-oncology) not possible |
|---|---|---|
| Favourable | Pembrolizumab + axitinib; nivolumab + cabozantinib; pembrolizumab + lenvatinib | Sunitinib, pazopanib; active surveillance for slow, low-volume disease |
| Intermediate/poor | Same three, **or nivolumab + ipilimumab** | Cabozantinib, sunitinib, pazopanib |
- **Rationale:** VHL (von Hippel–Lindau) loss → HIF (hypoxia-inducible factor) → VEGF (vascular endothelial growth factor), the target of TKIs (tyrosine kinase inhibitors). Checkpoint inhibitors release T-cell brakes.
- **Immunotherapy** trials (CheckMate 214, KEYNOTE-426, CheckMate 9ER, CLEAR) and toxicity: see 2024-Winter Paper 2, Q3e.

### Later lines: switch mechanism
- After IO: a VEGF TKI not used before (cabozantinib, axitinib, tivozanib) or **belzutifan** (HIF-2α inhibitor; LITESPARK-005).
- After TKI alone: nivolumab (CheckMate 025) or cabozantinib (METEOR).

### Cytoreductive nephrectomy (CN)
