- **CARMENA (2018):** sunitinib alone non-inferior to CN + sunitinib in intermediate/poor risk. **SURTIME:** deferred CN safe.
- Now: immediate CN only for fit, low-burden patients; **deferred CN** in responders; avoid in poor risk. Palliative nephrectomy or embolisation for bleeding.

### Metastasectomy and radiotherapy
- Complete resection of solitary or few metastases: 5-year survival 35–50%.
- **SBRT (stereotactic body radiotherapy)** for oligometastases; radiosurgery for brain; palliative radiotherapy for bone pain.

### Supportive and special groups
- Zoledronic acid or denosumab for bone metastases. Non-clear cell: trials, cabozantinib or sunitinib. Adjuvant pembrolizumab after complete metastasectomy (KEYNOTE-564).

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: biopsy + IMDC → IO combination → deferred CN in responders → switch class on progression.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - IMDC 6 factors; poor = ≥3.
> - IO/TKI for all risks; nivolumab + ipilimumab for intermediate/poor.
> - CARMENA and SURTIME: no routine upfront CN.
> - Belzutifan after IO and TKI.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 13 · Kidney & UTUC

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Upper Tract Urothelial Carcinoma (including TCC of Ureter): Diagnosis, Staging, Minimally Invasive, Endoscopic and Radical Management (LAQ)

- CTU is the best test; URS biopsy gives grade, not stage.
- Ureteroscopy is closed but cannot stage; percutaneous stages but can seed the tract.
- Pelvic tumour into parenchyma is T3; through kidney to perinephric fat is T4; any N+ is stage IV.
- EAU low risk needs all favourable features (unifocal, under 2 cm, low grade, no invasion); any single adverse feature makes it high risk.
- Ureteric tumours: 70% distal; hydronephrosis predicts invasion (up to 80%).
- Ureteric T3 does worse than pelvic T3 (no parenchymal barrier).
- Distal ureterectomy for selected distal tumours.
- RNU + cuff + single postoperative intravesical MMC: bladder recurrence 28% to 16% (ODMIT-C); adjuvant platinum (POUT).

#### Discuss differential diagnosis of small renal tumours. Describe techniques to achieve hemostasis during laparoscopic partial nephrectomy. (LAQ)

- SRM ≤4 cm: 20–30% benign; ≤2 cm about 40%.
- Oncocytoma is the commonest benign mimic; biopsy cannot exclude chromophobe RCC.
- Trifecta: negative margin, no major complication, warm ischaemia ≤25 min.
- Delayed haematuria after PN means pseudoaneurysm: angioembolise.

#### Nephron-sparing surgery for renal masses: methods, recent advances and neoadjuvant therapy (LAQ)

- Imperative, relative, elective; elective PN is standard for cT1a.
- Preserved parenchyma, not clamp time, governs function; trifecta warm ischaemia ≤25 min (minutes).
- Enucleation for hereditary/multifocal; ablation for cT1a <3 cm after biopsy.
- Off-clamp and super-selective clamping: no long-term eGFR gain in randomised trials.
- 3D models: randomised benefit.
- EORTC 30904: PN better function, similar cancer control.
- Neoadjuvant TKI shrinks tumours 10–30%; for imperative PN only.

#### Management of AML. (SAQ)

- Macroscopic fat on non-contrast CT is diagnostic.
- Treat if >6 cm, aneurysm >5 mm, growth, pregnancy or symptoms.
- SAE first for bleeding; partial nephrectomy preferred; everolimus for TSC >3 cm.

#### Discuss the management of Metastatic Renal Cell Carcinoma. (SAQ)

- IMDC 6 factors; poor = ≥3.
- IO/TKI for all risks; nivolumab + ipilimumab for intermediate/poor.
- CARMENA and SURTIME: no routine upfront CN.
- Belzutifan after IO and TKI.

# Section 15 · Renal, Nephrology & Transplant — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Discuss the role of tissue typing and cross match in live and cadaveric
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 3, Q2

*The question is incomplete; it is taken to mean "... live and cadaveric (deceased-donor) renal transplantation".*

### Introduction
- Two immune barriers decide compatibility: **preformed antibodies** (anti-A/B isoagglutinins and anti-HLA (human leukocyte antigen) antibodies) and **T-cell responses** to mismatched HLA.
- **Tissue typing** measures how well donor and recipient HLA match. **Antibody screening and crossmatch** detect preformed antibodies before the kidney meets them. Together they prevent **hyperacute rejection**, now practically eliminated.

### The HLA system
- HLA is the human MHC (major histocompatibility complex), a gene cluster on **chromosome 6 (short arm)**. Genes are inherited as two **haplotypes** (one from each parent) and expressed codominantly.

| | Class I | Class II |
|---|---|---|
| Loci | HLA-A, -B, -C | HLA-DR, -DQ, -DP |
| Expression | All nucleated cells | Antigen-presenting cells, endothelium |
| Presents to | CD8 (cytotoxic) T cells | CD4 (helper) T cells |

- **Sibling** chance: 25% two-haplotype identical, 50% one-haplotype, 25% none. A parent or child always shares one haplotype.

### Tissue typing methods
| Method | Principle | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **Serological** (microlymphocytotoxicity) | Lymphocytes + typed antisera + complement; dead cells take up dye | Low resolution; largely replaced |
| **Molecular** (DNA-based) | PCR (polymerase chain reaction) with SSP (sequence-specific primers), PCR-SSO (sequence-specific oligonucleotide probes), sequence-based typing, **NGS** (next-generation sequencing) | High (allele-level) resolution; now standard |

- **Matching:** usually reported as mismatches at **A, B and DR** (0–6). **DR matters most**, then B, then A; DQ increasingly typed. Fewer mismatches mean better graft survival and less sensitisation for a future transplant (Collaborative Transplant Study, Opelz). The effect is larger in deceased-donor grafts.

### ABO compatibility
- Same rules as transfusion: **O universal donor, AB universal recipient**.
- **A2 (non-A1) donors** can go to O or B recipients with low anti-A titre.
- **ABO-incompatible** living transplant is possible: lower titres (plasmapheresis or immunoadsorption, rituximab, IVIG (intravenous immunoglobulin)) to a centre target before surgery.

### Sensitisation and antibody screening
- Anti-HLA antibodies come from **pregnancy, blood transfusion, previous transplant** (and infection).
- **PRA (panel reactive antibody):** recipient serum tested against a panel of lymphocytes; result as % of panel killed.
- **Solid-phase (Luminex) single-antigen beads:** identify each antibody and its strength (MFI, mean fluorescence intensity). Antibodies against a donor are **DSA (donor-specific antibodies)**.
- **cPRA (calculated PRA):** % of donors the patient would be incompatible with, calculated from listed "unacceptable antigens". It replaced cell-panel PRA for allocation; very high cPRA gets allocation priority.

### Crossmatch
| Type | Method | Meaning if positive |
|---|---|---|
| **CDC** (complement-dependent cytotoxicity) | Donor T and B lymphocytes + recipient serum + complement; cell death scored. **DTT** (dithiothreitol) removes IgM autoantibody; AHG (antihuman globulin) increases sensitivity | **Positive T-cell CDC with IgG DSA = absolute contraindication** (hyperacute rejection; Patel and Terasaki, 1969) |
| **Flow cytometry crossmatch** | Antibody binding on donor cells detected by fluorescent anti-IgG | More sensitive; CDC-negative/flow-positive = higher risk of AMR (antibody-mediated rejection); desensitise or exchange |
| **Virtual crossmatch** | Compare recipient's bead-defined antibodies with donor HLA type | Speeds deceased-donor allocation; confirm by cell-based crossmatch |

- **B-cell positive** alone: class II antibody or non-specific binding; interpret with bead results.
- Crossmatch uses a **recent serum** plus historical peak sera.

### Living versus deceased donor
| | Living donor | Deceased (cadaveric) donor |
|---|---|---|
| Typing | ABO and HLA early in donor work-up; choose best of several donors | ABO and HLA at donor referral |
| Crossmatch | At work-up and a **final crossmatch shortly before surgery** | At the time of offer, often virtual first; done against the list rapidly (cold ischaemia) |
| Role of matching | Less critical: even unrelated living donors do better than well-matched deceased donors (Terasaki, 1995) | Allocation uses HLA match, sensitisation (cPRA), waiting time and donor quality (KDPI, Kidney Donor Profile Index) |
| If incompatible | **Paired (swap) exchange**, desensitisation, ABO-incompatible protocol | Patient waits; acceptable-mismatch programmes |

- **India:** swap (paired) donation is legal under the Transplantation of Human Organs (THOA) amendment, 2011.

### Managing the incompatible or sensitised recipient
- **Kidney paired donation**, including chains with altruistic donors.
- **Desensitisation:** plasmapheresis + low-dose IVIG, or high-dose IVIG, ± rituximab; **imlifidase** (cleaves IgG) for highly sensitised deceased-donor recipients (conditional EU approval). Desensitised HLA-incompatible living transplant gave better survival than waiting on dialysis (Montgomery, 2011).

### After transplant
- Monitor for **de novo DSA** (especially class II) with dd-cfDNA (donor-derived cell-free DNA) and biopsy; de novo DSA predicts graft loss.

### Recent advances
- **Eplet (epitope) matching**, high-resolution NGS typing, virtual crossmatch, imlifidase.

**Diagram to draw:** CDC crossmatch: donor lymphocytes + recipient serum + complement, dead cells take up dye (positive).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - HLA on chromosome 6; match A, B, DR; DR matters most.
> - Positive T-cell CDC crossmatch (IgG DSA) = no transplant: hyperacute rejection.
> - Flow and bead assays are more sensitive; cPRA guides allocation.
> - Incompatible pair: paired exchange (legal in India since 2011) or desensitisation.

## Vascular grafts and vascular surgery in urology
**Asked as:**
- Vascular Grafts in Urology. — *Summer 2021 Paper III Q2 (LAQ)*
- Vascular Surgery in Urology. — *Winter 2024 Paper III Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

(SAQ version answered in 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q3e)
(vascular grafts answered in 2021-Summer Paper 3, Q2)

*Assumed meaning: blood-vessel grafts and patches (conduits) used in urological surgery: transplantation, renal and IVC (inferior vena cava) tumour surgery, RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection), sarcoma, renovascular repair and autotransplantation, dialysis access and penile surgery. The chapters treat "vascular grafts" in this sense; tissue grafts (buccal mucosa) are not included.*

*Assumed meaning (vascular surgery): vascular procedures performed or met by the urologist (control and repair of vessels, caval surgery, transplant and renovascular surgery, penile vascular surgery and access), not vascular surgery as a specialty.*

### Introduction
- A vascular graft **replaces, bypasses, extends or widens** a vessel when primary repair would leave tension or narrowing.
- **Patch**: widens a partly resected wall. **Interposition graft**: replaces a segment. **Bypass**: routes flow around a lesion.

### Classification of graft materials
| Type | Examples | Advantages | Disadvantages |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Autologous vein** | Great saphenous, gonadal, superficial femoral, donor external iliac vein | Resists infection; suits small vessels and contaminated fields | Limited length; valves; harvest wound |
| **Autologous artery** | Internal iliac (hypogastric), **Carrel aortic patch** | Best for renal artery repair | Limited supply |
| **Allograft (homograft)** | Cadaveric vein or artery | Off the shelf; avoids xenograft | Availability; degeneration |
| **Xenograft (biological)** | **Bovine or porcine pericardium** | Pliable, strong patch, low infection | Patch only |
| **Synthetic** | **PTFE (polytetrafluoroethylene, Gore-Tex)**, ringed PTFE, Dacron (polyethylene terephthalate), collagen-impregnated Dacron | Large calibre, any length | Infection needs removal; venous grafts thrombose |

### Principles
- **Control first:** proximal and distal control with vessel loops, Rummel tourniquets and atraumatic clamps (bulldog, Satinsky, DeBakey). Artery before vein.
- **Grafts:** **autologous** vein or artery (tissue) for small vessels and contaminated fields; **synthetic** (PTFE or Dacron) for large, high-flow vessels with no bowel contamination.
- **Venous (low-pressure) grafts thrombose** more than arterial ones, so use the **widest graft** possible.
- Patch: **smooth side luminal**, rough thrombogenic side outward; edges must not invert.
- Systemic heparin before clamping; flush air and debris before tying; tension-free, everting, spatulated anastomosis with fine monofilament polypropylene (Prolene).
- **Sutures:** polypropylene (Prolene), double-armed. IVC or aorta 3-0/4-0; renal vessels 4-0/5-0; transplant anastomoses 5-0/6-0.
- Wrap synthetic grafts in **omentum** or retroperitoneal fat, away from bowel and urine.

### Applications in urology (setting → vascular procedure)
**1. Renal transplantation**
- Renal vein and artery end-to-side to external iliac vessels; Carrel patch; multiple arteries reconstructed on the bench.
- **Short right renal vein**: extend with donor IVC, free donor external iliac vein, or **spiral gonadal vein graft**.
- **Multiple arteries (18–43%)**: Carrel patch; on the back table, conjoined side-to-side or end-to-side reconstruction. **Preserve the lower-pole artery** (it supplies the ureter).
- **Atherosclerotic recipient iliac artery**: mount the graft artery on a **Gore-Tex or bovine pericardial patch**.
- **Children under 15 kg**: aorta and IVC anastomosis, transperitoneal.
- **Transplant renal artery stenosis (1–23%)**: angioplasty or stent first; surgical interposition or bypass risks graft loss.

**2. RCC (renal cell carcinoma) with IVC tumour thrombus**
- **Mayo levels 0–IV:** level I milking and partial caval clamp; II full caval control; III liver mobilisation ± venovenous bypass; IV cardiopulmonary bypass ± deep hypothermic arrest.

| Situation | Repair |
|---|---|
| Residual lumen ≥50% | Primary closure |
| Residual lumen <50% | **Patch cavoplasty**: bovine or autologous pericardium, PTFE, Dacron or saphenous vein |
| Circumferential invasion (wall invaded) | **Caval replacement: PTFE 16–20 mm**; contralateral renal vein reimplanted into the graft |
| Infrarenal bland thrombus | Filter or caval interruption, sparing lumbar collaterals |
- **Leibovich–Bower patch width** = normal caval diameter × % diameter resected × 3.
- Left tumour with suprarenal caval resection: the right kidney has no collaterals, so give it new drainage (autotransplantation, or **saphenous vein graft** to the splenic, portal or inferior mesenteric vein).
- After caval replacement: low-dose heparin, then **lifelong aspirin 81 mg**.

**3. PC-RPLND (post-chemotherapy RPLND)**
- Vascular procedures in **9–15%**; aorta most often.
- **IVC**: narrowing <25% → primary repair; >25% → patch or interposition graft. **Ligation is tolerated** (IVC may be ligated or patched; slow occlusion builds collaterals).
- **Aorta**: never ligated; must be repaired or grafted. Small injury → repair or pericardial patch; resection → **PTFE or Dacron** (about 1% need replacement). Graft also if the duodenum is injured, to prevent aortoduodenal fistula.

**4. Retroperitoneal sarcoma**
- Caval resection ± reconstruction (Dacron, PTFE, homograft); grafts **thrombose in 10–40%**; anticoagulate at least 3 months.

**5. Renovascular repair (disease) and autotransplantation**
- Ex vivo bench repair of branch aneurysm or stenosis (or ureteric loss) with internal iliac artery or saphenous vein, then **autotransplantation** (see Q3e). Angioplasty or stent first for fibromuscular dysplasia.
- Traumatic renal artery repair (main renal artery: endovascular stent): attempt open repair only for solitary kidney or bilateral injury (warm ischaemia <120 minutes).

**6. Dialysis access**
- Arteriovenous fistula first; **AV (arteriovenous) graft (PTFE)** when no native fistula is possible (veins are poor): usable in 2–3 weeks; more stenosis, thrombosis and infection than AVF (arteriovenous fistula).

**7. Iatrogenic vascular injury**
- SMA (superior mesenteric artery): repair or graft bypass. SMV (superior mesenteric vein): autologous vein graft; Gore-Tex only if no vein.

**8. Penile surgery (penis)**
- Priapism shunts: **Grayhack** saphenous vein–cavernosal shunt (priapism). Vein grafts for Peyronie tunical defects (survive by endothelial perfusion); synthetic grafts not recommended.
- **Arterial revascularisation** (inferior epigastric artery to dorsal artery) for young men with post-traumatic pudendal stenosis; venous ligation investigational.

**9. Endovascular**
- Angioembolisation for trauma, AML (angiomyolipoma) bleeding, pseudoaneurysm and AVF (arteriovenous fistula) after partial nephrectomy.

### Complications
- **Thrombosis**, anastomotic stenosis (intimal hyperplasia), bleeding, pseudoaneurysm.
- **Graft infection** (synthetic → usually removal); graft–enteric fistula.
- **Ureteric**: transient hydronephrosis after aorto-iliac grafts (12–20%), symptomatic stricture 1–2%, **ureteroarterial fistula** (treat with endovascular covered stent).

### Recent advances
- Endovascular covered stent grafts; bioengineered acellular vessels (under study); robotic autotransplantation.

**Diagram to draw:** IVC patch cavoplasty and PTFE caval replacement with left renal vein reimplanted; Mayo IVC thrombus levels with the matching vascular control.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Proximal and distal control, heparin, flush, everting Prolene anastomosis.
> - Autologous for small or contaminated fields; PTFE or Dacron for large vessels.
> - Mayo levels decide caval control and bypass.
> - IVC: lumen <50% → patch; invaded wall → PTFE 16–20 mm.
> - PC-RPLND: IVC may be ligated; aorta must be grafted.
> - Transplant: Carrel patch; preserve the lower-pole artery.
> - Bench surgery plus autotransplantation for complex renovascular lesions.

# Section 15 · Renal, Nephrology & Transplant — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Marginal Donor
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 3, Q3f

### Definition
- A donor whose kidney carries **more DGF (delayed graft function) and shorter graft survival** than a standard donor; used because of organ shortage.

### Categories
**1. ECD (expanded criteria donor), UNOS (United Network for Organ Sharing) 2002**: age **60 or over**, or **50–59 with 2 or more** of: hypertension, **creatinine over 1.5 mg/dL**, death from **CVA (cerebrovascular accident)**. Graft-loss risk about 70% higher.

**2. KDPI (Kidney Donor Profile Index) over 85%**: replaced ECD in the US (2014). **Longevity matching**: lowest-KDPI kidneys go to recipients with the best EPTS (Estimated Post-Transplant Survival).

**3. DCD (donation after circulatory death), Maastricht**
| Category | Description | Type |
|---|---|---|
| I / II | Dead on arrival / failed resuscitation | Uncontrolled |
| III | Arrest after withdrawal of support | **Controlled (commonest)** |
| IV | Arrest in a brain-dead donor | Either |
| V | Euthanasia | Controlled |
- Warm ischaemia raises DGF (roughly 40–50%); long-term survival is similar to DBD (donation after brain death).

**4. Others**: AKI (acute kidney injury) donors; **HCV (hepatitis C virus)-viraemic** donors with antivirals; **paediatric donors under 15 kg (en bloc)**; older living donors.
- **Excluded**: metastatic or haematological cancer, melanoma, untreatable viral infection.

### Assessment and use
- **Remuzzi biopsy score** (glomeruli, tubules, interstitium, vessels, 0–3 each): **0–3 single**, **4–6 dual kidney transplant**, **7 or more discard**.
- **HMP (hypothermic machine perfusion)** reduces DGF versus cold storage (Moers, 2009), without long-term survival difference; normothermic perfusion tests viability.
- **ESP (Eurotransplant Senior Program)**: donors 65 or over to recipients 65 or over, with short cold ischaemia.
- Recipient: informed consent; induction and delayed CNI (calcineurin inhibitor) if DGF.

### Urological relevance
- Older and ECD kidneys have more **urine leak and ureteric stricture** (ischaemic distal ureter), as do multiple arteries and DGF.

- **Ojo (2001)**: ECD transplantation improves survival over staying on dialysis.

**Diagram to draw:** Maastricht categories on a timeline of circulatory arrest.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ECD: 60 or over, or 50–59 with 2 of hypertension, creatinine over 1.5, CVA death.
> - Remuzzi 0–3 single, 4–6 dual, 7 or more discard.
> - HMP reduces DGF; Maastricht III is the commonest DCD.

## Tacrolimus.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q3c

### Introduction
- **Tacrolimus (FK506)**: macrolide CNI (calcineurin inhibitor) from *Streptomyces tsukubaensis*; about 10–100 times more potent than ciclosporin. With mycophenolate it is maintenance in **over 90%** of kidney recipients.

### Mechanism
```
Tacrolimus + FKBP12 (FK506-binding protein 12)
   → complex inhibits calcineurin (Ca²⁺/calmodulin phosphatase)
   → NFAT (nuclear factor of activated T cells) stays phosphorylated, cannot enter nucleus
   → no IL-2 (interleukin-2) transcription → T-cell activation blocked (signal 1)
```
- Ciclosporin binds **cyclophilin** instead; same end result.

### Pharmacokinetics
- Poor, variable oral bioavailability; reduced by food. Binds red cells, so **whole-blood trough (C0)** levels are monitored (narrow therapeutic index).
- Metabolised by **CYP3A4/3A5**; effluxed by **P-gp (P-glycoprotein)**. CYP3A5 expressers need higher doses.

### Formulations
- Immediate-release (twice daily); prolonged-release (Advagraf, once daily); LCP-tacrolimus (Envarsus, once daily, higher bioavailability); intravenous; topical. Once-daily forms aid adherence.

### Dosing and monitoring
- Started about 0.1–0.2 mg/kg/day in two doses, then adjusted to trough. Targets vary by centre and risk; **ELITE-Symphony low-dose arm: 3–7 ng/mL**.

### Toxicity
| System | Effect |
|---|---|
| Kidney | **Nephrotoxicity**: acute afferent vasoconstriction (reversible); chronic arteriolar hyalinosis, striped fibrosis; TMA (thrombotic microangiopathy) |
| Metabolic | **NODAT/PTDM (new-onset/post-transplant diabetes)**, more than ciclosporin; **hyperkalaemia**, **hypomagnesaemia**, hyperuricaemia |
| Neurological | **Tremor**, headache, paraesthesia, seizures, PRES (posterior reversible encephalopathy syndrome) |
| Others | Hypertension, GI (gastrointestinal) upset, alopecia, infection (CMV (cytomegalovirus), BK polyomavirus), malignancy (skin, PTLD (post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder)) |

### Drug interactions
- **Raise levels (CYP3A4 inhibitors):** azoles, clarithromycin/erythromycin, diltiazem, verapamil, protease inhibitors, grapefruit; diarrhoea.
- **Lower levels (inducers):** rifampicin, phenytoin, carbamazepine, St John's wort.
- Additive nephrotoxicity: NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs), aminoglycosides; ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitors add to hyperkalaemia.

### Versus ciclosporin
| | Tacrolimus | Ciclosporin |
|---|---|---|
| Acute rejection | Lower | Higher |
| Diabetes, tremor, alopecia | More | Less |
| Hirsutism, gum hypertrophy, hyperlipidaemia, hypertension | Less | More |
- **ELITE-Symphony (2007):** low-dose tacrolimus + mycophenolate + daclizumab + steroids gave the **lowest rejection, best GFR (glomerular filtration rate) and graft survival** at 1 year versus ciclosporin or sirolimus regimens.
- **Also know:** CNI minimisation with belatacept or everolimus; topical tacrolimus in genital dermatoses (lichen planus, Zoon balanitis).

**Diagram to draw:** T-cell showing FKBP12–calcineurin–NFAT–IL-2 pathway.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - FKBP12 → calcineurin → NFAT → IL-2 blocked.
> - CYP3A4/P-gp; trough-guided; many interactions.
> - Nephrotoxicity, NODAT, tremor, hyperkalaemia, hypomagnesaemia.
> - ELITE-Symphony: low-dose tacrolimus best.

## Assessment criteria for kidney donor.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q3a
(also answered in 2017-Summer Paper 2, Q3b)

*Assumed meaning: evaluation and acceptance criteria for a living kidney donor.*

### Principles
- **Donor safety first.** KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes) 2017 is risk-based: lifetime ESKD (end-stage kidney disease) risk vs the centre's threshold.
- Multidisciplinary team with an **independent donor advocate**.
- India: **THOA (Transplantation of Human Organs Act) 1994, amended 2011:** near relatives; others need authorisation committee approval; swap donation allowed.

### Assessment sequence

**1. Compatibility:** ABO group, HLA (human leukocyte antigen) typing, crossmatch, DSA (donor-specific antibody). Incompatible pair → paired exchange.

**2. Kidney function** (two methods, one measured, e.g. DTPA [diethylenetriamine pentaacetic acid] GFR)

| Test | Accept | Individualise | Decline |
|---|---|---|---|
| GFR (glomerular filtration rate), mL/min/1.73 m² | ≥90 | 60–89 | <60 |
| Albuminuria, ACR (albumin–creatinine ratio), mg/g | <30 | 30–100 | >100 |

- Urinalysis; persistent microscopic haematuria → cystoscopy, imaging, ± biopsy.

**3. Medical exclusions and limits**
- **Diabetes:** type 1 excludes; HbA1c (glycated haemoglobin) and OGTT (oral glucose tolerance test) if at risk.
- **Hypertension:** controlled on 1–2 drugs without organ damage may be acceptable.
- **Obesity:** individualised risk.
- **Stones:** metabolic work-up; bilateral stones generally exclude.
- Active cancer, chronic infection, cardiovascular disease exclude.

**4. Infection screen:** HIV (human immunodeficiency virus), hepatitis B and C, CMV (cytomegalovirus), syphilis, tuberculosis.

**5. Cardiopulmonary:** ECG (electrocardiogram), chest X-ray, echo.

**6. Age-appropriate cancer screening.**

**7. Anatomy:** **CT (computed tomography) angiography** for vessel number, early branching, stones, masses. Split function if asymmetric: **the better kidney stays with the donor**; left preferred (longer vein).

**8. Psychosocial:** voluntary, no coercion or payment.

### Counselling figures
- Function settles at 60–80% of baseline; mortality 3 per 10,000.
- **Muzaale (2014):** lifetime ESKD 90 per 10,000 donors vs 14 in matched healthy non-donors.

**Also know:** APOL1 (apolipoprotein L1) testing in African ancestry; more pre-eclampsia in later pregnancy.

**Diagram to draw:** donor work-up flowchart.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - KDIGO 2017: GFR ≥90 accept, <60 decline; ACR >100 decline.
> - Type 1 diabetes and bilateral stones exclude.
> - CT angiography; better kidney stays with the donor.
> - THOA: near relatives, else authorisation committee.

## Urological Complications post Kidney Transplantation.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 3, Q3d

### Introduction
- Urological complications are the **commonest surgical complications** (8–9.5%). Most arise from the **ureter and ureterovesical anastomosis**.
- The distal donor ureter depends on a single renal-artery blood supply, so ischaemia is the root cause. **Denervation** masks pain.
- A **routine stent** reduces leak and stricture; remove at **14–21 days** (earlier increases leak; later increases UTI [urinary tract infection]).

### Complications

| Complication | Key facts | Management |
|---|---|---|
| **Urine leak** (1.2–8.9%; commonest early) | Distal ureteric necrosis; lost lower-pole artery; technical | Fluid creatinine far above serum. Foley + PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy) + antegrade stent; early reimplant or native ureteroureterostomy if large |
| **Ureteric stricture** (0.5–9%; commonest beyond 3 months) | Ischaemia; long cold ischaemia, rejection, BK (BK polyomavirus) and CMV (cytomegalovirus) | Nephrostomy first; balloon or laser endoureterotomy; **open reimplant, native ureteroureterostomy or Boari flap best long-term** |
| **Lymphocele** (symptomatic 0.03–26%; peak 6 weeks) | Divided lymphatics; mTOR (mammalian target of rapamycin) inhibitors | Treat only if symptomatic; **laparoscopic peritoneal fenestration** best |
| VUR (vesicoureteric reflux) | 50–86% radiological; <1% significant | Treat cause; endoscopic injection low grade; reimplant |
| Haematuria | Anastomotic; persistent after biopsy = AV fistula | Self-limiting; selective embolisation |
| Stones (about 1%) | Hyperparathyroidism; calcineurin inhibitors | As native kidney; watch silent obstruction |
| UTI (commonest infection) | Stent, reflux, steroids | Treat 14–21 days if pyelonephritis |

- **Obstruction:** early is technical, oedema or compression (haematoma, lymphocele); late is stricture, stone or lymphocele.
- **Encrusted stent:** CT, nephrostomy, then PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy), RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery) or ESWL (shock-wave lithotripsy).
- **Lower tract:** bladder capacity recovers; BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) treated by TURP (transurethral resection of prostate) after 3 weeks, never with a stent in place.
- **Also know:** ED (erectile dysfunction) persists in 20–50%; bladder cancer risk about threefold; RCC (renal cell carcinoma) mostly in native kidneys; renal artery stenosis and thrombosis.

**Diagram to draw:** transplant kidney in iliac fossa showing leak, stricture and lymphocele sites.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ureter is the weak point; ischaemia is the cause.
> - Leak early, stricture late; open repair best.
> - Stent out at 2–3 weeks; laparoscopic fenestration for lymphocele.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 15 · Renal, Nephrology & Transplant

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Discuss the role of tissue typing and cross match in live and cadaveric (LAQ)

- HLA on chromosome 6; match A, B, DR; DR matters most.
- Positive T-cell CDC crossmatch (IgG DSA) = no transplant: hyperacute rejection.
- Flow and bead assays are more sensitive; cPRA guides allocation.
- Incompatible pair: paired exchange (legal in India since 2011) or desensitisation.

#### Vascular grafts and vascular surgery in urology (LAQ)

- Proximal and distal control, heparin, flush, everting Prolene anastomosis.
- Autologous for small or contaminated fields; PTFE or Dacron for large vessels.
- Mayo levels decide caval control and bypass.
- IVC: lumen <50% → patch; invaded wall → PTFE 16–20 mm.
- PC-RPLND: IVC may be ligated; aorta must be grafted.
- Transplant: Carrel patch; preserve the lower-pole artery.
- Bench surgery plus autotransplantation for complex renovascular lesions.

#### Marginal Donor (SAQ)

- ECD: 60 or over, or 50–59 with 2 of hypertension, creatinine over 1.5, CVA death.
- Remuzzi 0–3 single, 4–6 dual, 7 or more discard.
- HMP reduces DGF; Maastricht III is the commonest DCD.

#### Tacrolimus. (SAQ)

- FKBP12 → calcineurin → NFAT → IL-2 blocked.
- CYP3A4/P-gp; trough-guided; many interactions.
- Nephrotoxicity, NODAT, tremor, hyperkalaemia, hypomagnesaemia.
- ELITE-Symphony: low-dose tacrolimus best.

#### Assessment criteria for kidney donor. (SAQ)

- KDIGO 2017: GFR ≥90 accept, <60 decline; ACR >100 decline.
- Type 1 diabetes and bilateral stones exclude.
- CT angiography; better kidney stays with the donor.
- THOA: near relatives, else authorisation committee.

#### Urological Complications post Kidney Transplantation. (SAQ)

- Ureter is the weak point; ischaemia is the cause.
- Leak early, stricture late; open repair best.
- Stent out at 2–3 weeks; laparoscopic fenestration for lymphocele.

# Section 16 · BPH — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## TUR Syndrome.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q3f

### Definition
- **TUR (transurethral resection) syndrome:** neurological and cardiovascular disturbance from **absorption of hypo-osmolar, non-conducting irrigant** during monopolar TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate). About 1–2% in large series; falling.

### Pathophysiology
- Prostatic venous pressure is about **10 mmHg**; irrigant at higher pressure enters open sinuses, or the periprostatic space after perforation.
- Monopolar needs ion-free fluid: **glycine 1.5% (~200 mOsm/kg)**, sorbitol, water (plasma ~290).
- **Fluid overload** → hypertension, pulmonary oedema.
- **Dilutional hyponatraemia and hypo-osmolality** → water moves into brain → **cerebral oedema**.
- **Glycine toxicity**: ammonia → encephalopathy; inhibitory retinal neurotransmitter → **transient blindness**.
- Even routine resection absorbs 800–1000 mL.

### Risk factors
- Gland **>45 mL**, resection **>90 minutes** (cooperative study), high irrigation pressure, capsular perforation, sterile water.

### Clinical features
- Awake patient: restlessness, confusion, **nausea, vomiting**, **visual disturbance**.
- Cardiovascular: **hypertension and bradycardia**, later hypotension, tachypnoea, ECG (electrocardiogram) changes.
- Severe: seizures, coma, death. **Na <120 mEq/L** is dangerous.

### Prevention
- **Bipolar TURP or laser with saline** (eliminates it; NNT (number needed to treat) about 50).
- Bag **~60 cm** above patient (each extra 10 cm more than doubles absorption); continuous-flow resectoscope; low-pressure irrigation.
- Limit resection time (stage large glands); check sodium in long cases.
- Spinal anaesthesia for early detection; **1% ethanol tagging** (breath test) monitors absorption.

### Treatment
1. Stop surgery, achieve haemostasis, insert catheter.
2. ABC (airway, breathing, circulation); oxygen; monitor; send **sodium, osmolality**, glucose.
3. Fluid overload: **furosemide**; restrict fluids.
4. Severe neurological signs or Na <120: **3% saline 100 mL bolus** (raises Na 2–3 mEq/L), repeated at 10 minutes up to 300 mL. **Target rise 4–6 mEq/L.**
5. Correction limit: up to **12 mEq/L in 24 h** for acute hyponatraemia (8–10 mEq/L if symptoms begin >48 h after surgery), avoiding **osmotic demyelination (central pontine myelinolysis)**.
6. Seizures: benzodiazepines; ICU (intensive care unit) care.
- **Also know:** occurs in TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour), PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) and hysteroscopy; sorbitol → hyperglycaemia; mannitol → osmotic diuresis.

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: absorption → overload + hyponatraemia → cerebral oedema.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Venous pressure 10 mmHg; risk gland >45 mL, time >90 min.
> - Glycine: ammonia and transient blindness.
> - 3% saline 100 mL boluses; ≤12 mEq/L in 24 h.
> - Bipolar saline TURP prevents it.

## IPSS score
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q3e

### Definition
- **IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score):** validated, self-administered questionnaire grading male LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) over the past month.
- Based on the **AUA-SI (American Urological Association Symptom Index, 1992)**; adopted by WHO (World Health Organization) in 1993 by adding one quality-of-life question.

### Items

| No. | Symptom | Phase |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Incomplete emptying | Voiding |
| 2 | Frequency (again within 2 hours) | Storage |
| 3 | Intermittency | Voiding |
| 4 | Urgency | Storage |
| 5 | Weak stream | Voiding |
| 6 | Straining | Voiding |
| 7 | Nocturia | Storage |

- **Scoring:** items 0–5. 0 not at all; 1 less than 1 time in 5; 2 less than half the time; 3 about half; 4 more than half; 5 almost always. **Nocturia scored by number of times** (0 to ≥5).
- **Total 0–35:** voiding subscore 4 items, storage 3 (not validated).

| Severity | Score |
|---|---|
| Mild | 0–7 |
| Moderate | 8–19 |
| Severe | 20–35 |

- **QoL (quality of life)/bother question:** "If you were to spend the rest of your life with your urinary condition as it is now, how would you feel?" Scored 0 (delighted) to 6 (terrible); reported separately, outside the 35.

### Uses
- First step in LUTS work-up (AUA and EAU [European Association of Urology]); grades severity and bother.
- Guides treatment: mild, non-bothersome → watchful waiting; bothersome → medical therapy or surgery.
- Monitors response: **a 3-point fall is the minimum perceptible change**.
- Standard end point in trials.

### Limitations
- **Not disease-specific:** women score similarly; does not diagnose BOO (bladder outlet obstruction) or BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia); correlates poorly with urodynamics, prostate size and flow.
- No incontinence, pain or post-micturition items.
- Needs literacy.

### Alternatives
- **VPSS (Visual Prostate Symptom Score):** pictures, for low literacy.
- BPH Impact Index; ICIQ-MLUTS (International Consultation on Incontinence Questionnaire–Male LUTS); DAN-PSS (Danish Prostate Symptom Score); OAB-q (overactive bladder questionnaire); LURN (Lower Urinary Tract Research Network) SI-29 adds incontinence and pain.

**Diagram to draw:** IPSS chart with scoring columns.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 7 items × 0–5 = 0–35; bands 0–7, 8–19, 20–35.
> - QoL 0–6 is separate; IPSS = AUA-SI + QoL.
> - 3-point change is noticeable; IPSS does not diagnose BOO.

## Laparoscopic Trans vesical Prostatectomy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2025-Winter Paper 3, Q3d

*Assumed meaning: laparoscopic transvesical (suprapubic) simple prostatectomy for large BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia).*

### Introduction
- Laparoscopic version of the Freyer suprapubic operation: the adenoma is enucleated through a **cystotomy** in the **surgical capsule plane** (compressed peripheral zone). First LSP (laparoscopic simple prostatectomy): Mariano, 2002.

### Indications
- Gland **>80–100 g** with refractory LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms), retention, recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection), haematuria or obstructive nephropathy.
- **Transvesical route preferred** for a large intravesical median lobe, bladder stones or a diverticulum needing surgery.
- Work-up: PSA (prostate-specific antigen) ± MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) to exclude cancer; TRUS (transrectal ultrasound) volume; cystoscopy.

### Steps
1. General anaesthesia, supine, Trendelenburg. **Ports:** 10-mm umbilical camera, two 10-mm working ports, 5-mm and 12-mm assistant ports. Transperitoneal or extraperitoneal (Retzius).
2. **Cystotomy** with stay sutures; identify ureteric orifices.
3. **Circumferential bladder-neck incision** (start 3 to 9 o'clock posteriorly), away from orifices.
4. Develop the **posterior plane first** to the apex, then the lateral lobes.
5. Divide anterior commissure; **transect urethra just proximal to the verumontanum** (protects sphincter).
6. **Figure-of-eight sutures at 5 and 7 o'clock** (prostatic arteries enter posterolaterally).
7. **Bladder-neck mucosal advancement** to urethral mucosa covers the fossa and reduces bleeding.
8. 22 Fr three-way catheter; two-layer cystotomy closure.

### Outcomes versus open
- Less blood loss, shorter catheter time and stay, less analgesia; **same functional results** (IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score), flow rate, residual).
- Limitations: steep learning curve, poor ergonomics; **robotic simple prostatectomy** has largely replaced it. **Single-port transvesical robotic enucleation** (2.7 cm suprapubic incision, pneumovesicum) allows same-day discharge.

### Complications
- Bleeding (transfusion rare), urine leak, ureteric orifice injury, bladder neck contracture (<1%; open 2–6%).
- **Retrograde ejaculation 80–90%**; ED (erectile dysfunction) 3–5%; incontinence rare.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal view of cystotomy, bladder-neck incision and enucleation plane.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Indication: gland >80 g, especially with median lobe, stones or diverticulum.
> - Posterior plane first; divide urethra proximal to verumontanum.
> - 5 and 7 o'clock sutures plus mucosal advancement.
> - Same function as open, less morbidity.

## Lasers in BPH, including HoLEP
**Asked as:**
- Various lasers in treatment of BPH — *Practice Set 6 Paper III Q3a (SAQ)*
- HOLEP — *Practice Set 7 Paper III Q3f (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 2, Q3a; also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3d)

*HOLEP read as: holmium laser enucleation of the prostate for BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia).*

### Principle
- LASER (light amplification by stimulated emission of radiation) turns light into local heat. The **chromophore** (water or haemoglobin) and wavelength decide **penetration depth**.
- About 60 °C coagulates (delayed sloughing); 100 °C or more vaporises. Coagulation lasers are obsolete.
- **HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation of the prostate)** removes the whole adenoma along the **surgical capsule**: the endoscopic equivalent of open simple prostatectomy.
- **Ho:YAG (holmium:yttrium–aluminium–garnet), 2120 nm**: water absorbs it; pulsed; **0.4 mm penetration**; coagulates vessels. Saline irrigation, so no TUR (transurethral resection) syndrome.

### Lasers used in BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia)

| Laser | Wavelength | Chromophore | Penetration | Procedure |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Ho:YAG** (holmium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet), pulsed | 2120 nm | Water | 0.4 mm | **HoLEP** (holmium laser enucleation of the prostate) |
| **Thulium:YAG** (continuous) / **TFL** (thulium fibre laser) | 2013 / 1940 nm | Water (TFL at absorption peak, about 4× holmium) | 0.2 mm | ThuLEP (enucleation), ThuVEP (vapoenucleation), ThuVARP (vaporesection) |
| **KTP (potassium titanyl phosphate) / LBO (lithium triborate)** | 532 nm | Haemoglobin | 0.8 mm | **PVP** (photoselective vaporisation), 120–180 W |
| Diode | Various | Mixed | 0.5–5 mm | Vaporisation |
| Nd:YAG (neodymium) | 1064 nm | — | About 1 cm | Abandoned (sloughing) |

- **KTP:** Nd:YAG beam halved to 532 nm by a crystal; haemoglobin absorbs it, so vaporisation is bloodless.

### HoLEP
#### Indications
- **Size-independent** (<30, 30–80, >80 mL), with or without median lobe.
- **Anticoagulated or high bleeding risk** (AUA [American Urological Association]); alternative to open prostatectomy for large glands.

#### Steps
- 60–150 W laser, 550 µm end-firing fibre, 24–28 Fr scope; **2.0 J × 40–50 Hz** for enucleation.
1. Bladder-neck incisions at 5 and 7 o'clock (median lobe) or one at 6 o'clock.
2. Perimontanal incision; **early apical release** protects the sphincter.
3. Develop the capsular plane to 12 o'clock; push lobes into the bladder (**en bloc, two- or three-lobe**).
4. Haemostasis with a defocused beam.
5. **Morcellation** with a full bladder; three-way catheter.

#### Limitation
- **Steep learning curve** (about 50 cases).

### Evidence and outcomes
- **HoLEP:** endoscopic equivalent of open prostatectomy; **size-independent**. The only endoscopic procedure **superior to TURP** (versus TURP) (transurethral resection of the prostate) for IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) and Qmax (maximum flow rate): **better IPSS and Qmax**, durable. Less bleeding, shorter catheter time and stay; longer operating time; steep learning curve. Retreatment rare (whole transition zone removed).
- **ThuLEP:** equals HoLEP.
- **GOLIATH** (180 W PVP vs TURP, about 270 men, ≤100 cc): PVP non-inferior at 2 years; shorter catheter time and stay. Retreatment high above 80 cc.
- **AUA (American Urological Association):** HoLEP or ThuLEP for any size; PVP for under 80 cc; HoLEP, ThuLEP or PVP for **higher bleeding risk** (anticoagulated men).

### Complications
- Dysuria and storage symptoms (worst after PVP); transient storage symptoms and early / transient stress incontinence (SUI, stress urinary incontinence); retrograde ejaculation in most (**retrograde ejaculation about 75%** after HoLEP); bladder neck contracture (BNC) 0–3.2%; stricture; capsular perforation; ureteric orifice injury; **morcellator bladder injury**. No TUR (transurethral resection) syndrome (saline).
- Erectile function unchanged.
- **Safety:** wavelength-specific eyewear for all; laser safety officer.

### Recent advances
- **Moses pulse modulation**; ThuLEP (thulium enucleation); median-lobe-only HoLEP (antegrade ejaculation about 90%); same-day discharge.

**Diagram to draw:** HoLEP: adenoma enucleated to the capsule, pushed into bladder, morcellated; sagittal prostate with enucleation plane along the capsule.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Penetration: thulium 0.2, holmium 0.4, KTP 0.8 mm, Nd:YAG 1 cm.
> - Ho:YAG 2120 nm, water chromophore, 0.4 mm.
> - HoLEP is size-independent and superior to TURP (only endoscopic method superior to TURP); suits anticoagulated men.
> - Retrograde ejaculation about 75%.
> - GOLIATH: PVP non-inferior to TURP; avoid PVP above 80 cc.

## Describe in details anatomy of prostate with special reference to newer concept in surgical management of localized CA prostate
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 7 Paper 3, Q3b
(also answered in Practice Set 2 Paper 4, Q1)

### Anatomy
- About 20 g; base at bladder neck, apex at urogenital diaphragm. **No true capsule**.

| Zone (McNeal) | Glands | Relevance |
|---|---|---|
| Peripheral | 70% | **70% of cancers**; posterolateral extension along nerves |
| Central | 25% | Around ejaculatory ducts |
| Transition | 5% | BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia); anterior cancers |
| AFMS (anterior fibromuscular stroma) | None | Anterior margin |

- **Fascial planes:** intrafascial (most nerves, more margins), **interfascial (standard)**, extrafascial (widest margin). Denonvilliers fascia excised if posterior extension suspected.
- **NVB (neurovascular bundle):** cavernous nerves posterolateral, **5 and 7 o'clock** at the apex; diffuse "hammock" distribution. Injured by heat and traction.
- **Accessory pudendal arteries:** preserve. **DVC (dorsal venous complex)** tamponaded by pneumoperitoneum.
- **Continence:** striated sphincter at apex; anterior support (puboprostatic ligaments, detrusor apron).
- **Lymphatics:** obturator, external and internal iliac, presacral nodes.

### Newer surgical concepts
1. **RARP (robot-assisted radical prostatectomy):** Yaxley 2016 RCT (randomised controlled trial): function equal to open, less blood loss.
2. **Risk-graded nerve sparing** using mpMRI (multiparametric MRI) and biopsy, side by side.
3. **NeuroSAFE:** frozen section of posterolateral margin guides nerve sparing.
4. **Retzius-sparing** (Bocciardi 2010), through the pouch of Douglas: earlier continence, more margins anteriorly; **Hood technique** preserves anterior support.
5. **Apical dissection:** maximal urethral length, cold division (commonest margin site); posterior (Rocco) reconstruction.
6. **Extended PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection)** when nodal risk >5% (EAU [European Association of Urology]): captures about 63% of drainage; staging, not therapeutic.
7. **Single-port robot**, extraperitoneal; **focal therapy** of the index lesion (HIFU [high-intensity focused ultrasound], cryotherapy).

**Diagram to draw:** axial prostate with three fascial planes and NVB at 5 and 7 o'clock.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Peripheral zone: 70% of cancers; no true capsule.
> - Interfascial plane standard; apex commonest margin site.
> - Retzius-sparing: early continence, more margins.

# Section 17 · Prostate Cancer — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Management of newly Metastatic prostate cancer
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2025-Winter Paper 3, Q3b

### Definitions
- **mHSPC (metastatic hormone-sensitive prostate cancer):** no prior hormones. **Synchronous** (at diagnosis) or **metachronous** (after local therapy).
- **CHAARTED high volume:** ≥4 bone metastases with ≥1 outside the axial skeleton, or any visceral metastasis.
- **LATITUDE high risk:** ≥2 of Gleason ≥8, ≥3 bone lesions, visceral metastasis.

### Work-up
- CT (computed tomography) and bone scan; PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET (positron emission tomography) finds more, but trials used conventional imaging.
- **Germline and somatic testing** for all (>10% have DNA-repair mutations).

### Systemic therapy
- **ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) is the backbone, never alone:** LHRH (luteinising hormone-releasing hormone) agonist with antiandrogen flare cover, or antagonist/orchidectomy (for impending cord compression).
- **Doublets:** ADT plus one of:

| Agent | Trial | Overall survival HR (hazard ratio) |
|---|---|---|
| Docetaxel 75 mg/m², 6 cycles | **CHAARTED** (benefit in high volume only); STAMPEDE | 0.61; 0.78 |
| Abiraterone 1000 mg + prednisone 5 mg | **LATITUDE**; STAMPEDE arm G | 0.62; 0.63 |
| Enzalutamide | ARCHES (radiographic PFS (progression-free survival)); ENZAMET | 0.67 (ENZAMET) |
| Apalutamide | **TITAN** | 0.67 |

- Hormonal agents work **regardless of volume**; docetaxel needs high volume (GETUG-AFU 15 negative).
- **Triplets** (ADT + docetaxel + abiraterone or darolutamide): **PEACE-1** (abiraterone, synchronous; overall survival HR 0.75 with docetaxel) and **ARASENS** (darolutamide; overall survival HR 0.68). For chemotherapy-fit men, especially high volume.

### Local and directed therapy
- **RT (radiotherapy) to the prostate in low-volume synchronous disease:** STAMPEDE arm H, survival HR 0.68 (3-year 81% vs 73%); HORRAD negative overall.
- Cytoreductive prostatectomy: trials only. **MDT (metastasis-directed therapy)** for oligometastases (STOMP, ORIOLE) delays ADT.

### Follow-up
- PSA (prostate-specific antigen) and bloods 3–6-monthly; testosterone if PSA rises (rising PSA with castrate testosterone = CRPC (castration-resistant prostate cancer)); bone and cardiovascular health.
- *Guideline note:* the chapter lists ADT plus docetaxel as an option; current EAU (European Association of Urology) guidance gives docetaxel only within a triplet.

**Diagram to draw:** algorithm splitting by volume and fitness to doublet or triplet ± prostate RT.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ADT alone is no longer standard.
> - CHAARTED volume vs LATITUDE risk definitions.
> - Triplets: PEACE-1, ARASENS.
> - Prostate RT only for low-volume synchronous disease (STAMPEDE arm H).

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 17 · Prostate Cancer

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Management of newly Metastatic prostate cancer (SAQ)

- ADT alone is no longer standard.
- CHAARTED volume vs LATITUDE risk definitions.
- Triplets: PEACE-1, ARASENS.
- Prostate RT only for low-volume synchronous disease (STAMPEDE arm H).
